JSS1 ENGLISH STUDIES LESSON NOTES FIRST TERM

WEEK 1 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO SPEECH; ORGANS OF SPEECH

WHAT IS SPEECH? Speech is the ability to produce sounds using the vocal organs to communicate ideas, thoughts, and feelings.
Components of Speech:

Sound production

Voice modulation

Articulation (clear pronunciation)

Intonation (rise and fall of voice)

THE ORGANS OF SPEECH:

THE LUNGS:

Provide air for speech

Push air through windpipe

Control breath for speaking

THE LARYNX (Voice Box):

Located in the throat

Contains vocal cords

Produces voice/sound
Controls pitch

THE VOCAL CORDS:

Two bands of muscle in larynx

Vibrate to create sound

Open for breathing, close for speaking

THE PHARYNX (Throat):

Passage between mouth and larynx

Resonates sound

Shapes voice quality

THE SOFT PALATE (Velum):

Soft back part of roof of mouth

Raises/lowers to direct air

Controls nasal vs oral sounds

THE HARD PALATE:

Hard front part of roof of mouth

Helps shape sounds

Tongue touches it for certain sounds

THE TONGUE:

Most important articulator

Very flexible, moves in all directions

Different parts: tip, blade, front, back

Shapes vowels and consonants

THE TEETH:
Upper and lower teeth

Help produce sounds like /f/, /v/, /θ/, /ð/

Tongue touches teeth for some sounds

THE LIPS:

Shape the mouth opening

Round for sounds like /w/, /u:/

Spread for sounds like /i:/

Press together for /p/, /b/, /m/

THE ALVEOLAR RIDGE:

Bumpy ridge behind upper teeth

Tongue touches it for /t/, /d/, /n/, /l/, /s/, /z/

THE JAW:

Opens and closes mouth

Lowers for open sounds

Position affects sound quality

THE NASAL CAVITY:

Space behind nose

Produces nasal sounds /m/, /n/, /ŋ/

Air passes through for nasal consonants

HOW SPEECH IS PRODUCED:

Three Processes:

RESPIRATION (Breathing):
Lungs provide air

Air pushed up through windpipe

Controlled exhalation for speech

PHONATION (Voice Production):

Air passes through larynx

Vocal cords vibrate

Creates voiced sounds

ARTICULATION (Sound Shaping):

Organs modify airflow

Tongue, lips, teeth shape sounds

Creates specific phonemes

TYPES OF SPEECH SOUNDS:

VOWELS:

Air flows freely

No obstruction

Voice always used

Examples: /a/, /e/, /i/, /o/, /u/

CONSONANTS:

Air partially or completely blocked

Uses articulators (tongue, lips, teeth)

May be voiced or voiceless

Examples: /p/, /t/, /k/, /b/, /d/, /g/

ARTICULATORS:

Active Articulators (Move):

Tongue (most mobile)

Lower lip

Soft palate

Lower jaw

Passive Articulators (Stationary):

Upper teeth

Alveolar ridge

Hard palate

Upper lip (less mobile)

IMPORTANCE OF ORGANS OF SPEECH:

Enable human communication

Allow language production

Differentiate humans from animals

Create variety of sounds

Express emotions and ideas

EVALUATION:

Define speech and state three components of speech production

List and explain the functions of five organs of speech
Describe the three processes involved in producing speech sounds

What is the difference between vowels and consonants in terms of speech production?

Draw and label a simple diagram showing five organs of speech

ASSIGNMENT:

List all twelve organs of speech and write one sentence describing each organ's function

Explain the role of the tongue in speech production (5 sentences)

Identify which organs of speech are used to produce these sounds: (a) /p/ (b) /n/ (c) /s/ (d)
/m/ (e) /l/

Describe how air flows from the lungs to produce speech sounds (8 sentences)

Why are the organs of speech important for communication? Give five reasons
WEEK 1 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: PARTS OF SPEECH; NOUNS – MEANING; TYPES OF NOUNS WITH EXAMPLES

PARTS OF SPEECH:

The parts of speech are the categories into which words are classified based on their function in a sentence.
The Eight Parts of Speech:

Noun – naming word

Pronoun – replaces noun

Verb – action/state word

Adjective – describes noun

Adverb – modifies verb/adjective

Preposition – shows relationship

Conjunction – connects words/sentences

Interjection – expresses emotion

NOUNS:

Definition: A noun is a naming word. It names people, places, things, animals, ideas, or qualities.

Examples:

People: boy, teacher, Mary, doctor

Places: school, Lagos, market, home

Things: book, table, car, phone
Animals: dog, cat, lion, bird

Ideas: love, happiness, freedom, honesty

TYPES OF NOUNS:

COMMON NOUNS: General names for people, places, things, or animals. They do not start with capital letters (unless at the beginning of a sentence).
Examples:

boy, girl, man, woman, teacher, student

city, school, market, house, river

book, pen, car, table, chair

dog, cat, bird, fish, cow

Sentences:

The boy went to school

A teacher gave me a book

The dog ate its food

PROPER NOUNS: Specific names of particular people, places, or things. They always begin with capital letters.
Examples:

People: John, Mary, Dr. Adeleke, President Buhari

Places: Nigeria, Lagos, River Niger, Aso Rock

Days: Monday, Tuesday, Sunday

Months: January, February, December
Organizations: United Nations, Red Cross

Titles: Mr., Mrs., Dr., Chief

Sentences:

John lives in Lagos

Mary goes to Queen's College

We celebrate on Christmas Day

ABSTRACT NOUNS: Names of things we cannot see, touch, or measure but can feel or think about. They represent ideas, qualities, feelings, or states.
Examples:

Feelings: love, happiness, sadness, anger, joy, fear

Qualities: honesty, bravery, kindness, wisdom, beauty

Ideas: freedom, justice, peace, democracy

States: childhood, sleep, death, life

Sentences:

Honesty is the best policy

Love conquers all

Wisdom comes with age

His bravery saved many lives

CONCRETE NOUNS: Names of things we can see, touch, smell, taste, or hear. They are physical and tangible.
Examples:
Things we see: tree, house, book, car

Things we touch: stone, cloth, water

Things we smell: flower, perfume, food

Things we taste: sugar, salt, bread

Things we hear: bell, music, thunder

Sentences:

The tree is tall

I touched the stone

The flower smells sweet

COLLECTIVE NOUNS: Names for groups of people, animals, or things considered as one unit.
Common Examples: People:
a team of players

a class of students

a crowd of people

a choir of singers

an army of soldiers

a crew of sailors

Animals:

a herd of cattle/elephants

a flock of sheep/birds
a pack of wolves/dogs

a swarm of bees/insects

a pride of lions

a school of fish

Things:

a bunch of keys/flowers

a bundle of sticks

a pack of cards

a library of books

Sentences:

The team won the match

A flock of birds flew away

The class is studying

COUNTABLE NOUNS: Nouns that can be counted and have both singular and plural forms.

Examples:

Singular: book, pen, boy, dog, house

Plural: books, pens, boys, dogs, houses

Rules:

Can use numbers: one book, two books, three dogs

Can use "a/an": a pen, an apple

Can use "many/few": many books, few students

Sentences:
I have three books

There are many students in class

She bought five oranges

UNCOUNTABLE NOUNS (Mass Nouns): Nouns that cannot be counted individually. They have no plural form.
Examples:

Liquids: water, milk, oil, tea, coffee

Substances: sugar, salt, rice, flour, sand

Abstract: advice, information, knowledge, news

Materials: wood, paper, gold, silver

Rules:

Cannot use numbers: ✗ two waters, ✗ three rices

Cannot use "a/an": ✗ a rice, ✗ an advice

Use "much/little": much water, little sugar

Use "some/any": some rice, any milk

Sentences:

I need some water

She gave me good advice

There is little sugar left

Note: To count uncountable nouns, use measurement words:

a cup of tea, two cups of tea

a bag of rice, three bags of rice
a piece of advice, some pieces of advice

FORMING PLURALS:

Regular Rules:

Add -s: book → books, pen → pens

Add -es (ending in s, x, z, ch, sh): class → classes, box → boxes

Consonant + y → change y to ies: baby → babies, city → cities

Vowel + y → add s: boy → boys, key → keys

f/fe → change to ves: knife → knives, wife → wives

Irregular Plurals:

man → men, woman → women, child → children

tooth → teeth, foot → feet, mouse → mice

sheep → sheep, fish → fish, deer → deer

EVALUATION:

Define noun and give five examples of each type: common, proper, and abstract nouns

What is the difference between countable and uncountable nouns? Give three examples of each
Write the collective nouns for: (a) sheep (b) cattle (c) students (d) bees (e) singers

Identify the types of nouns in these sentences: "John showed great courage when he saved the child from the fire"
Write the plural forms of: (a) baby (b) knife (c) sheep (d) woman (e) box
ASSIGNMENT:

List the eight parts of speech with one example for each

Write ten common nouns, ten proper nouns, and ten abstract nouns

Give five examples of collective nouns and use each in a sentence

Explain the difference between concrete and abstract nouns with five examples each

Identify and classify all nouns in this passage: "Mary went to the market in Lagos with her mother. They bought rice, tomatoes, and fish. The crowd was large, and Mary felt happiness when they returned home."

WEEK 1 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: THE FAMILY; VOCABULARY – INTRODUCTION TO LANGUAGE SKILLS (LSRW)

PART A: THE FAMILY

DEFINITION: A family is a group of people related by blood, marriage, or adoption who live together or maintain close relationships.

TYPES OF FAMILIES

Nuclear Family

Father, mother, and children

Small family unit

Common in urban areas

Extended Family

Includes grandparents, aunts, uncles, cousins
Large family unit

Several generations together

Common in African culture

Single-Parent Family

One parent raising children alone

Due to death, divorce, or choice

Blended/Step Family

Parents remarried with children from previous marriages

Children have step-parents and step-siblings

FAMILY MEMBERS AND RELATIONSHIPS

Immediate Family:

Father/Dad/Papa – male parent

Mother/Mum/Mama – female parent

Son – male child

Daughter – female child

Brother – male sibling

Sister – female sibling

Extended Family:

Grandfather/Grandpa – father's/mother's father

Grandmother/Grandma – father's/mother's mother

Uncle – father's/mother's brother, aunt's husband

Aunt/Aunty – father's/mother's sister, uncle's wife
Cousin – uncle's/aunt's child

Nephew – brother's/sister's son

Niece – brother's/sister's daughter

In-laws – spouse's family (father-in-law, mother-in-law, brother-in-law, sister-in-law)

FAMILY ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES

Father:

Provides for family

Protects family members

Guides and disciplines children

Makes important decisions

Role model

Mother:

Cares for children

Manages household

Prepares meals

Nurtures and comforts

Teaches values

Children:

Respect and obey parents

Help with household chores

Study and attend school

Care for younger siblings
Learn family values

IMPORTANCE OF FAMILY

Provides love and security

Teaches values and morals

Offers emotional support

Meets basic needs (food, shelter, clothing)

Preserves culture and traditions

Prepares children for society

Creates sense of belonging

PART B: INTRODUCTION TO LANGUAGE SKILLS (LSRW)

THE FOUR LANGUAGE SKILLS: Abilities needed to communicate effectively

TWO CATEGORIES:

RECEPTIVE SKILLS (Input) – Receiving and understanding information PRODUCTIVE SKILLS (Output) – Producing and expressing information

LISTENING (Receptive)

Definition: Receiving and understanding spoken information

Activities:

Listening to teacher in class

Following instructions

Listening to radio/news
Understanding conversations

How to Improve:

Pay attention when others speak

Avoid distractions

Ask questions if unclear

Practice active listening

Listen to English programs

Importance:

Understand instructions

Learn from others

Build relationships

Avoid misunderstandings

READING (Receptive)

Definition: Understanding written information

Types:

Reading aloud (oral)

Silent reading

Intensive reading (detailed)

Extensive reading (general)

Activities:

Reading textbooks

Reading newspapers and stories
Reading instructions

How to Improve:

Read daily

Read various materials

Look up new words

Practice pronunciation

Read fiction and non-fiction

Importance:

Gain knowledge

Improve vocabulary

Enjoy literature

Access information

SPEAKING (Productive) Definition: Expressing ideas orally Types:
Formal speaking (presentations, speeches)

Informal speaking (conversations)

Monologue (one person)

Dialogue (conversation)

Activities:

Answering questions in class

Giving presentations
Telling stories

Participating in discussions

How to Improve:

Practice speaking English daily

Participate in class discussions

Join debate clubs

Record and listen to yourself

Learn correct pronunciation

Importance:

Express thoughts and feelings

Participate in discussions

Build confidence

Make presentations

WRITING (Productive)

Definition: Expressing ideas in written form

Types:

Formal writing (letters, reports, essays)

Informal writing (notes, messages)

Creative writing (stories, poems)

Academic writing (assignments, exams)

Activities:

Writing essays and letters
Taking notes

Answering exam questions

Writing stories

How to Improve:

Write daily (diary, journal)

Practice different types of writing

Read to improve vocabulary

Learn grammar rules

Proofread your work

Importance:

Record information

Communicate formally

Express creativity

Demonstrate knowledge

RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN THE FOUR SKILLS

Integration: All skills work together for effective communication

Listening → Speaking (hear, then respond)

Reading → Writing (read examples, then write)

Listening/Reading → Understanding (input leads to comprehension)

Speaking/Writing → Expression (output shows learning)

Order of Acquisition: Natural language learning order

Listening (babies listen first)
Speaking (then start talking)

Reading (learn to read)

Writing (learn to write)

SUMMARY TABLE

Skill	Type	Definition	Example Activities

Listening	Receptive	Understanding spoken
language
Following instructions, listening to news
Reading	Receptive	Understanding written
language
Reading books, newspapers, textbooks

Speaking	Productive	Expressing ideas orally	Presentations, discussions,
conversations

Writing	Productive	Expressing ideas in writing	Essays, letters, notes, stories

EVALUATION:

Define family and name 3 types with examples

List 5 extended family members and explain their relationships

What are the four language skills? Group them into receptive and productive

Explain 3 responsibilities of parents in a family

How can you improve: (a) listening skills (b) reading skills (c) speaking skills?

ASSIGNMENT:

Draw your family tree showing at least 10 family members with their relationships to you

Write a paragraph (10 sentences) describing your family (type, members, roles)
Explain the four language skills (LSRW) with 2 examples of activities for each

List 5 ways each skill is important: (a) Listening (b) Reading (c) Speaking (d) Writing

Describe how the four language skills work together in a classroom setting (8 sentences)
COMPOSITION

WEEK 1: MEANING OF COMPOSITION; TYPES – NARRATIVE, DESCRIPTIVE, ARGUMENTATIVE, EXPOSITORY

CONTENT:

WHAT IS COMPOSITION?

Definition: Composition is the art of putting words together in a structured and organized way to express ideas, thoughts, or tell a story. It is written expression of ideas in sentences and paragraphs.
Other Names:

Essay writing

Continuous writing

Creative writing

Purpose:

Express ideas clearly

Communicate thoughts

Demonstrate language skills

Inform, entertain, or persuade

CHARACTERISTICS OF GOOD COMPOSITION:

Clear Purpose: Know what you want to achieve

Good Organization: Logical arrangement of ideas

Unity: All parts relate to main idea
Coherence: Ideas flow smoothly

Correct Grammar: Proper sentence structure

Appropriate Vocabulary: Right words for the topic

Proper Punctuation: Correct use of marks

Good Paragraphing: Ideas grouped logically

TYPES OF COMPOSITION:

NARRATIVE COMPOSITION (NARRATIVE ESSAY):

Definition: A narrative composition tells a story or relates events in chronological order (time sequence). It has characters, setting, plot, and usually teaches a lesson.
Characteristics:

Tells a story

Has beginning, middle, end

Uses past tense (usually)

Includes characters and dialogue

Has a plot (sequence of events)

Often written in first person (I, we)

Descriptive and engaging

Purpose:

Tell a story

Share personal experiences

Entertain readers

Teach lessons through stories
Common Topics:

My First Day at School

A Day I Will Never Forget

My Last Holiday

An Unforgettable Experience

The Happiest Day of My Life

A Journey I Made

Key Elements:

Characters: People in the story (I, my friend, my mother)

Setting: Where and when the story happens

Plot: What happens (events in order)

Climax: Most exciting part

Lesson: What was learned

Example Opening: "I will never forget the day I got lost in the market. It was a bright Saturday morning in December when my mother and I went to buy Christmas items..."
Useful Expressions:

Once upon a time...

One day...

Last week/month/year...

First, Then, Next, After that, Finally

Suddenly, Immediately, Later

At that moment, Meanwhile
DESCRIPTIVE COMPOSITION (DESCRIPTIVE ESSAY):

Definition: A descriptive composition paints a picture with words. It describes a person, place, object, or event in detail using sensory language (what you see, hear, smell, taste, touch).
Characteristics:

Creates vivid mental images

Uses sensory details (five senses)

Rich in adjectives and adverbs

Present tense (usually)

Organized spatially (top to bottom, left to right)

Focuses on details

Purpose:

Describe something clearly

Help readers visualize

Create mental pictures

Appeal to senses

Common Topics:

My School

My Best Friend

My Village/Town

My Bedroom

A Market Scene

A Festival

An Interesting Place I Visited

Key Elements:

Physical appearance: What it looks like

Size and shape: Dimensions and form

Color: Shades and hues

Texture: How it feels

Sounds: What you hear

Smells: What you smell

Tastes: What you taste (if applicable)

Location: Where things are positioned

Example Opening: "My school stands majestically at the end of Kings Road. The large, white- painted building has two stories, with wide windows that shine brightly in the morning sun..."
Useful Expressions:

It looks like...

It is located...

To the left/right of...

In the center/middle...

Surrounding it are...

The most striking feature is...

Beautiful, colorful, magnificent, enormous

Bright, sparkling, gleaming, radiant
ARGUMENTATIVE COMPOSITION (ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY):

Definition: An argumentative composition presents a position on a topic and supports it with reasons, evidence, and examples to convince readers. It includes opposing views and refutes them.
Characteristics:

States a clear position/thesis

Presents arguments with evidence

Addresses counter-arguments

Uses logical reasoning

Formal tone

Present tense (usually)

Persuasive language

Purpose:

Convince readers

Present and defend a viewpoint

Show logical thinking

Persuade through reasoning

Common Topics:

School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory

Television Does More Harm Than Good

Boarding School is Better Than Day School

Sports Are More Important Than Academics

Mobile Phones Should Be Banned in Schools
Capital Punishment Should Be Abolished

Structure:

Introduction: State your position (thesis)

Body Paragraph 1-3: Present arguments with evidence

Counter-argument: Present opposing view

Refutation: Explain why opposing view is wrong

Conclusion: Restate position, summarize arguments

Example Opening: "School uniforms should be compulsory in all schools. While some argue that uniforms limit self-expression, the benefits far outweigh this concern. This essay will demonstrate why uniforms promote equality, discipline, and school identity..."
Useful Expressions:

I strongly believe that...

It is evident that...

First/Firstly, Second/Secondly, Finally

For example, For instance

However, Nevertheless, On the other hand

Some people argue that...

This view is flawed because...

In conclusion, Therefore, Thus

EXPOSITORY COMPOSITION (EXPOSITORY ESSAY):
Definition: An expository composition explains, informs, or describes a topic clearly and objectively using facts, statistics, and examples. It does not include personal opinions or try to persuade.
Characteristics:

Informative and educational

Objective (factual, no opinions)

Clear and organized

Uses facts and examples

Formal tone

Third person (he, she, it, they)

Present tense (usually)

Purpose:

Explain how something works

Inform about a topic

Describe a process

Define concepts

Compare and contrast

Common Topics:

How to Prepare Jollof Rice

The Importance of Education

Types of Transportation

HIV/AIDS: Understanding the Disease

The Water Cycle
Causes of Road Accidents

Benefits of Reading

Types of Expository Essays:

Process (How-to): Explains steps (How to make...)"

Cause and Effect: Shows relationships (Causes of...Effects of...)

Compare and Contrast: Shows similarities/differences

Problem-Solution: Presents problem and solutions

Definition: Explains what something means

Example Opening: "Education is the foundation of personal and national development. It equips individuals with knowledge, skills, and values necessary for productive living. This essay examines the importance of education in modern society..."
Useful Expressions:

This essay explains...

There are several types/kinds...

First, Second, Third, Finally

The process involves...

As a result, Consequently, Therefore

For example, For instance, Such as

In addition, Furthermore, Moreover

In summary, In conclusion

COMPARISON OF TYPES:

Type	Purpose	Tone	Person	Tense
NarrativeTell storyPersonal, engagingFirst person (I, we)PastDescriptiveDescribe/paint pictureVivid, sensoryFirst/third personPresentArgumentativePersuade/convinceFormal, persuasiveThird person mainlyPresentExpositoryExplain/informObjective, formalThird personPresent

CHOOSING THE RIGHT TYPE:

Question/Topic Analysis:

"Describe..." → Descriptive

"Narrate...," "Tell a story..." → Narrative

"Do you agree...?," "Should...?" → Argumentative

"Explain...," "Discuss...," "What is...?" → Expository

EVALUATION:

Define composition and state four characteristics of good composition

Explain the four types of composition with one example topic for each

What is the difference between narrative and descriptive composition?

List five topics suitable for: (a) narrative composition (b) argumentative composition

Which type of composition would you use for these topics: (a) My Last Birthday (b) Why Students Should Read More (c) The Market Near My House (d) How to Make Fried Rice?

ASSIGNMENT:

Create a comparison table showing the four types of composition with their: (a) definition
(b) purpose (c) characteristics (d) common topics
Write the opening paragraph (5 sentences) for: (a) A narrative essay about "My First Day at School" (b) A descriptive essay about "My Classroom"
List ten transitional expressions commonly used in: (a) narrative composition (b) argumentative composition
Explain three differences between expository and argumentative composition

For each topic, identify the type of composition: (a) Life in the Village (b) A Narrow Escape (c) Television is Harmful to Children (d) The Causes of Examination Malpractice
(e) An Exciting Football Match
5. LITERATURE IN ENGLISH WEEK 1: WHAT IS LITERATURE?

CONTENT:

DEFINITION OF LITERATURE:

Literature is the art of written works that express ideas, emotions, and experiences in beautiful, imaginative, and meaningful ways. It is creative writing that has lasting artistic value.
Simple Definition: Literature is any written work that is considered to have artistic or intellectual value.

CHARACTERISTICS OF LITERATURE:

CREATIVE/IMAGINATIVE:

Uses imagination and creativity

Not just stating facts

Creates new worlds and characters

Original and unique

ARTISTIC:

Beautiful use of language

Careful choice of words

Attention to style and form

Aesthetic appeal

MEANINGFUL:

Conveys important messages
Explores human experiences

Teaches lessons about life

Has depth and significance

PERMANENT/LASTING:

Survives across time

Relevant to different generations

Universal themes

Enduring value

EMOTIONAL:

Appeals to feelings

Evokes emotions in readers

Makes readers laugh, cry, think

Creates connections

ENTERTAINING:

Provides pleasure

Engages readers

Holds attention

Makes reading enjoyable

PURPOSES/FUNCTIONS OF LITERATURE:

ENTERTAINMENT:

Provides enjoyment and pleasure

Offers escape from reality
Makes leisure time valuable

Creates memorable experiences

EDUCATION:

Teaches about life, culture, history

Increases knowledge

Develops vocabulary

Improves language skills

MORAL INSTRUCTION:

Teaches right from wrong

Shows consequences of actions

Develops character

Instills values

CULTURAL PRESERVATION:

Preserves traditions and customs

Records history

Passes knowledge to next generation

Maintains cultural identity

SOCIAL COMMENTARY:

Reflects society's problems

Criticizes social ills

Promotes social change

Raises awareness

EMOTIONAL EXPRESSION:
Allows writers to express feelings

Helps readers understand emotions

Provides emotional release

Creates empathy

SOURCES OF LITERATURE:

PERSONAL EXPERIENCE:

Writer's own life events

Autobiographical elements

Direct observations

IMAGINATION:

Creative invention

Fantasy and fiction

What-if scenarios

HISTORY:

Past events

Historical figures

Real occurrences

CULTURE AND TRADITION:

Folktales and myths

Cultural practices

Traditional beliefs

SOCIETY:
Current events

Social issues

Community experiences

OTHER LITERATURE:

Influenced by other works

Adaptations and retellings

Literary traditions

QUALITIES OF GOOD LITERATURE:

UNIVERSALITY:

Appeals to people everywhere

Transcends time and place

Deals with universal human experiences

Relevant across cultures

ORIGINALITY:

Unique and creative

Fresh perspective

Not copied or imitated

Original voice and style

INTELLECTUAL VALUE:

Makes readers think

Raises important questions

Explores complex ideas
Stimulates the mind

ARTISTIC MERIT:

Beautiful language

Well-crafted structure

Effective use of literary devices

Stylistic excellence

EMOTIONAL APPEAL:

Touches the heart

Evokes genuine feelings

Creates emotional impact

Memorable and moving

TRUTHFULNESS:

Authentic portrayal of life

Honest exploration of human nature

Believable characters and situations

Rings true to experience

LITERATURE VS NON-LITERATURE: LITERATURE:
Creative and imaginative

Artistic use of language

Focuses on human experiences

Has lasting value
Examples: novels, poems, plays, short stories

NON-LITERATURE:

Factual and informative

Straightforward language

Focuses on information/facts

May have temporary value

Examples: textbooks, news articles, instruction manuals, reports

WHY STUDY LITERATURE?

LANGUAGE DEVELOPMENT:

Improves vocabulary

Enhances grammar

Develops writing skills

Improves communication

CULTURAL UNDERSTANDING:

Learn about different cultures

Understand traditions

Appreciate diversity

Build tolerance

CRITICAL THINKING:

Analyze and interpret

Make connections

Evaluate ideas
Think deeply

EMPATHY:

Understand others' perspectives

Experience different viewpoints

Develop compassion

Connect with humanity

IMAGINATION:

Stimulates creativity

Encourages original thinking

Expands possibilities

Inspires innovation

LIFE LESSONS:

Learn from characters' experiences

Understand consequences

Make better decisions

Grow personally

ENTERTAINMENT:

Enjoyable and pleasurable

Escape and relaxation

Fun and engaging

Lifelong hobby

LITERATURE AND LIFE:
Literature Reflects Life:

Shows real human experiences

Mirrors society's problems and joys

Presents true emotions

Depicts real relationships

Literature Shapes Life:

Influences how we think

Changes perspectives

Inspires actions

Molds character

Literature Enriches Life:

Makes life more meaningful

Provides beauty and pleasure

Offers wisdom and insight

Creates shared experiences

ELEMENTS THAT MAKE WRITING LITERARY:

FIGURATIVE LANGUAGE:

Metaphors, similes, personification

Creates vivid images

Adds depth and beauty

SYMBOLISM:

Objects represent ideas
Deeper meanings

Layered significance

THEME:

Central message or idea

Universal truths

Meaningful insights

CHARACTERIZATION:

Well-developed characters

Realistic personalities

Character growth

PLOT/STRUCTURE:

Organized sequence

Engaging storyline

Effective pacing

STYLE:

Distinctive voice

Unique expression

Artistic presentation

EVALUATION:

Define literature and state four characteristics that make a work literary

Explain five purposes or functions of literature with examples

What is the difference between literature and non-literature? Give two examples of each
List and explain four qualities of good literature

Why should students study literature? Give five reasons

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a comprehensive definition of literature in your own words (8 sentences)

Explain six characteristics of literature with examples

Describe how literature: (a) entertains (b) educates (c) preserves culture (d) provides moral instruction
List five sources of literature and explain how each inspires literary works

Create a table comparing literature and non-literature based on: purpose, language, style, examples, and lasting value
WEEK 2 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: PURE VOWEL SOUNDS (MONOPHTHONGS) WITH EXAMPLES

CONTENT:

WHAT ARE VOWELS?

Vowels are speech sounds produced with an open vocal tract. Air flows freely from the lungs through the mouth without obstruction.
Characteristics:

Voice is always used (voiced sounds)

No blockage of airflow

Tongue position varies but doesn't block

Form the nucleus of syllables

More sonorous than consonants

TYPES OF VOWELS:

PURE VOWELS (MONOPHTHONGS): Single vowel sounds where the tongue stays in one position throughout pronunciation.
DIPHTHONGS: Vowel sounds that glide from one position to another (studied later).

THE TWELVE PURE VOWEL SOUNDS IN ENGLISH: SHORT VOWELS (7):
/ɪ/ (as in "sit"):

Short, quick sound
Tongue high and front

Lips slightly spread

Examples: sit, bit, his, give, women, busy, England

/e/ (as in "bed"):

Short sound

Tongue mid-front

Lips spread

Examples: bed, red, pen, any, said, friend, bread

/æ/ (as in "cat"):

Short sound

Tongue low-front

Mouth open fairly wide

Examples: cat, hat, man, bad, apple, happy, family

/ɒ/ (as in "hot"):

Short sound

Tongue low-back

Lips slightly rounded

Examples: hot, pot, dog, want, wash, because (British)

/ʊ/ (as in "book"):

Short sound

Tongue high-back

Lips rounded

Examples: book, look, good, could, put, woman
/ʌ/ (as in "cup"):

Short sound

Tongue mid-central

Lips neutral

Examples: cup, but, sun, love, come, blood, done

/ə/ (schwa - as in "about"):

The most common English sound

Very short and weak

Only in unstressed syllables

Tongue central

Examples: about, again, teacher, doctor, banana (last 'a')

LONG VOWELS (5):

/iː/ (as in "see"):

Long sound

Tongue very high-front

Lips spread and tense

Examples: see, meet, tea, he, people, key, field

/ɑː/ (as in "car"):

Long sound

Tongue low-back

Mouth wide open

Examples: car, father, arm, heart, class, grass (British)
/ɔː/ (as in "door"):

Long sound

Tongue mid-back

Lips rounded

Examples: door, four, ball, thought, daughter, walk, war

/uː/ (as in "too"):

Long sound

Tongue high-back

Lips tightly rounded

Examples: too, food, blue, true, move, soup, through

/ɜː/ (as in "bird"):

Long sound

Tongue mid-central

Lips neutral

Examples: bird, her, turn, work, learn, journey

KEY DIFFERENCES:

Short vs Long:

Short: Quick, unstressed, relaxed

Long: Prolonged, can be stressed, tense

Front vs Back:

Front vowels: Tongue toward front (/iː/, /ɪ/, /e/, /æ/)

Back vowels: Tongue toward back (/ɑː/, /ɒ/, /ɔː/, /ʊ/, /uː/, /ʌ/)
Central vowels: Tongue in center (/ə/, /ɜː/)

Rounded vs Unrounded:

Rounded: Lips rounded (/ɒ/, /ɔː/, /ʊ/, /uː/)

Unrounded: Lips spread or neutral (most others)

THE VOWEL CHART:

Position by Tongue Height:

High/Close: /iː/, /ɪ/, /ʊ/, /uː/

Mid: /e/, /ə/, /ɜː/, /ɔː/, /ʌ/

Low/Open: /æ/, /ɑː/, /ɒ/

Position by Tongue Placement:

Front: /iː/, /ɪ/, /e/, /æ/

Central: /ə/, /ɜː/, /ʌ/

Back: /ɑː/, /ɒ/, /ɔː/, /ʊ/, /uː/

PRACTICE WORDS:

Minimal Pairs (words differing by one vowel):

sit /ɪ/ - seat /iː/

bit /ɪ/ - beat /iː/

pet /e/ - pat /æ/

cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔː/

full /ʊ/ - fool /uː/

cup /ʌ/ - carp /ɑː/

COMMON SPELLING PATTERNS:

/iː/: ee (see), ea (tea), e (he), ie (field), ei (receive) /ɪ/: i (sit), y (happy), e (England) /e/: e (bed),
ea (head), a (any) /æ/: a (cat) /ɑː/: a (father), ar (car), ear (heart) /ɒ/: o (hot), a (want) /ɔː/: or (door), au (daughter), aw (law), ough (thought) /ʊ/: oo (book), u (put), ou (could) /uː/: oo (food), u (true), ue (blue), ou (soup) /ʌ/: u (cup), o (love), ou (country) /ɜː/: ir (bird), er (her), ur (turn), or (work), ear (learn) /ə/: a (about), e (teacher), o (doctor)

PRONUNCIATION TIPS:

Length matters: Hold long vowels longer

Tongue position: Practice front, central, back placement

Lip shape: Round or spread as needed

Stress: Vowels in stressed syllables are clearer

Context: Same letter can make different sounds

Practice: Use minimal pairs to hear differences

EVALUATION:

What is a monophthong/pure vowel? How many pure vowels are in English?

List the five long vowel sounds with three examples for each

Pronounce and identify the vowel sounds in: (a) seat (b) book (c) bird (d) father (e) pot

What is the difference between /ɪ/ and /iː/? Give two examples of each

Write five minimal pairs showing different pure vowel sounds

ASSIGNMENT:
Create a table showing all twelve pure vowels with: (a) phonetic symbol (b) example word (c) whether short or long (d) tongue position
List ten words each containing: (a) /iː/ sound (b) /ɑː/ sound (c) /ɔː/ sound

Identify all pure vowel sounds in this sentence: "The good teacher put the book on the seat"
Explain the difference between short and long vowels with five examples

Practice and write ten minimal pairs (words that differ by only one vowel sound)
WEEK 2: GRAMMAR

TOPIC: PRONOUNS – MEANING, IDENTIFICATION, TYPES

CONTENT:

WHAT IS A PRONOUN?

Definition: A pronoun is a word used in place of a noun to avoid repetition. It refers to a person, place, thing, or idea without naming it again.
Purpose:

Avoid repeating nouns

Make sentences flow better

Make language less awkward

Create variety

Example:

Without pronoun: John went to John's school. John met John's friend there.

With pronoun: John went to his school. He met his friend there.

THE ANTECEDENT:

The antecedent is the noun that the pronoun replaces or refers to.

Example:

Mary lost her book. (Mary = antecedent; her = pronoun)

The boys played until they were tired. (boys = antecedent; they = pronoun)

TYPES OF PRONOUNS:
PERSONAL PRONOUNS:

Refer to specific persons or things. They change form based on:

Person (1st, 2nd, 3rd)

Number (singular, plural)

Case (subject, object, possessive)

Subject Pronouns (do the action):

Singular: I, you, he, she, it

Plural: we, you, they

Examples:

I am a student

She reads books

They play football

Object Pronouns (receive the action):

Singular: me, you, him, her, it

Plural: us, you, them

Examples:

The teacher called me

I saw him yesterday

Give it to them Chart:
Person	Subject	Object

1st Singular	I	me
2nd Singularyouyou3rd Singular (male)hehim3rd Singular (female)sheher3rd Singular (thing)itit1st Pluralweus2nd Pluralyouyou3rd Pluraltheythem

POSSESSIVE PRONOUNS:

Show ownership or possession.

Two Forms:

Possessive Adjectives (before nouns):

my, your, his, her, its, our, your, their

Examples:

My book is new

Her mother is a teacher

Their house is big

Possessive Pronouns (stand alone):

mine, yours, his, hers, ours, yours, theirs

Examples:

This book is mine

The car is theirs

Is this pen yours?
Note: "Its" (possessive adjective) vs "It's" (it is) - don't confuse!

REFLEXIVE PRONOUNS:

Refer back to the subject. Formed by adding -self (singular) or -selves (plural).

List:

myself, yourself, himself, herself, itself

ourselves, yourselves, themselves

Uses:

When subject and object are the same:

I hurt myself

She looked at herself in the mirror

They enjoyed themselves

For emphasis:

I myself did the work

The president himself came

She herself cooked the food

Examples:

He taught himself to play guitar

We prepared ourselves for the exam

The cat cleaned itself Common Mistake:
✗ Me and John went (should be: John and I)
✗ Myself will come (should be: I will come)

DEMONSTRATIVE PRONOUNS:

Point to specific things. Show whether something is near or far, singular or plural.

List:

this (singular, near)

that (singular, far)

these (plural, near)

those (plural, far)

Examples:

This is my book (near me)

That is your house (far from me)

These are fresh fruits (near, plural)

Those are old buildings (far, plural)

Usage:

As pronouns: This is good (stands alone)

As adjectives: This book is good (before noun - technically "demonstrative adjective")

INTERROGATIVE PRONOUNS:

Used to ask questions about people or things.

List:

who (asks about people - subject)
whom (asks about people - object)

whose (asks about possession)

which (asks about choice/selection)

what (asks about things/information)

Examples:

Who is that man?

Whom did you see?

Whose book is this?

Which do you prefer?

What happened?

Who vs Whom:

Who: Subject of verb (Who called? - who is doing the calling)

Whom: Object of verb (Whom did you call? - whom receives the action)

RELATIVE PRONOUNS:

Connect clauses and relate back to a noun (antecedent) mentioned earlier. Introduce relative clauses.
List:

who (for people - subject)

whom (for people - object)

whose (possession)

which (for things/animals)

that (for people, things, animals)
Examples:

The boy who won the race is my friend (who = the boy)

The book which I bought is interesting (which = the book)

The man whose car was stolen reported to police (whose shows possession)

The dog that barked is big (that = the dog)

Differences:

Who/Whom: People only

Which: Things/animals

That: People, things, animals (more flexible)

Whose: Shows possession

INDEFINITE PRONOUNS:

Refer to non-specific persons or things. Don't identify exactly who or what.

Common Indefinite Pronouns:

Referring to people:

someone, somebody, anyone, anybody, no one, nobody, everyone, everybody

Referring to things:

something, anything, nothing, everything

Referring to quantity:

some, any, none, all, both, few, several, many, much, more, most, each, either, neither, one, other, another
Examples:

Everyone is present
Someone knocked on the door

Nothing is impossible

Few understand this

Each has a role to play

Neither is correct

Agreement:

Most indefinite pronouns are singular: Everyone is here (not "are")

Some are plural: few, many, several, both

Some can be both: all, some, none (depends on context)

RECIPROCAL PRONOUNS:

Show mutual action between two or more people.

List:

each other (two people)

one another (more than two people)

Examples:

John and Mary love each other (two people)

The students help one another (more than two)

They greeted each other

Note: Some grammar books say this distinction isn't strict anymore, and both can be used interchangeably.

PRONOUN AGREEMENT:
Pronouns must agree with their antecedents in:

Number (singular/plural):

The boy lost his book (singular)

The boys lost their books (plural)

Gender (male/female/neutral):

John did his homework (male)

Mary did her homework (female)

The dog wagged its tail (neutral)

Person (1st, 2nd, 3rd):

I finished my work (1st person)

You finished your work (2nd person)

She finished her work (3rd person)

COMMON PRONOUN ERRORS:

Wrong case:

✗ Me and John went (should be: John and I)

✗ Between you and I (should be: Between you and me)

Unclear antecedent:

✗ John told Peter that he was late (Who was late? Unclear)

✓ John told Peter, "I am late" or "You are late"

Agreement error:
✗ Everyone brought their book (should be: his/her book - technically, though "their" is increasingly accepted)
✗ The team won their match (should be: its match - collective noun is singular)

Reflexive misuse:

✗ Myself and Mary will go (should be: Mary and I)

✗ Give it to myself (should be: Give it to me)

EVALUATION:

Define pronoun and explain its purpose with three examples

List and explain five types of pronouns with two examples each

What is the difference between: (a) subject and object pronouns (b) possessive adjectives and possessive pronouns?
Fill in with correct pronouns: (a) Mary lost	book (b) The boys enjoyed	(c) This pen is	(mine/me)
Correct these errors: (a) Me and John went home (b) The dog wagged it's tail (c) Everyone brought their books

ASSIGNMENT:

Create a chart showing all personal pronouns (subject, object, possessive adjective, possessive pronoun) for all persons and numbers
Write ten sentences using different types of pronouns. Identify the type and underline the antecedent
Explain five types of pronouns not covered in personal pronouns (demonstrative, interrogative, relative, indefinite, reflexive) with three examples each
Identify all pronouns in this passage and classify them: "I saw Mary yesterday. She told me that her brother lost his book. This made him very sad. They searched everywhere but found nothing. Someone must have taken it."
Correct the pronoun errors in these sentences: (a) Him and me are friends (b) The teacher gave we the books (c) John hurt hisself (d) Between you and I (e) Everyone must do their best
WEEK 2 - READING AND COMPREHENSION TOPIC: READING STRATEGIES

CONTENT:

WHAT ARE READING STRATEGIES?

Reading strategies are techniques or methods used to improve understanding, speed, and retention when reading. They help you become an active, efficient reader.
Why Use Reading Strategies?

Improve comprehension

Increase reading speed

Remember information better

Identify important points

Make reading purposeful

Enjoy reading more

TYPES OF READING:

SKIMMING:

Definition: Reading quickly to get the general idea or main points without reading every word.

Purpose:

Get overview of text

Decide if text is relevant

Find specific section

Preview before detailed reading
How to Skim:

Read title and headings

Read first and last paragraphs

Read first sentence of each paragraph

Look at pictures, graphs, charts

Notice bold/italicized words

Don't read every word

When to Skim:

Previewing a chapter

Reading newspaper headlines

Browsing through book in library

Deciding what to read in detail

Example: Before reading a chapter on "The Water Cycle," skim by reading the title, headings (Evaporation, Condensation, Precipitation), first paragraph, and last paragraph to get the general idea.

SCANNING:

Definition: Looking quickly through text to find specific information without reading everything.

Purpose:

Find particular fact, number, name, date

Locate specific information quickly

Answer specific questions

How to Scan:
Know what you're looking for

Move eyes quickly over page

Look for keywords

Don't read sentences fully

Stop when you find information

When to Scan:

Looking up word in dictionary

Finding phone number in directory

Locating date in text

Searching for specific fact

Example: To find "When was Nigeria's independence?", scan the text looking for numbers that might be years (1960), or the word "independence."

INTENSIVE READING:

Definition: Reading carefully and slowly to understand every detail.

Purpose:

Understand completely

Learn new information thoroughly

Prepare for exams

Analyze text deeply

How to Read Intensively:

Read slowly and carefully

Read every word
Look up unknown words

Take notes

Reread difficult parts

Analyze meaning

When to Read Intensively:

Studying textbooks

Reading exam questions

Following instructions

Learning new concepts

Example: Reading a mathematics problem or science experiment instructions requires intensive reading to understand every step.

EXTENSIVE READING:

Definition: Reading longer texts for general understanding and enjoyment without focusing on every detail.
Purpose:

Develop reading habit

Improve overall reading ability

Enjoy literature

Build vocabulary naturally

How to Read Extensively:

Read for pleasure

Don't stop for every unknown word
Focus on overall meaning

Read at comfortable pace

Choose interesting materials

When to Read Extensively:

Reading novels/stories for fun

Reading magazines

Leisure reading

Building reading stamina

Example: Reading a Harry Potter book for enjoyment, not worrying about every single word, but understanding the story.

BEFORE READING STRATEGIES:

PREVIEW:

Look at title, headings, pictures

Check length

Read introduction/conclusion

Get general sense of content

SET PURPOSE:

Why am I reading this?

What do I want to learn?

What questions do I want answered?

ACTIVATE PRIOR KNOWLEDGE:

What do I already know about this topic?
Have I read something similar?

What do I expect to learn?

PREDICT:

What will this be about?

What might happen?

What questions might be answered?

DURING READING STRATEGIES:

MONITOR UNDERSTANDING:

Am I understanding this?

Does this make sense?

Do I need to reread?

VISUALIZE:

Create mental images

Picture what you're reading

Imagine scenes and characters

MAKE CONNECTIONS:

Text-to-self: Relate to personal experience

Text-to-text: Connect to other readings

Text-to-world: Link to world knowledge

ASK QUESTIONS:

What does this mean?

Why did this happen?
What will happen next?

ANNOTATE:

Underline important points

Write notes in margins

Mark unfamiliar words

Highlight key ideas

ADJUST READING SPEED:

Slow down for difficult parts

Speed up for easier sections

Reread when confused

AFTER READING STRATEGIES:

SUMMARIZE:

What was this about?

What were the main points?

Retell in your own words

REVIEW:

Look back at notes

Check highlighted sections

Recall important details

REFLECT:

What did I learn?

What was important?
How does this connect to what I knew?

EVALUATE:

Did I achieve my reading purpose?

Do I need to reread anything?

What questions remain?

STRATEGIES FOR DIFFICULT TEXT:

BREAK IT DOWN:

Read one paragraph at a time

Summarize each paragraph

Build understanding gradually

USE CONTEXT CLUES:

Figure out unknown words from surrounding words

Look for definitions in text

Use examples given

REREAD:

Go back and read again

Read aloud

Read more slowly

SEEK HELP:

Use dictionary for difficult words

Ask teacher/friend for clarification

Look for supplementary materials

VOCABULARY STRATEGIES WHILE READING:

CONTEXT CLUES: Look at surrounding words to guess meaning

Types:

Definition: "A herbivore, an animal that eats only plants..."

Example: "Reptiles such as snakes and lizards..."

Contrast: "Unlike mammals, reptiles are cold-blooded"

Synonym: "The enormous, huge building..."

WORD PARTS:

Prefix: un-, re-, pre-, dis-

Root: Latin/Greek roots

Suffix: -tion, -ness, -ly, -ful

SKIP AND CONTINUE:

If word isn't crucial, skip it

Continue reading

Meaning may become clear later

USE DICTIONARY:

Look up important unknown words

Write down definitions

Use words in sentences

IMPROVING READING SPEED:

Tips:
Avoid reading word by word

Read in phrases/chunks

Don't vocalize (say words aloud or in head)

Limit regression (going back)

Practice regularly

Use finger/pointer to guide eyes

Expand peripheral vision

Read more often

ACTIVE READING TECHNIQUES:

SQ3R METHOD (will be covered in detail next week):

Survey, Question, Read, Recite, Review

NOTE-TAKING:

Write key points

Create outlines

Use symbols and abbreviations

QUESTIONING:

Ask before, during, after reading

Who, what, when, where, why, how

DISCUSSING:

Talk about what you read

Share ideas with others

Join reading groups

CHOOSING APPROPRIATE STRATEGY:

Consider:

Purpose: Why are you reading?

Text type: Fiction, textbook, article?

Time available: Quick or thorough?

Difficulty level: Easy or challenging?

Match Strategy to Purpose:

Entertainment → Extensive reading

Finding fact → Scanning

Getting overview → Skimming

Studying for exam → Intensive reading

Learning new concept → Intensive + SQ3R

EVALUATION:

What are reading strategies and why are they important?

Explain the difference between skimming and scanning with examples of when to use each
Describe three strategies you should use before, during, and after reading

What is intensive reading and when should you use it?

How can you figure out the meaning of an unknown word while reading? Give three methods
ASSIGNMENT:

Create a comparison table showing skimming, scanning, intensive, and extensive reading with their: (a) definitions (b) purposes (c) when to use (d) how to do it
Choose a passage from your textbook and: (a) Preview it (b) Write what you predict it's about (c) List three questions you expect it to answer (d) After reading, check if your predictions were correct
Explain five "during reading" strategies with examples of how to use each

Describe a situation where you would use: (a) skimming (b) scanning (c) intensive reading (d) extensive reading
List ten tips for improving reading speed and explain why each helps
WEEK 2 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: ELEMENTS OF COMPOSITION – INTRODUCTION, BODY, CONCLUSION

CONTENT:

STRUCTURE OF A COMPOSITION:

Every good composition has three main parts that work together to create a complete, well- organized piece of writing:
Introduction (Beginning)

Body (Middle)

Conclusion (End)

Why Structure Matters:

Organizes ideas logically

Guides reader through your thoughts

Makes writing clear and easy to follow

Ensures completeness

Creates professional appearance

1. THE INTRODUCTION:

Definition: The introduction is the opening paragraph that introduces the topic, captures attention, and prepares the reader for what follows.
Length: Usually one paragraph (4-6 sentences for JSS1)

PURPOSES OF INTRODUCTION:
CAPTURE ATTENTION (Hook): Get reader interested immediately

INTRODUCE TOPIC: Tell what the composition is about

PROVIDE BACKGROUND: Give necessary context

STATE THESIS/MAIN IDEA: Present your position or main point

PREVIEW CONTENT: Hint at what's coming in body paragraphs

TYPES OF HOOKS (Attention-Getters):

QUESTION: Start with an interesting question

"Have you ever wondered why the sky is blue?"

"What would you do if you found a million naira?"

QUOTATION: Begin with a relevant saying or quote

"As the saying goes, 'Rome wasn't built in a day'"

"My grandmother always said, 'Education is the key to success'"

INTERESTING FACT/STATISTIC: Present surprising information

"Did you know that an ostrich's eye is bigger than its brain?"

"Over 70% of the Earth is covered by water"

ANECDOTE (Short Story): Tell a brief, relevant personal experience

"Last Tuesday, something happened that changed my life forever"

"I was only seven when I learned the importance of honesty"

DESCRIPTIVE SCENE: Paint a vivid picture

"The market was bustling with activity as vendors called out to potential buyers"

"Rain pounded on the roof as I sat alone in the dark room"

DRAMATIC STATEMENT: Make bold, attention-grabbing claim
"That day taught me a lesson I would never forget"

"Reading saved my life"

COMPONENTS OF A GOOD INTRODUCTION:

Hook (1-2 sentences): Attention-grabber

Background/Context (2-3 sentences): Necessary information to understand topic

Thesis Statement (1 sentence): Main idea or position (especially in argumentative/expository)

Preview (1 sentence - optional): What will be covered

SAMPLE INTRODUCTIONS:

Narrative Essay - "My First Day at School":

I will never forget the 5th of September, 2023. It was my first day at Government Secondary School, and I was both excited and nervous. As my mother helped me put on my new uniform that morning, I had no idea what adventures awaited me. That day turned out to be one of the most memorable days of my life.
Analysis:

Hook: Dramatic statement about remembering the date

Background: First day at school, emotions

Preview: Hints at adventures

Sets narrative tone

Descriptive Essay - "My Classroom":

Walking into my classroom is like entering a world of learning and friendship. Our classroom, located on the second floor of the school building, is where we spend most of our day. It is a
spacious, well-organized room filled with desks, books, and educational charts. This special place has become my second home.
Analysis:

Hook: Comparison "like entering a world"

Background: Location and purpose

Preview: Mentions what will be described

Sets descriptive tone

Argumentative Essay - "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory":

The debate about school uniforms has continued for decades. Some students complain that uniforms limit their freedom of expression, while others appreciate the sense of equality they create. However, the benefits of school uniforms far outweigh any disadvantages. This essay will demonstrate why school uniforms should be compulsory in all schools.
Analysis:

Hook: Mentions ongoing debate

Background: Different viewpoints

Thesis: Clear position stated

Preview: What essay will prove

Expository Essay - "The Importance of Education":

Education is often called the key to success, and for good reason. Throughout history, educated people have been leaders, innovators, and change-makers in society. Education transforms individuals and nations, opening doors to opportunities and development. This essay explores the many ways education impacts our lives and why it is essential for everyone.
Analysis:
Hook: Common saying about education

Background: Historical perspective

Main idea: Education's transformative power

Preview: What essay will cover

WHAT TO AVOID IN INTRODUCTIONS:

Too Broad/General:

✗ "Many things happen in life"

✗ "Since the beginning of time..."

Dictionary Definitions (overdone):

✗ "According to the dictionary, friendship is..."

Apologizing:

✗ "I don't know much about this but..."

✗ "This may not be interesting but..."

Announcing Intentions Obviously:

✗ "In this essay, I am going to tell you about..."

✗ "I will now describe..."

Too Long:

Should be about 10-15% of total composition

Revealing Everything:

Save details for body

Don't tell the whole story in introduction

2. THE BODY:

Definition: The body is the main part of the composition where you develop your ideas, present arguments, tell your story, or provide detailed description.
Length: Usually 2-4 paragraphs (depending on topic and total length)

PURPOSES OF BODY:

DEVELOP MAIN IDEAS: Expand on points introduced

PROVIDE DETAILS: Give specific information, examples, evidence

TELL THE STORY: Present events in order (narrative)

DESCRIBE THOROUGHLY: Paint complete picture (descriptive)

PRESENT ARGUMENTS: Support thesis with reasons (argumentative)

EXPLAIN CLEARLY: Provide information and explanations (expository)

ORGANIZING BODY PARAGRAPHS:

Each Body Paragraph Should Have:

TOPIC SENTENCE: States the main idea of the paragraph

First sentence usually

Introduces what paragraph is about

SUPPORTING SENTENCES: Provide details, examples, evidence, explanations

4-6 sentences usually

Develop the topic sentence

CONCLUDING SENTENCE: Wraps up paragraph and transitions to next
Last sentence

Links to next paragraph

PARAGRAPH UNITY:

All sentences in a paragraph should relate to one main idea (topic sentence).

Good Paragraph (Unity): Topic: My mother is a hardworking woman

She wakes up at 5 AM every day

She prepares breakfast for the family

She goes to work and returns late

She still cooks dinner when she returns

On weekends, she does laundry and cleaning

Poor Paragraph (No Unity): Topic: My mother is hardworking

She wakes up at 5 AM

I have three siblings

Our house is big

I like football

She cooks dinner

PARAGRAPH COHERENCE:

Ideas should flow smoothly using transitions.

Transitional Words/Phrases: Time Order (Narrative):
First, Then, Next, After that, Finally
Later, Meanwhile, Soon, Eventually

Before, During, After

Spatial Order (Descriptive):

To the left/right, In front of, Behind

Above, Below, Beside, Near

In the center, At the top/bottom

Logical Order (Argumentative/Expository):

First/Firstly, Second/Secondly, Third/Thirdly

Furthermore, Moreover, Additionally, Also

However, Nevertheless, On the other hand

Therefore, Thus, Consequently, As a result

For example, For instance, Such as

In fact, Indeed, Actually

BODY ORGANIZATION BY TYPE: NARRATIVE:
Chronological order (time sequence)

Beginning → Middle → End

Build to climax

Include dialogue, action, description

DESCRIPTIVE:

Spatial order (physical arrangement)

General to specific OR specific to general
Most to least important features

Sensory details throughout

ARGUMENTATIVE:

Order of importance (usually weakest to strongest)

One argument per paragraph

Evidence and examples for each

Counter-argument paragraph

EXPOSITORY:

Logical order

Categories, causes/effects, comparisons

Clear explanations

Examples and facts

SAMPLE BODY PARAGRAPHS:

Narrative (from "My First Day at School"):

First Paragraph: The morning began with a mixture of excitement and nervousness. I woke up earlier than usual and carefully put on my new uniform. My mother had prepared my favorite breakfast, jollof rice, to give me energy for the day. As we walked to school, she kept giving me advice: "Be respectful to your teachers, make new friends, and study hard." I nodded, but my mind was racing with questions about what school would be like.
Analysis:

Topic: Morning preparations

Chronological order

Details about actions, thoughts, emotions
Dialogue included

Transitions: "As we walked," "but"

Second Paragraph: When we arrived at the school gate, I was amazed by what I saw. Hundreds of students in the same uniform rushed around the compound. Some were chatting with friends, while others stood alone looking as nervous as I felt. The school building was much larger than I had imagined, with three stories and many classrooms. My mother walked me to my assigned class, JSS 1A, where I met my class teacher, Mrs. Adeleke. She welcomed me warmly and showed me to my seat.
Analysis:

Topic: Arrival at school

Descriptive details

Comparison ("as nervous as I felt")

Action and dialogue

Transitions: "When," "while," "where"

Descriptive (from "My Classroom"):

First Paragraph: As you enter through the blue wooden door, the first thing you notice is the neat arrangement of desks. Twenty brown desks are arranged in four rows of five, each desk designed for two students. In front of the desks stands the teacher's large table, made of dark wood with drawers on both sides. Behind the teacher's table, a green chalkboard spans almost the entire wall, usually covered with notes and diagrams. On the left side of the board, our class timetable is permanently displayed.
Analysis:

Topic: Physical layout
Spatial organization (as you enter → front → behind → left)

Specific details (numbers, colors, materials)

Descriptive vocabulary

Transitions: "As you enter," "In front of," "Behind," "On the left"

Argumentative (from "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory"):

First Argument: First and foremost, school uniforms promote equality among students. When everyone wears the same clothes, economic differences become less visible. Rich students cannot show off expensive designer clothes, and poor students don't feel embarrassed about wearing cheaper clothing. This creates a more inclusive environment where students are judged by their character and achievements rather than their parents' wealth. For example, in schools with uniforms, bullying related to clothing choices decreases significantly.
Analysis:

Topic: Uniforms promote equality

Clear argument with explanation

Cause and effect reasoning

Example provided

Transitions: "First and foremost," "When," "For example"

Expository (from "The Importance of Education"):

First Paragraph: Education provides individuals with knowledge and skills necessary for success in life. Through education, people learn to read, write, and perform mathematical calculations—basic skills required in almost every job. Furthermore, education teaches critical thinking, enabling individuals to solve problems creatively and make informed decisions. For
instance, an educated person can analyze information, distinguish between fact and opinion, and draw logical conclusions. These skills are invaluable in both personal and professional life.
Analysis:

Topic: Education provides knowledge and skills

Clear explanations

Examples provided

Cause and effect

Transitions: "Through," "Furthermore," "For instance"

3. THE CONCLUSION:

Definition: The conclusion is the final paragraph that wraps up the composition, summarizes main points, and leaves a lasting impression.
Length: Usually one paragraph (4-5 sentences for JSS1)

PURPOSES OF CONCLUSION:

SIGNAL THE END: Let reader know composition is finishing

SUMMARIZE MAIN POINTS: Briefly recap what was discussed

RESTATE THESIS: Return to main idea (in different words)

PROVIDE CLOSURE: Give sense of completeness

LEAVE LASTING IMPRESSION: Make final statement reader will remember

REFLECTION (for narrative): Share what was learned or how you felt

COMPONENTS OF A GOOD CONCLUSION:
Transition Signal (1 phrase/sentence): Indicates conclusion is beginning

In conclusion, To conclude, Finally, In summary

As we have seen, To sum up

Summary/Restatement (2-3 sentences): Recap main points or restate thesis

Final Thought (1-2 sentences): Memorable closing statement:

Call to action

Prediction

Recommendation

Thought-provoking statement

Personal reflection

TYPES OF CONCLUSIONS:

SUMMARY CONCLUSION: Restates main points briefly

RECOMMENDATION CONCLUSION: Suggests what should be done

CALL-TO-ACTION CONCLUSION: Urges reader to do something

PREDICTION CONCLUSION: Forecasts future implications

REFLECTIVE CONCLUSION: Shares personal insights or feelings (narrative)

THOUGHT-PROVOKING CONCLUSION: Leaves reader with something to ponder

SAMPLE CONCLUSIONS:

Narrative (from "My First Day at School"):

That first day at school was truly unforgettable. Although I started the day feeling nervous and uncertain, I ended it with new friends and exciting experiences. I learned that trying new things,
even when scary, often leads to wonderful opportunities. Looking back now, I realize that first day was just the beginning of an amazing journey. I'm grateful my mother encouraged me to be brave and embrace the adventure.
Type: Reflective Elements:

Refers back to beginning

Shares what was learned

Personal feeling/reflection

Sense of closure

Descriptive (from "My Classroom"):

In conclusion, my classroom is more than just a room with desks and a chalkboard. It is a special place where we learn, grow, and build friendships. Every element, from the educational charts on the walls to the library corner filled with books, contributes to creating an environment that inspires learning. I am proud to call this well-organized, welcoming space my classroom. It truly is my second home.
Type: Summary + Reflection Elements:

Restates main idea ("more than just a room")

Summarizes key features

Personal connection

Returns to introduction ("second home")

Argumentative (from "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory"):

In conclusion, school uniforms should be mandatory in all educational institutions. As demonstrated, uniforms promote equality, enhance discipline, and create a sense of school identity. While some may argue that uniforms limit self-expression, these minor concerns are far
outweighed by the significant benefits uniforms provide. Therefore, schools, parents, and policymakers should work together to implement uniform policies. Our students deserve an environment focused on learning, not fashion competitions.
Type: Summary + Call to Action Elements:

Clear conclusion signal

Restatement of position

Summary of arguments

Addresses counter-argument briefly

Call to action

Strong final statement

Expository (from "The Importance of Education"):

To summarize, education plays a vital role in individual and societal development. It provides knowledge and skills, opens economic opportunities, promotes social mobility, and fosters informed citizenship. Without education, progress would be impossible and potential would remain unrealized. Every child deserves access to quality education. As Nelson Mandela said, "Education is the most powerful weapon which you can use to change the world." Let us recognize and support education as the foundation of a better future.
Type: Summary + Quotation + Call to Action Elements:

Conclusion signal

Summary of main points

Broader implications

Relevant quotation

Call to action

Forward-looking statement

WHAT TO AVOID IN CONCLUSIONS:

New Information:

✗ Don't introduce new ideas or arguments

✗ Don't bring up points not discussed in body

Apologizing:

✗ "I'm sorry this wasn't very good"

✗ "I know I didn't explain this well"

Contradicting Your Position:

✗ Don't suddenly change your stance

✗ Don't undermine your arguments

Being Too Long:

Should be brief (about 10% of composition)

Don't repeat everything in detail

Using "The End":

✗ Don't write "The End" at the end

Composition should naturally conclude

Exact Repetition:

✗ Don't copy sentences from introduction or body word-for-word

Rephrase and summarize

Phrases Like "In this essay I talked about...":

Too obvious and unnecessary
Better: naturally summarize

CONNECTING THE THREE PARTS:

Flow and Coherence:

Introduction → Body:

Thesis/main idea introduced in introduction should be developed in body

Preview in introduction prepares for body content

Body → Conclusion:

Main points in body are summarized in conclusion

Arguments/descriptions/events lead to conclusion

Introduction ↔ Conclusion:

Echo each other

Conclusion often returns to idea/image from introduction

Creates circular structure

Example - Narrative:

Introduction: "I will never forget that day"

Conclusion: "That day taught me a lesson I'll never forget"

COMPLETE COMPOSITION EXAMPLE:

Topic: My Best Friend

INTRODUCTION: Everyone needs a friend they can trust and rely on. I am lucky to have Sarah as my best friend. We met three years ago when she moved into the house next to mine, and we have been inseparable ever since. Sarah is not just a friend; she is like a sister to me.
BODY PARAGRAPH 1: Sarah is one of the most kind-hearted people I know. She always thinks about others before herself. Whenever I am sad or worried, she listens patiently and offers comforting words. She also helps other students in class who struggle with their schoolwork.
Last week, she spent her lunch break tutoring a classmate in Mathematics instead of playing with us. Her kindness makes everyone around her feel valued and cared for.
BODY PARAGRAPH 2: Moreover, Sarah is very reliable and trustworthy. I can share my secrets with her knowing she will never tell anyone. She keeps her promises, no matter how small. Once, she promised to help me with my Science project, and even when she fell ill, she still found time to assist me. Her loyalty and dependability have strengthened our friendship over the years. I know I can always count on her.
BODY PARAGRAPH 3: In addition to being kind and reliable, Sarah has a great sense of humor. She knows how to make me laugh even when I'm having a bad day. Her funny jokes and stories brighten any situation. During our break time at school, she entertains our group with hilarious imitations of our favorite cartoon characters. Life is never boring when Sarah is around.
CONCLUSION: In conclusion, Sarah is truly my best friend. Her kindness, reliability, and sense of humor make her a wonderful person to have in my life. I am grateful for our friendship and the joy she brings to every day. True friends like Sarah are rare and precious. I hope our friendship lasts forever.

ANALYSIS OF EXAMPLE:

Structure:

✓ Clear introduction with hook and thesis

✓ Three body paragraphs, each with one main point

✓ Logical organization

✓ Proper conclusion
Unity:

✓ Each paragraph focuses on one trait

✓ All sentences relate to topic sentence

✓ Everything connects to main idea (Sarah as best friend)

Coherence:

✓ Transitions used (Moreover, In addition, Once, During)

✓ Ideas flow smoothly

✓ Logical progression

Content:

✓ Specific examples provided

✓ Details support main points

✓ Personal connection clear

EVALUATION:

What are the three main parts of a composition? Explain the purpose of each

List and explain five types of hooks that can be used in an introduction

What should a good body paragraph contain? Explain each component

Write an introduction paragraph (5 sentences) for a narrative essay titled "An Unforgettable Experience"
What should you avoid in a conclusion? Give three things and explain why

ASSIGNMENT:
Write a complete short composition (150-200 words) on "My School" with clearly labeled introduction, body (2 paragraphs), and conclusion
Explain five characteristics of a good introduction and five characteristics of a good conclusion
Create an outline for a composition on "The Happiest Day of My Life" showing: (a) Introduction (hook, background, thesis) (b) Three body paragraphs (topic sentences) (c) Conclusion (summary and final thought)
Identify and label the introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion in any composition from your textbook. Explain what makes each part effective
Write three different types of conclusions (summary, reflective, and call-to-action) for the topic "The Importance of Reading"
WEEK 2 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: FEATURES OF LITERATURE; TYPES – ORAL, HISTORICAL, DANCE DRAMA, MIMING

PART A: FEATURES OF LITERATURE

Key Features:

IMAGINATIVE/CREATIVE

Product of writer's imagination

Creates new worlds and characters

USE OF LANGUAGE

Beautiful, artistic expression

Figurative language (metaphors, similes)

EMOTIONAL APPEAL

Evokes feelings and empathy

Engages emotions

FORM AND STRUCTURE

Organized presentation

Attention to format (prose, poetry, drama)

PERMANENCE

Lasting value

Transcends time

UNIVERSALITY

Appeals across cultures
Timeless themes

MULTIPLE INTERPRETATIONS

Layers of meaning

Encourages analysis

AESTHETIC QUALITY

Beautiful and pleasing

Artistic craftsmanship

PART B: TYPES OF LITERATURE

ORAL LITERATURE

Definition: Literature composed, performed, and transmitted by word of mouth, passed from generation to generation through speech and memory.
Characteristics:

Verbal Transmission: Spoken, not written; relies on memory

Performance-Based: Uses voice, gesture, facial expressions

Communal: Belongs to community, performed at gatherings

Flexible/Variable: Changes with each performance

Functional: Serves education, entertainment, ritual purposes

Traditional: Part of cultural heritage

Types of Oral Literature:

Folktales - Traditional stories teaching lessons

Myths - Sacred stories explaining origins

Legends - Stories about heroes and historical figures
Proverbs - Short wise sayings (e.g., "When two elephants fight, the grass suffers")

Riddles - Puzzles for entertainment and education

Praise Poetry - Poems praising individuals or deities

Songs - Work songs, lullabies, war songs

Oral Epics - Long narrative poems about heroic deeds

Performance Features:

Call and response

Repetition and formulaic language

Rhythmic patterns

Music and drumming

Audience participation

Functions:

Entertainment and education

Historical preservation

Moral instruction

Cultural identity

Social control

Performers: Griots, elders, parents, community members

HISTORICAL LITERATURE

Definition: Literary works based on or set in historical periods, dealing with real historical events and figures, often with fictional elements.
Characteristics:
Based on historical events and real figures

Accurate historical setting and period details

Educational value

Blend of fact and fiction

Types:

Historical Fiction - Novels set in the past

Historical Drama/Plays - Plays about historical events

Historical Poetry - Poems commemorating battles and heroes

Biographies - Life stories of real people

Chronicles - Written accounts blending history and storytelling

Purposes:

Preserve and educate about history

Inspire and build identity

Learn from the past

Commemorate heroes

Examples: Stories of Queen Amina, Shaka Zulu, resistance against colonialism

DANCE DRAMA

Definition: Dramatic performance combining storytelling with dance movements, told primarily through dance and gesture with minimal or no spoken dialogue.
Characteristics:

Movement-Based: Dance is primary expression

Musical Accompaniment: Drums and instruments
Symbolic Gestures: Movements represent actions and emotions

Costumes and Props: Elaborate, colorful costumes

Minimal Dialogue: Story understood through action

Elements:

Plot/Story conveyed through dance

Characters portrayed by dancers

Music and rhythm

Choreography

Costumes and makeup

Types of Movements:

Representational: Mimic real actions (planting, fighting)

Symbolic: Abstract movements with cultural meaning

Narrative: Sequence tells complete story

Purposes: Entertainment, cultural preservation, storytelling, ritual, education

Examples: Masquerade performances (Egungun, Ekpo), festival dances, harvest celebrations

MIMING (MIME/PANTOMIME)

Definition: Dramatic performance where story is told entirely through body movements, gestures, and facial expressions without words.
Characteristics:

Silent: No spoken words

Exaggerated Gestures: Clear and visible movements

Facial Expressions: Express emotions clearly
Physical Acting: Entire body involved

Imaginary Objects: Creates illusion of invisible props

Universal Understanding: No language barrier

Techniques:

Illusion Creation: Pretending to touch/hold objects, climbing invisible stairs

Isolation: Moving one body part while keeping others still

Spatial Awareness: Defining boundaries and environment

Weight and Resistance: Showing heaviness or effort

Character Through Movement: Posture reveals traits

Common Scenarios:

Trapped in a box

Walking against wind

Climbing rope/ladder

Carrying heavy object

Opening/closing doors

Purposes: Entertainment, universal communication, artistic expression, education

Elements of Successful Mime: Clarity, precision, imagination, expression, practice

COMPARISON TABLE

TypeMain FeaturePrimary MediumAudience RoleOral LiteratureSpoken wordVoice, memoryOften participatoryHistorical LiteratureBased on historyWritten/oral textPassiveDance DramaMovement & musicDance, gestureObserversMimingSilent gestureBody movementObservers

RELATIONSHIP BETWEEN TYPES

Common Elements:

All are performance arts

Storytelling element

Cultural preservation

Entertainment and education

MODERN APPLICATIONS

Oral Literature: Radio storytelling, spoken word poetry, traditional festivals

Historical Literature: Historical novels, biographies, films

Dance Drama: Cultural festivals, theatre productions, school performances

Miming: Street performances, theatre scenes, drama education

IMPORTANCE

Cultural Value: Preserve heritage, maintain traditions, build identity

Educational Value: Teach history and values, develop creativity, improve communication

Social Value: Bring communities together, create shared experiences

EVALUATION

Define oral literature and list four characteristics
Explain three types of oral literature with examples

What is historical literature? Give three African examples

Define dance drama and miming. State three differences

Why are these forms important for cultural preservation?

ASSIGNMENT

Create a comparison table for all four types showing: (a) definitions (b) main features (c) purposes (d) examples
Collect three proverbs from your culture. Explain their meanings and lessons

Research one Nigerian historical figure (200 words). Explain their importance

Describe how to tell "The Tortoise and the Birds" using: (a) oral storytelling (b) dance drama (c) miming
List five functions of oral literature in traditional African society with examples
WEEK 3 - SPEECH WORK TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /I:/

CONTENT:

THE LONG VOWEL SOUND /i:/

Characteristics:

Long, prolonged sound

Tongue very high at front of mouth

Lips spread wide (tense smile position)

Tense, not relaxed

Higher and longer than /ɪ/

Mouth Position:

Tongue tip behind lower front teeth

Front of tongue raised very high

Lips stretched horizontally

Jaw barely open

Teeth almost together

SPELLING PATTERNS FOR /i:/ SOUND:

"ee" spelling (most common):

see, bee, tree, free, three

feel, feet, meet, sweet, street

need, feed, seed, speed
sleep, keep, deep, sheep

green, between, screen

"ea" spelling:

sea, tea, pea, flea

eat, meat, heat, seat, beat, treat

read (present tense), lead (verb), teach, reach

clean, mean, bean,ream

please,ease, tease

"e" at end of syllable:

he, she, we, me, be

even, evening, female

decent, recent, secret

complete, delete

"ie/ei" spelling:

field, yield, shield, wield

piece, niece, brief, grief, belief, relief, chief, thief

receive, deceive, perceive, ceiling, conceit

"i" spelling (rare):

machine, police, prestige

ski, kiwi, regime

"ey/y" spelling (word endings):

key, donkey, monkey, hockey

happy, lucky, funny, sunny, baby, lady (unstressed)
"eo" spelling:

people

Other rare spellings:

quay /ki:/ (dock)

amoeba /əˈmi:bə/

COMMON WORDS WITH /i:/ SOUND:

Short words:

be, he, me, we, she, see, tea, sea, key, bee, tree, free

Medium words:

feet, meet, sweet, green, clean, dream, speak, teach, reach, beach

Long words:

believe, achieve, complete, between, fifteen, thirteen -Receive, deceive, ceiling, people, machine

PRACTICE SENTENCES:

We need to meet at three

She can see the green tree

Please keep the seat clean

He dreams of the sea and beach

The people believe in freedom

Fifteen sheep sleep under the tree
MINIMAL PAIRS: /i:/ vs /ɪ/

These pairs show the difference between the long /i:/ and short /ɪ/ sounds:

seat /i:/ - sit /ɪ/

feet /i:/ - fit /ɪ/

beat /i:/ - bit /ɪ/

sheep /i:/ - ship /ɪ/

seen /i:/ - sin /ɪ/

deal /i:/ - dill /ɪ/

feel /i:/ - fill /ɪ/

keen /i:/ - kin /ɪ/

wheel /i:/ - will /ɪ/

week /i:/ - wick /ɪ/ Key Difference:
/i:/ is LONG - hold the sound: "seeeee"

/ɪ/ is SHORT - quick sound: "sit"

Test yourself:

"I need to sit in my seat" (both sounds in one sentence)

"She will turn the wheel" (both sounds)

PRONUNCIATION TIPS:

To Produce /i:/ Correctly:

Smile wide:

Stretch lips horizontally
Feel corners of mouth pull back

Like showing your teeth

Raise tongue high:

Front of tongue up

Almost touching roof of mouth

Tip stays behind lower teeth

Make it long:

Hold the sound longer

Don't cut it short

Steady, continuous sound

Keep tense:

Muscles tight, not relaxed

More effort than /ɪ/

Definite, clear sound

Close jaw slightly:

Teeth nearly together

Just small opening

Don't open wide

COMMON MISTAKES:

Confusion with /ɪ/:

✗ Saying "sit" when you mean "seat"
✗ Short sound instead of long

Practice: Exaggerate the length difference

Incorrect spelling pronunciation:

✗ "great" is /eɪ/ NOT /i:/ (irregular)

✗ "head" is /e/ NOT /i:/ (irregular)

✗ "break" is /eɪ/ NOT /i:/ (irregular)

Adding extra sounds:

✗ "feel-uh" (adding schwa at end)

✓ "feel" (clean ending)

Not tensing enough:

✗ Relaxed, short sound

✓ Tense, long, deliberate sound

IRREGULAR PRONUNCIATIONS TO WATCH:

Not all "ea" spellings are /i:/:

bread, head, dead, ready = /e/ sound

break, great, steak = /eɪ/ sound
