heart, hearth = /ɑ:/ sound Context helps determine pronunciation:
read (present): /i:/ - "I read books"

read (past): /e/ - "I read that book yesterday"

lead (verb): /i:/ - "Lead the way"
lead (metal): /e/ - "Lead is heavy"

PRACTICE EXERCISES:

Reading Practice: Read these sentences aloud, emphasizing /i:/ sounds:

The teacher needs to meet three people

We believe she can achieve her dream

Please keep the green leaves clean

He feels weak after fifteen weeks

Listening Practice: Which word do you hear: /i:/ or /ɪ/?

ship or sheep?

fit or feet?

fill or feel?

bin or bean?

Speaking Practice: Say these word groups, maintaining long /i:/:

sea, see, seat, seek

tea, team, teach, tease

fee, feed, feel, feet

Sentence Creation: Make sentences using these /i:/ words:

need, sleep, dream

people, believe, achieve

clean, green, between

WORD STRESS AND /i:/ SOUND:
In stressed syllables (clear /i:/):

beLIEVE, comPLETE, aGREE

reCEIVE, deCEIVE, rePEAT

In unstressed syllables:

Sometimes shortened or less clear

"happy" - the 'y' may sound closer to /ɪ/ in casual speech

But maintain /i:/ in careful pronunciation

HOMOPHONES WITH /i:/ SOUND:

Words that sound the same but spelled differently:

meet/meat - "Let's meet at the meat shop"

see/sea - "Can you see the sea?"

be/bee - "Be careful of that bee"

peace/piece - "We need peace, not just a piece of it"

week/weak - "After a week, I still feel weak"

feat/feet - "Running on your feet is a great feat"

LINKING /i:/ SOUNDS:

When /i:/ sound comes at the end of a word followed by a vowel:

Add slight /j/ sound (like 'y')

"see it" sounds like "see-yit"

"three apples" sounds like "three-yapples"

"she is" sounds like "she-yis"
Practice:

We are → "we-yare"

See everyone → "see-yeveryone"

The apple → "the-yapple"

CONNECTED SPEECH:

In natural, fast speech:

/i:/ may be slightly shortened (but still longer than /ɪ/)

Still distinguishable from short /ɪ/

Maintain difference even when speaking quickly

Example: "I need to see three people between three and four"

Even said quickly, all /i:/ sounds remain longer than /ɪ/

TONGUE TWISTERS FOR PRACTICE:

Three free trees for me to see

She sees cheese in the breeze by the sea

Peter Piper peacefully sleeps beneath the peach tree

Fifteen people believe in the teacher's scheme

Keep the green tea clean and sweet between meals

PHYSICAL PRACTICE:

Exaggeration Exercise:

Start with extreme smile - lips pulled back very far
Make /i:/ sound as long as possible (5 seconds)

Feel tongue position - very high and front

Gradually reduce exaggeration to natural

Maintain correct position

Comparison Exercise:

Say "sit" (short /ɪ/)

Then "seat" (long /i:/)

Back to "sit"

Notice the difference

Repeat 10 times

Mirror Exercise:

Watch your mouth in mirror

See lips stretch for /i:/

Compare with /ɪ/ (less stretched)

Check consistent position

EVALUATION:

Describe how to produce the /i:/ sound (tongue position, lip shape, length)

Write ten words with "ee" spelling that have /i:/ sound

Write ten words with "ea" spelling that have /i:/ sound

What is the difference between /i:/ and /ɪ/? Give five minimal pairs

Identify which words have /i:/ sound: (a) bread (b) feet (c) head (d) see (e) great (f) meat
(g) sit (h) complete

ASSIGNMENT:

List twenty words containing /i:/ sound, showing different spelling patterns (ee, ea, e, ie, ei, i)
Write ten sentences using words with /i:/ sound, underlining the /i:/ words

Create five minimal pairs showing /i:/ vs /ɪ/ contrast and use each pair in a sentence

Practice reading aloud and record: "Three clean sheep sleep between fifteen green trees. The teacher believes people need to achieve their dreams. Please keep the sweet tea by the beach."
Explain three common mistakes people make when pronouncing /i:/ sound and how to correct them
WEEK 3 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: VERBS – DEFINITION, IDENTIFICATION; TYPES

WHAT IS A VERB?

Definition: A verb is a word that expresses an action, occurrence, or state of being.

Simple Definition: An action or being word.

Functions:

Show action: run, jump, eat, write

Show occurrence: happen, become, occur

Show state/condition: be, seem, appear, feel

Identifying Verbs - Ask:

What is happening?

What is being done?

Can you put "to" before it? (to run, to eat)

Does it change with time? (walk/walked)

Can you add -ing? (walking, eating)

TYPES OF VERBS

CLASSIFICATION 1: BY OBJECT REQUIREMENT

TRANSITIVE VERBS

Definition: Require a direct object to complete meaning. Action passes to an object.

Structure: Subject + Transitive Verb + Object

How to Identify: Ask "What?" or "Whom?" after verb
Examples:

She bought a book (Bought what? A book)

I love my mother (Love whom? My mother)

He kicks the ball (Kicks what? The ball)

Common Transitive Verbs: eat, drink, buy, sell, make, take, love, hate, like, want, need, write, read, speak, teach, kick, throw, catch, bring, send, give, show, tell

INTRANSITIVE VERBS

Definition: Do NOT require direct object. Action is complete by itself. Structure: Subject + Intransitive Verb (+ optional adverb/phrase) How to Identify: Ask "What?" or "Whom?" - no answer needed Examples:
The baby cried (Cried what? No answer needed)

Birds fly (Complete without object)

She laughed loudly ("loudly" describes how, not object)

Common Intransitive Verbs: sleep, wake, sit, stand, lie, rise, come, go, arrive, depart, return, cry, laugh, smile, die, live, exist, grow
Note: Some verbs can be both depending on context

"She runs every morning" (intransitive)

"She runs a business" (transitive - business = object)

CLASSIFICATION 2: BY FORM CHANGE

REGULAR VERBS
Definition: Form past tense and past participle by adding -ed, -d, or -t

Pattern: Base form + -ed = Past form

Rules:

Most verbs - add -ed: walk → walked, play → played

Verbs ending in -e - add -d: love → loved, dance → danced

Consonant + y - change y to i, add -ed: study → studied, cry → cried

CVC pattern - double final consonant, add -ed: stop → stopped, plan → planned

Common Regular Verbs: ask → asked, call → called, clean → cleaned, cook → cooked, finish
→ finished, help → helped, listen → listened, open → opened, visit → visited, wash → washed

IRREGULAR VERBS

Definition: Do NOT add -ed for past forms. Must be memorized.

Categories:

All three forms same:

cut → cut → cut

put → put → put

hit → hit → hit

hurt → hurt → hurt

Past and past participle same:

bring → brought → brought

buy → bought → bought

teach → taught → taught

sit → sat → sat
make → made → made

say → said → said

All three forms different:

go → went → gone

see → saw → seen

eat → ate → eaten

write → wrote → written

take → took → taken

give → gave → given

Vowel pattern change:

sing → sang → sung

drink → drank → drunk

swim → swam → swum

begin → began → begun

Common Irregular Verbs:

BasePastPast Participlebewas/werebeencomecamecomedodiddonefindfoundfoundgetgotgot/gottenhavehadhadknowknewknownspeakspokespokentelltoldtoldthinkthoughtthought

CLASSIFICATION 3: BY FUNCTION

AUXILIARY VERBS (Helping Verbs)

Definition: Help main verb form tenses, moods, or voices

Primary Auxiliaries:

be (am, is, are, was, were, been, being)

have (has, had, having)

do (does, did)

Uses:

be: Continuous tenses (I am eating), Passive voice (It was done)

have: Perfect tenses (I have eaten)

do: Questions (Do you like it?), Negatives (I do not know), Emphasis (I do love you)

Examples:

She is reading (auxiliary: is, main: reading)

They have finished (auxiliary: have, main: finished)

I do not understand (auxiliary: do, main: understand)

Modal Auxiliaries:

Express possibility, permission, ability, obligation, advice
List:

can - ability, possibility

could - past ability, polite request

may - permission, possibility

might - slight possibility

must - obligation, necessity

shall - future (formal)

should - advice, expectation

will - future, willingness

would - polite request, hypothetical

ought to - advice

Characteristics:

Followed by base form of verb (no -s, -ing, -ed)

No third person -s

Cannot be used alone

Examples:

I can swim (ability)

You should study (advice)

She will come (future)

They must obey (obligation)

Common Errors:

✗ He cans swim → ✓ He can swim
✗ She must goes → ✓ She must go

✗ They will going → ✓ They will go

LEXICAL VERBS (Main Verbs)

Definition: Carry main meaning in sentence; the "real" action or state verbs

Characteristics:

Express main action/state

Can stand alone

Have full meaning

Examples:

I run every day (lexical verb only)

She ate rice (lexical verb only)

Lexical vs. Auxiliary:

"I have a pen" (have = lexical/main verb)

"I have eaten" (have = auxiliary, eaten = lexical)

VERB PHRASES

Definition: Main verb + one or more auxiliary verbs

Examples:

is eating (auxiliary + main verb)

have been studying (aux + aux + main verb)

will be going (modal + aux + main verb)
should have come (modal + aux + main verb)

SUMMARY TABLE

TypeDefinitionExamplesTransitiveNeeds objecteat, buy, love, kickIntransitiveNo object neededsleep, arrive, laugh, dieRegularAdd -ed for pastwalk → walkedIrregularSpecial past formsgo → went, see → sawAuxiliaryHelp main verbam, is, have, do, can, willLexicalMain meaning verbrun, eat, sleep, study

COMMON ERRORS

Transitive/Intransitive confusion:

✗ "I explained him the answer" → ✓ "I explained the answer to him"

✗ "She married with John" → ✓ "She married John"

Irregular verb forms:

✗ "I goed home" → ✓ "I went home"

✗ "She has wrote" → ✓ "She has written"

Modal auxiliary mistakes:

✗ "He cans swim" → ✓ "He can swim"

✗ "They will going" → ✓ "They will go"

Missing verb:
✗ "She to school" → ✓ "She goes to school"

EVALUATION:

Define verb and state 3 functions

Explain difference between transitive and intransitive verbs with 3 examples each

What's the difference between regular and irregular verbs? Give 5 examples of each

List modal auxiliary verbs and what each expresses

Identify and classify verbs: (a) She has eaten breakfast (b) Birds fly (c) I bought a pen yesterday (d) They can swim well (e) He sleeps soundly

Write 20 irregular verbs with base form, past form, and past participle

Identify and classify all verbs as transitive/intransitive: "Mary woke up early. She ate breakfast quickly and went to school. The teacher arrived late. Students waited patiently. She taught Mathematics. They listened carefully."
Create sentences using: (a) run, sing, eat, write, read as transitive AND intransitive (b) have, will, can, should, must with main verbs
Fill in correct verb forms: (a) I	(go) to school yesterday (b) She	(eat) rice now (c) They	(be) happy tomorrow (d) He	(write) three letters (e) We
 	(study) when you came

Explain difference between lexical and auxiliary verbs with 5 examples showing same verb used both ways
WEEK 3 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: SQ3R TECHNIQUE; VOCABULARY – MEMBERS OF THE FAMILY

PART A: THE SQ3R TECHNIQUE

What is SQ3R? A proven reading and study method that improves comprehension and retention.

Full Form:

S - Survey

Q - Question

R - Read

R - Recite

R - Review

Why Use SQ3R?

Improves understanding and retention

Makes studying more active

Saves time in exam preparation

Identifies important information

THE FIVE STEPS

STEP 1: SURVEY (Preview)

Definition: Quickly look over material to get the "big picture"

How to Survey:

Read title, introduction, and conclusion

Read all headings and subheadings
Look at pictures, diagrams, charts

Read first sentence of each paragraph

Notice bold/italicized words Time: 5-10 minutes for a chapter Example: Surveying "The Water Cycle"
Title and headings: Evaporation, Condensation, Precipitation, Collection

Diagram shows cycle process

Creates mental framework

STEP 2: QUESTION

Definition: Turn headings into questions to give reading purpose

How to Create Questions:

Use: What, Who, When, Where, Why, How

Write questions down

Example Questions for "Evaporation":

What is evaporation?

What causes water to evaporate?

Where does evaporation occur?

Why is evaporation necessary?

Benefits: Creates focus and improves concentration

STEP 3: READ

Definition: Read actively to answer your questions
How to Read Effectively:

Read one section at a time

Look for answers to your questions

Highlight/underline key points

Take notes on main ideas and definitions

Make connections to prior knowledge

Example: Reading "Evaporation"

Answer: Evaporation is liquid water changing to water vapor

Note: Caused by sun's heat

Connection: Like puddles drying after rain

STEP 4: RECITE

Definition: After each section, recall main points in your own words without looking at text

How to Recite:

Close book, look away

Answer questions aloud or in writing

Use your own words

Summarize key ideas

Check text to verify accuracy

If you can't recite, reread

Time: 2-3 minutes per section
Example: After reading "Evaporation" "Evaporation is when liquid water becomes water vapor due to sun's heat. It happens from oceans, lakes, rivers. Water vapor rises into atmosphere. First step of water cycle."
Benefits: Strengthens memory, identifies gaps

STEP 5: REVIEW

Definition: Go over entire chapter to consolidate learning

How to Review:

Immediate Review (right after reading):

Skim entire chapter

Review notes and highlights

Answer end-of-chapter questions

Recite main points

Ongoing Review:

Review next day, before class, before exam

Make summary/outline

Apply knowledge to examples

Time: 10-15 minutes immediately, then brief reviews later

Benefits: Moves information to long-term memory, reduces forgetting

COMPLETE SQ3R EXAMPLE: "Photosynthesis"

SURVEY (2 min): Title, headings (What is it, Requirements, Process, Importance), diagram
QUESTION (1 min): What is photosynthesis? What do plants need? How does it work?

READ (10 min):

Photosynthesis = plants make food using sunlight

Needs: sunlight, water, CO₂, chlorophyll

Process: CO₂ + water + light → glucose + oxygen

RECITE (4 min): Close book and summarize each section

REVIEW (5 min): Plants are food factories; review key equation and diagram

TIPS FOR EFFECTIVE SQ3R DO:
Take time with each step

Write things down

Use your own words in recitation

Review multiple times

DON'T:

Skip steps

Rush through

Just highlight without thinking

Read passively

PART B: VOCABULARY – MEMBERS OF THE FAMILY IMMEDIATE FAMILY (NUCLEAR FAMILY)
Parents:
Father/Dad/Papa

Mother/Mum/Mama

Parent (gender-neutral)

Children:

Son, Daughter, Child

Offspring (formal)

Siblings:

Brother, Sister, Sibling

Twin, Triplets, Quadruplets

Birth Order:

Elder/Older, Younger

Eldest/Oldest (first-born)

Youngest (last-born)

Middle child

EXTENDED FAMILY

Grandparents:

Grandfather/Grandpa, Grandmother/Grandma

Great-grandfather, Great-grandmother

Grandchildren:

Grandson, Granddaughter, Grandchild

Uncles and Aunts:

Uncle: Father's/mother's brother or aunt's husband
Paternal uncle (father's side)

Maternal uncle (mother's side)

Aunt/Aunty: Father's/mother's sister or uncle's wife

Paternal aunt, Maternal aunt

Nieces and Nephews:

Nephew: Brother's/sister's son

Niece: Brother's/sister's daughter

Cousins:

Cousin: Uncle's/aunt's child

First cousin, Second cousin

IN-LAWS (RELATIVES BY MARRIAGE)

Parent's In-Laws:

Father-in-law: Spouse's father

Mother-in-law: Spouse's mother

Sibling's In-Laws:

Brother-in-law: Sister's husband, spouse's brother

Sister-in-law: Brother's wife, spouse's sister

Children's In-Laws:

Son-in-law: Daughter's husband

Daughter-in-law: Son's wife

STEP-RELATIONS (BLENDED FAMILIES)
Step-Parents:

Stepfather: Mother's new husband (not biological father)

Stepmother: Father's new wife (not biological mother)

Step-Siblings:

Stepbrother, Stepsister

Half-Siblings:

Half-brother, Half-sister (share one parent only)

SPECIAL RELATIONSHIPS

Guardians:

Guardian, Foster parent, Foster child

Adopted child, Adoptive parent

Godparents:

Godfather, Godmother, Godchild

Others:

Spouse, Husband, Wife

Partner (not married)

Fiancé (male), Fiancée (female)

Widow (woman), Widower (man)

Orphan (parents died)

POSSESSIVE FORMS (Showing Relationships)

My father's brother = my uncle
My mother's mother = my grandmother

My sister's daughter = my niece

My uncle's son = my cousin

My son's wife = my daughter-in-law

USEFUL EXPRESSIONS

Describing Relationships:

"He is my elder brother"

"She is my maternal grandmother"

"They are my paternal cousins"

"I am an only child"

Talking About Family:

"I come from a large family"

"I have three siblings"

"I am the youngest of five children"

"We are a close-knit family"

COMMON QUESTIONS

How many people are in your family?

Do you have any brothers or sisters?

Are you the eldest, youngest, or middle child?

How many cousins do you have?

Do your grandparents live with you?

FAMILY VOCABULARY IN CONTEXT

"My family is quite large. My parents are still alive. I have two siblings - an elder brother and a younger sister. My brother is married, so I have a sister-in-law and two nephews. My grandparents on my mother's side live in the village with my maternal uncle and his family. We often visit them during holidays."

Why is the Recite step important? How should you do it?

What is the difference between immediate and periodic review?

(b) 3 questions created

(c) Main points from Reading

(d) Recite summary

(e) Review notes

(a) Your father's sister

(b) Your sister's daughter

(c) Your mother's father

(d) Your uncle's son

(e) Your brother's wife

(a) Aunt vs. niece

(b) Nephew vs. cousin

(c) Stepbrother vs. half-brother

(d) Father-in-law vs. stepfather

Write a paragraph (150 words) describing your family using at least 15 family vocabulary words
WEEK 3 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: ELEMENTS OF COMPOSITION – INTRODUCTION, BODY, CONCLUSION
(Reinforcement and Practice)

REVIEW: THE THREE PARTS

Introduction: Opens composition, introduces topic, hooks reader

Body: Develops ideas with details, examples, explanations

Conclusion: Wraps up, summarizes, leaves lasting impression

ANALYZING SAMPLE COMPOSITIONS

SAMPLE 1: NARRATIVE - "A Visit to the Zoo"

INTRODUCTION: "Last Saturday was one of the most exciting days of my life. My school organized an excursion to the National Zoo, and I had been looking forward to it for weeks. As our bus pulled into the zoo's parking lot, I could hardly contain my excitement. Little did I know that this visit would teach me important lessons about wildlife conservation."
Analysis:

✓ Hook: "most exciting days"

✓ Background and personal feeling

✓ Preview of lesson learned

✓ Sets scene

BODY PARAGRAPHS (3 paragraphs):

Paragraph 1: Lions - descriptive details, specific facts, personal actions

Paragraph 2: Monkeys - entertaining incident, guide's warning, reflection
Paragraph 3: Reptile house - contrasting emotions, conservation issue

Key Features:

Each paragraph has clear topic

Specific details (golden mane, monkey stealing cap)

Transitions: "When," "Next," "The highlight"

Personal involvement and reflection

Chronological order

CONCLUSION: "As we boarded the bus to return to school, I felt both happy and thoughtful. The zoo visit had been educational and fun, exposing me to animals I had only read about in books. More importantly, I learned about the threats facing wildlife and the need to protect these beautiful creatures. I promised myself to support conservation efforts in whatever way I could.
That day at the zoo changed my perspective on animals and nature. I will never forget it."

Analysis:

✓ Mixed feelings expressed

✓ Summarizes main points

✓ States main lesson

✓ Personal commitment

✓ Returns to introduction theme

✓ Provides closure

SAMPLE 2: DESCRIPTIVE - "My Favorite Teacher"

INTRODUCTION: "Among all the teachers in my school, Mrs. Okafor stands out as my favorite. She teaches English Language, my best subject, but it's not just her subject that makes her special. Mrs. Okafor has a unique combination of qualities that make her an exceptional
teacher. Her teaching style, personality, and genuine care for students have earned her the respect and admiration of everyone in our school."
Analysis:

✓ Clear hook

✓ Thesis statement

✓ Preview: teaching style, personality, care

BODY PARAGRAPHS (3 paragraphs):

Paragraph 1: Physical appearance - systematic description (height, complexion, clothing, accessories)
Paragraph 2: Teaching style - interactive methods, real-life examples, patience

Paragraph 3: Personality - patient, respectful, humorous, fair, caring

Key Features:

One main idea per paragraph

Specific examples throughout

Varied sentence structure

Smooth transitions

CONCLUSION: "Mrs. Okafor is more than just a teacher; she is a mentor and role model. Her combination of professional excellence and personal warmth makes her truly exceptional. She has taught me not just English Language but also important life lessons about kindness, patience, and dedication. Every student who has been fortunate enough to be in her class leaves with improved knowledge and character. I am grateful to have such an inspiring teacher, and I hope to emulate her qualities in my own life. Mrs. Okafor embodies what every teacher should aspire to be."
Analysis:

✓ Elevation of subject

✓ Summary of qualities

✓ Dual learning emphasized

✓ Broader impact noted

✓ Personal gratitude and aspiration

IMPROVING WEAK WRITING WEAK INTRODUCTION:
"I will write about my best friend. Her name is Sarah. She is a good person. I like her."

Problems: Too simple, no hook, announcing intention, lacks interest

IMPROVED:

"Everyone needs a true friend who stands by them through thick and thin. I am fortunate to have found such a friend in Sarah Adamu. Since we met three years ago when she transferred to my school, Sarah has proven to be not just a friend but a sister. Her loyalty, kindness, and sense of humor make her truly special."
Improvements: Universal statement hook, natural introduction, background, preview, sophisticated language

WEAK BODY PARAGRAPH:

"My mother is nice. She cooks for us. She helps me with homework. She is kind. I love my mother. She works hard. She is the best mother."
Problems: Choppy sentences, repetitive, no examples, no transitions, list-like
IMPROVED:

"My mother's kindness touches everyone around her. Every morning, she wakes up at 5 AM to prepare breakfast for our family, ensuring we start the day well-nourished. Despite working full- time as a nurse, she still finds time to help me with my homework, patiently explaining difficult concepts until I understand. Her kindness extends beyond our family; she often visits sick neighbors and brings them food. Last month, when our elderly neighbor fell ill, my mother visited her daily for two weeks, bringing meals and medicine. Such selfless acts demonstrate why I admire her so deeply."
Improvements: Topic sentence, specific examples (5 AM, nurse, neighbor), varied sentences, transitions, unified focus

WEAK CONCLUSION:

"That is all about my school. My school is good. I like my school. Thank you. The end." Problems: Abrupt, repetitive, inappropriate phrases, no summary, no closure IMPROVED:
"Government Secondary School has shaped me into the person I am today. Its excellent teachers, supportive environment, and diverse opportunities have provided me with both academic knowledge and life skills. The friendships I've formed and lessons I've learned extend far beyond textbooks. I am proud to be a student of this institution and grateful for everything it has given me. As I continue my education here, I know I am in a place that will prepare me well for the future."
Improvements: Reflects on impact, summarizes key points, expresses gratitude, forward- looking, provides closure

SELF-CHECK QUESTIONS

Introduction:
✓ Hook present?

✓ Topic clearly introduced?

✓ Preview included?

Body:

✓ Each paragraph has one main idea?

✓ Specific details and examples?

✓ Logical flow?

✓ Transitions used?

Conclusion:

✓ Summarizes main points?

✓ Final thought or reflection?

✓ Provides closure?

✓ No new information?

Overall:

✓ Well-organized?

✓ Unified (stays on topic)?

✓ Coherent (flows smoothly)?

✓ Complete?

EVALUATION:

Identify introduction, body, and conclusion in "A Visit to the Zoo"
List 5 characteristics of an effective body paragraph

What improvements were made to the weak introduction about "My Best Friend"?

Why are transitions important? Give 5 examples

Write an introduction paragraph (5 sentences) for "My Favorite Sport"

Analyze any composition from your textbook: (a) label parts (b) identify hook type (c) list topic sentences (d) list transitions (e) describe conclusion type
Rewrite this weak paragraph: "My room is nice. It has a bed. There is a table. I have a wardrobe. The walls are blue. I like my room."

Write 2 conclusions (reflective and call-to-action) for "Keeping Our Environment Clean"

My Best Friend

Requirements:

Clear introduction with hook
2-3 body paragraphs with topic sentences

Specific details and examples

Appropriate transitions

WEEK 3 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: PURPOSE/FUNCTIONS OF LITERATURE; INTRODUCTION TO PROSE

PART A: FUNCTIONS OF LITERATURE

12 Main Functions:

Entertainment/Pleasure

Provides enjoyment and escape

Creates suspense, excitement, humor

Relaxation and stress relief

Education/Instruction

Teaches language, history, culture, life skills

Makes learning enjoyable and memorable

Simplifies complex ideas

Moral/Ethical Instruction

Teaches values: honesty, kindness, courage, respect

Shows consequences of actions

Shapes character and guides behavior

Example: Folktales teach "Honesty is the best policy"

Cultural Preservation

Records traditions, language, history, beliefs

Maintains cultural identity

Connects generations

Example: Myths, legends, folktales preserve heritage
Social Commentary/Criticism

Reflects and critiques society

Exposes problems (poverty, corruption, injustice)

Advocates for change

Example: Achebe's "Things Fall Apart" critiques colonialism

Emotional Expression/Catharsis

Allows expression and release of emotions

Helps process feelings safely

Develops empathy

Aesthetic Pleasure

Provides beauty through language

Appreciation of artistry and craftsmanship

Poetry's musical quality, prose's flowing sentences

Imagination/Creativity Stimulation

Fires imagination and encourages original thinking

Presents alternative worlds and possibilities

Enhances problem-solving

Communication/Language Development

Improves vocabulary, grammar, expression

Enhances reading, writing, speaking skills

Builds language confidence

Empathy and Understanding

Helps understand different perspectives
Reduces prejudice, increases tolerance

Builds compassion

Identity Formation

Helps individuals/groups develop sense of self

Builds national and cultural pride

Example: Nigerian literature builds Nigerian identity

Historical Documentation

Records events, daily life, attitudes

Preserves history for future study

Example: Slave narratives document slavery

Note: Most literary works serve multiple functions simultaneously.

PART B: INTRODUCTION TO PROSE

DEFINITION: Prose is writing organized in sentences and paragraphs, following natural speech patterns (not poetry or drama).
CHARACTERISTICS:

Written in sentences and paragraphs

Natural, grammatical language

No regular meter or rhyme

Detailed and expansive

Narrative focus

TYPES OF PROSE:
FICTION (Imaginative)

Novel

Long narrative (50,000+ words)

Complex plot, multiple characters

Examples: "Things Fall Apart," "Purple Hibiscus"

Novella

Medium-length (20,000-50,000 words)

Focused plot, fewer characters

Examples: "Animal Farm," "The Old Man and the Sea"

Short Story

Brief narrative (under 20,000 words)

Single plot, few characters, one theme

Read in one sitting

Examples: "The Sacrificial Egg," "Civil Peace"

NON-FICTION (Factual)

Biography: Life story written by someone else

Autobiography: Life story written by oneself (first person)

Memoir: Account of specific period/theme from author's life

Essay: Short piece on single subject (expository, argumentative, descriptive, etc.)

Travel Writing: Accounts of journeys and places

Journalism: Reports of current events

Historical Writing: Accounts of past events
ELEMENTS OF PROSE (Fiction):

PLOT

Sequence of events

Structure: Exposition → Rising Action → Climax → Falling Action → Resolution

CHARACTER

Types: Protagonist, antagonist, major, minor

Complexity: Flat (simple) vs. Round (complex)

Development: Static (no change) vs. Dynamic (changes)

Characterization: Direct (author tells) vs. Indirect (through actions, speech, thoughts, effects, looks)
SETTING

Time and place where story occurs

Includes historical period, location, social environment, atmosphere

Influences characters and plot

THEME

Central idea or message

Usually inferred, not stated directly

Can have multiple themes

Examples: good vs. evil, tradition vs. change, coming of age

POINT OF VIEW

First Person: "I" (narrator is character)

Third Person Limited: "He/She" (knows one character's thoughts)

Third Person Omniscient: "He/She" (knows all characters' thoughts)
Second Person: "You" (rare)

STYLE

Author's distinctive use of language

Includes: diction, sentence structure, tone, imagery, figurative language, voice

CONFLICT

Problem driving the story

Types: Man vs. Man, Man vs. Self, Man vs. Society, Man vs. Nature, Man vs. Fate

PROSE VS. OTHER GENRES:

AspectProsePoetryDramaFormSentences, paragraphsLines, stanzasDialogue, stage directionsLanguageNatural, expansiveCompressed, musicalSpokenPurposeTell storiesExpress emotionsShow action

HOW TO STUDY YOUR PROSE TEXT:

First Reading: Read for understanding and enjoyment

Second Reading: Identify plot, characters, setting, theme; take notes

Analysis: Character study, plot outline, theme identification, style observation

Critical Thinking: Why characters acted; author's message; real-life connections

Exam Prep: Character profiles, plot summary, themes with examples, practice answers

EVALUATION:

List 5 functions of literature with examples
Define prose and state 4 characteristics

Explain 3 elements of prose

Differentiate: (a) novel/short story (b) biography/autobiography (c) first/third person POV
State title, author, and type of your recommended prose text

ASSIGNMENT:

Explain 10 functions of literature with examples

Create comparison table: prose vs. poetry vs. drama

Read first 3 chapters of prose text; write: (a) summary (b) main characters (c) setting (d) one theme (e) opinion
Define 7 elements of prose with examples

Choose a short story; identify: (a) conflict type (b) POV (c) theme (d) protagonist/antagonist (e) 3 literary devices

WEEK 4 - SPEECH WORK TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɪ/

THE SHORT VOWEL SOUND /ɪ/

Characteristics:

Short, quick sound

Tongue high at front (lower than /i:/)

Lips slightly spread

Relaxed, not tense

Common in unstressed syllables

Mouth Position:

Tongue raised toward front

Lips neutral/slightly spread

Jaw slightly open

Tongue tip behind lower teeth

SPELLING PATTERNS FOR /ɪ/ SOUND

"i" spelling (most common):

sit, bit, hit, big, dig, swim

pin, tin, win, begin

fish, dish, wish

milk, silk
give, live (verb)

"y" spelling (unstressed syllables):

happy, lucky, funny, pretty

baby, lady, city

study, carry, worry

"e" spelling (rare):

pretty, English, women

"ui" spelling (rare):

build, building, guilt, biscuit

Other rare spellings:

sieve (ie), women (o)

COMMON WORDS WITH /ɪ/

Short words:

in, is, if, it, his, this, which, will, with, think

Medium words:

sister, little, children, minute, begin, finish, visit, ticket

Long words:

difficult, important, interesting, business, different, minister

MINIMAL PAIRS: /ɪ/ vs /i:/ Key Difference:
/ɪ/ is SHORT and RELAXED (sit)
/i:/ is LONG and TENSE (seat)

Practice Pairs:

sit /ɪ/ - seat /i:/

fit /ɪ/ - feet /i:/

bit /ɪ/ - beat /i:/

ship /ɪ/ - sheep /i:/

live /ɪ/ - leave /i:/

fill /ɪ/ - feel /i:/

chip /ɪ/ - cheap /i:/

slip /ɪ/ - sleep /i:/

rich /ɪ/ - reach /i:/

Test Sentences:

This ship carries sheep

Can you feel the fill in the bottle?

He will slip if he doesn't sleep well

The bit of food helped him beat hunger

PRONUNCIATION TIPS

To Produce /ɪ/ Correctly:

Keep it short: Don't prolong the sound; quick and brief

Relax: Don't tense your mouth; comfortable position

Tongue position: High and front, but not as high as /i:/

Lips: Slightly spread or neutral, not stretched wide
Common in unstressed syllables: "begin," "happy"

WORD STRESS AND /ɪ/

In Stressed Syllables:

SIT, BIG, MISS, FISH (clear /ɪ/ sound)

In Unstressed Syllables:

hapPY, luckY, citY, baBY (even shorter/weaker)

Examples:

VISit (first syllable stressed)

DIFficult (first syllable stressed)

COMMON MISTAKES

Using /i:/ instead of /ɪ/:

✗ Saying "feel" when you mean "fill"

Fix: Keep /ɪ/ short and relaxed

Using /e/ instead of /ɪ/:

✗ "pen" when you mean "pin"

Fix: Raise tongue higher for /ɪ/

Making it too long:

✗ "siiit" (sounds like "seat")

✓ "sit" (quick)

Confusing "live" and "leave":
live /ɪ/ (verb): "I live in Lagos"

leave /i:/: "I will leave tomorrow"

PRACTICE EXERCISES

Minimal Pair Drills:

sit/seat, bit/beat, hit/heat

ship/sheep, chip/cheap

fill/feel, will/wheel

Word Lists:

List 1: big, sit, fit, hit, bit

List 2: fish, wish, dish, finish, English

List 3: begin, ticket, minute, visit, limit

Sentence Practice:

It is difficult to finish this in a minute

The big fish swim in the river

Women and children visit the city

Which sister will begin to sing?

Tongue Twisters:

Six sick sheep sit in the ship

Which wristwatch is a Swiss wristwatch?

The myth of Miss Smith's mist list

DISTINGUISHING /ɪ/, /i:/, /e/
Three different sounds:

/ɪ/ (sit): Short, tongue high-front, relaxed

/i:/ (seat): Long, tongue very high-front, tense

/e/ (set): Short, tongue mid-front, relaxed

Practice Trio:

sit /ɪ/ - seat /i:/ - set /e/

bit /ɪ/ - beat /i:/ - bet /e/

fill /ɪ/ - feel /i:/ - fell /e/

CONNECTED SPEECH

Weak Forms (common words use /ɪ/):

it: "Get it done"

is: "He is coming"

his: "His book"

been: "I've been there"

High-Frequency Words:

in, is, it, if, his, with, this, which, will

SPELLING VS. PRONUNCIATION

Tricky Spellings: "i" can be /ɪ/ or /aɪ/:
bit /ɪ/ vs bite /aɪ/

sit /ɪ/ vs site /aɪ/
"y" at end usually /ɪ/:

happy, lucky, city

PHYSICAL PRACTICE

Exaggeration Exercise:

Say /i:/ (seat) - stretch lips wide, tongue very high

Relax to /ɪ/ (sit) - lips relax, tongue slightly lower

Feel the difference - /ɪ/ is more comfortable

Practice switching: seat-sit-seat-sit

Length Practice:

Hold /i:/ for 3 seconds: "seeeeeat"

Make /ɪ/ as short as possible: "sit" (instant)

EVALUATION

Describe how to produce the /ɪ/ sound (tongue position, lip shape, length)

Write ten words with "i" spelling that have /ɪ/ sound

Write ten words with "y" spelling that have /ɪ/ sound

What is the main difference between /ɪ/ and /i:/? Give five minimal pairs

Identify which words have /ɪ/ sound: (a) city (b) tree (c) big (d) sleep (e) fish (f) teach (g) sit (h) difficult

ASSIGNMENT

List twenty words containing /ɪ/ sound with different spelling patterns (i, y, e, ui)
Write ten sentences using minimal pairs of /ɪ/ and /i:/ to show the difference (e.g., "Please sit in your seat")
Create five sets of three-way contrasts: /ɪ/ vs /i:/ vs /e/ (like sit/seat/set) and use each in a sentence
Practice and record yourself reading: "It is difficult for children to finish this interesting activity. His sister will visit the city to begin business with the minister. Which women will swim in the big river?"
Explain three common mistakes people make when pronouncing /ɪ/ and how to correct them
WEEK 4 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: VERBS (CONTINUED) – TYPES AND FUNCTIONS

REVIEW: MAIN VERB TYPES

Transitive - needs object

Intransitive - no object needed

Regular - add -ed for past

Irregular - special past forms

Auxiliary - help main verb

Lexical - main meaning verb

FINITE VERBS

Definition: Verbs that show tense and agree with subject. Can stand alone.

Examples:

I go, He goes (present)

I went, She went (past)

I will go (future)

NON-FINITE VERBS

Definition: Do NOT show tense or agree with subject. Cannot stand alone.

INFINITIVES

Forms: to go (to-infinitive), go (bare infinitive)

Uses:
Subject: To read is important

Object: I want to go

Purpose: She came to help

After modals: I can swim (bare)

After certain verbs: Let me go (bare)

PARTICIPLES

PRESENT PARTICIPLE (-ing):

Continuous tenses: I am going

As adjective: running water

In phrases: Walking home, I saw her

PAST PARTICIPLE (-ed/irregular):

Perfect tenses: I have finished

Passive voice: The door was closed

As adjective: broken window

GERUNDS (-ing as noun)

Subject: Swimming is fun

Object: I enjoy dancing

After prepositions: Thank you for helping

Compound nouns: swimming pool

GERUND VS. PRESENT PARTICIPLE:

Gerund (noun): Swimming is fun (can replace with "Music is fun")

Participle (verb): I am swimming (cannot replace with noun)
COMPARISON TABLE

TypeExampleShows Tense?Finitegoes, wentYesInfinitiveto go, goNoPresent ParticiplegoingNoPast Participlegone, writtenNoGerundswimmingNo

VERB FUNCTIONS

Action: run, think, speak

State: be, seem, remain

Possession: have, own

Linking: be, feel, look, taste

CAUSATIVE VERBS

Show someone causing something to happen.

Patterns:

make + object + bare infinitive: She made me go

have + object + bare infinitive: I'll have him call you

get + object + to-infinitive: I got him to help

let + object + bare infinitive: Let me go

help + object + (to) infinitive: Help me (to) carry this
STATIVE VS. DYNAMIC VERBS

STATIVE (NOT used in continuous):

Emotions: love, hate, like, want

Thinking: know, believe, understand

Senses: see, hear, smell (ability)

Possession: have, own, belong

Examples: ✓ I know / ✗ I am knowing

DYNAMIC (CAN use continuous):

Actions: run, eat, write, study

Examples: ✓ I am eating / ✓ She is running

BOTH:

think: I think so (opinion) / I am thinking (process)

have: I have a car (possess) / I am having dinner (activity)

see: I see it (ability) / I am seeing him tomorrow (meet)

PHRASAL VERBS

Verb + particle = new meaning

Separable: turn off the light / turn the light off Inseparable: look after the baby (NOT: look the baby after) Common Examples:
give up = quit

look after = take care of
put off = postpone

break down = stop working

find out = discover

SUBJECT-VERB AGREEMENT

Rules:

Singular: The boy runs / She eats

Plural: The boys run / They eat

Collective nouns: The team is ready

"And": John and Mary are friends

"Or/nor": agree with nearest

Indefinite pronouns:

Singular: everyone, somebody, each (Everyone is here)

Plural: few, many, both (Few are ready)

COMMON ERRORS

✗ Yesterday I go → ✓ Yesterday I went

✗ He go → ✓ He goes

✗ I can goes → ✓ I can go

✗ I have went → ✓ I have gone

✗ I am knowing → ✓ I know
EVALUATION

Explain finite vs. non-finite verbs with examples

What are the three types of non-finite verbs? Give examples

Explain gerund vs. present participle with examples

What are stative verbs? List five

Define phrasal verbs. Give five examples

ASSIGNMENT

Identify and classify verbs as finite/non-finite: "I want to go home. Mary is studying now. Having finished her work, she decided to rest. She enjoys reading."
Write ten sentences using gerunds: 5 as subjects, 5 as objects

Write two example sentences for each causative verb: make, have, get, let, help

Create sentences using: give up, look after, turn off, run into, get over

Correct and explain:

I am knowing him

She can goes

They was playing

He have went home

Yesterday I am eating rice

WEEK 4 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: IDENTIFYING MAIN AND SUPPORTING IDEAS; VOCABULARY – YOUR SCHOOL SUBJECTS

PART A: IDENTIFYING MAIN AND SUPPORTING IDEAS WHAT ARE MAIN IDEAS?
Definition: The most important point or central message in a paragraph. What the paragraph is mostly about.
Also Called: Central idea, Topic sentence, Main point

Characteristics:

Most general statement

Covers the whole paragraph

Supported by other sentences

Answers: "What is this paragraph mainly about?"

WHERE TO FIND MAIN IDEAS

Beginning (Most Common - 60-70%): Topic sentence first, then supporting details.

End (20-25%): Supporting details build to main idea at end.

Middle (5-10%): Introduction, then main idea, then more details.

Implied (5-10%): Not directly stated; reader must infer from details.

HOW TO IDENTIFY MAIN IDEAS
Steps:

Read the entire paragraph

Ask: "What is the main point?"

Look for clues: repeated words, first or last sentence, general statement

Test: Does it cover the whole paragraph?

MAIN IDEA VS. TOPIC

Topic: General subject (1-2 words) Main Idea: Complete thought about topic (full sentence)

Example:

Topic: Nigeria's diversity

Main Idea: Nigeria's ethnic diversity is both a strength and a challenge

WHAT ARE SUPPORTING IDEAS?

Definition: Details, facts, examples, and explanations that develop or prove the main idea.

Purpose: Prove, explain, give examples, and clarify the main idea

TYPES OF SUPPORTING IDEAS

FACTS AND STATISTICS: Specific, verifiable information

EXAMPLES: Specific instances

REASONS: Explanations for why

DESCRIPTIONS: Detailed characteristics

EXPLANATIONS: Clarifications of how or why
MAJOR VS. MINOR SUPPORTING IDEAS

Major: Directly support main idea; essential to understanding

Minor: Support major ideas; provide additional detail; can be removed

Example:

Main Idea: Exercise is essential for good health

Major: It strengthens the heart

Minor: Hearts pump blood more efficiently

PRACTICE PASSAGE

"Studying in a quiet environment improves concentration. When there are no distractions, students focus better. Research shows students in quiet areas remember 40% more information. Libraries provide such quiet spaces. Therefore, students should seek quiet places when studying."
Analysis:

Topic: Studying in quiet environments

Main Idea: First sentence

Supporting Ideas: Reason (focus better), Fact (40% more), Example (libraries), Conclusion

TIPS FOR SUCCESS

DO: Read entire paragraph, look for repeated words, check first/last sentences

DON'T: Choose a detail as main idea, pick something too general

PART B: VOCABULARY – YOUR SCHOOL SUBJECTS
CORE SUBJECTS

MATHEMATICS: Numbers, quantities, shapes

Words: add, subtract, equation, fraction, calculate

ENGLISH LANGUAGE: English communication

Words: noun, verb, essay, vocabulary, spelling

SCIENCE:

Biology: Living things - cell, photosynthesis, organism

Chemistry: Substances - atom, molecule, acid, laboratory

Physics: Matter and energy - force, gravity, electricity

SOCIAL STUDIES:

History: Past events - civilization, war, artifact

Geography: Earth features - continent, mountain, climate, map

Civic Education: Citizenship - rights, democracy, election

RELIGIOUS STUDIES: Religions and morals

Words: scripture, prayer, faith, morality

GENERAL SCHOOL VOCABULARY

Classroom Items: desk, board, textbook, pen, ruler Actions: study, read, write, calculate, discuss, revise Assessment: test, examination, homework, grade, pass Time: period, lesson, term, timetable
People: teacher, student, principal, classmate
EVALUATION

Explain the difference between main idea and supporting ideas

Where are main ideas usually located? Give three positions

List four types of supporting details

Name ten school subjects and what is studied in each

Identify main idea and three supporting details in this passage: "Mathematics is useful in everyday life. We use it shopping, cooking, telling time, and managing money."

ASSIGNMENT

Write five paragraphs (5 sentences each) with clear main ideas. Underline main idea in each
Read any passage. Identify: (a) topic (b) main idea (c) three supporting details (d) types of support
Create vocabulary list for five favorite subjects: (a) 10 words per subject (b) use 5 words in sentences
Explain major vs. minor supporting details with examples

Match details to main ideas:

Main Ideas: (1) Exercise is beneficial (2) Reading improves knowledge (3) Water is essential
Details: (a) Books provide information (b) Bodies need hydration (c) Physical activity strengthens muscles (d) Literature expands vocabulary (e) Water regulates temperature
WEEK 4 – COMPOSITION

TOPIC: DIFFERENCES BETWEEN DESCRIPTIVE AND NARRATIVE ESSAYS

BASIC DEFINITIONS

Descriptive Essay: Writing that paints a picture with words, describing a person, place, object, or event using sensory language.
Narrative Essay: Writing that tells a story or relates events in chronological order, with characters, setting, and plot.

KEY DIFFERENCES

PRIMARY PURPOSE Descriptive:
Purpose: To describe; show what something is like

Focus: How something looks, sounds, feels, smells, tastes

Answer: "What is it like?"

Example: "My Classroom" - help reader visualize

Narrative:

Purpose: To tell a story; relate events

Focus: What happened, when, to whom

Answer: "What happened?"

Example: "My First Day in That Classroom" - tell what occurred

ORGANIZATION
Descriptive:

Spatial order: top to bottom, left to right, near to far

Order of importance: most to least noticeable

No time sequence needed

Narrative:

Chronological order: what happened first, next, then, finally

Clear beginning, middle, end

Time is essential

TENSE Descriptive:
Usually present tense

Examples: "The school stands majestically..." "My father is a tall man..."

Narrative:

Usually past tense

Examples: "I woke up early..." "She walked to school..."

CONTENT FOCUS Descriptive:
Static: Describes things as they are

Detailed descriptions: colors, sizes, textures, sounds, smells

Sensory language: appeals to five senses

Abundant adjectives and adverbs
Figurative language: metaphors, similes

Key Elements:

Physical characteristics

Sensory details

Spatial relationships

Atmosphere created

Example: "The ancient tree towers over the schoolyard, its thick trunk scarred with carved initials. Gnarled branches spread like protective arms, casting cool shade. The leaves rustle softly in the breeze, creating a gentle whisper."

Narrative:

Dynamic: Describes actions and changes

Event descriptions: what happened, who did what

Action verbs and dialogue

Plot progression

Key Elements:

Characters (who)

Actions (what they did)

Sequence (when, order)

Cause and effect

Conflict and resolution

Example: "I arrived at school early that Monday morning. As I walked toward the ancient tree, I noticed something strange. Someone had carved a huge message on the trunk overnight. My friends and I spent the entire break trying to figure out who had done it."

POINT OF VIEW Descriptive:
Third person most common: "The school has..." "It stands..."

Observer perspective

Narrative:

First person very common: "I went..." "We saw..."

Active participation in events

STRUCTURE Descriptive:
Introduction: General impression, what is being described

Body: Organized by space or importance; rich sensory details

Conclusion: Overall impression, significance, emotional response

Narrative:

Introduction: Setting scene, introducing characters, creating interest

Body: Events in time order, rising action, complications, climax

Conclusion: Resolution, what was learned, reflection

LANGUAGE FEATURES Descriptive:
Adjectives: beautiful, tall, bright, magnificent

Sensory words: shimmering, fragrant, melodious, rough
Similes/Metaphors: "as white as snow," "like a fortress"

Spatial transitions: to the left, above, below, nearby

Example: "The majestic building stands proudly at the corner, its white walls gleaming like pearls in the sunlight. Surrounding it are lush green gardens that spread like a soft carpet."

Narrative:

Action verbs: ran, jumped, shouted, discovered

Time adverbs: yesterday, then, suddenly, finally

Dialogue: Direct speech

Time transitions: first, then, next, after, meanwhile, finally

Example: "I ran quickly to the building. Suddenly, the door flew open and my friend appeared. 'Quick!' she shouted, 'Come see what we found!' I hurried inside, my heart pounding."

READER EXPERIENCE Descriptive:
Reader sees: creates mental picture

Like looking at a photograph (static)

Emotional response to beauty, atmosphere

Narrative:

Reader follows: accompanies the journey

Like watching a movie (dynamic)

Emotional response to action, conflict, resolution
COMPARISON TABLE

AspectDescriptiveNarrativePurposeDescribe/paint pictureTell storyOrganizationSpatial/importanceChronologicalTenseUsually presentUsually pastFocusWhat it's likeWhat happenedLanguageAdjectives, sensory wordsAction verbs, dialogueTransitionsSpatial (left, above)Time (then, next)Point of ViewOften 3rd personOften 1st personMovementStaticDynamic

RECOGNIZING THE TYPE FROM TOPIC

Descriptive Topics (Key Words: Describe, What...like):

Describe your school

My best friend

The market near my house

A beautiful place I visited

Narrative Topics (Key Words: Tell, Narrate, Day, Experience):

My first day at school

An unforgettable experience

The day I learned a lesson

How I spent my last holiday
CAN A TOPIC BE BOTH?

Yes! Example: "My Grandmother"

Descriptive Approach: Describe her appearance, personality, characteristics "My grandmother is a petite woman with silver hair always tied in a neat bun. Her warm brown eyes crinkle when she smiles. She wears traditional wrappers in bright colors and always smells faintly of palm oil..."
Narrative Approach: Tell a story about an experience with her "I will never forget the day my grandmother taught me to make jollof rice. It was a hot Saturday morning when she called me into the kitchen. 'Today, you learn our family secret,' she announced..."

MIXING BOTH IN ONE ESSAY

In Narrative Essays: Include description to set scenes and make story vivid

Example: "I entered the dimly lit room (narrative). Dust particles danced in the sunlight streaming through the cracked window. The air smelled musty (description). As I took another step, the floorboard creaked (narrative continues)."
In Descriptive Essays: Include brief narrative moments to illustrate characteristics

PRACTICE: IDENTIFYING THE TYPE

Passage 1: "The old library building stands at the end of University Road. Its red brick walls have weathered fifty years. Three wide steps lead to massive wooden doors. Inside, the smell of old books fills the air. Tall shelves line every wall."
Type: Descriptive Clues: Present tense, spatial organization, sensory details, adjectives, no events
Passage 2: "Last Saturday, I decided to visit the old library. I climbed the three steps and pushed open the heavy door. It creaked loudly. Embarrassed, I quickly found a table and sat down. I spent three hours reading. When I looked up, the library was closing."
Type: Narrative Clues: Past tense, chronological order, events in sequence, first-person experience

WRITING TIPS

For Descriptive Essays:

Use all five senses

Be specific

Use figurative language

Organize logically (spatial order)

Show, don't just tell

For Narrative Essays:

Have a clear story arc

Include dialogue

Build to a climax

Use specific details

Include reflection/lesson learned

COMMON MISTAKES

In Descriptive:

✗ Including too much action
✗ Using past tense for current descriptions

✗ Being too vague

In Narrative:

✗ Describing instead of telling what happened

✗ Mixing up chronological order

✗ No clear point or lesson

EVALUATION

State five main differences between descriptive and narrative essays

What tense is typically used in: (a) descriptive essays (b) narrative essays? Why?

How is organization different in descriptive vs. narrative essays?

Identify whether these require descriptive or narrative essays:

(a) My Last Birthday

(b) The Busiest Market in Town

(c) An Act of Kindness I Witnessed

(d) My Best Friend

(e) How I Learned to Swim

Write two short paragraphs (5 sentences each) on "My School": one descriptive, one narrative

ASSIGNMENT

Create a comparison table showing ten differences between descriptive and narrative essays with examples
Take the topic "My Mother" and write:

(a) A descriptive paragraph (100 words) on her appearance and character

(b) A narrative paragraph (100 words) telling a story about an experience with her

Identify which passage is descriptive and which is narrative, giving three reasons for each:

Passage A: "The sun rose slowly over the mountains, painting the sky pink and orange. The air was crisp and cool. Birds began their morning songs. Dew sparkled on the grass like tiny diamonds."
Passage B: "I woke up early to watch the sunrise. I climbed to the top of the hill and waited. When the sun finally appeared, I gasped at its beauty. I stayed there for an hour, just watching."
List ten transitions commonly used in descriptive essays and ten used in narrative essays

Explain why dialogue is common in narrative but not descriptive essays. Give examples
WEEK 4 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: ELEMENTS OF PROSE – PLOT, STYLE, CHARACTERIZATION; TYPES OF ORAL LITERATURE

PART A: ELEMENTS OF PROSE

PLOT

Definition: The sequence of events that make up a story.

PLOT STRUCTURE (Five Parts):

EXPOSITION:

Introduction to story

Characters and setting introduced

Background information

Example: "John was a hardworking farmer in a small village..."

RISING ACTION:

Conflict introduced

Problems develop

Tension builds

Example: "Strange things began happening. Crops failed. Villagers blamed John..."

CLIMAX:

Most intense moment

Highest point of tension

Turning point
Example: "John confronted the spirit king. The battle was fierce..."

FALLING ACTION:

Events after climax

Tension decreases

Conflicts begin resolving

Example: "The spirit king explained and lifted the curse..."

RESOLUTION:

Conclusion

All conflicts resolved

Final outcome

Example: "John returned as a hero. The village lived in harmony..."

TYPES OF CONFLICT:

Man vs. Man

Man vs. Self

Man vs. Society

Man vs. Nature

Man vs. Fate

CHARACTERIZATION

Definition: Methods an author uses to develop characters.

DIRECT CHARACTERIZATION: Author directly tells us about character
Example: "John was a brave man."

INDIRECT CHARACTERIZATION (STEAL Method):

S - Speech: What character says

Example: "Please, kind sir..." (polite person)

T - Thoughts: What character thinks

Example: John smiled but thought, "This man is a fool." (diplomatic but judgmental)

E - Effects on Others: How others react

Example: Everyone smiled when Mary entered. (shows she's loved)

A - Actions: What character does

Example: She gave her coat to the shivering child. (selfless, compassionate)

L - Looks: Physical appearance

Example: His clothes were always neat. (orderly, disciplined)

CHARACTER TYPES:

By Complexity:

Flat: Simple, one-dimensional, stereotypical

Round: Complex, multi-dimensional, realistic

By Development:

Static: Don't change throughout story

Dynamic: Change and grow

By Role:

Protagonist: Main character
Antagonist: Opposes protagonist

Supporting: Help tell the story

Minor: Brief appearances

STYLE

Definition: The distinctive way an author uses language.

ELEMENTS OF STYLE:

DICTION (Word Choice):

Formal: Sophisticated vocabulary, proper grammar

Informal: Everyday language, conversational, slang

Technical: Specialized vocabulary

Regional: Dialect, local expressions

SENTENCE STRUCTURE:

Simple: Short, direct, creates pace

Complex: Long, detailed, elaborate

Varied: Mix of both, creates rhythm

TONE: Author's attitude toward subject

Serious, humorous, ironic, sarcastic, formal, informal, optimistic, cynical, nostalgic

IMAGERY: Descriptive language appealing to senses

Visual (sight), Auditory (sound), Olfactory (smell), Gustatory (taste), Tactile (touch)

FIGURATIVE LANGUAGE:

Simile: Comparison using "like/as" ("Her voice was like honey")
Metaphor: Direct comparison ("Time is money")

Personification: Human qualities to non-human ("Wind whispered")

Hyperbole: Exaggeration ("I've told you a million times")

Symbolism: Object represents idea (dove = peace)

VOICE:

Unique sound/personality of writing

Author's recognizable style

PART B: TYPES OF ORAL LITERATURE

Definition: Literature composed, performed, and transmitted orally.

FOLKTALES: Traditional fictional stories teaching lessons

Types:

Animal Tales: Animals as characters teaching lessons

Trickster Tales: Clever character overcomes stronger opponents

"Why" Stories: Explain natural phenomena (Why Tortoise has cracked shell)

Moral Tales: Directly teach lessons

Characteristics: "Once upon a time" openings, stock characters, moral lessons

MYTHS: Sacred stories explaining origins, featuring gods and supernatural beings

Types:

Creation Myths: How world was created

Explanatory Myths: Why things are the way they are
Divine Myths: Stories of gods and goddesses

Example: Yoruba creation myth (Olodumare creating world)

LEGENDS: Traditional stories about heroes based partially on fact but embellished

Examples:

Sundiata (Mali)

Queen Amina of Zaria

Shaka Zulu

Queen Moremi of Ile-Ife

Characteristics: Historical basis, heroic deeds, cultural pride

PROVERBS: Short, wise sayings expressing truths

Examples:

"When two elephants fight, the grass suffers"

"A tree cannot make a forest"

"A river that forgets its source will dry up"

Functions: Teach wisdom, guide behavior, settle arguments

RIDDLES: Puzzles testing wit and intelligence

Examples:

"I have teeth but cannot eat." (A comb)

"What runs but never walks?" (A river)

"What gets wetter as it dries?" (A towel)

PRAISE POETRY: Poems praising people, gods, or places

Types:

Oriki (Yoruba): Praise names for individuals, families, deities

Praise for Rulers: Kings, chiefs, leaders

Praise for Warriors: Celebrates bravery

Characteristics: Repetition, rhythmic, often with music/drums

SONGS:

Types:

Work Songs: Sung during labor

Lullabies: Soothe babies

War Songs: Inspire courage

Festival Songs: Celebrate occasions

Love Songs: Express romance

ORAL EPICS: Long narrative poems about heroes

Examples:

Sundiata Epic (Mali)

Ozidi Saga (Nigeria/Ijaw)

Characteristics: Very long, heroic deeds, performed by griots

ORAL HISTORY: Historical accounts passed down orally
Content: Migrations, wars, founding of kingdoms, important rulers

TONGUE TWISTERS: Phrases difficult to say quickly

Examples:

"She sells seashells by the seashore"

"Red lorry, yellow lorry"

PERFORMANCE FEATURES:

Evening gatherings, festivals, ceremonies

Storyteller uses voice modulation, gestures, facial expressions

Audience participation: call and response, singing, clapping

Music and drumming

Formulaic language: "Once upon a time..."

FUNCTIONS OF ORAL LITERATURE:

Entertainment

Education

Moral instruction

Cultural preservation

Social control

Historical record

Community bonding

Language development

EVALUATION

Explain the five parts of plot structure with an example for each

Describe the STEAL method of indirect characterization with examples

What is the difference between flat and round characters? Between static and dynamic?

Explain five elements of an author's style

Compare folktales, myths, and legends: (a) Definition (b) Characteristics (c) Purpose (d) Examples

ASSIGNMENT

Choose any story and analyze its plot: Identify (a) Exposition (b) Rising Action (c) Climax (d) Falling Action (e) Resolution
Create a character using indirect characterization: Write a paragraph (100 words) showing personality through STEAL method WITHOUT directly stating traits
Read a passage and analyze style: (a) Diction type (b) Sentence structure (c) Tone (d) Imagery (e) Figurative language (f) Voice
Collect and write: (a) 5 proverbs with meanings (b) 3 riddles with answers (c) One folktale summary (150 words)
Explain direct vs. indirect characterization with three examples of each from any story

WEEK 6 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /e/

THE SHORT VOWEL /e/ (as in "bed"): Production:
Tongue mid-front position

Lips slightly spread

Jaw moderately open

Short, quick sound

Common Spellings:

"e": bed, red, pen, men, tell, best

"ea": head, bread, dead, ready, weather, heavy

"ai": said, again

"a": any, many

"ie": friend

"eo": leopard

"u": bury

Minimal Pairs (/e/ vs /æ/):

pen /e/ - pan /æ/

bed /e/ - bad /æ/

met /e/ - mat /æ/
set /e/ - sat /æ/

Practice Sentence: "Ten men said the red pen was better than any pen in the test."

WEEK 6 - GRAMMAR
TOPIC: ADJECTIVES – DEFINITION, TYPES; COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES

DEFINITION: An adjective is a word that describes or modifies a noun or pronoun.

TYPES OF ADJECTIVES:

Descriptive (Qualitative): Describe quality, size, shape, color, etc.

beautiful, large, round, red, happy, old

Quantitative: Show quantity (how much/many)

some, many, few, much, little, several, enough

Demonstrative: Point to specific things

this, that, these, those

Possessive: Show ownership

my, your, his, her, its, our, their

Interrogative: Used in questions

which, what, whose

Numeral:

Cardinal: one, two, three (how many)

Ordinal: first, second, third (order)

Proper Adjectives: From proper nouns

Nigerian food, African culture, Christian values

COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES:

Three Degrees:

POSITIVE (Absolute): Basic form, no comparison
tall, big, beautiful, good

COMPARATIVE: Comparing two things

Formation:

Add -er: tall → taller, big → bigger

Use "more": beautiful → more beautiful

Irregular: good → better, bad → worse

Pattern: adjective + -er + than OR more + adjective + than

Examples: She is taller than me. This is more beautiful than that.

SUPERLATIVE: Comparing three or more (highest degree)

Formation:

Add -est: tall → tallest, big → biggest

Use "most": beautiful → most beautiful

Irregular: good → best, bad → worst

Pattern: the + adjective + -est OR the most + adjective

Examples: She is the tallest girl. This is the most beautiful flower.

Comparison Table:

PositiveComparativeSuperlativetalltallertallestbigbiggerbiggesthappyhappierhappiestbeautifulmore beautifulmost beautifulgoodbetterbestbadworseworstmany/muchmoremostlittlelessleast

Rules:

One syllable: add -er/-est (small, smaller, smallest)

Two syllables ending in -y: change y to i, add -er/-est (happy, happier, happiest)

Two+ syllables: use more/most (careful, more careful, most careful)

Irregular forms must be memorized

EVALUATION:

Define adjective and give 5 examples

List 6 types of adjectives with 2 examples each

Write the comparative and superlative forms: (a) strong (b) careful (c) good (d) bad (e) little
Fill in: John is	(tall) than Peter but Mary is the	(tall) in the class

Identify adjectives and their types: "That beautiful Nigerian girl has three blue bags"

ASSIGNMENT:

List 10 adjectives of each type: descriptive, quantitative, demonstrative, possessive

Write 10 sentences using different types of adjectives

Compare these in three degrees: fast, intelligent, many, far, old
Correct errors: (a) She is more taller (b) He is the most good student (c) This is the bigger house

WEEK 6 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: DIFFERENTIATING MAIN IDEAS FROM SUPPORTING IDEAS IN A PASSAGE
MAIN IDEA:

The most important point in a paragraph

What the paragraph is mostly about

Usually the topic sentence

General statement

WHERE TO FIND:

Beginning (most common - 70%)

End (20%)

Middle (5%)

Implied (not stated - 5%)

SUPPORTING IDEAS:

Details that explain, prove, or develop the main idea

Examples, facts, reasons, descriptions

Specific statements

TYPES OF SUPPORT:

Facts/Statistics: specific data

Examples: instances illustrating main idea

Reasons: why main idea is true

Descriptions: detailed characteristics
Explanations: how or why something works

HOW TO IDENTIFY:

Main Idea:

Ask: "What is this paragraph mainly about?"

Look for general statement

Check first/last sentence

Find repeated concepts

Supporting Ideas:

Ask: "What details explain the main idea?"

Look for specific information

Find examples and evidence

PRACTICE PASSAGE:

"Regular exercise is essential for good health. It strengthens the heart and improves circulation. Exercise also helps maintain healthy weight and reduces disease risk. Studies show that people who exercise regularly live longer. Additionally, physical activity boosts mental health by reducing stress and anxiety."
Analysis:

Main Idea: Regular exercise is essential for good health (first sentence)

Supporting Ideas:

Strengthens heart, improves circulation

Maintains weight, reduces disease

Studies show longer life

Boosts mental health, reduces stress

EVALUATION:

Define main idea and supporting idea

Where are main ideas usually located in paragraphs?

List 5 types of supporting details

Read this passage and identify the main idea: "Water is vital for life. The human body is 60% water. We need water for digestion, circulation, and temperature regulation. Without water, a person cannot survive more than three days."
From the passage in Q4, list 3 supporting details

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a paragraph with a clear main idea and 3 supporting details on "Benefits of Reading"
Read any passage from your textbook. Identify: (a) Topic (b) Main idea (c) Three supporting details (d) Type of support used
Create a graphic organizer showing main idea and supporting details for this topic: "Why students should eat breakfast"
Differentiate between main idea and topic using examples

Analyze this passage - identify main and supporting ideas: "My grandmother is very kind. She helps sick neighbors daily. She volunteers at the orphanage every weekend. Last year, she paid school fees for poor children. Everyone loves and respects her."
WEEK 6 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: PARAGRAPH WRITING – LOGICAL SEQUENCE WITH INTRODUCTION & CONCLUSION
WHAT IS A PARAGRAPH? A group of sentences about one main idea.

STRUCTURE OF A PARAGRAPH:

TOPIC SENTENCE (Introduction):

States main idea

Usually first sentence

General statement about the topic

SUPPORTING SENTENCES (Body):

3-6 sentences

Develop the main idea

Give details, examples, explanations

All relate to topic sentence

CONCLUDING SENTENCE (Conclusion):

Last sentence

Wraps up the paragraph

Restates main idea or provides final thought

May link to next paragraph

LOGICAL SEQUENCE: Ideas must flow in order:

Time order: first, then, next, finally (for events)

Space order: left to right, top to bottom (for descriptions)
Order of importance: least to most important

TRANSITIONS: Connect ideas smoothly:

Addition: also, furthermore, moreover, additionally

Time: first, then, next, after, finally, meanwhile

Contrast: but, however, although, on the other hand

Result: therefore, thus, consequently, as a result

Example: for example, for instance, such as

PARAGRAPH UNITY: All sentences must relate to the topic sentence.

SAMPLE PARAGRAPH:

"My school library is a wonderful place to study. (TOPIC SENTENCE) It has comfortable chairs and quiet study areas where students can concentrate. (SUPPORT 1) The library contains over 5,000 books on various subjects, making research easy. (SUPPORT 2) Friendly librarians are always available to help students find materials. (SUPPORT 3) Therefore, the library is truly an ideal environment for learning. (CONCLUDING SENTENCE)"

EVALUATION:

Define a paragraph and state its three parts

What is a topic sentence and where is it usually found?

What is paragraph unity?

List 5 transitions used for: (a) time order (b) adding information

Write a paragraph (6 sentences) on "My Best Friend" with clear topic, supporting, and concluding sentences

ASSIGNMENT:
Write 3 paragraphs (6-7 sentences each) on different topics showing clear structure

Label the topic sentence, supporting sentences, and concluding sentence in each paragraph you write
Identify the parts in this paragraph: "Football is the most popular sport in Nigeria. Millions of people watch matches every weekend. Children play football in streets and fields across the country. Nigeria has produced world-class players who compete internationally. Clearly, football unites Nigerians from all backgrounds."
Write a paragraph using time order transitions on "How I Prepare for School"

Explain why logical sequence is important in paragraph writing
WEEK 6 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO DRAMA; TYPES & ELEMENTS OF DRAMA; CHARACTERIZATION & THEMES IN RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXT
PART A: INTRODUCTION TO DRAMA

DEFINITION: Drama is literature written to be performed by actors before an audience.

CHARACTERISTICS:

Written in dialogue form

Meant for performance

Uses stage directions

Has acts and scenes

Requires actors, stage, audience

DIFFERENCE FROM PROSE:

Drama: dialogue, performance, stage directions

Prose: narrative, paragraphs, description

TYPES OF DRAMA:

TRAGEDY:

Serious, sad ending

Hero suffers/dies

Explores serious themes

Example: Macbeth

COMEDY:
Humorous, happy ending

Makes audience laugh

Light themes

Example: School comedies

TRAGICOMEDY:

Mix of tragedy and comedy

Serious themes with humor

May have mixed ending

ELEMENTS OF DRAMA:

PLOT: Sequence of events in the play

CHARACTER: People in the play

Protagonist (hero)

Antagonist (opponent)

Supporting characters

DIALOGUE: Conversations between characters

Reveals character

Advances plot

THEME: Central message/idea

SETTING: Time and place

STAGE DIRECTIONS: Instructions for actors

[Enter John], [Exit], [Aside], [Angrily]

Written in italics/brackets
CONFLICT: Struggle/problem driving the action

ACT & SCENE:

Act: Major division

Scene: Smaller division within act

PART B: CHARACTERIZATION & THEMES IN PROSE TEXT CHARACTERIZATION (Review):
Methods:

Direct: Author tells us about character

Indirect (STEAL):

Speech: what character says

Thoughts: what character thinks

Effect: how others react

Actions: what character does

Looks: physical appearance

Character Types:

Protagonist vs. Antagonist

Major vs. Minor

Round (complex) vs. Flat (simple)

Dynamic (changes) vs. Static (no change)

THEMES:

Definition: Central idea or message in the text
Common Themes:

Love and sacrifice

Good vs. evil

Coming of age

Tradition vs. change

Courage and bravery

Family relationships

Social injustice

Power and corruption

Finding Themes:

What is the author trying to say about life?

What lessons does the story teach?

What universal truths are explored?

In Your Prose Text:

Identify 2-3 main themes

Support with evidence from text

Explain how themes are developed

EVALUATION:

Define drama and state 3 differences from prose

Name and explain 3 types of drama

List 8 elements of drama

What are stage directions and why are they important?

In your prose text: (a) Who is the protagonist? (b) Name one main theme

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a short drama scene (1 page) with 3 characters, including dialogue and stage directions
Explain 5 elements of drama with examples

Compare how character is revealed in drama vs. prose

Read Chapters 4-6 of your prose text. Write: (a) Character analysis of the protagonist (b) Two themes identified so far with examples
Define these drama terms: soliloquy, aside, monologue, act, scene

THE SHORT VOWEL /æ/ (as in "cat"):

Tongue low-front position

Mouth fairly wide open

Lips spread

Short sound

Common Spellings:

"a": cat, hat, man, bad, apple, family, happy

cat /æ/ - cut /ʌ/

man /æ/ - men /e/
