WEEK 8 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: ADVERBS – DEFINITION, IDENTIFICATION, TYPES

DEFINITION: An adverb is a word that modifies a verb, adjective, or another adverb. It tells how, when, where, or to what extent.
IDENTIFICATION:

Ask: How? When? Where? How often? To what extent?

Many end in -ly (quickly, slowly, carefully)

Not all -ly words are adverbs (lovely, friendly are adjectives)

TYPES OF ADVERBS:

ADVERBS OF MANNER (How?): Tell how an action is done

Formation: adjective + ly (quick → quickly, careful → carefully)

Examples: slowly, fast, well, hard, badly, beautifully, quietly, loudly

Position: usually after verb or object

"She sings beautifully." "He ran quickly."

ADVERBS OF TIME (When?): Tell when an action occurs

Examples: now, today, tomorrow, yesterday, soon, later, early, late, already, recently, eventually
Position: usually at beginning or end of sentence

"I will go tomorrow." "Yesterday, she came."

ADVERBS OF PLACE (Where?): Tell where an action occurs

Examples: here, there, everywhere, nowhere, inside, outside, above, below, nearby, far
Position: usually after verb or at end

"Come here." "They went outside."

ADVERBS OF FREQUENCY (How often?): Tell how often an action occurs

Examples: always (100%), usually (90%), often (70%), sometimes (50%), rarely (10%), never (0%), daily, weekly, monthly, yearly, once, twice
Position: usually before main verb, after 'be'

"I always wake up early." "She is never late."

ADVERBS OF DEGREE (To what extent?): Tell the intensity or degree

Examples: very, quite, too, almost, nearly, extremely, rather, fairly, completely, totally, hardly, barely
Position: usually before adjective or adverb

"She is very tall." "He ran too fast."

COMPARISON OF ADVERBS:

PositiveComparativeSuperlativefastfasterfastesthardharderhardestearlyearlierearliestquicklymore quicklymost quicklycarefullymore carefullymost carefullywellbetterbestbadlyworseworst

Rules:
One syllable: add -er/-est

Two+ syllables: use more/most

Irregular: memorize (well-better-best)

EVALUATION:

Define adverb and state what it modifies

Give 5 examples each of adverbs of: manner, time, place, frequency

Form adverbs from: quick, careful, happy, easy, good

Identify adverbs and their types: "She always sings beautifully here."

Write 5 sentences using different types of adverbs

ASSIGNMENT:

List 10 adverbs of each type: manner, time, place, frequency, degree

Write 10 sentences, each using a different type of adverb

Convert these adjectives to adverbs: slow, loud, quiet, bad, angry, peaceful, heavy, soft, bright, clear
Compare these adverbs: fast, carefully, well, badly, early

Identify and classify all adverbs: "Yesterday, she spoke very softly and walked slowly outside. She never talks loudly here."
WEEK 8 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: READING TO ANSWER SPECIFIC QUESTIONS (INTERPRETATION & PROJECTION LEVELS)
LEVELS OF COMPREHENSION QUESTIONS:

LITERAL LEVEL (Easy):

Answers found directly in text

Facts, details explicitly stated

Who? What? When? Where?

Example: "What is the boy's name?"

INTERPRETATION LEVEL (Medium):

Read between the lines

Infer meaning not directly stated

Understand implications

Why? How? What does this mean?

Example: "Why do you think the boy was sad?"

PROJECTION LEVEL (Difficult):

Go beyond the text

Predict, imagine, apply

Personal opinion with text support

What if? What might happen? What would you do?

Example: "What might happen next?"
INTERPRETATION QUESTIONS:

What They Ask:

Infer character feelings/motivations

Understand cause-effect relationships

Identify author's purpose/message

Explain why something happened

Draw conclusions from facts

How to Answer:

Use clues from text

Combine facts to draw conclusions

Think about "why" behind actions

Consider context

Signal Words:

Why, How, What does this suggest, What can you infer, This implies, The author means

PROJECTION QUESTIONS:

What They Ask:

Predict future events

Give personal opinions

Apply to own life

Suggest alternatives

Create new scenarios

How to Answer:
Base predictions on text evidence

Use logical reasoning

Connect to personal experience

Imagine possibilities

Signal Words:

What might, What if, What would you, Predict, In your opinion, Do you think, How would you

PRACTICE PASSAGE:

"Tunde had studied only one hour before the exam. His friends studied for weeks. As he sat looking at the questions, his hands shook. The questions looked unfamiliar. He glanced at his friend Bola, who was writing confidently."
Questions:

Literal: What is the boy's name? (Tunde)

Interpretation: Why were Tunde's hands shaking? (He was nervous because he didn't prepare well)
Projection: What do you think will happen to Tunde in the exam? (He will likely fail because he didn't study enough)

EVALUATION:

Name and explain the three levels of comprehension questions

What is the difference between interpretation and projection questions?

Read this and answer: "Mary found a wallet with money but no ID. She thought about keeping it but remembered her mother's advice about honesty."
Literal: What did Mary find?

Interpretation: Why did Mary think about keeping the wallet?

Projection: What do you think Mary will do?

Give 3 signal words for interpretation questions

Give 3 signal words for projection questions

ASSIGNMENT:

Read any passage from your textbook. Write 3 questions at each level: literal, interpretation, projection (9 total)
Explain how to answer interpretation questions vs. literal questions

Practice: "The village was silent. Shops were closed. People hurried past with covered faces. The town crier had made an announcement at dawn." Write: (a) 2 literal questions
2 interpretation questions (c) 2 projection questions - provide answers

Why are projection questions more difficult than literal questions?

Create a passage (100 words) and write 5 questions: 2 literal, 2 interpretation, 1 projection
WEEK 8 – COMPOSITION

TOPIC: DESCRIPTIVE ESSAY – A MARKET PLACE DESCRIPTIVE ESSAY REVIEW:
Purpose: Paint vivid picture with words

Uses: Sensory details (sight, sound, smell, taste, touch)

Organization: Spatial order (location-based)

Tense: Usually present

Language: Rich adjectives, imagery, figurative language

WRITING ABOUT A MARKET PLACE: WHAT TO DESCRIBE:
General Appearance:

Size (large, small, sprawling)

Location (in town center, by the road)

Structure (open-air, covered, modern, traditional)

Overall atmosphere

Sights (Visual):

Stalls and arrangement

Goods displayed (fruits, vegetables, clothes, etc.)

Colors (bright, colorful, vibrant)

People (traders, buyers, children)

Activities (selling, bargaining, carrying)
Sounds (Auditory):

Traders calling out: "Fresh tomatoes! Buy now!"

Bargaining conversations

Laughter, greetings

Vehicle horns

General noise and bustle

Smells (Olfactory):

Fresh fruits and vegetables

Cooked food (suya, akara)

Fish section (strong smell)

Spices (pepper, ginger)

Mix of various scents

Touch/Physical Sensations:

Crowded, jostling

Hot (sun beating down)

Humid

Bumping into people

Activities/Movement:

Traders arranging goods

Buyers examining items

Haggling/bargaining

Porters carrying loads

Children running around

ORGANIZATION:

Introduction:

General impression of market

Location and time

Why it's interesting/important

Body: Paragraph 1: General appearance and layout Paragraph 2: Sights - what you see
Paragraph 3: Sounds and smells Paragraph 4: Activities and atmosphere

Conclusion:

Overall impression

Significance of market

Personal feelings

USEFUL VOCABULARY:

Adjectives: bustling, crowded, vibrant, colorful, noisy, lively, chaotic, organized, sprawling, vast, busy
Verbs: bustle, jostle, bargain, haggle, call out, display, arrange, examine, weigh, pack

Nouns: stall, vendor, trader, buyer, goods, produce, merchandise, section, aisle, canopy

SAMPLE OUTLINE:

Title: The Bustling City Market

Introduction

Located in city center
Vibrant, lively atmosphere

Heart of community

Physical Description

Sprawling open-air market

Hundreds of stalls

Organized by sections

Sights

Colorful displays

Various goods

Busy people

Sounds and Smells

Traders calling

Mix of aromas

Noisy atmosphere

Conclusion

Exciting, essential place

Reflects community life

SAMPLE PARAGRAPH:

"The city market is a feast for the senses. Rows upon rows of stalls stretch as far as the eye can see, each overflowing with colorful goods. The vegetable section displays pyramids of bright red tomatoes, green peppers, and golden oranges. Traders in vibrant wrapper sit behind their wares, calling out to potential customers in melodious voices. The air is thick with the mingled scents of fresh fish, ripe mangoes, and sizzling akara from nearby food stalls. Shoppers weave through the
narrow aisles, baskets on heads, bargaining loudly over prices. The constant hum of activity - voices, laughter, and the occasional blaring horn - creates an energetic symphony that is unmistakably the market."

EVALUATION:

What is the purpose of a descriptive essay?

List 5 things you would describe about a market place

What sensory details are important in describing a market?

Write a topic sentence for a descriptive paragraph about a market

List 10 descriptive words suitable for describing a busy market

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a complete descriptive essay (250-300 words) titled "A Market Place" or "The Busy Market"
Create an outline for your essay showing: introduction, 3 body paragraph topics, conclusion
Write one paragraph (6-7 sentences) describing the sights in a market

Write one paragraph (6-7 sentences) describing the sounds and smells in a market

List 20 vocabulary words (adjectives, nouns, verbs) useful for describing a market
WEEK 8 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO DRAMA; TYPES & ELEMENTS; CHARACTERIZATION & THEMES IN RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXT
(Reinforcement from Week 6 with focus on prose text study) DRAMA REVIEW:
Elements:

Plot - sequence of events

Character - people in play

Dialogue - conversations

Theme - central message

Setting - time and place

Stage directions - instructions

Conflict - problem/struggle

Acts & Scenes - divisions

Dramatic Techniques:

Soliloquy: Character speaks thoughts aloud when alone

Aside: Character speaks to audience; others don't hear

Monologue: Long speech by one character to others

FOCUS: PROSE TEXT STUDY CHARACTERIZATION ANALYSIS:
For Your Prose Text, Analyze: Protagonist:
Physical description

Personality traits

Strengths and weaknesses

Goals/motivations

Relationships with others

How character changes

Methods of characterization used

Other Major Characters:

Role in story

Relationship to protagonist

Character traits

Contribution to plot/theme

Character Relationships:

Conflicts between characters

Alliances and friendships

Family dynamics

How relationships develop

THEME ANALYSIS:

In Your Prose Text: Identifying Themes:
What message is the author conveying?

What life lessons are taught?

What issues are explored?

What patterns do you see?

Common Themes to Look For:

Tradition vs. modernity

Coming of age/growing up

Family relationships

Social injustice

Courage and determination

Identity and belonging

Consequences of actions

Good vs. evil

Love and sacrifice

Cultural conflict

Supporting Themes with Evidence:

Quote specific passages

Cite events that illustrate theme

Show how characters embody themes

Explain how theme develops through plot
STUDY GUIDE FOR PROSE TEXT:

Character Analysis Template: Character Name:
Physical Description:

Age, appearance, clothing

Personality:

Three main traits with examples

Role:

Protagonist/antagonist/supporting

Importance to plot

Development:

How character changes

What causes change

Relationships:

With other characters

Conflicts and alliances

Significance:

What character represents

Contribution to themes

Theme Analysis Template: Theme:
Definition:
What the theme means

Evidence:

Events showing this theme

Character actions related to theme

Quotes supporting theme

Development:

How theme appears early

How it develops throughout

Resolution at end

Significance:

Why this theme matters

What author wants us to learn

WEEK 9 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: PREPOSITIONS – DEFINITION, IDENTIFICATION, TYPES & FUNCTIONS

DEFINITION: A preposition is a word that shows the relationship between a noun/pronoun and another word in the sentence.
COMMON PREPOSITIONS: at, in, on, by, with, from, to, for, of, about, under, over, between, among, through, during, before, after, since, until

TYPES OF PREPOSITIONS:

PREPOSITIONS OF TIME: Show when something happens

at: specific times (at 3 o'clock, at noon, at night)

in: months, years, centuries, long periods (in June, in 2024, in the morning)

on: days and dates (on Monday, on June 5th, on my birthday)

Others: before, after, during, since, for, until, by

Examples:

I wake up at 6 AM

She was born in 2010

The meeting is on Friday

We arrived before noon

He has lived here since 2020

PREPOSITIONS OF PLACE: Show where something is located

at: specific points (at the door, at the bus stop, at home)
in: enclosed spaces (in the room, in Lagos, in the box)

on: surfaces (on the table, on the wall, on the floor)

Others: under, over, above, below, beside, behind, in front of, between, among, near, by

Examples:

The book is on the table

She lives in Lagos

The cat is under the chair

Stand beside me

The bank is between the school and the hospital

PREPOSITIONS OF DIRECTION/MOVEMENT: Show movement from one place to another
to: toward a destination (go to school, walk to the market)

from: starting point (come from home, travel from Lagos)

into: entering (walk into the room)

out of: exiting (come out of the house)

through: passing through (walk through the gate)

across: from one side to another (across the road)

along: following a line (along the street)

Examples:

I go to school daily

She came from Abuja

He walked into the room
They ran across the field

PREPOSITIONS OF MANNER: Show how something is done

by: method/means (by car, by train, by hand)

with: using/accompanying (with a knife, with friends)

in: manner/way (in a hurry, in style)

Examples:

She travels by bus

Cut it with a knife

He spoke in anger

PREPOSITIONS OF AGENT: Show who does the action (used in passive voice)

by: the doer of an action

with: instrument used

Examples:

The book was written by Achebe

The tree was cut with an axe

FUNCTIONS OF PREPOSITIONS:

Show Time Relationships:

"I'll see you at 5 PM"

Show Location:

"The book is on the shelf"
Show Direction:

"She went to school"

Show Manner:

"He spoke with confidence"

Show Possession:

"The house of my father"

Connect Words:

Link nouns/pronouns to other words

Create meaning in sentences

PREPOSITIONAL PHRASES: Preposition + noun/pronoun = prepositional phrase

Examples:

at the door (preposition: at; object: the door)

in the morning (preposition: in; object: the morning)

with my friend (preposition: with; object: my friend)

COMMON MISTAKES:

Time:

✗ in Monday (✓ on Monday)

✗ at morning (✓ in the morning)

✗ on night (✓ at night)

Place:
✗ in the table (✓ on the table)

✗ at Lagos (✓ in Lagos)

EVALUATION:

Define preposition and give 10 examples

List 5 prepositions of: (a) time (b) place (c) direction

Fill in with correct prepositions: (a) I live	Lagos (b) She arrives	Monday (c) The
book is	the table (d) He goes	school	7 AM

What is a prepositional phrase? Give 5 examples

Correct the errors: (a) She lives at Nigeria (b) I wake up on the morning (c) The pen is under the table (correct if wrong)

ASSIGNMENT:

Write 20 sentences using different prepositions (at least one from each type)

List 15 prepositional phrases and use 10 in sentences

Fill in with appropriate prepositions: "I woke up	6 AM	Monday morning. I went

 	the bathroom and took my bath. After breakfast, I walked	school	my friends. We arrived	school	7:30 AM. Our classroom is	the second floor		the administrative block."
Identify all prepositions and state their types: "In the morning, she walked to school with her sister. They arrived at 8 o'clock and sat on the bench under the tree."

WEEK 9 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: APPLYING SQ3R TO A GIVEN PASSAGE SQ3R TECHNIQUE (Review):
S - SURVEY: Quickly preview the material

Read title, headings, subheadings

Look at pictures, diagrams

Read introduction and conclusion

Scan first sentences of paragraphs

Time: 2-5 minutes

Q - QUESTION: Turn headings into questions

Use: Who? What? When? Where? Why? How?

Create questions you want answered

Write questions down

R - READ: Read carefully to answer questions

Read actively, not passively

Look for answers to your questions

Take notes

Highlight key points

Time: Main reading time

R - RECITE: Recall what you read

Look away from text

Recite main points aloud or in writing
Answer your questions

Summarize in own words

Check accuracy by looking back

R - REVIEW: Go over material again

Review notes

Recite main points again

Answer questions

Make sure you understand

Time: 5-10 minutes

APPLYING SQ3R TO A PASSAGE:

SAMPLE PASSAGE:

Title: The Importance of Water

Water is essential for all living things. The human body is about 60% water, and we need to drink water daily to replace what we lose through breathing, sweating, and digestion.
Water has many important functions in the body. It helps transport nutrients to cells and removes waste products. Water also regulates body temperature through sweating. Without enough water, the body cannot function properly.
Experts recommend drinking at least 8 glasses of water per day. However, the exact amount needed depends on factors like age, activity level, and climate. People who exercise or live in hot climates need more water.
Dehydration occurs when the body loses more water than it takes in. Symptoms include thirst, dry mouth, tiredness, and headache. Severe dehydration can be dangerous and requires medical attention. Therefore, it is important to drink water regularly throughout the day.
STEP-BY-STEP APPLICATION:

SURVEY (2 minutes):

Title: "The Importance of Water"

4 paragraphs

Topics: water in body, functions, daily needs, dehydration

Seems to explain why water is important

QUESTION (1 minute):

Why is water essential?

How much water does the body contain?

What does water do in the body?

How much water should we drink?

What is dehydration?

READ (5 minutes): Read carefully, looking for answers:

Body is 60% water

Water transports nutrients, removes waste, regulates temperature

Need 8 glasses daily (varies by person)

Dehydration = too little water; causes thirst, tiredness, headache

RECITE (2 minutes): Close book and recall: "Water is essential because the body is 60% water. It transports nutrients, removes waste, and controls temperature. We need about 8 glasses daily. Without enough water, we get dehydrated, which causes thirst and tiredness."
Check for accuracy.

REVIEW (2 minutes):

Main point: Water is vital for health
Key facts: 60% body water, 8 glasses daily, multiple functions

Can I answer my questions? Yes

Do I understand? Yes

BENEFITS OF SQ3R:

Improves comprehension

Better retention (remembering)

Active reading (not passive)

Organized approach

Saves time in long run

EVALUATION:

What does SQ3R stand for?

Explain the 5 steps of SQ3R briefly

Why is the Survey step important?

What happens in the Recite step?

Apply SQ3R to a short passage from your textbook: write what you did in each step

ASSIGNMENT:

Apply SQ3R to a passage from your textbook (at least 300 words). Write a report showing what you did at each step
Explain the difference between the Read and Recite steps

Why is it important to turn headings into questions in the Question step?
Apply SQ3R to this passage and answer: [Provide or use a passage from their textbook] Write: (a) What you found during Survey (b) 3 questions you created (c) Main points from Reading (d) What you recited (e) Review summary
Create a poster or diagram showing the 5 steps of SQ3R with brief explanations
WEEK 9 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: LETTER WRITING – INTRODUCTION AND TYPES

WHAT IS A LETTER? A written message from one person to another.

PURPOSES OF LETTERS:

Communicate with distant people

Request information or permission

Apply for jobs/admission

Express feelings

Maintain relationships

Conduct business

TYPES OF LETTERS:

FORMAL LETTERS: Official letters to authorities, organizations, strangers

Types:

Application letters (job, admission)

Business letters

Complaint letters

Request letters

Official correspondence

Characteristics:

Formal language

Polite tone

Complete addresses
Proper format

No slang/contractions

Respectful

INFORMAL LETTERS (Personal/Friendly): Letters to family, friends, people you know well
Types:

Letters to friends

Letters to family members

Thank you letters

Congratulation letters

Invitation letters (informal)

Characteristics:

Casual, friendly language

Personal tone

Can use contractions

Emotional expression

Conversational style

DIFFERENCES:

AspectFormalInformalRecipientAuthorities, strangersFriends, familyLanguageFormal, politeCasual, friendlyToneProfessionalPersonal, warmAddressBoth addresses requiredUsually one addressPurposeOfficial businessPersonal mattersClosingYours faithfully/sincerelyYours lovingly, Best wishes

BASIC LETTER FORMAT:

Both types include:

Writer's address (top right)

Date

Salutation (greeting)

Body (message)

Closing

Signature

Name

Formal letters add:

Recipient's address (left, below date)

Subject line

FORMAL LETTER FORMAT:

23, Benson Street, Ikeja,
Lagos State.

18th October, 2024.

The Principal, Kings College, Ikeja,
Lagos State.

Dear Sir,

REQUEST FOR PERMISSION TO BE ABSENT

Body paragraphs...

Yours faithfully, [Signature]
John Adebayo

INFORMAL LETTER FORMAT:
23, Benson Street, Ikeja,
Lagos State.

18th October, 2024.

Dear Tunde,

Body paragraphs...

Yours lovingly, [Signature] John

SALUTATIONS:

Formal:

Dear Sir, (unknown man)

Dear Madam, (unknown woman)

Dear Sir/Madam, (unknown gender)

Dear Mr. Ojo, (known man)

Dear Mrs. Ojo, (known married woman)

Dear Ms. Ojo, (known woman, marital status unknown)

Informal:
Dear Tunde,

My dear Brother,

Dearest Mother,

Hello Mary,

CLOSINGS:

Formal:

Yours faithfully, (if Dear Sir/Madam)

Yours sincerely, (if Dear Mr./Mrs./Ms.)

Yours respectfully,

Informal:

Yours lovingly,

With love,

Best wishes,

Warm regards,

Your friend,

LETTER WRITING STEPS:

Plan: What to write, order of ideas

Write address and date

Write salutation

Write body: 3-4 paragraphs

Opening paragraph: State purpose
Middle paragraphs: Details

Closing paragraph: Conclusion, request action

Write closing and sign

Proofread: Check spelling, grammar, format

EVALUATION:

What are the two main types of letters? Explain the difference

Write appropriate salutations for: (a) Unknown principal (b) Your friend (c) Known teacher Mr. Ojo
What closing is used with "Dear Sir"? What closing is used with "Dear Brother"?

WEEK 9 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: FOLKTALES

Introduction, Definition, Types; Narration of a Folktale; Recommended Drama Text – Plot & Setting
PART A: FOLKTALES

DEFINITION: Traditional stories passed down orally, teaching lessons through entertainment. Features animals or people, with moral lessons.
CHARACTERISTICS:

Oral tradition (told, not written)

Passed through generations

Teach moral lessons

Entertain

Simple language

Formulaic openings/closings ("Once upon a time..." / "That is why...")

Stock characters (tortoise, hare, lion)

Magical elements

TYPES OF FOLKTALES:

ANIMAL TALES: Animals as main characters, acting like humans

Example: Tortoise and the Birds, Why Tortoise has Cracked Shell

Lesson: Consequences of greed, trickery

TRICKSTER TALES: Clever character uses wit to overcome stronger opponents
Characters: Tortoise (Ijapa), Anansi Spider, Hare

Lesson: Intelligence over strength; but trickery has consequences

"WHY" STORIES (Origin Tales): Explain natural phenomena or animal characteristics

Examples: Why Sky is Far, Why Bat Flies at Night, Why Mosquito Buzzes

Lesson: Various moral lessons

MORAL TALES: Directly teach values and behavior

Focus: Honesty, kindness, respect, hard work

Lesson: Clear right and wrong, consequences of actions

COMMON CHARACTERS:

Tortoise (Ijapa): Cunning, greedy, clever but often foolish

Hare/Rabbit: Quick, witty, escapes danger

Lion: Powerful, king of animals, sometimes proud

Spider (Anansi): Trickster, wise, mischievous

Elephant: Strong, wise, sometimes slow

Hyena: Foolish, greedy, laughable

FUNCTIONS OF FOLKTALES:

Entertainment: Amuse audience

Education: Teach about life, culture

Moral Instruction: Right vs. wrong

Cultural Preservation: Maintain traditions

Social Control: Enforce norms
Community Bonding: Bring people together

SAMPLE FOLKTALE NARRATION:

Title: Why Tortoise Has a Cracked Shell

Long ago, there was a great feast in the sky. All the birds were invited. Tortoise, who loved food, wanted to go but had no wings.
Cleverly, Tortoise convinced the birds to each lend him a feather. He promised to be their spokesperson at the feast. The birds agreed.
At the feast, Tortoise asked, "Who is all this food for?" The hosts replied, "For all of you." Tortoise then announced to the birds, "My name is 'All of You,' so this food is mine!" He ate everything while the birds watched hungrily.
Angry, the birds took back their feathers. Tortoise, now wingless in the sky, asked Parrot to tell his wife to bring soft things for his landing. But Parrot told the wife to bring hard things instead.
Tortoise fell from the sky onto the hard objects. His shell broke into many pieces. A medicine man put it back together, but the cracks remain.
Moral Lesson: Greed and trickery lead to punishment. Those who deceive others will eventually suffer consequences.

PART B: DRAMA TEXT - PLOT & SETTING PLOT IN DRAMA:
Definition: Sequence of events in the play

Plot Structure:

Exposition: Introduction, background, characters

Rising Action: Conflict develops, complications arise

Climax: Turning point, most intense moment
Falling Action: After climax, moving toward resolution

Resolution: Conclusion, conflicts resolved

In Your Drama Text:

What is the main conflict?

Who is involved in the conflict?

What complications arise?

What is the climax?

How is it resolved?

SETTING IN DRAMA:

Definition: Time and place where play occurs

Elements:

Time Period: When (historical era, season, time of day)

Place: Where (country, city, specific location)

Social Context: Cultural, economic, political environment

Atmosphere: Mood created

Stage Directions Show Setting:

[A village square in the morning]

[Inside the palace, evening]

[The forest, night time]

Importance:

Influences characters and actions

Creates mood
Provides context

Affects plot development

ANALYZING YOUR DRAMA TEXT:

Plot Questions:

What happens in each act/scene?

What is the central conflict?

Who are the main characters involved?

What complications develop?

What is the climax of the play?

How are conflicts resolved?

Setting Questions:

When does the play take place?

Where is it set?

How does setting affect characters?

What is the social/cultural context?

How is setting established (stage directions, dialogue)?

In your drama text: (a) Summarize the plot (b) Describe the setting

WEEK 10 - SPEECH WORK TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɒ/
THE SHORT VOWEL /ɒ/ (as in "hot"):

Production:

Tongue low-back position

Lips slightly rounded

Mouth fairly open

Short sound

Common Spellings:

"o": hot, pot, got, box, top, dog, shop, clock, doctor, coffee, option, problem

"a": want, wash, watch, what, squad, quality

Minimal Pairs:

pot /ɒ/ - port /ɔ:/

cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔ:/

not /ɒ/ - nought /ɔ:/

shot /ɒ/ - short /ɔ:/

Practice Sentence: "The doctor got a hot pot from the shop and stopped at the top of the rock."

Note: British vs. American pronunciation differs for some words (e.g., "hot" - British /ɒ/, American /ɑ:/)

WEEK 10 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: CONJUNCTIONS – DEFINITION, TYPES (SUBORDINATING & COORDINATING), FUNCTIONS
DEFINITION: A conjunction is a word that connects words, phrases, or clauses.

FUNCTIONS:

Join words, phrases, sentences

Show relationships between ideas

Create complex sentences

Make writing flow smoothly

TYPES OF CONJUNCTIONS:

COORDINATING CONJUNCTIONS:

Definition: Join words, phrases, or independent clauses of equal importance

The Seven: FANBOYS

F - For (reason)

A - And (addition)

N - Nor (negative addition)

B - But (contrast)

O - Or (choice)

Y - Yet (contrast)

S - So (result)

Uses:
Joining Words:

John and Mary

Red or blue

Smart but lazy

Joining Phrases:

In the morning and in the evening

Over the river and through the woods

Joining Independent Clauses:

I studied hard, and I passed.

She wanted to go, but she was sick.

He must leave now, or he will be late.

I was tired, so I slept.

Punctuation: Use comma before conjunction when joining independent clauses.

SUBORDINATING CONJUNCTIONS:

Definition: Join a dependent clause to an independent clause, showing relationship

Common Subordinating Conjunctions:

Time: when, while, before, after, since, until, as soon as, whenever

"I will call you when I arrive."

Reason/Cause: because, since, as

"She stayed home because she was sick."

Condition: if, unless, provided that, in case

"If it rains, we will cancel."
Contrast: although, though, even though, whereas, while

"Although he studied, he failed."

Purpose: so that, in order that

"I study hard so that I can pass."

Manner: as, as if, as though

"She sings as if she's on stage."

Place: where, wherever

"Go where you like."

STRUCTURE:

Pattern 1: Independent clause + subordinating conjunction + dependent clause

"I will wait until you come back."

Pattern 2: Subordinating conjunction + dependent clause, independent clause

"Until you come back, I will wait."

(Use comma after dependent clause when it comes first)

CORRELATIVE CONJUNCTIONS:

Definition: Work in pairs to connect equal elements

Common Pairs:

both...and: Both John and Mary came.

either...or: You can have either tea or coffee.

neither...nor: Neither John nor Mary came.

not only...but also: She is not only smart but also hardworking.
whether...or: I don't know whether she'll come or go.

DIFFERENCES:

Coordinating	Subordinating

Connect equal parts	Connect unequal parts

FANBOYS	Many types

Both clauses can stand alone	Creates dependent clause

Comma before (for clauses)	Comma after (if dependent comes first)

COMMON MISTAKES:

Comma Splices:

✗ I went home, she stayed. (Two independent clauses, no conjunction)

✓ I went home, and she stayed. (Add conjunction)

✓ I went home. She stayed. (Separate sentences)

Run-on Sentences:

✗ I went home she stayed. (No punctuation or conjunction)

✓ I went home, and she stayed.

Comma Misuse:

✗ When it rains we stay indoors. (Need comma after dependent clause)

✓ When it rains, we stay indoors.

EVALUATION:

Define conjunction and state its function
What does FANBOYS stand for? Give an example sentence for each

List 5 subordinating conjunctions showing: time, reason, condition

Join these with appropriate conjunctions: (a) I was tired. I went to bed. (b) She studied hard. She wanted to pass. (c) You can have tea. You can have coffee.
Identify conjunctions and their types: "I will wait until you come, but I cannot stay long because I have work."

ASSIGNMENT:

Write 10 sentences using different coordinating conjunctions (FANBOYS)

Write 10 sentences using different subordinating conjunctions (at least 2 from each category: time, reason, condition, contrast)
Join these sentences with appropriate conjunctions: (a) It was raining. We went out. (b) Study hard. You will fail. (c) She is smart. She is hardworking. (d) I woke up. The sun rose. (e) He didn't study. He was sick.

WEEK 10 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: ASKING QUESTIONS TO COVER ALL COMPREHENSION LEVELS

(who, where, when, why, how, what)

THE 6 QUESTION WORDS:

WHO (Person):

Asks about people/characters

Literal: Who is the main character?

Interpretation: Who do you think is responsible?

Projection: Who would you choose to help you?

WHAT (Thing/Action):

Asks about objects, events, actions

Literal: What happened in the story?

Interpretation: What does this event mean?

Projection: What might happen next?

WHEN (Time):

Asks about time

Literal: When did this happen?

Interpretation: When do you think he realized his mistake?

Projection: When should she have acted differently?

WHERE (Place):

Asks about location

Literal: Where does the story take place?
Interpretation: Where do you think they were going?

Projection: Where would you go in this situation?

WHY (Reason):

Asks about causes, reasons, purposes

Literal: Why did he go? (if stated)

Interpretation: Why do you think she acted that way? (infer)

Projection: Why would you make that choice?

HOW (Manner/Process):

Asks about method, manner, process

Literal: How did he do it? (if described)

Interpretation: How do you think she felt?

Projection: How would you solve this problem?

COVERING ALL COMPREHENSION LEVELS:

LITERAL LEVEL (Easy): Information directly stated in text

Who is the main character? (Answer in text)

What is his name? (Stated)

When did they arrive? (Stated)

Where does she live? (Stated)

INTERPRETATION LEVEL (Medium): Read between the lines, infer

Why do you think he was sad? (Not directly stated)

What does this suggest about her character? (Infer)

How did she feel? (Infer from clues)
Who do you think is responsible? (Analyze)

PROJECTION LEVEL (Difficult): Beyond the text, predict, apply

What might happen next? (Predict)

What would you do in this situation? (Apply)

How would you solve this problem? (Personal response)

Why would this be important in your life? (Relate)

CREATING BALANCED QUESTIONS:

For Any Passage, Create:

2-3 literal questions (facts)

2-3 interpretation questions (analysis)

1-2 projection questions (application)

Use All Question Words:

Include questions starting with who, what, when, where, why, how

PRACTICE PASSAGE:

"Tunde woke up early on Saturday morning. He had an important football match at 10 AM. He quickly ate breakfast and gathered his gear. As he left, his mother reminded him to play fairly. The match was tough, and Tunde's team was losing. In the final minutes, Tunde had a chance to cheat and win, but he remembered his mother's words. He played fairly, and though they lost, he felt proud."
Sample Questions:

Literal:

Who is the main character? (Tunde)
What day did this happen? (Saturday)

When was the match? (10 AM)

Where was Tunde going? (Football match)

Interpretation: 5. Why do you think Tunde felt proud? (He chose honesty over winning) 6. How did his mother's advice affect his decision? (It reminded him of values) 7. What does this tell us about Tunde's character? (He is honest, has integrity)
Projection: 8. What might happen if Tunde had cheated? (He might have won but felt guilty) 9. What would you do in Tunde's situation? (Personal response) 10. Why is playing fairly important in life? (Apply to real life)

EVALUATION:

List the 6 question words and what each asks about

Create 2 literal, 2 interpretation, and 1 projection question for this passage: "Mary found a wallet full of money. She looked around but saw no one. She thought about keeping it but decided to take it to the police station."
Explain the difference between asking "What happened?" (literal) and "What might happen?" (projection)
Why is it important to ask questions at all three comprehension levels?

Which question words are most common at: (a) literal level (b) interpretation level (c) projection level?

ASSIGNMENT:

Read a passage from your textbook. Create 15 questions: 5 literal, 5 interpretation, 5 projection (use all 6 question words)
For each comprehension level, write 2 questions starting with: Who, What, When, Where, Why, How (18 total questions)
Analyze this passage and create balanced questions: [Use a passage from their textbook]

Explain with examples: (a) What is a literal question? (b) What is an interpretation question? (c) What is a projection question?
Practice: Write 10 questions (mixed levels) for any short story you know
WEEK 10 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: FEATURES OF AN INFORMAL LETTER INFORMAL LETTER REVIEW:
Definition: Personal letter to friends, family, or people you know well

Purpose:

Share news

Maintain relationships

Express feelings

Make plans

Thank, congratulate, invite

FEATURES OF INFORMAL LETTERS:

WRITER'S ADDRESS:

Top right corner

Your full address

No name

Example:

15, Ademola Street, Ikeja,
Lagos State.

DATE:
Below writer's address

Various formats acceptable

Day, Month, Year

Examples:

20th November, 2024

November 20, 2024

20/11/2024

SALUTATION (Greeting):

Friendly, personal

Comma after name

Left side

Examples:

Dear Tunde,

My dear Brother,

Dearest Mother,

Hello Mary,

Dear Friend,

BODY:

Opening Paragraph:

Greet recipient warmly

Ask about their welfare
State purpose of letter

Example: "How are you? I hope you and your family are doing well. I am writing to tell you about my recent experience..."
Middle Paragraphs:

Main message (2-3 paragraphs)

Details and descriptions

Personal sharing

Can be emotional

Closing Paragraph:

Final thoughts

Greetings to family

Hope to hear back

Future plans

Example: "I hope to hear from you soon. Give my regards to your parents. Take care of yourself."

COMPLIMENTARY CLOSE:

Warm, friendly

Comma after

Right side (below body)

Examples:

Yours lovingly,

With love,
Best wishes,

Warm regards,

Your friend,

Affectionately yours,

SIGNATURE:

Below closing

Your name (first name usually sufficient)

Can be just nickname

Example:

Yours lovingly, [Signature] Bola

LANGUAGE FEATURES:

Tone:

Friendly, warm, conversational

Emotional expression allowed

Personal feelings shared

Language:

Informal, casual

Can use contractions (I'm, don't, can't)

Simple, everyday words
Can use slang (appropriately)

Personal pronouns (I, you, we)

Style:

Conversational

Like talking to the person

Can include jokes, expressions

Personal anecdotes

SAMPLE INFORMAL LETTER:

23, Benson Street, Ikeja,
Lagos State.

20th November, 2024.

Dear Tunde,

How are you doing? I hope this letter meets you well. I'm writing to share some exciting news with you!

Guess what? I won the first prize in our school's debate competition last week! I still can't believe it. Remember how nervous I was when I told you about it? Well, I practiced every day just like you advised, and it really paid off. The topic was "Technology and Education," and I argued that technology has improved learning. My teachers were really impressed!
The prize was a beautiful trophy and a scholarship worth 50,000 naira. My parents are so proud, and I immediately thought of you because you always encouraged me to join the debate club.
Thank you for believing in me!

How are things at your new school? Have you made new friends? I really miss having you around. Please write back soon and tell me all about your experiences.

Give my warm regards to your family. I hope we can see each other during the holidays.

Your friend, [Signature] Bola

DIFFERENCES FROM FORMAL LETTERS:

FeatureInformalFormalRecipient's addressNot requiredRequiredSalutationDear + first nameDear Sir/MadamLanguageCasual, contractions OKFormal, no contractionsToneWarm, friendlyProfessionalClosingYours lovinglyYours faithfullyPurposePersonal mattersOfficial business

COMMON MISTAKES:
Including recipient's address (not needed in informal)

Using formal language (Dear Sir instead of Dear Tunde)

Being too stiff and formal

Forgetting to ask about recipient's welfare

Using "Yours faithfully" (that's formal!)

No comma after salutation

Too brief (should be warm and detailed)

EVALUATION:

Write an appropriate opening paragraph for an informal letter to your friend

WEEK 10 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO FIGURES OF SPEECH – SIMILE, METAPHOR, IRONY PART A: DRAMA TEXT STUDY
Continue studying your drama text focusing on: Characters:
Main characters and their roles

Character development

Relationships between characters

Conflicts

Themes:

Main themes in the play

How themes are developed

Evidence from text

Plot:

Summary of each act/scene

Main conflicts

Climax and resolution

Setting:

Time and place

How setting affects action

PART B: FIGURES OF SPEECH
DEFINITION: Figures of speech are expressions that use words in non-literal ways to create special effects or meanings.
Purpose:

Make language more interesting

Create vivid images

Express ideas creatively

Add beauty to language

Make writing memorable

SIMILE:

Definition: Direct comparison using "like" or "as"

Pattern:

X is like Y

X is as [adjective] as Y

Examples:

Her smile is like sunshine

He runs like the wind

She is as brave as a lion

The baby's skin is as soft as silk

Time passes like a river flowing

Purpose:

Show similarity

Create vivid image
Help reader understand by comparing to something familiar

In Sentences:

"The classroom was as quiet as a graveyard."

"Her voice was like music to my ears."

"He fought like a warrior."

METAPHOR:

Definition: Direct comparison WITHOUT "like" or "as"; saying one thing IS another

Pattern:

X is Y (not literally true)

Examples:

Time is money (time = money)

Her smile is sunshine (smile = sunshine)

Life is a journey

He is a lion in battle

The classroom is a zoo

Difference from Simile:

Simile: Her smile is like sunshine (comparison with "like")

Metaphor: Her smile is sunshine (direct statement)

Purpose:

Create stronger, more direct comparison

More powerful than simile

Makes writing vivid
In Sentences:

"The world is a stage." (Shakespeare)

"Knowledge is power."

"Laughter is the best medicine."

IRONY:

Definition: When what happens or is said is opposite of what is expected or meant

Types:

Verbal Irony: Saying opposite of what you mean (like sarcasm)

Saying "What lovely weather!" during a storm

Calling a giant man "Tiny"

"Oh great! I failed again" (meaning: This is not great)

Situational Irony: When outcome is opposite of what's expected

A fire station burns down

A traffic policeman gets a speeding ticket

A swimming champion drowns

You study wrong topics and those are the ones that appear in exam

Dramatic Irony: Audience knows something characters don't

In Romeo and Juliet, we know Juliet is alive but Romeo doesn't

Horror movie: we see the danger but character doesn't

Purpose:

Create humor

Show contrast
Make point strongly

Surprise audience

Examples:

"How nice of you to break my phone!" (verbal irony - not really nice)

A thief's house gets robbed (situational irony)

In a play, character says "Nothing bad will happen" but audience knows danger is coming (dramatic irony)

COMPARISON TABLE:

FigureDefinitionExampleUsesSimileComparison with like/asShe is like a roseLike, asMetaphorDirect comparison (is)She is a roseNo like/asIronyOpposite of expectedThe fire station burnedContrast, humor

OTHER FIGURES OF SPEECH:

Personification: Giving human qualities to non-human things

"The wind whispered"

"Death smiled"

Hyperbole: Exaggeration

"I'm so hungry I could eat a horse"

"I've told you a million times"

Alliteration: Repetition of beginning sounds

"Peter Piper picked..."

IDENTIFYING FIGURES OF SPEECH:

In Your Drama/Prose Text:

Look for comparisons

Find "like" or "as" = simile

Find "is/are" with unusual comparison = metaphor

Look for opposite situations = irony

Note the effect created

EVALUATION:

Define figures of speech and state their purpose

What is a simile? Give 5 examples

What is a metaphor? Give 5 examples

Explain the difference between simile and metaphor with examples

What is irony? Give 3 examples of different types

ASSIGNMENT:

Find 5 similes and 5 metaphors in your recommended texts (drama or prose). Write them down with page numbers
Create 10 similes and 10 metaphors of your own

Identify the figure of speech in each: (a) Life is a roller coaster (b) She sings like an angel
The stars danced in the sky (d) A fire station burned down (e) He is as strong as an ox

Write 5 sentences using similes and 5 using metaphors
Write a short paragraph (100 words) describing your school using at least 3 similes and 2 metaphors
WEEK 11 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /Ɔ:/

THE LONG VOWEL /ɔ:/ (as in "door"):

Production:

Tongue mid-back position

Lips rounded and protruded

Long, prolonged sound

Common Spellings:

"or": door, floor, form, born, horn, torn, worn, sport, short, morning

"au": August, author, daughter, cause, because, pause, fault

"aw": law, draw, saw, raw, dawn, yawn, awful

"ough": bought, brought, thought, fought, ought, taught, caught

"al": talk, walk, chalk, always, also, ball, call, tall, fall, wall

"our": four, your, pour, court

"oa": broad, board

Minimal Pairs:

pot /ɒ/ - port /ɔ:/

cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔ:/

not /ɒ/ - nought /ɔ:/

cod /ɒ/ - cord /ɔ:/

Practice Words: door, floor, more, store, before, four, your, pour, born, torn, form, storm, short, sport, morning, call, tall, ball, walk, talk, law, saw, draw, bought, brought, thought
Practice Sentence: "Your daughter bought four balls and thought about sport before dawn this morning."

EVALUATION:

Produce /ɔ:/ sound correctly with 10 words

Differentiate /ɔ:/ from /ɒ/ with 5 minimal pairs

Identify /ɔ:/ words: (a) door (b) dog (c) caught (d) cot (e) sport

Write 5 sentences using /ɔ:/ words

List 5 different spelling patterns that produce /ɔ:/ sound with examples

ASSIGNMENT:

List 30 words with /ɔ:/ sound (covering different spellings: or, au, aw, ough, al)

Practice: "Your daughter bought four balls before dawn and thought about sport in the morning"
Create 5 minimal pairs: /ɔ:/ vs /ɒ/ and /ɔ:/ vs /əʊ/

Write 10 sentences using words with /ɔ:/ sound

Group these words by sound /ɔ:/ or /ɒ/: door, dog, bought, hot, sport, spot, call, doll, taught, top
WEEK 11 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: ADVERBIALS – FEATURES, FUNCTIONS, TYPES

DEFINITION: Adverbials are words, phrases, or clauses that function like adverbs, modifying verbs, adjectives, or entire sentences.
ADVERB vs. ADVERBIAL:

Adverb: Single word (quickly, often, very)

Adverbial: Can be word, phrase, or clause (very quickly, in the morning, when I arrived)

FEATURES OF ADVERBIALS:

Modify verbs, adjectives, other adverbs, or sentences

Answer questions: How? When? Where? Why? How often? To what extent?

Can be moved in sentence (flexible position)

Optional (not essential for grammatical correctness)

Various forms: single word, phrase, clause

TYPES OF ADVERBIALS:

ADVERBIALS OF MANNER (How?): Tell how an action is done

Forms:

Single adverb: quickly, carefully, well

Prepositional phrase: with care, in a hurry, in style

Clause: as if he were angry

Examples:

She spoke softly
He left in a hurry

She acted as if nothing happened

ADVERBIALS OF TIME (When?): Tell when an action occurs

Forms:

Single adverb: yesterday, soon, now, today

Prepositional phrase: in the morning, on Monday, at noon

Clause: when I arrived, before she left

Examples:

I will go tomorrow

We met in the evening

Call me when you arrive

ADVERBIALS OF PLACE (Where?): Tell where an action occurs

Forms:

Single adverb: here, there, everywhere

Prepositional phrase: in the room, at school, on the table

Clause: where you left it

Examples:

Come here

She lives in Lagos

Put it where you found it
ADVERBIALS OF FREQUENCY (How often?): Tell how often

Forms:

Single adverb: always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never

Prepositional phrase: every day, once a week

Clause: whenever I can

Examples:

I always wake up early

We visit every Sunday

Come whenever you want

ADVERBIALS OF DEGREE (To what extent?): Tell intensity or extent

Forms:

Single adverb: very, quite, too, extremely, completely

Prepositional phrase: to a great extent

Clause: as much as possible

Examples:

She is very tall

He succeeded beyond expectations

Try as hard as you can

ADVERBIALS OF REASON (Why?): Tell why something happens

Forms:

Prepositional phrase: because of the rain, due to illness
Clause: because he was sick, since she was tired

Examples:

She stayed home because of the rain

Because he was sick, he missed school

POSITION OF ADVERBIALS:

Beginning (Initial Position):

Yesterday, I went to school

Fortunately, nobody was hurt

In the morning, we exercise

Middle (Medial Position):

I often go there

She always arrives early

He is never late

End (Final Position):

She spoke softly

We will meet tomorrow

They live in Lagos

Note: Some adverbials can move, others have fixed positions

Adverbs of frequency usually go before main verb

Adverbs of manner usually go after verb/object

FUNCTIONS OF ADVERBIALS:
Modify verbs:

She runs quickly

Modify adjectives:

He is very tall

Modify other adverbs:

She spoke too quickly

Modify entire sentences:

Unfortunately, we lost

Show relationships:

Between actions and time/place/manner

Add information:

About how, when, where, why, how often

COMMON ADVERBIAL PHRASES:

Time:

in the morning, at night, on Monday, last week, next year

Place:

at home, in the garden, on the table, under the tree

Manner:

with care, in a hurry, like a professional

Frequency:

every day, once a week, from time to time
IDENTIFYING ADVERBIALS:

Ask questions:

How? → manner

When? → time

Where? → place

Why? → reason

How often? → frequency

To what extent? → degree

Example Sentence: "Yesterday, I carefully walked to school because of the rain."

Yesterday = time (When?)

carefully = manner (How?)

to school = place (Where?)

because of the rain = reason (Why?)

EVALUATION:

Define adverbial and explain how it differs from an adverb

State 5 types of adverbials with 2 examples each

Identify the adverbials and their types: "Yesterday, she quickly ran to school because she was late."
Give 3 examples each of adverbials in: initial, medial, and final positions

What questions do adverbials answer?

ASSIGNMENT:
Write 10 sentences using different types of adverbials (at least one of each type)

Create adverbial phrases (not single words) for: time, place, manner, reason, frequency

Identify all adverbials and classify them: "Last week, my friend very carefully walked into the room because of the wet floor. She always moves slowly in such situations."
Rewrite these sentences, adding appropriate adverbials: (a) She spoke (b) We will meet (c) He studies (d) They arrived (e) I was happy
Explain the three positions where adverbials can appear in sentences with examples
WEEK 11 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: ASKING SPECIFIC QUESTIONS TO ELICIT STUDENT OPINIONS OPINION QUESTIONS:
Definition: Questions that ask for personal views, judgments, or feelings about a topic. No single "correct" answer.
Purpose:

Encourage critical thinking

Personal engagement with text

Apply to own life

Evaluate and judge

Express preferences

CHARACTERISTICS:

Subjective (based on personal view)

No right/wrong answer (opinions vary)

Require reasoning (support your opinion)

Based on text (but go beyond it)

Personal response (what YOU think)

TYPES OF OPINION QUESTIONS:

AGREEMENT/DISAGREEMENT: Do you agree or disagree?

Examples:
Do you agree with the character's decision?

Do you think the author is right?

Should the character have acted differently?

How to Answer:

State your position (agree/disagree)

Give reasons

Use examples from text

Explain your thinking

EVALUATION/JUDGMENT: Judge the quality, rightness, goodness

Examples:

Was the character's action right or wrong?

Is this a good solution to the problem?

Did the character make the best choice?

How to Answer:

State your judgment

Explain why

Consider alternatives

Use evidence

PERSONAL PREFERENCE: What do you prefer, like, favor?

Examples:

Which character do you like best?
What is your favorite part?

Which solution do you prefer?

How to Answer:

State preference clearly

Explain reasons

Compare options

Give examples

APPLICATION TO SELF: Relate to your own life

Examples:

What would you do in this situation?

How would you feel?

Have you experienced something similar?

Would you make the same choice?

How to Answer:

Connect to personal experience

Explain your response

Compare to character's action

Be honest

ADVICE/RECOMMENDATION: What should be done?

Examples:

What advice would you give the character?
What should happen next?

How would you solve this problem?

How to Answer:

Give specific advice

Explain why

Consider consequences

Be practical

SIGNAL WORDS FOR OPINION QUESTIONS:

Do you think...?

In your opinion...

Do you agree...?

What would you...?

How do you feel...?

Should...?

Would you...?

What is your view...?

HOW TO ANSWER OPINION QUESTIONS:

Structure:

State Your Opinion Clearly:

"I think..."

"In my opinion..."
"I believe..."

"I agree/disagree because..."

Give Reasons:

"Firstly,..."

"The reason is..."

"This is because..."

Provide Evidence/Examples:

From text

From personal experience

From general knowledge

Conclude:

Restate opinion

Sum up reasoning

SAMPLE PASSAGE:

"John found 10,000 naira on the school playground. No one was around. He thought about keeping it to buy the sneakers he wanted. But he remembered his father's teaching about honesty. He took the money to the principal's office. A week later, a poor woman came to claim it - it was all she had for her sick child's treatment."
Opinion Questions:

Agreement: "Do you agree with John's decision to return the money?"

Sample Answer: "Yes, I agree with John's decision. Honesty is more important than material things like sneakers. Also, the money was very important to someone else - a sick child needed it. John did the right thing by following his values."
Evaluation: "Was John's action right or wrong?"

Sample Answer: "John's action was absolutely right. He could have kept the money, but he chose to be honest. Because of his honesty, a sick child got the treatment needed. This shows that doing the right thing can save lives."
Personal Application: "What would you do in John's situation?"

Sample Answer: "I would do the same thing as John. Even though I might want something for myself, someone else's need is more important, especially if it's for medical treatment. I would feel guilty keeping money that isn't mine."
Advice: "What advice would you give to someone who finds money?"

Sample Answer: "I would advise them to try to find the owner or report it to authorities. Money lost could be someone's savings or emergency fund. By being honest, you help others and build good character."

TIPS FOR ANSWERING: DO:
State opinion clearly

Give reasons

Use examples

Be honest

Support with evidence

Consider different views

DON'T:

Just say "yes" or "no"

Leave it unsupported
Copy from text without thinking

Be vague

Contradict yourself

PRACTICE:

Passage: "A student saw his friend cheating during an exam. He knew his friend needed to pass to avoid repeating the class. He also knew cheating was wrong."
Opinion Questions:

Should the student report his friend?

What would you do in this situation?

Is it ever okay to cheat? Why or why not?

What advice would you give to the friend who is cheating?

How do you think the student felt when he saw his friend cheating?

EVALUATION:

What are opinion questions? How are they different from literal questions?

List 5 signal words/phrases that indicate opinion questions

Read this passage: "A girl had to choose between helping her sick grandmother or going to an important interview." Write 3 opinion questions about this passage
What should you include when answering an opinion question?

Answer this opinion question: "Do you think students should have homework every day? Why or why not?"
ASSIGNMENT:

Read any passage from your textbook. Write 5 different types of opinion questions: agreement, evaluation, preference, application, advice
For each opinion question you created in Q1, write a full answer (3-4

sentences) showing how to properly support your opinion 3. Explain the difference between: (a) A literal question and an opinion question (b) An interpretation question and an opinion question
4. Practice: Answer these opinion questions with full support: (a) Should students be allowed to use phones in school? (b) Is it better to have a few close friends or many acquaintances? (c) What is the most important subject in school? 5. Create 5 opinion questions about your prose or drama text and answer one of them fully
WEEK 11 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: INFORMAL LETTER – FEATURES (CONTINUED PRACTICE) REVIEW: STRUCTURE OF INFORMAL LETTER
Writer's Address (top right) Date

Salutation,

Opening paragraph (greet, ask about welfare, state purpose)

Middle paragraphs (2-3) - main message, details

Closing paragraph (final thoughts, greetings, hope to hear back)

Complimentary Close, Signature
Name

COMMON INFORMAL LETTER PURPOSES:

SHARING NEWS: Telling about recent events in your life

Topics:

School experiences
Family events

Achievements

New activities

Changes in life

Example Opening: "I have exciting news to share with you! Last week, I won first prize in the science competition..."
INVITING: Asking someone to visit or attend an event

Include:

What event

When (date, time)

Where (location)

Why (occasion)

Request for response

Example: "I am writing to invite you to my birthday celebration on December 15th at 4 PM..."

THANKING: Expressing gratitude for gift, help, or hospitality

Include:

What you're thankful for

Why it was meaningful

How you felt/feel

Impact on you

Example: "Thank you so much for the wonderful birthday gift. The book you gave me is exactly what I wanted..."

APOLOGIZING: Saying sorry for something

Include:

What you're sorry for

Why it happened

How you feel

Promise to do better

Example: "I am writing to apologize for missing your birthday party last Saturday. I was really looking forward to it, but I fell sick..."

CONGRATULATING: Celebrating someone's success or good news

Include:

What you're congratulating them for

Your happiness for them

Encouraging words

Wishes for continued success

Example: "Congratulations on your excellent exam results! I was so happy when I heard the news..."

GIVING ADVICE: Helping friend with a problem

Include:

Understanding of their situation

Thoughtful advice
Support and encouragement

Offer to help further

Example: "I understand you're having difficulty choosing subjects. Let me share my thoughts..."

LANGUAGE TIPS:

Opening Paragraphs - Warm Greetings:

"How are you? I hope this letter finds you in good health."

"I hope you and your family are doing well."

"It's been so long since we last talked! How have you been?"

"I was so happy to receive your letter yesterday."

Middle Paragraphs - Main Message:

Be conversational

Use personal pronouns (I, you, we)

Share details

Express emotions

Ask questions

Use contractions (I'm, don't, can't)

Closing Paragraphs:

"I hope to hear from you soon."

"Please write back and tell me your news."

"Give my regards to your family."

"I can't wait to see you during the holidays."

"Take care of yourself and study hard."

PRACTICE LETTER - SHARING NEWS:

45, Church Street, Surulere,
Lagos State.

15th November, 2024.

Dear Amina,

How are you doing? I hope you, your parents, and your little brother are all in good health. I'm writing to share some exciting news with you!

You'll never guess what happened! I was chosen to represent our school at the inter-school debate competition next month. Remember how nervous I used to be about speaking in public? Well, I've been practicing, and our English teacher said I've improved a lot. The topic is "The Role of Youth in Nation Building," and I'm really excited about it.

Also, my elder brother got admission to study Medicine at the University of Lagos! We're all so proud of him. He's been working towards this for years, and it finally paid off. The whole family celebrated last weekend.

How are things at your school? Have you started preparing for your exams? I know you mentioned you were worried about Mathematics. If you need any help, I'd be happy to share the notes our teacher gave us.
Please give my warm regards to your parents. I miss our conversations and hope we can see each other soon. Maybe you can visit during the mid-term break?

Your best friend, [Signature] Zainab
Analysis:

✓ Proper format and structure

✓ Warm, friendly tone

✓ Personal details and emotions

✓ Questions showing interest

✓ Appropriate closing

✓ Natural, conversational language

COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID:

Too formal:

✗ "I am writing this epistle to inform you..."

✓ "I'm writing to tell you..."

Too brief:

✗ One paragraph only

✓ At least 3 paragraphs

Forgetting to ask about recipient:
✗ Only talking about yourself

✓ Ask about their welfare and life

Wrong closing:

✗ "Yours faithfully" (that's formal!)

✓ "Yours lovingly," "Your friend,"

Including recipient's address:

Not needed in informal letters

Being too stiff:

Should sound like talking to friend

TRANSITION WORDS FOR INFORMAL LETTERS:

To add information:

Also, Besides, By the way, Additionally

To show time:

Recently, Last week, Yesterday, Soon, Later

To show contrast:

However, But, Although, On the other hand

To give examples:

For example, For instance, Like

To conclude:

Finally, In conclusion, Anyway, So
EVALUATION:

List the 6 parts of an informal letter in correct order

Write an appropriate opening paragraph for an informal letter to your cousin

What are 5 common purposes of informal letters?

Write 3 appropriate closings for informal letters

What makes language in informal letters different from formal letters?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a complete informal letter (250 words) to your friend: (a) Sharing news about a recent school event OR (b) Inviting them to visit during holidays OR (c) Thanking them for a gift
Write the opening, middle, and closing paragraphs for: (a) A letter congratulating a friend on their success (b) A letter apologizing for missing their birthday (c) A letter giving advice about choosing subjects
Compare these two letters (one formal, one informal) and list 8 differences you observe [provide sample letters]
Correct these informal letter mistakes: (a) Using "Dear Sir" to address a friend (b) Writing recipient's address (c) Using "Yours faithfully" as closing (d) Too formal language throughout
Write 5 warm opening sentences and 5 friendly closing sentences suitable for informal letters
WEEK 11 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT; FIGURES OF SPEECH – SIMILE, METAPHOR, IRONY (CONTINUED)
PART A: DRAMA TEXT STUDY (Continued)

By Week 11, students should have covered most of the drama text. Focus Areas:
COMPLETE PLOT SUMMARY:

Act I: [Summary of events]

Act II: [Summary of events]

Act III: [Summary of events]

Overall story arc

CHARACTER DEVELOPMENT:

How major characters have changed

Character relationships evolved

Conflicts resolved or ongoing

THEMES:

Main themes fully developed

Evidence throughout play

Author's message clear

DRAMATIC TECHNIQUES:

Dialogue effectiveness

Use of stage directions
Dramatic irony

Soliloquy/Aside usage

Climax and resolution

SETTING SIGNIFICANCE:

How setting affects plot

Time period importance

Place influence on characters

STUDY QUESTIONS FOR DRAMA TEXT:

Plot:

Summarize the plot in 5 sentences

What is the main conflict?

When does the climax occur?

How is the conflict resolved?

What is the most important event?

Characters:

Who is the protagonist and antagonist?

How does the protagonist change?

What are the relationships between major characters?

Which character do you admire? Why?

Which character faces the biggest challenge?

Themes:

What are 3 main themes?
How is each theme developed?

What is the author's message?

Which theme is most important?

How do themes relate to real life?

Dramatic Elements:

Give an example of dramatic irony

Find an important soliloquy

How do stage directions help understanding?

What is the significance of the setting?

How does dialogue reveal character?

PART B: FIGURES OF SPEECH (Continued Practice) REVIEW:
SIMILE: Comparison using like/as 2. METAPHOR: Direct comparison without like/as 3. IRONY: Opposite of what's expected

ADVANCED PRACTICE: IDENTIFYING IN TEXTS:
From Drama/Prose: Look for:

Comparisons (simile/metaphor)

Unexpected outcomes (irony)

Descriptive language

Powerful imagery
Example from Text: "Her heart was like stone" - SIMILE "Her heart was stone" - METAPHOR Character says "I'll never leave" but leaves immediately - IRONY

CREATING FIGURES OF SPEECH:

Simile Creation:

Formula: [subject] is like [comparison]

Examples:

Her voice is like honey

The classroom is as cold as ice

He runs like the wind

She is as gentle as a lamb

Your Turn: Create similes for:

A busy market → "The market is like a beehive"

A difficult exam → "The exam was as hard as climbing a mountain"

A kind teacher → "Our teacher is like a second mother"

Metaphor Creation:

Formula: [subject] is [comparison]

Examples:

Time is money

She is sunshine

The world is a stage

Life is a journey
Your Turn: Create metaphors for:

A book → "This book is a treasure"

Knowledge → "Knowledge is power"

Hope → "Hope is a light in darkness"

Irony Creation:

Think of opposites:

What's unexpected?

What's contradictory?

What's surprising?

Examples:

A doctor who is always sick (situational)

"Oh wonderful!" when something bad happens (verbal)

In a play, audience knows danger but character doesn't (dramatic)

ANALYZING EFFECT:

Why Authors Use These: Simile/Metaphor:
Make description vivid

Help readers visualize

Create memorable images

Express complex ideas simply

Add beauty to language
Irony:

Create humor

Show contrast

Emphasize point

Surprise audience

Make readers think

FINDING FIGURES OF SPEECH:

In Your Texts:

Steps:

Read carefully

Look for comparisons

Check for "like" or "as" (simile)

Check for direct comparison (metaphor)

Note unexpected situations (irony)

Record page numbers

Explain the effect

Recording Format:

Quote: "Her smile was like sunshine"

Type: Simile

Page: 45

Effect: Shows she has a bright, warm smile that makes others happy
PRACTICE EXERCISES:

Exercise 1: Identify the Figure of Speech:

"Life is a roller coaster" - METAPHOR

"She sings like an angel" - SIMILE

A fire station burns down - IRONY (situational)

"The stars danced in the sky" - PERSONIFICATION (bonus!)

"He is as brave as a lion" - SIMILE

"What lovely weather!" (during storm) - IRONY (verbal)

"The world is your oyster" - METAPHOR

"Her eyes were diamonds" - METAPHOR

Exercise 2: Create Your Own:

Similes for:

Happy child → "The child was as happy as..."

Fast car → "The car moved like..."

Beautiful sunset → "The sunset was like..."

Metaphors for:

Difficult problem → "This problem is..."

Good friend → "A good friend is..."

Busy city → "The city is..."

Exercise 3: Find in Your Text:

From your drama or prose text, find:
3 similes (with page numbers)

3 metaphors (with page numbers)

2 examples of irony (describe the situation)

OTHER FIGURES OF SPEECH:

PERSONIFICATION: Giving human qualities to non-human things

"The wind whispered secrets"

"Death knocked on his door"

"The sun smiled down"

HYPERBOLE: Extreme exaggeration for effect

"I'm so hungry I could eat a horse"

"I've told you a million times"

"She's older than the hills"

ALLITERATION: Repetition of beginning consonant sounds

"Peter Piper picked a peck..."

"She sells seashells..."

"Big brown bear"

ONOMATOPOEIA: Words that sound like what they mean

Buzz, hiss, bang, crash, meow, moo

USING FIGURES OF SPEECH IN WRITING:

Tips:

Don't overuse (too many becomes confusing)
Make them original (avoid clichés like "white as snow")

Make them relevant (fit the context)

Make them clear (readers should understand)

Good: "Her eyes sparkled like stars in the night sky" Overused: "Her eyes were as bright as stars" (cliché)

EXAM PREPARATION:

Common Questions:

Identify the figure of speech in this line: "	"

Explain the meaning of this metaphor/simile

What effect does this figure of speech create?

Find two examples of similes in the passage

Create a metaphor for [subject]

How to Answer:

Identify correctly

Quote if asked

Explain the meaning

Discuss the effect

Give examples when asked

EVALUATION:

Identify the figure of speech: (a) "Life is a journey" (b) "He runs like a cheetah" (c) A water truck runs out of water (d) "The classroom is a zoo" (e) "She is as sweet as honey"
From your drama/prose text, find one example each of simile and metaphor (write quote and page number)
Create 3 similes and 3 metaphors about school life

Explain the difference between simile and metaphor using examples

Your school

Friendship

JSS1 ENGLISH STUDIES LESSON NOTES SECOND TERM

WEEK 1 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: VOWEL SOUNDS /u/ AND /u:/

CONTENT:

The Short Vowel Sound /u/

This is a short, relaxed sound produced with lips slightly rounded

The tongue is raised towards the back of the mouth

Examples: book, look, cook, good, foot, put, pull, full, wood, stood, hook, took, could, should, would
The Long Vowel Sound /u:/

This is a longer sound with lips more tightly rounded

The tongue is raised higher at the back

Examples: food, mood, moon, soon, spoon, school, cool, pool, tool, boot, root, shoot, troop, group, soup
Key Differences:

/u/ is SHORT and relaxed (as in "book")

/u:/ is LONG and tense (as in "boot")

Incorrect pronunciation can change meaning: "fool" /u:/ vs "full" /u/

Practice Pairs:

look /u/ - Luke /u:/
pull /u/ - pool /u:/

soot /u/ - suit /u:/

good /u/ - food /u:/

EVALUATION:

Pronounce these words correctly: book, boot, look, Luke, full, fool
