WEEK 1 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: ADVERBIALS – FREQUENCY AND MANNER

Adverbials of Frequency These tell us HOW OFTEN an action happens. Common Adverbials of Frequency:
Always (100%)

Usually/Normally/Generally (90%)

Often/Frequently (70%)

Sometimes/Occasionally (50%)

Seldom/Rarely (10%)

Never (0%)

Daily, weekly, monthly, yearly

Once, twice, three times (a day/week/month)

Position in Sentences:

Usually before the main verb: "She always comes early"

After the verb 'to be': "He is always late"

At the beginning or end for emphasis: "Sometimes I walk to school"

Examples:

I always do my homework

They usually play football on Saturdays

We rarely go to the cinema

She is never absent from school

He visits his grandmother twice a month
Adverbials of Manner These tell us HOW an action is performed.

Formation: Most are formed by adding -ly to adjectives

Quick → quickly

Careful → carefully

Beautiful → beautifully

Quiet → quietly

Irregular Forms:

Good → well

Fast → fast

Hard → hard

Late → late

Position in Sentences:

Usually after the verb or object: "She speaks English fluently"

Can come at the beginning for emphasis: "Carefully, he opened the door"

Examples:

The boy ran quickly

She sings beautifully

He answered the question correctly

They worked hard on the project

The baby sleeps peacefully

EVALUATION:

Define adverbials of frequency and give five examples
Arrange in order from most frequent to least frequent: rarely, always, sometimes, never, often
Form adverbs of manner from: slow, happy, bad, good, easy

Write three sentences using adverbials of frequency

Write three sentences using adverbials of manner

ASSIGNMENT:

List ten adverbials of frequency

List ten adverbials of manner

Write five sentences using both adverbials of frequency and manner in each sentence

Identify the adverbials in these sentences and state their types: (a) She always sings beautifully (b) They rarely speak loudly (c) He often works hard
Rewrite these sentences adding appropriate adverbials: (a) I go to church (b) She completed the task (c) They play football
WEEK 1 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: ASKING SPECIFIC QUESTIONS REQUIRING CONCLUSIONS

What are Conclusion Questions? These are questions that require you to:

Make inferences from the passage

Draw logical conclusions based on given information

Go beyond what is directly stated

Use reasoning and evidence from the text

Types of Conclusion Questions:

"What can we conclude/infer from the passage?"

"The passage suggests that..."

"Based on the information, we can say that..."

"Why do you think...?" (requiring reasoning)

"What is the main idea/lesson/message?" How to Answer Conclusion Questions:
Step 1: Read the passage carefully Step 2: Identify key facts and details Step 3: Look for patterns, causes, and effects Step 4: Think about what the facts suggest Step 5: Make logical connections Step 6: Support your conclusion with evidence from the text
Sample Passage: "Amina woke up at 5:30 AM every morning. She studied for two hours before breakfast. During break time at school, while others played, she revised her notes. Her teachers often praised her. At the end of term, she received the Best Student Award."
Direct Question: When did Amina wake up? Answer: 5:30 AM (stated directly)

Conclusion Questions:

What can we conclude about Amina's character?
Answer: She is hardworking, dedicated, and disciplined (we infer this from her actions)
Why do you think Amina received the Best Student Award?

Answer: Because she was committed to her studies and made sacrifices to excel (we conclude from the evidence)
Key Signal Words in Conclusion Questions:

Infer, conclude, suggest, imply

Probably, likely, presumably

Why, how, what caused

Main idea, purpose, lesson

Common Mistakes to Avoid:

Making wild guesses without evidence

Including personal opinions unrelated to the text

Copying direct statements instead of inferring

Ignoring important details in the passage

EVALUATION: Read the passage and answer the questions:

"The village marketplace was empty. The shops were closed, and no traders displayed their goods. Streets that usually buzzed with activity were silent. Only a few people hurried past, their faces covered. The town crier's bell had announced the news at dawn."
What can we conclude about what happened in the village?

Why do you think people's faces were covered?

What does "the town crier's bell had announced the news at dawn" suggest?

How would you describe the atmosphere in the village? Give reasons from the passage.
Explain the difference between a direct question and a conclusion question using examples from the passage.

ASSIGNMENT:

Read any short story or article of your choice (200-300 words)

Write three direct questions and their answers from the passage

Write three conclusion questions and their answers from the passage

Explain how you arrived at your conclusions for question 3

State three signal words that help identify conclusion questions
WEEK 1 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: FORMAL LETTER – FEATURES; WRITING TO PRINCIPAL FOR PERMISSION
What is a Formal Letter? A formal letter is an official letter written to people in authority, organizations, or institutions using polite, respectful, and standard language.

Features of a Formal Letter:

Writer's Address (top right corner)

Date (below writer's address)

Recipient's Address (left side, below date)

Salutation (Dear Sir/Madam, Dear Principal)

Title/Subject (underlined or bold)

Body (Introduction, Main content, Conclusion)

Closing (Yours faithfully/sincerely)

Signature and Full Name Format Layout:
23, Ademola Street, Ikeja,
Lagos State.

18th October, 2025.

The Principal,

Government Junior Secondary School,
Ikeja,

Lagos State.

Dear Sir,

REQUEST FOR PERMISSION TO BE ABSENT FROM SCHOOL

Introduction paragraph...

Body paragraphs...

Conclusion paragraph...

Yours faithfully, [Signature] Adeyemi Johnson
Language Features:

Formal/polite tone

No contractions (use "I am" not "I'm")

No slang or informal expressions

Clear and direct sentences

Respectful language

Proper punctuation

Sample Letter:

15, Olowu Street, Mushin,
Lagos State.

18th October, 2025.

The Principal,

Community Grammar School, Mushin,
Lagos State.

Dear Ma,

REQUEST FOR PERMISSION TO ATTEND MY SISTER'S WEDDING

I write to request permission to be absent from school on Friday, 25th October, 2025.

My elder sister's wedding ceremony has been scheduled for that date. As a member of the family, my presence is important and required. The ceremony will take place in our hometown in Ibadan, Oyo State.
I promise to collect notes from my classmates and make up for any work missed during my absence. I will also ensure that I catch up with all lessons taught on that day.

I hope you will grant my request. Thank you for your understanding.

Yours faithfully, [Signature] Blessing Okonkwo JSS 1B
Common Reasons for Writing to the Principal:

Permission to be absent

Request for transfer certificate

Complaint about facilities

Request for recommendation letter

Permission for excursion/trip

Explanation for lateness/absence

Important Points:

Be polite and respectful

State your reason clearly

Be brief and direct

Use proper format

Check spelling and grammar

Sign your letter

EVALUATION:

State five features of a formal letter

What is the difference between "Yours faithfully" and "Yours sincerely"?

Where is the writer's address positioned in a formal letter?

Write the correct salutation for a letter to: (a) The Principal (b) An unknown person (c) A known female person
List three reasons for writing a formal letter to your Principal

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a formal letter to your Principal requesting permission to leave school early for a medical appointment
Identify and label all eight features of a formal letter in your letter

Write out the recipient's address for a letter to: The Headmaster, Unity High School, Port Harcourt, Rivers State
Explain why formal language is important in official letters

Rewrite this informal sentence formally: "I wanna tell you that I can't come to school tomorrow cos I'm sick"
WEEK 1 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: FOLKTALES – AFRICAN TALES (FEATURES: DIDACTIC, ENTERTAINMENT, MAGICAL); RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXT

CONTENT:

What are Folktales? Folktales are traditional stories passed down orally from generation to generation. They are part of the cultural heritage of a people and teach important lessons about life, values, and society.
Origin of African Folktales:

Told by elders to children

Performed around evening fires

Passed from parents to children

Part of African oral tradition

Preserve history, culture, and values

Features of African Folktales:

DIDACTIC (Teaching Moral Lessons)

Every folktale teaches a lesson

Lessons about good behavior, honesty, kindness, hard work, respect

Shows consequences of bad behavior

Examples of lessons:

"Honesty is the best policy"

"Greed leads to destruction"
"Pride comes before a fall"

"Kindness is rewarded"

ENTERTAINMENT

Told to amuse and entertain

Use of humor and interesting characters

Exciting plots and adventures

Call and response patterns

Songs and chants included

Dramatic performances

MAGICAL ELEMENTS

Animals talk and behave like humans

Supernatural powers and events

Magical transformations

Use of charms and spells

Extraordinary happenings

Mythical creatures

Other Features:

Simple Language: Easy to understand

Repetition: Phrases and events repeated for emphasis

Stock Characters: Tortoise (cunning), Lion (brave), Spider (trickster)

Formulaic Opening: "Once upon a time..." "Long, long ago..."

Formulaic Ending: "That is why..." "And that is the end of the story"

Common Characters in African Folktales:

Tortoise (Ijapa): Cunning, clever, greedy, sometimes foolish

Spider (Anansi): Trickster, wise, clever

Hare: Quick, witty, small but smart

Lion: King, brave, powerful

Hyena: Foolish, greedy, laughable

Humans: Kings, farmers, hunters, beautiful maidens

Types of African Folktales:

Animal Tales: Stories where animals are main characters

Trickster Tales: Stories about clever characters who outwit others

Origin Tales: Stories that explain why things are the way they are

Moral Tales: Stories that directly teach lessons

Example Summary - "Why Tortoise Has a Cracked Shell": Long ago, Tortoise was invited to a feast in the sky. He borrowed feathers from birds to fly. At the feast, he tricked the birds and ate all the food. Angry, the birds took back their feathers. Tortoise fell from the sky and his shell cracked into pieces. That is why tortoise has a cracked shell today.
Lessons: Greed is punished; Trickery has consequences

Elements Present:

Didactic: Teaches against greed

Entertainment: Humorous and interesting

Magical: Animals talk, tortoise flies, feast in the sky
Functions of Folktales:

Educate children about morals and values

Preserve cultural traditions

Entertain and bond communities

Explain natural phenomena

Pass down history and wisdom

Develop imagination and creativity

EVALUATION:

Define folktales and explain their origin in African culture

Explain the three main features of African folktales with examples

List five common characters in African folktales and describe their traits

Why are folktales important in African society? Give three reasons

Using the story "Why Tortoise Has a Cracked Shell," identify the didactic, entertainment, and magical elements

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a summary of any African folktale you know (150-200 words)

Identify and explain the didactic lesson in your chosen folktale

Identify and explain the entertainment elements in your folktale

Identify and explain the magical features in your folktale

State the title and author of your recommended prose text for this term
WEEK 2 - SPEECH WORK TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /U:/
The Long Vowel Sound /u:/

This is a long vowel sound produced with tightly rounded lips

The tongue is raised high at the back of the mouth

The sound is tense and prolonged

It is represented by different spelling patterns

Spelling Patterns for /u:/ Sound:

"oo" spelling:

food, mood, moon, noon, soon, spoon, room, broom, bloom, gloom

boot, root, shoot, hoot

cool, pool, tool, school, fool, stool

"u" spelling:

rude, crude, prude

June, tune, dune

rule, mule

"ew" spelling:

flew, blew, drew, grew, knew, threw

chew, stew, few, new

"ue" spelling:

blue, true, glue, clue, due

continue, pursue, rescue

"ui" spelling:
fruit, juice, suit, cruise, bruise

"ou" spelling:

soup, group, troupe

through, you, youth

Common Words with /u:/ Sound:

two, who, move, prove, lose, whose, shoes, choose

flute, parachute, absolute

wound (injury), tomb, womb

Words to Practice:

The moon is blue in June

Choose your food at noon

The youth flew through the room

Fruit juice is good for you

Who knew the truth about the clue?

Exceptions (words that look like /u:/ but are not):

book, look, cook, good, foot = /u/ (short sound)

flood, blood = /ʌ/ (different sound)

Articulation Tips:

Round your lips tightly as if whistling

Keep the sound long and steady

Raise the back of your tongue

The sound should feel tense, not relaxed

EVALUATION:

Produce the /u:/ sound and give ten words containing this sound

Identify the spelling pattern of /u:/ in these words: (a) blue (b) food (c) grew (d) fruit (e) rule
Which of these words contain the /u:/ sound: book, boot, moon, good, juice, foot?

Write five sentences using words with the /u:/ sound

Explain the difference between /u/ and /u:/ sounds using examples

ASSIGNMENT:

List ten words with "oo" spelling that produce the /u:/ sound

List five words each with "ew," "ue," and "ui" spellings that produce /u:/ sound

Write ten sentences using different words with /u:/ sound

Identify words with /u:/ sound in this sentence: "The rude youth threw his blue shoe through the school room"
Practice reading this passage aloud, paying attention to /u:/ sounds: "In June, the new student knew the rules. He would choose fruit juice at noon and move to his room to do his school work."
WEEK 2 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: TENSES – SIMPLE PRESENT TENSE

What is the Simple Present Tense? The Simple Present Tense is used to describe actions that happen regularly, facts, habits, general truths, and permanent situations.
Formation of Simple Present Tense:

For Regular Verbs:

I/You/We/They + base form of verb

I play, You eat, We run, They sing

He/She/It + base form + s/es

He plays, She eats, It runs, Mary sings

Rules for Adding -s or -es:

Add -s to most verbs:

work → works, play → plays, eat → eats, sleep → sleeps

Add -es to verbs ending in -s, -sh, -ch, -x, -z, -o:

wash → washes, teach → teaches, go → goes, fix → fixes, buzz → buzzes

For verbs ending in consonant + y, change y to i and add -es:

cry → cries, fly → flies, try → tries, study → studies

For verbs ending in vowel + y, just add -s:

play → plays, enjoy → enjoys, say → says

Uses of Simple Present Tense:

Habits and Routines:

I wake up at 6 AM every day
She brushes her teeth twice daily

They go to church on Sundays

General Truths and Facts:

The sun rises in the east

Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius

Birds fly in the sky

Nigeria is in West Africa

Permanent Situations:

I live in Lagos

My father works in a bank

She speaks three languages

Scheduled Events:

The train leaves at 8 PM

School starts at 7:30 AM

The match begins at 4 PM

Negative Form:

I/You/We/They + do not (don't) + base verb

I don't like rice

They don't play football

He/She/It + does not (doesn't) + base verb

He doesn't eat meat

She doesn't live here

Question Form:
Do + I/you/we/they + base verb?

Do you speak English?

o Do they go to school?

Does + he/she/it + base verb?

Does he play tennis?

Does she know you?

Time Expressions Used with Simple Present:

Always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never

Every day/week/month/year

On Mondays/Tuesdays, etc.

In the morning/afternoon/evening

At night

Examples:

I study English every day

My mother cooks delicious food

The teacher explains the lesson clearly

Dogs bark at strangers

He doesn't smoke

Do you understand the question?

Common Mistakes to Avoid:

❌ He go to school (Correct: He goes to school)

❌ She don't like it (Correct: She doesn't like it)
❌ Do he play? (Correct: Does he play?)

❌ The sun rise in the east (Correct: The sun rises in the east)

EVALUATION:

Define Simple Present Tense and state four uses with examples

Write the correct form of these verbs in Simple Present for "He/She": (a) wash (b) cry (c) go (d) study (e) play
Change to negative: (a) She likes mangoes (b) They speak French (c) He goes to work daily
Change to questions: (a) You read books (b) She knows the answer (c) They live in Abuja
Fill in the blanks with the correct form: (a) The baby	(cry) every night (b) I  		 (not/eat) meat (c)	he	(go) to school? (d) Birds		(fly) (e) She  	 (teach) Mathematics

ASSIGNMENT:

Change these sentences to question form: (a) You speak English (b) He likes reading (c) They live here (d) She knows me (e) It works well

WEEK 2 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: ASKING PROJECTION QUESTIONS; VOCABULARY – THE LIBRARY PART A: ASKING PROJECTION QUESTIONS
What are Projection Questions? Projection questions require readers to predict, imagine, or extend beyond the given text. They ask "What might happen next?" or "What would happen if...?"
Types of Projection Questions:

Prediction Questions:

What do you think will happen next?

How will the story end?

What might the character do?

Hypothetical Questions:

What would happen if...?

How would you feel if...?

What would you do in this situation?

Extension Questions:

What might happen after the story ends?

How could this situation be different?

What other solutions are possible?

Personal Connection Questions:

Have you experienced something similar?

How would you react in this situation?

What would you do differently?
How to Answer Projection Questions:

Base predictions on clues in the text

Use logical reasoning

Consider character traits and motivations

Think about cause and effect

Use your imagination while staying realistic

Support your answer with evidence from the passage

Sample Passage: "Tunde had studied for only one hour the night before the examination. His friends had studied for weeks. As he sat in the examination hall looking at the questions, his hands began to shake. The questions looked unfamiliar."
Projection Questions & Answers:

What do you think will happen to Tunde in the exam?

Answer: Tunde will likely perform poorly because he didn't prepare adequately, unlike his friends who studied for weeks.
What might Tunde do differently next time?

Answer: He might start studying earlier and prepare more thoroughly for his examinations.
What would you do if you were in Tunde's situation?

Answer: I would try to stay calm, answer the questions I know, and learn from this experience to prepare better next time.
Signal Words for Projection Questions:

What might/could/would...?

Predict, imagine, suppose

If..., then...?
What do you think...?

How would...?

PART B: VOCABULARY – THE LIBRARY

What is a Library? A library is a place where books, magazines, newspapers, and other reading materials are kept for people to read, borrow, or reference.
Library Vocabulary:

Places in the Library:

Reading room: Area for reading silently

Reference section: Books that cannot be borrowed

Circulation desk: Where books are borrowed and returned

Catalogue room: Where book records are kept

Periodicals section: Newspapers and magazines

Children's section: Books for young readers

Study carrel: Individual study space

People in the Library:

Librarian: Person who manages the library

Library assistant: Helps the librarian

Borrower/Patron: Person who uses the library

Reader: Person reading in the library

Library Materials:

Fiction: Imaginative stories (novels, short stories)

Non-fiction: Factual books (biographies, textbooks)
Reference books: Dictionaries, encyclopedias, atlases

Periodicals: Newspapers, magazines, journals

Audiovisual materials: CDs, DVDs, tapes

E-books: Digital books

Library Activities:

Borrow: Take books home temporarily

Return: Bring books back

Renew: Extend borrowing period

Reserve: Book a material in advance

Browse: Look through books casually

Research: Search for information

Catalogue: List of all library materials

Library Rules:

Maintain silence

Handle books with care

Return books on time

No eating or drinking

Replace books properly

Show library card when borrowing

Pay fines for late returns

No tearing or marking books

Parts of a Book:

Cover: Outside protection
Spine: Back edge of book

Title page: Page showing book title and author

Table of contents: List of chapters

Index: Alphabetical list of topics

Bibliography: List of references

Glossary: Dictionary of terms used

Appendix: Additional information

Library Card Information:

Name of borrower

Registration number

Date of issue

Expiry date

Library rules

EVALUATION:

Explain what projection questions are and give three examples

Read this passage: "Ada found a wallet on the street with money inside but no identification." Ask three projection questions based on this passage
Define a library and state five items you can find there

Explain the difference between fiction and non-fiction books with examples

List five library rules and explain why they are important

(c) What might happen after?

WEEK 2 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: FORMAL LETTER – TO THE SCHOOL AUTHORITY

Writing to School Authority School authority refers to people in charge of school administration such as the Principal, Vice Principal, Head of Department, or School Management Board.
Types of Letters to School Authority:

Request Letters:

Permission for absence

Request for facilities

Permission for events/activities

Request for transfer certificate

Complaint Letters:

About damaged facilities

About school services

About unfair treatment

Recommendation Requests:

For scholarships

For competitions

For applications

Information Letters:

Reporting incidents

Explaining circumstances

Providing feedback
Format Reminder:

Writer's Address Date

Recipient's Designation and Address

Salutation

SUBJECT (UNDERLINED/BOLD)

Body:

Introduction (state purpose)

Main content (give details)

Conclusion (courteous closing)

Complimentary close Signature
Full Name

Class (if applicable)

Language Features:

Formal and polite tone

Clear and direct

Complete sentences
No contractions

Respectful language

Proper grammar and punctuation

Brief but complete

Important Phrases:

Opening:

I write to...

I wish to...

This is to...

I am writing to inform you...

May I respectfully request...

Body:

I would like to...

I wish to bring to your attention...

The reason for this is...

I believe that...

It is necessary to...

Closing:

I hope you will consider...

Thank you for your attention...

I look forward to...

I would appreciate...

Hoping for a favorable response...

Sample Letter 1: Request for Facilities

12, Benson Street, Surulere,
Lagos State.

20th October, 2025.

The Principal, Kings College, Surulere, Lagos State.

Dear Sir,

REQUEST FOR LIBRARY EXTENSION HOURS

I write on behalf of the students of JSS 1 to request for extension of the library opening hours.

Currently, the library closes at 3 PM, which is the same time our lessons end. This does not give us adequate time to use the library facilities for research and private study. Many students wish to spend more time reading and doing assignments in the library.
We respectfully request that the library hours be extended to 5 PM on weekdays. This will enable students to benefit more from the library resources and improve our academic performance.

We hope you will consider this request favorably. Thank you for your attention.

Yours faithfully, [Signature]
Adebayo Oluwaseun Library Prefect, JSS 1

Sample Letter 2: Complaint Letter

22, Unity Road, Kano,
Kano State.

20th October, 2025.

The Vice Principal (Administration), Government Secondary School, Kano,
Kano State.

Dear Ma,

COMPLAINT ABOUT DAMAGED CLASSROOM WINDOWS

I wish to bring to your attention the poor condition of windows in our classroom, JSS 1C.

Several windows in the classroom are broken and have been in this state for two months. During rainy days, water enters the classroom and damages our books and notes. The broken windows also allow dust and insects into the classroom, making learning uncomfortable.

We request that the windows be repaired as soon as possible to provide a conducive learning environment for students.

Thank you for your understanding and anticipated prompt action.

Yours faithfully, [Signature] Maryam Ibrahim
Class Captain, JSS 1C

Points to Remember:

State your purpose clearly in the first paragraph

Provide necessary details in the middle

Be respectful even when complaining
Suggest solutions where possible

Keep it brief (3-4 paragraphs)

Use "Yours faithfully" for formal titles (Principal, Vice Principal)

Include your class if you're a student

Check for errors before submitting

Common Topics:

Request to organize an excursion

Complaint about cafeteria services

Request for sports equipment

Permission to use school hall for event

Request for extra classes

Complaint about broken furniture

Request for improved sanitation

EVALUATION:

List four types of letters you can write to school authority

Write out the proper format for addressing a letter to your school Principal

What is the appropriate complimentary close for a letter to "The Principal"?

Give three examples of topics you might write to school authority about

Rewrite this sentence formally: "Please sir, I want you to fix our class chairs because they are all spoilt"
ASSIGNMENT:

WEEK 2 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO POETRY – DEFINITION, FEATURES (CONCISE, UNIQUE, ETC.)

CONTENT:

What is Poetry? Poetry is a type of literature that uses carefully chosen words, rhythm, and imagery to express feelings, ideas, and experiences in a creative and artistic way. It appeals to emotions and imagination.

Differences Between Poetry and Prose: Poetry:
Written in lines and stanzas

Uses rhythm and sometimes rhyme

More compact and concentrated

Uses figurative language heavily

Often emotional and imaginative

Can be read aloud with musical quality

Prose:

Written in sentences and paragraphs

No specific rhythm pattern

More straightforward

Less figurative language

Can be fiction or non-fiction
Everyday language

Features of Poetry:

CONCISE (Brief and Compact)

Poetry uses few words to express big ideas

Every word is carefully chosen

No unnecessary words

Compact expression of thoughts and feelings

Example: "The sun smiles" (instead of "The sun is shining brightly and looks happy")

UNIQUE/CREATIVE USE OF LANGUAGE

Poets use words in special ways

Creative word arrangements

Fresh and original expressions

Unexpected word combinations

Plays with meaning and sound

LINES AND STANZAS

Line: A single row of words in a poem

Stanza: A group of lines forming a unit (like a paragraph)

Poems are divided into stanzas, not paragraphs

Line breaks are meaningful

RHYTHM AND METER

Rhythm: Pattern of stressed and unstressed syllables

Creates musical quality
Makes poetry pleasant to hear

Can be regular or free

RHYME

Rhyme: Similar sounds at the end of lines

Examples: cat/hat, moon/soon, day/way

Not all poems rhyme

Rhyme scheme: Pattern of rhyme (AABB, ABAB, etc.)

FIGURATIVE LANGUAGE

Simile: Comparison using "like" or "as" (Her smile is like sunshine)

Metaphor: Direct comparison (Her smile is sunshine)

Personification: Giving human qualities to non-human things (The wind whispered)

Imagery: Descriptive language appealing to senses

EMOTIONAL AND IMAGINATIVE

Expresses feelings (love, joy, sadness, anger)

Appeals to imagination

Creates mental pictures

Touches the heart

THEME

Central message or idea

What the poem is about

Common themes: love, nature, death, freedom, childhood

Types of Poetry:
Narrative Poetry:

Tells a story

Has characters and plot

Example: Epics, ballads

Lyric Poetry:

Expresses personal feelings

Musical quality

Example: Odes, sonnets

Descriptive Poetry:

Describes scenes, people, things

Rich in imagery

Free Verse:

No regular rhythm or rhyme

Modern style

Flexible form

Sample Poem:

THE RAIN

By Anonymous

The rain falls softly on the roof, Pitter-patter, gentle and aloof, Washing the earth with loving care,
Bringing freshness everywhere.

Plants dance in joyful celebration, Children run in happy recreation, The rain brings life to all around, What a beautiful, soothing sound!

Analysis:

Concise: Uses simple words to paint a picture

Lines and Stanzas: Two stanzas of four lines each

Rhyme: roof/aloof, care/everywhere (AABB pattern)

Rhythm: Regular beat throughout

Figurative Language: "Plants dance" (personification), "washing the earth with loving care" (personification)
Imagery: We can hear and see the rain

Theme: Rain brings life and joy

How to Read Poetry:

Read slowly

Pay attention to word choice

Notice rhythm and sound

Look for imagery and figurative language

Think about feelings and emotions
Read aloud to hear the musical quality

Don't worry if you don't understand everything at first

Elements to Look for When Reading Poems:

Title (gives clues about the poem)

Speaker (who is talking?)

Tone (mood of the poem)

Setting (where and when?)

Theme (main message)

Poetic devices used

EVALUATION:

Define poetry and state three ways it differs from prose

Explain five features of poetry with examples

What is the difference between a line and a stanza in poetry?

Identify the figurative language in these expressions: (a) The stars danced in the sky (b) Her voice is as sweet as honey (c) Time is money
Read the sample poem "The Rain" and identify: (a) the rhyme scheme (b) one example of personification (c) the theme

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a simple four-line poem about your school with a clear rhyme scheme
Define these poetic terms with examples: (a) simile (b) metaphor (c) personification (d) imagery (e) stanza
Identify three features of poetry in any poem you have read

Explain why poetry is described as "concise" and "unique"

State the title, author, and brief summary (5 sentences) of your recommended prose text for this term
WEEK 3 - SPEECH WORK TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɑ:/
The Long Vowel Sound /ɑ:/

This is a long, open vowel sound produced at the back of the mouth

The mouth is wide open

The tongue is low and flat

The lips are not rounded

It is similar to the sound a doctor asks you to make when examining your throat: "Ahhh"

Spelling Patterns for /ɑ:/ Sound:

"ar" spelling (most common):

car, far, bar, jar, tar, star, scar

arm, farm, harm, charm, alarm

art, part, cart, start, heart, smart

dark, park, mark, bark, shark

card, hard, yard, guard, regard

March, arch, starch

large, charge, barge

"a" spelling (before certain consonants):

father, rather

bath, path, class, grass, pass, glass, task, ask, cast, fast, last, past, master

"al" spelling:

calm, palm, balm, psalm

half, calf
"ear" spelling:

heart

"au/a" spelling (rare):

aunt, laugh

British vs. American Pronunciation: Note: Some words have /ɑ:/ in British English but /æ/ in American English:
bath, path, grass, class, dance, chance, plant

Common Words with /ɑ:/ Sound:

afternoon, army, argue, article, artist

banana (second 'a'), tomato (second 'a')

barber, carpet, garden, market, party

carve, starve, marble

Practice Sentences:

The car is parked in the dark yard

My father is a smart artist

The shark swims far in the large water

Martha's heart was calm at the party

The guard marched past the farm

Words That Look Similar But Sound Different:

war, warm, ward = /ɔ:/ sound (NOT /ɑ:/)

carry, marry, Harry = /æ/ sound (NOT /ɑ:/)
Articulation Tips:

Open your mouth wide

Keep tongue flat and low

Don't round your lips

Make the sound long and clear

Think of the sound when you're at the doctor

Practice Pairs:

had /æ/ - hard /ɑ:/

cap /æ/ - carp /ɑ:/

mad /æ/ - marred /ɑ:/

cat /æ/ - cart /ɑ:/

EVALUATION:

Produce the /ɑ:/ sound correctly and explain how it is articulated

Give ten words containing the /ɑ:/ sound using "ar" spelling

Identify which words contain /ɑ:/ sound: (a) car (b) carry (c) dark (d) war (e) farm (f) warm (g) part (h) party
Write the /ɑ:/ sound words from this sentence: "The farmer's smart daughter walked to the large market in the dark"
Explain the difference in pronunciation between: (a) had/hard (b) cat/cart (c) pat/part

ASSIGNMENT:

List fifteen words with "ar" spelling that contain the /ɑ:/ sound
List five words each with "a" and "al" spellings that produce the /ɑ:/ sound

Write ten sentences using words with /ɑ:/ sound, underlining the words

Read aloud and practice: "Martha's father is a barber at the market. He starts work before dark and parks his car in the yard"
Identify and correct the pronunciation errors in these words if mispronounced with wrong vowel sounds: farm, warm, class, carry, march
WEEK 3 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: TENSES – SIMPLE PAST TENSE

What is the Simple Past Tense? The Simple Past Tense is used to describe actions that happened and were completed in the past. It shows that something started and finished at a specific time in the past.
Formation of Simple Past Tense:

Regular Verbs: Base form + ed Rules for Adding -ed:
Most verbs - add -ed:

walk → walked

play → played

talk → talked

work → worked

listen → listened

Verbs ending in -e - add only -d:

live → lived

move → moved

dance → danced

arrive → arrived

hope → hoped

Verbs ending in consonant + y - change y to i and add -ed:

cry → cried

study → studied
try → tried

carry → carried

worry → worried

Verbs ending in vowel + y - just add -ed:

play → played

enjoy → enjoyed

stay → stayed

pray → prayed

One-syllable verbs ending in consonant-vowel-consonant - double the final consonant and add -ed:
stop → stopped

plan → planned

drop → dropped

shop → shopped

clap → clapped

Irregular Verbs: These verbs do not follow the -ed rule. They have special past tense forms that must be memorized:
go → went

come → came

see → saw

eat → ate

drink → drank
run → ran

sit → sat

stand → stood

write → wrote

read → read (pronunciation changes)

buy → bought

bring → brought

teach → taught

catch → caught

think → thought

take → took

make → made

give → gave

get → got

have/has → had

do/does → did

is/am/are → was/were

say → said

tell → told

sleep → slept

leave → left

meet → met

find → found
lose → lost

break → broke

speak → spoke

drive → drove

fly → flew

know → knew

grow → grew

begin → began

sing → sang

swim → swam

drink → drank

Uses of Simple Past Tense:

Completed actions in the past:

I visited my grandmother yesterday

She finished her homework last night

They traveled to Abuja last month

Past habits (no longer true):

I lived in Lagos when I was young

He played football every weekend

We went to the beach every summer

Series of completed actions:

I woke up, brushed my teeth, and went to school
She entered the room, greeted everyone, and sat down

Negative Form:

Subject + did not (didn't) + base verb

I didn't go to school yesterday

She didn't eat lunch

They didn't see the movie

He didn't finish his work

Note: Use the base form of the verb after "didn't," not the past tense.

✓ I didn't go (Correct)

✗ I didn't went (Wrong)

Question Form:

Did + subject + base verb...?

Did you see him?

Did she finish the book?

Did they arrive on time?

Did he play football?

Time Expressions Used with Simple Past:

Yesterday

Last (night/week/month/year/Monday, etc.)

Ago (two days ago, a week ago)
In 2020, in 1999 (specific past years)

When I was young

During my childhood

This morning (if morning is over)

Just now

Examples:

I played football yesterday

She visited the museum last week

They lived in Kano two years ago

My father bought a car in 2023

We didn't go to church last Sunday

Did you see the accident?

The teacher taught us English this morning

I met my friend at the market

Common Mistakes to Avoid:

✗ I goed to school (Correct: I went)

✗ She didn't came (Correct: She didn't come)

✗ Did you went? (Correct: Did you go?)

✗ He don't go yesterday (Correct: He didn't go)

✗ They was happy (Correct: They were happy)
EVALUATION:

Define Simple Past Tense and state three uses with examples

Write the past tense form of these verbs: (a) study (b) run (c) stop (d) enjoy (e) see (f) teach (g) buy (h) carry (i) write (j) have
Change to Simple Past Tense: (a) I go to school (b) She eats rice (c) They play football (d) He reads books (e) We are happy
Change to negative: (a) I saw him yesterday (b) She came to school (c) They finished the work (d) He bought a new book (e) We went to the market
Change to questions: (a) You visited your uncle (b) She cooked rice yesterday (c) They arrived early (d) He played well (e) It rained last night

Correct the errors: (a) I goed home (b) She didn't came (c) Did he went? (d) They was late (e) He don't finish yesterday
Change this passage from Present to Past Tense: "I wake up early. I brush my teeth and take my bath. I eat breakfast and go to school. I meet my friends and we play together."
WEEK 3 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: ASKING RECALL QUESTIONS; VOCABULARY – MARRIAGE-RELATED WORDS
PART A: ASKING RECALL QUESTIONS

What are Recall Questions? Recall questions are questions that require readers to remember and retrieve specific information directly stated in the passage. The answers are found word-for- word or very clearly in the text.
Characteristics of Recall Questions:

Answers are explicitly stated in the text

No inference or interpretation needed

Test your memory of what you read

Have clear, definite answers

Focus on facts, details, and specific information

Types of Recall Questions:

Who Questions:

Who is the main character?

Who did...?

Who said...?

What Questions:

What happened?

What did the character do?

What is the name of...?
When Questions:

When did the event occur?

When did the character...?

Where Questions:

Where did the story take place?

Where was...?

How Many/How Much Questions:

How many people...?

How much did it cost?

Why Questions (for stated reasons):

Why did he go? (if reason is given)

Signal Words in Recall Questions:

Name, list, identify, state

According to the passage

The passage states/says

What is the name of...?

How many...?

When exactly...?

How to Answer Recall Questions:
Step 1: Read the question carefully Step 2: Identify key words in the question Step 3: Scan the passage for those key words Step 4: Read the relevant section carefully Step 5: Write the answer using words from the passage Step 6: Check that your answer directly addresses the question
Sample Passage:

"Amina Bello is a 12-year-old student at Government Secondary School, Lagos. She lives at 23 Benson Street with her parents and two younger brothers. Every morning, she wakes up at 5:30 AM to prepare for school. Her favorite subject is Mathematics, and she wants to become an engineer. Last term, she scored 95% in her Mathematics examination and received the Best Student Award in her class, JSS 1B."
Recall Questions and Answers:

What is the full name of the student?

Answer: Amina Bello

Where does Amina live?

Answer: 23 Benson Street (or: She lives at 23 Benson Street with her parents)

What time does she wake up every morning?

Answer: 5:30 AM

What is her favorite subject?

Answer: Mathematics

What percentage did she score in Mathematics last term?

Answer: 95%

How many brothers does she have?

Answer: Two younger brothers

What does she want to become?

Answer: An engineer
Which class is she in?

Answer: JSS 1B

Difference Between Recall and Other Question Types:

Recall Question: What award did Amina receive? (Answer is in the text: Best Student Award)

Inference Question: Why do you think Amina received the award? (Requires reasoning: Because she works hard and scored high marks)
Projection Question: What might Amina become in the future? (Requires prediction: She might become a successful engineer)

PART B: VOCABULARY – MARRIAGE-RELATED WORDS

Marriage Vocabulary:

People Involved:

Bride: The woman getting married

Groom/Bridegroom: The man getting married

Couple: The bride and groom together

Spouse: Husband or wife

Husband: Married man

Wife: Married woman

Fiancé: Man engaged to be married

Fiancée: Woman engaged to be married

Best man: Groom's chief attendant

Maid of honor/Chief bridesmaid: Bride's chief attendant

Bridesmaids: Female attendants to the bride
Groomsmen: Male attendants to the groom

Parents-in-law: Parents of your spouse (father-in-law, mother-in-law)

Officiating minister/Pastor/Imam: Person conducting the ceremony

Types of Marriage:

Monogamy: Marriage to one person

Polygamy: Marriage to more than one person

Civil marriage: Legal marriage conducted by government officials

Religious marriage: Marriage conducted by religious leaders

Traditional marriage: Marriage according to cultural customs

Court wedding: Marriage at the court/registry

Marriage Events and Ceremonies:

Engagement: Formal agreement to marry

Introduction: When families meet formally

Bridal shower: Party for the bride before wedding

Bachelor party: Party for the groom before wedding

Wedding ceremony: The marriage event

Wedding reception: Celebration after the ceremony

Honeymoon: Holiday taken by newlyweds after wedding

Marriage Items and Symbols:

Wedding ring: Ring exchanged during marriage

Engagement ring: Ring given during engagement

Wedding gown/dress: Special dress worn by bride

Suit/Tuxedo: Formal wear for groom
Veil: Cloth covering bride's face

Bouquet: Bunch of flowers carried by bride

Wedding cake: Special cake for celebration

Wedding invitation: Card inviting people to wedding

Marriage Expressions and Actions:

Propose: To ask someone to marry you

Accept proposal: To agree to marry someone

Exchange vows: To make marriage promises

Walk down the aisle: Bride's walk to the altar

Give away the bride: Father presenting bride to groom

Say "I do": Accept marriage vows

Kiss the bride: Traditional kiss after vows

Throw bouquet: Bride tosses flowers to unmarried women

Cut the cake: Couple cuts wedding cake together

First dance: Couple's first dance as married people

Marriage-Related Verbs:

Marry, wed, propose, engage

Divorce, separate (end of marriage)

Celebrate, honor, cherish

Marriage Documents:

Marriage certificate: Official document of marriage

Marriage license: Permission to marry

Wedding program: Order of ceremony events
Traditional Marriage Terms (African Context):

Bride price/Dowry: Payment to bride's family

Traditional attire: Cultural clothing for ceremony

Wine carrying: Traditional ceremony element

Breaking of kola nut: Traditional ritual

EVALUATION:

Explain what recall questions are and how they differ from inference questions

Read this passage and answer the recall questions: "Mr. and Mrs. Johnson got married on December 15, 2020, at St. Paul's Church. They have two children: a boy named David (5 years old) and a girl named Grace (3 years old). They live in Abuja." Questions: (a) When did Mr. and Mrs. Johnson get married? (b) Where did they get married? (c) How many children do they have? (d) What are the names of their children? (e) Where do they live?
Define these marriage terms: (a) bride (b) groom (c) spouse (d) engagement (e) honeymoon
List five items or symbols associated with weddings

Write sentences using these words correctly: (a) propose (b) ceremony (c) reception (d) bride (e) vows

Groom (e) Bride

WEEK 3 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: GUIDED COMPOSITION – THE FEDERAL ROAD SAFETY COMMISSION (FRSC)
What is Guided Composition? Guided composition is a writing exercise where you are given specific information, points, or guidelines to help you write. You organize the given information into complete sentences and paragraphs.
THE FEDERAL ROAD SAFETY COMMISSION (FRSC)

Background Information:

What is FRSC? The Federal Road Safety Commission (FRSC) is a Nigerian government agency responsible for road safety management, enforcement of traffic rules, and reduction of road accidents.
Key Information About FRSC:

Establishment:

Created by Decree Number 45 of 1988

Became operational on February 18, 1988

Founded to address increasing road accidents in Nigeria

Motto and Logo:

Motto: "Safety First"

Logo: Features the map of Nigeria in green and white

Uniform: White and green colors representing peace and safety

Functions/Duties:

Prevent and reduce road traffic accidents

Clear obstruction on highways

Educate motorists and road users on safety
Enforce traffic rules and regulations

Issue driver's licenses

Conduct driving tests

Register vehicles

Provide first aid and rescue during accidents

Patrol highways and roads

Recommend road safety measures to government

Road Safety Rules Enforced by FRSC:

Use of seat belts (mandatory for all)

Speed limits must be obeyed

No use of phone while driving

Valid driver's license required

Vehicle registration and insurance

No overloading of vehicles

No drinking and driving

Proper use of road signs

Use of child restraints

Working lights and indicators

Safety Tips from FRSC:

Obey all traffic signs and signals

Maintain safe speed

Keep safe distance from other vehicles

Be alert and avoid distractions
Wear seat belts always

Don't drink and drive

Ensure your vehicle is roadworthy

Use pedestrian crossings when walking

Be visible at night (wear reflective clothing)

Penalties for Traffic Offenses:

Fines for various violations

Impounding of vehicles

Suspension of driver's license

Court prosecution for serious offenses

Special Operations:

Ember months (September-December) special patrols

Public holiday operations

School resumption traffic management

Accident-prone area monitoring

FRSC Offices:

Headquarters in Abuja

Zonal and state commands

Unit commands in local areas

Mobile patrol teams

GUIDED POINTS FOR WRITING:

Paragraph 1 - Introduction:
Name of the organization

When it was established

Why it was created

Its motto

Paragraph 2 - Functions:

Main responsibilities

Traffic rules enforcement

Licensing and registration

Educational activities

Paragraph 3 - Road Safety Rules:

Important traffic rules

Safety measures for drivers

Safety tips for pedestrians

Paragraph 4 - Conclusion:

Importance of FRSC

Appeal for cooperation

Benefits of obeying traffic rules

SAMPLE GUIDED COMPOSITION:

THE FEDERAL ROAD SAFETY COMMISSION

The Federal Road Safety Commission, popularly known as FRSC, is a Nigerian government agency responsible for ensuring safety on our roads. It was established by Decree Number 45 of 1988 and became operational on February 18, 1988. The commission was created to address the increasing number of road accidents and deaths on Nigerian roads. Its motto is "Safety First," which reminds everyone that nothing is more important than staying safe on the road.
The FRSC has many important functions. It prevents and reduces road traffic accidents through enforcement of traffic rules and public education. The commission issues driver's licenses to qualified drivers and registers vehicles. FRSC officers patrol highways and roads to ensure motorists obey traffic laws. They also provide first aid and rescue services during accidents, clear obstructions on highways, and educate motorists and other road users about safe driving practices. Additionally, they make recommendations to the government on road safety measures.
There are many important road safety rules that the FRSC enforces. All drivers and passengers must wear seat belts while in a moving vehicle. Drivers must not use mobile phones while driving, as this causes distractions. Every driver must have a valid driver's license and ensure their vehicle is properly registered and insured. Speed limits must be obeyed at all times.
Vehicles should not be overloaded beyond their capacity. Drinking and driving is strictly prohibited as it impairs judgment. All vehicle lights, indicators, and safety features must be in good working condition.
The Federal Road Safety Commission plays a vital role in protecting lives and property on Nigerian roads. Everyone must cooperate with FRSC officers and obey all traffic rules and regulations. By following road safety measures, we can reduce accidents and make our roads safer for all users. Remember, safety first!
Writing Tips:

Use formal language

Organize information logically

Write in complete sentences

Use proper paragraphing

Connect ideas smoothly

Include relevant details

Stay focused on the topic

Proofread your work

Useful Linking Words:
Additionally, furthermore, moreover (to add information)

Therefore, thus, consequently (to show result)

However, nevertheless, although (to contrast)

Firstly, secondly, finally (to list points)

For example, for instance (to give examples)

EVALUATION:

When was the Federal Road Safety Commission established and what is its motto?

State five functions of the FRSC

List five road safety rules enforced by the FRSC

Explain what guided composition means

Why is the Federal Road Safety Commission important to Nigeria?

WEEK 3 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: POETRY – TYPES (EPIC, LYRIC, ETC.); ORAL VS WRITTEN POETRY TYPES OF POETRY
Poetry can be classified into different types based on content, form, and purpose.

EPIC POETRY

Definition: Long narrative poems that tell heroic stories of legendary figures, nations, or civilizations.
Features:

Very long (hundreds or thousands of lines)

Tells stories of heroes and their adventures

Involves supernatural beings (gods, spirits)

Covers important historical or legendary events

Uses elevated/formal language

Begins "in medias res" (in the middle of action)

Contains lists, catalogs, and long speeches

Has a serious tone

Examples:

The Iliad and The Odyssey by Homer (Greek)

Beowulf (English)

The Divine Comedy by Dante (Italian)

Paradise Lost by John Milton (English)

African oral epics like Sundiata (Mali) and Ozidi (Nigeria)

Typical Epic Content:
Battles and wars

Journeys and quests

Founding of nations

Heroic deeds

Cultural values and traditions

LYRIC POETRY

Definition: Short poems that express personal feelings, emotions, and thoughts of a single speaker.
Features:

Personal and subjective

Expresses emotions (love, joy, sadness, anger)

Usually short

Musical quality

First-person point of view (I, me, my)

Focuses on feelings rather than storytelling

Often has regular rhythm and rhyme

Types of Lyric Poetry:

Ode:

Formal lyric poem

Celebrates or praises a person, thing, or idea

Serious and dignified tone

Example: "Ode to a Nightingale" by John Keats
Sonnet:

14-line poem

Usually about love

Specific rhyme scheme

Example: Shakespearean sonnets

Elegy:

Sad poem

Mourns death or loss

Reflective and melancholic

Example: "Elegy Written in a Country Churchyard" by Thomas Gray

Song/Ballad:

Meant to be sung

Simple language

Tells a story with emotion

NARRATIVE POETRY

Definition: Poetry that tells a story with characters, plot, and setting.

Features:

Has a storyline

Contains characters

Has beginning, middle, and end

Combines elements of poetry and prose

May be long or short
Types:

Ballads (folk stories in verse)

Epic poetry (long narratives)

Mock epic (humorous imitation of epic)

DRAMATIC POETRY

Definition: Poetry written in the voice of a character, often as a monologue or dialogue.

Features:

Character speaks directly

Reveals personality and situation

Creates dramatic effect

Often addresses someone

Types:

Dramatic monologue

Soliloquy

Dialogue poems

DESCRIPTIVE POETRY

Definition: Poetry that vividly describes people, places, objects, or experiences.

Features:

Rich in imagery

Appeals to the five senses

Creates clear mental pictures
Uses figurative language heavily

Focus on showing, not telling

FREE VERSE

Definition: Poetry without regular rhythm, rhyme, or structure.

Features:

No fixed pattern

Natural speech rhythm

Modern and flexible

Focuses on imagery and meaning

Freedom in form

ORAL VS WRITTEN POETRY ORAL POETRY
Definition: Poetry that is composed, performed, and transmitted by word of mouth, not written down.
Characteristics:

Performance-Based:

Meant to be spoken/sung aloud

Uses voice, gestures, and facial expressions

Interactive with audience

Performed at gatherings, ceremonies, festivals

Memory-Based:
Passed from generation to generation orally

Memorized, not read

May change over time as it's retold

Uses repetition to aid memory

Cultural Context:

Part of community traditions

Performed at specific occasions (weddings, funerals, celebrations)

Teaches cultural values and history

Preserves heritage

Stylistic Features:

Repetition of words, phrases, lines

Call and response patterns

Strong rhythm and rhyme

Simple, memorable language

Use of formulas and stock phrases

Accompanied by music, drums, or dance

Types of Oral Poetry:

Praise poems (oriki in Yoruba)

Work songs

War songs

Lullabies

Festival chants

Funeral dirges
Folk ballads

Epic poems (like Ozidi)

Examples in African Context:

Praise poetry for kings and warriors

Moonlight tales with poetic elements

Harvest songs

Initiation chants

Wedding songs

Functions of Oral Poetry:

Entertainment

Preservation of history

Teaching moral lessons

Expressing emotions

Strengthening community bonds

Celebrating achievements

Honoring the dead

WRITTEN POETRY

Definition: Poetry that is composed in written form and transmitted through texts.

Characteristics:

Text-Based:
Written down and published

Read silently or aloud

Permanent and fixed form

Can be analyzed and studied

Individual Authorship:

Created by known poets

Original compositions

Copyright protected

Attributed to specific authors

Literary Context:

Found in books, magazines, journals

Studied in schools

Part of literary tradition

Subject to literary criticism

Stylistic Features:

Complex language and imagery

Sophisticated techniques

Visual arrangement on page

Line breaks and stanzas matter

May use unusual punctuation

Appeals to eye as well as ear

Types of Written Poetry:

Sonnets, odes, elegies
Free verse

Haiku

Concrete/shape poetry

Modern experimental poetry

COMPARISON TABLE

AspectOral PoetryWritten PoetryTransmissionSpoken/performedWritten/printedAuthorshipOften anonymousKnown authorPreservationMemoryBooks/documentsPerformanceLive with audienceRead individuallyChangeMay vary over timeFixed textLanguageSimple, repetitiveCan be complexPurposeCommunity functionArtistic expression

Relationship Between Oral and Written:

Many written poems draw from oral traditions

Some oral poems have been written down

Both use similar poetic devices

Both express human experiences

Modern poets often blend both traditions

African Context:

Rich tradition of oral poetry
Oral poetry predates written forms

Many African languages have strong oral poetry traditions

Contemporary African poets combine oral and written traditions

Examples: Wole Soyinka, Chinua Achebe, Niyi Osundare use oral elements in written poetry

EVALUATION:

Define epic poetry and state four of its features

Explain the difference between lyric poetry and narrative poetry with examples

What is oral poetry? List five characteristics of oral poetry

Compare oral poetry and written poetry, stating three differences

Name three types of lyric poetry and briefly describe each

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a short lyric poem (8 lines) expressing your feelings about school, nature, or friendship
List five examples of oral poetry found in your culture or community

Explain five functions of oral poetry in African society
