WEEK 4 - SPEECH WORK TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /ɜ:/
The Long Vowel Sound /ɜ:/

This is a long, central vowel sound produced in the middle of the mouth

The tongue is in the center, neither high nor low

The lips are slightly spread, not rounded

It is a tense, prolonged sound

Similar to the sound when hesitating: "Er..."

Spelling Patterns for /ɜ:/ Sound:

"ir" spelling:

bird, girl, first, shirt, skirt, third, thirst, birthday

dirt, firm, fir, sir, stir, whirl, twirl

circle, circus, confirm, dirty, thirty, thirteen

"er" spelling:

her, term, serve, verb, nerve, herb

certain, person, perfect, merchant, verse

teacher, mother, father, sister, brother (unstressed syllables)

prefer, refer, infer, defer, confer

"ur" spelling:

turn, burn, church, hurt, nurse, purse, turkey

fur, blur,pur, spur, slur

disturb, furniture, purple, purpose, curtain
Thursday, surname, burden, murder

"ear" spelling:

learn, earth, early, earn, earnest, search, pearl

heard, rehearse

"or" spelling (after 'w'):

word, work, world, worm, worth, worship

worse, worst

"our" spelling:

journey, journal, courtesy, courtesy

Common Words with /ɜ:/ Sound:

return, concern, urgent, service, surface

german, perfect, merchant, deserve

burden, curtain, murder, turkey

circular, emergency, commercial

Practice Sentences:

The girl with the purple shirt was first

My sister is a nurse at the church

The merchant returned early from his journey

I heard the birds chirping on Thursday

Her thirst for learning is perfect

Words That Look Similar But Sound Different:
her /ɜ:/ - here /ɪə/ (different sound)

were /ɜ:/ - where /eə/ (different sound)

for /ɔ:/ - fur /ɜ:/ (different sounds)

Articulation Tips:

Position your tongue in the center of your mouth

Don't raise or lower it too much

Keep lips slightly spread and relaxed

Make the sound long and steady

Don't round your lips

Practice Pairs:

bad /æ/ - bird /ɜ:/

bun /ʌ/ - burn /ɜ:/

hot /ɒ/ - hurt /ɜ:/

word /ɜ:/ - ward /ɔ:/

Common Mistakes: Many speakers confuse /ɜ:/ with /ɔ:/ or /ʌ/

fur /ɜ:/ NOT for /ɔ:/

burn /ɜ:/ NOT born /ɔ:/

hurt /ɜ:/ NOT heart /ɑ:/

EVALUATION:

Produce the /ɜ:/ sound correctly and describe how your tongue and lips are positioned

Give fifteen words containing the /ɜ:/ sound using different spelling patterns
Identify which words contain /ɜ:/ sound: (a) bird (b) board (c) turn (d) town (e) word (f) world (g) worm (h) warm
Write five sentences using words with /ɜ:/ sound, underlining the words

Explain the difference in pronunciation between: (a) fur/for (b) burn/born (c) word/ward
(d) hurt/heart

ASSIGNMENT:

List ten words each with "ir," "er," and "ur" spellings that contain the /ɜ:/ sound

List five words with "ear" and "or" (after w) spellings that produce /ɜ:/ sound

Write ten sentences using different words with /ɜ:/ sound

Practice reading aloud: "The nurse in the purple shirt served turkey at the church on Thursday. She heard the birds singing early in the morning and learned a new verse"
Group these words by their vowel sounds - /ɜ:/, /ɔ:/, or /ɑ:/: bird, board, burn, born, cart, curl, first, force, farm, fur, for, father
WEEK 4 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: TENSES – PRESENT CONTINUOUS TENSE

What is the Present Continuous Tense? The Present Continuous Tense (also called Present Progressive Tense) describes actions that are happening now, at this moment, or around this time. It shows ongoing or temporary actions.
Formation of Present Continuous Tense: Structure: Subject + am/is/are + verb-ing Using am/is/are:
I + am (I'm)

He/She/It + is (He's/She's/It's)

You/We/They + are (You're/We're/They're)

Examples:

I am reading

She is cooking

They are playing

We are studying

Rules for Adding -ing:

Most verbs - add -ing directly:

play → playing

read → reading

cook → cooking

sing → singing
talk → talking

Verbs ending in -e - drop the -e and add -ing:

write → writing

make → making

come → coming

dance → dancing

take → taking

have → having

NOTE: Exceptions: be → being, see → seeing, agree → agreeing

One-syllable verbs ending in consonant-vowel-consonant - double the final consonant:

run → running

sit → sitting

stop → stopping

swim → swimming

cut → cutting

put → putting

get → getting

Two-syllable verbs with stress on second syllable - double the final consonant:

begin → beginning

forget → forgetting

prefer → preferring

admit → admitting

Verbs ending in -ie - change -ie to -y and add -ing:
lie → lying

die → dying

tie → tying

Uses of Present Continuous Tense:

Actions happening now (at the moment of speaking):

I am writing a letter now

She is eating lunch at the moment

They are watching TV right now

Look! The baby is crying

Listen! Someone is knocking at the door

Temporary actions (happening around this time but not necessarily right now):

I am reading a novel these days

She is staying with her aunt this week

He is working in Lagos this month

We are studying English this term

Changing or developing situations:

The weather is getting colder

My English is improving

The population is increasing

Technology is advancing rapidly

Planned future arrangements:

I am traveling to Abuja tomorrow
We are having a party next week

She is visiting her grandmother on Sunday

They are starting school on Monday

Annoying habits (with 'always'):

He is always complaining

She is always arriving late

You are always losing your keys

They are always making noise

Negative Form:

Subject + am/is/are + not + verb-ing

Examples:

I am not sleeping

She is not (isn't) coming today

They are not (aren't) playing football

We are not (aren't) watching TV

Question Form:

Am/Is/Are + subject + verb-ing...?

Examples:

Am I disturbing you?

Is she cooking dinner?

Are they playing outside?

Are you listening to me?

Wh- Questions:
What/Where/Why/Who/How + am/is/are + subject + verb-ing?

Examples:

What are you doing?

Where is he going?

Why are they crying?

Who is she calling?

How are you feeling?

Time Expressions Used with Present Continuous:

Now, right now, at the moment, at present

Today, this week, this month, this year, this term

Currently, presently

Look! Listen! (to draw attention)

Still (for ongoing action: He is still sleeping)

Verbs NOT Usually Used in Continuous Tenses: Some verbs describe states, not actions, and are rarely used in continuous form:
Thinking/Feeling:

know, believe, understand, remember, forget, prefer, want, need, like, love, hate, seem

Possession:

have (meaning possess), own, belong, possess

Senses:

see, hear, smell, taste (when describing ability, not action)

Others:
be, exist, contain, consist, depend, matter, mean

Use Simple Present instead:

✓ I know the answer (Correct)

✗ I am knowing the answer (Wrong)

✓ She loves chocolate (Correct)

✗ She is loving chocolate (Wrong) Present Continuous vs Simple Present: Present Continuous:
Temporary: I am living in Lagos (temporarily)

Now: She is eating (at this moment)

Changing: The weather is getting cold

Simple Present:

Permanent: I live in Lagos (permanent home)

Habitual: She eats breakfast at 7 AM (routine)

General truth: The weather is cold in December

Common Mistakes to Avoid:

✗ I am knowing (Correct: I know)

✗ She is haveing a car (Correct: She has a car)

✗ They runing now (Correct: They are running)

✗ Is you coming? (Correct: Are you coming?)

✗ He working now (Correct: He is working now)
EVALUATION:

Define Present Continuous Tense and state four uses with examples

Write the -ing form of these verbs: (a) run (b) write (c) study (d) lie (e) stop (f) begin (g) dance (h) get (i) prefer (j) have
Change to Present Continuous Tense: (a) I write a letter (b) She reads a book (c) They play football (d) He eats rice (e) We go to school
Change to negative: (a) I am sleeping (b) She is coming today (c) They are watching TV
(d) He is working now (e) We are studying

Change to questions: (a) You are listening (b) She is cooking (c) They are playing (d) He is reading (e) It is raining

ASSIGNMENT:

Explain the difference between Present Continuous and Simple Present Tense with five examples each
Write ten sentences about what is happening in your classroom right now using Present Continuous Tense
Fill in the blanks with the correct form: (a) I	(read) a book now (b) She
(not/sleep) at the moment (c)	they	(come) today? (d) What	you
 	(do) now? (e) The baby	(cry)

Correct the errors: (a) He running now (b) I am knowing the answer (c) Is you coming?
They are haveing lunch (e) She am studying

Rewrite this paragraph in Present Continuous Tense: "The students write in their books. The teacher explains the lesson. Some students ask questions. One boy sleeps. The girls laugh."
WEEK 4 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: READING TO UNDERSTAND AUTHOR'S MOOD (ANGER, DOUBT, JOY, ETC.)

CONTENT:

What is Author's Mood? The author's mood (or tone) is the feeling or attitude the writer expresses toward the subject or audience through their choice of words, style, and the way they present information.

Why Understanding Mood is Important:

Helps you understand the author's purpose

Gives deeper meaning to the text

Helps you interpret the message correctly

Enhances your reading comprehension

Allows you to respond appropriately to the text

Common Moods/Tones in Writing:

HAPPY/JOYFUL MOOD:

Words: delightful, wonderful, exciting, cheerful, pleased, thrilled, overjoyed, elated, glad

Tone: Enthusiastic, positive, optimistic, celebratory

Indicators: Exclamation marks, positive adjectives, pleasant descriptions
Example Passage: "What a wonderful day it was! The sun shone brilliantly, birds sang melodiously, and everyone walked with smiles on their faces. The atmosphere was filled with laughter and joy."

SAD/SORROWFUL MOOD:

Words: unfortunate, tragic, devastating, heartbreaking, miserable, gloomy, depressing

Tone: Melancholic, mournful, sympathetic, regretful

Indicators: Negative descriptions, expressions of loss, emotional language

Example Passage: "The village was engulfed in deep sorrow. Tears flowed freely as people mourned the loss of their beloved leader. The once lively streets were now silent and empty, reflecting the heavy hearts of the people."

ANGRY/FURIOUS MOOD:

Words: outrageous, unacceptable, disgusting, infuriating, intolerable, ridiculous, absurd

Tone: Hostile, indignant, bitter, resentful, critical

Indicators: Strong accusations, harsh words, rhetorical questions, exclamation marks

Example Passage: "This is absolutely unacceptable! How dare they treat us with such disrespect! It is outrageous that they continue to ignore our complaints. We will not tolerate this injustice any longer!"

FEARFUL/ANXIOUS MOOD:

Words: terrifying, frightening, worried, nervous, uneasy, concerned, alarmed, dangerous

Tone: Apprehensive, tense, uncertain, cautious

Indicators: Descriptions of danger, uncertainty, tension-building language
Example Passage: "The dark forest loomed ahead. Strange sounds echoed in the distance, making her heart pound rapidly. Every shadow seemed threatening. She hesitated, uncertain whether to proceed or turn back."

DOUBTFUL/SKEPTICAL MOOD:

Words: questionable, uncertain, supposedly, allegedly, claims, dubious, unclear

Tone: Questioning, suspicious, unconvinced, hesitant

Indicators: Question marks, words like "perhaps," "maybe," "supposedly," "if"

Example Passage: "The official claims the project will be completed in six months, but many doubt this promise. Previous projects were delayed for years. Is it realistic to believe this one will be different? Perhaps, but history suggests otherwise."

HOPEFUL/OPTIMISTIC MOOD:

Words: promising, encouraging, positive, bright, potential, opportunity, possible, confident
Tone: Encouraging, positive, forward-looking

Indicators: Future-focused language, positive predictions, encouraging words

Example Passage: "Despite the challenges, there is reason for hope. New opportunities are emerging, and the future looks promising. With determination and hard work, success is certainly achievable. Better days lie ahead."

SERIOUS/FORMAL MOOD:

Words: important, significant, crucial, essential, vital, critical, necessary

Tone: Professional, objective, matter-of-fact, authoritative

Indicators: Formal language, no emotional expressions, factual presentation
Example Passage: "The research indicates significant findings. It is essential that immediate action be taken to address these issues. The data clearly demonstrates the need for policy reform in this area."

HUMOROUS/PLAYFUL MOOD:

Words: funny, amusing, hilarious, comical, entertaining, ridiculous (in a funny way)

Tone: Light-hearted, witty, sarcastic (positively), joking

Indicators: Jokes, exaggerations for effect, funny descriptions, wordplay

Example Passage: "My cat thinks he's the king of the house. He struts around like royalty, demanding meals at precisely 6 AM, and gives me that look of utter disdain if I'm late. Who's the owner here, really?"
How to Identify Author's Mood:

Look at Word Choice (Diction):

Positive words suggest positive mood

Negative words suggest negative mood

Strong words suggest intense emotion

Notice Punctuation:

Exclamation marks (!) = excitement, anger, emphasis

Question marks (?) = doubt, inquiry, uncertainty

Ellipsis (...) = hesitation, uncertainty, trailing thought

Observe Sentence Structure:

Short, abrupt sentences = tension, anger, urgency

Long, flowing sentences = calmness, description, reflection

Incomplete sentences = confusion, hesitation
Consider the Context:

What is the subject matter?

What is the author's purpose?

Who is the intended audience?

Look for Direct Emotional Expressions:

"I am delighted to announce..."

"It is with deep regret that..."

"I strongly disagree with..."

Identify Figurative Language:

Metaphors and similes reveal feelings

Imagery creates mood

Personification adds emotion

Practice Identifying Mood:

Passage 1: "The examination results were excellent. Out of 50 students, 48 passed with flying colors. The principal praised the teachers and students for their outstanding performance. It was indeed a moment of celebration."
Mood: Joyful/Proud

Clue Words: excellent, flying colors, praised, outstanding, celebration

Passage 2: "Corruption continues to plague our society. Officials steal public funds with impunity while citizens suffer. This shameful behavior must stop immediately. We demand accountability and justice!"
Mood: Angry/Indignant
Clue Words: plague, steal, impunity, shameful, demand, exclamation mark

Passage 3: "The storm approached rapidly. Dark clouds gathered overhead, and thunder rumbled ominously. The villagers watched nervously, wondering if their homes could withstand the coming tempest."
Mood: Fearful/Anxious

Clue Words: storm, dark, ominously, nervously, wondering

Multiple Moods: Sometimes an author may express different moods in different parts of a passage. Always consider the overall dominant mood and any shifts in tone.

EVALUATION:

Define author's mood and explain why it is important to identify it when reading

List and explain five different moods an author can express in writing

Read this passage and identify the mood: "The team celebrated wildly as the final whistle blew. They had won! Jumping, cheering, and hugging each other, they could hardly contain their excitement. What a glorious victory!" Explain your answer with evidence from the passage
What clues help readers identify an author's mood? List five

Write two short paragraphs (4 sentences each): one showing an angry mood and another showing a joyful mood

ASSIGNMENT:

Identify the mood in each of these passages and give reasons: (a) "The abandoned house stood in ruins. Broken windows stared like empty eyes. Weeds choked the pathway, and silence hung heavily in the air." (b) "Can we really believe these promises? They have
failed us before. What guarantee do we have that this time will be different?" (c) "Success is within reach! With dedication and perseverance, we can achieve our goals. The future is bright with possibilities."
Write a paragraph (80 words) expressing a sad mood about a lost pet

Write a paragraph (80 words) expressing an excited mood about a school excursion

List ten words associated with: (a) angry mood (b) happy mood

Rewrite this neutral sentence in three different moods: "The government announced a new policy." (a) Angry mood (b) Happy mood (c) Doubtful mood
WEEK 4 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: NARRATIVE ESSAY – THE MOST MEMORABLE DAY OF MY LIFE

What is a Narrative Essay? A narrative essay is a type of writing that tells a story about a personal experience. It has characters (usually including yourself), a setting, a plot with a beginning, middle, and end, and often teaches a lesson or leaves an impression.
Features of a Narrative Essay:

First-Person Point of View:

Uses "I," "me," "my," "we"

Tells your personal experience

Shares your thoughts and feelings

Chronological Order:

Events arranged in time sequence

What happened first, next, then, finally

Clear progression of events

Descriptive Details:

Vivid descriptions of people, places, events

Sensory details (what you saw, heard, felt, smelled, tasted)

Helps readers experience the story

Characters:

You are the main character

Other people involved in the story

Character descriptions and actions

Setting:
Where the story takes place

When it happened

Description of the environment

Plot:

Introduction (background)

Rising action (events building up)

Climax (most important/exciting moment)

Resolution (how it ended)

Conclusion (reflection/lesson learned)

Dialogue (Optional):

Conversations between characters

Makes story more alive and interesting

Shows character personalities

Emotional Connection:

Express your feelings

Show why the day was memorable

Make readers feel your emotions

Structure of "The Most Memorable Day of My Life": INTRODUCTION (Paragraph 1):
Hook: Attention-grabbing opening sentence

Brief introduction to the memorable day

When and where it happened
Why it was special

Example Opening: "I will never forget the 15th of August, 2024. It was the most memorable day of my life, a day filled with joy, excitement, and unexpected surprises."

BODY PARAGRAPHS (2-4 paragraphs): Paragraph 2 - Beginning of the Day:
How the day started

Your expectations or feelings

Setting the scene

Paragraph 3 - Main Events:

What happened (chronologically)

Important details

Actions and reactions

Conversations (if any)

Paragraph 4 - The Climax:

The most important/exciting moment

Peak of the story

Your feelings at that moment

CONCLUSION (Final Paragraph):

How the day ended

Your feelings afterward

Reflection on why it was memorable
Lesson learned or lasting impact

Final thoughts

USEFUL EXPRESSIONS:

Time Connectors:

First, second, third, finally

At first, then, next, after that, later, eventually

In the morning, in the afternoon, by evening

Before, during, after

Suddenly, immediately, meanwhile

Soon, shortly after

Describing Feelings:

I felt excited/nervous/happy/scared/proud

My heart was pounding/racing

I could hardly contain my excitement

I was overwhelmed with joy/fear

I felt as if I was dreaming

Tears of joy filled my eyes

A wave of happiness washed over me

Descriptive Adjectives:

Wonderful, amazing, incredible, unforgettable
Spectacular, magnificent, extraordinary

Thrilling, exciting, nerve-wracking

Surprising, unexpected, shocking

SAMPLE NARRATIVE ESSAY:

THE MOST MEMORABLE DAY OF MY LIFE

The 10th of December, 2024, is a date permanently etched in my memory. It was the day I won first prize in the National Mathematics Competition, and it remains the most memorable day of my life. That day brought me joy, pride, and a sense of achievement I had never experienced before.
The day began like any other Saturday morning. I woke up early, feeling nervous and excited about the competition. My mother prepared my favorite breakfast, jollof rice and chicken, hoping it would calm my nerves. As we drove to the National Stadium where the competition was being held, I kept reviewing formulas in my head. The stadium was packed with students from all over Nigeria, and the atmosphere was electric with anticipation.
The competition started at 10 AM sharp. I sat at my designated seat, my hands trembling slightly as I received the question paper. The first few questions were challenging, but I took a deep breath and focused. Time seemed to fly as I worked through each problem carefully. Some questions were extremely difficult, and I could see other students struggling as well. During the break, I met a girl from Kano who was equally nervous, and we encouraged each other. When the final round began, I gave it my absolute best, double-checking every answer before submitting my paper.
The most thrilling moment came during the award ceremony. As the judges announced the third and second positions, my heart pounded so loudly I thought everyone could hear it. Then I heard my name called for first position! I couldn't believe my ears. I walked to the stage in a daze, my legs feeling weak with excitement. As I received the trophy and a scholarship certificate worth two million naira, I looked into the crowd and saw my parents. My mother was crying with joy,
and my father had the biggest smile on his face. The applause was deafening, and at that moment, I felt like I was on top of the world.
That evening, our house was filled with relatives and friends who came to celebrate. Everyone congratulated me, and I felt so proud and grateful. As I lay in bed that night, holding my trophy, I reflected on the months of hard work and sacrifice that had led to this moment. That memorable day taught me that dedication and perseverance truly pay off. It boosted my confidence and confirmed my dream of becoming a mathematician. Years from now, when I look back on my life, I know December 10th, 2024, will always shine brightly in my memory as the day that changed my life forever.

WRITING TIPS:

DO:

Choose a truly memorable experience

Include specific details and descriptions

Show emotions, don't just tell them

Use dialogue to bring scenes alive

Write in past tense

Maintain chronological order

Make your conclusion meaningful

Proofread for errors

DON'T:

Rush through important moments

Use vague descriptions

Jump around in time confusingly
Forget to explain why it was memorable

Make it too long or too short (aim for 250-350 words for JSS1)

Copy someone else's experience

Exaggerate unrealistically

Possible Topics for Practice:

My first day at secondary school

The day I won a competition

When I received unexpected good news

A special birthday celebration

The day I overcame my fear

Meeting someone important

A family reunion or celebration

An accident or scary experience

The day I learned an important lesson

A surprise that changed everything

EVALUATION:

Define a narrative essay and list five features of narrative writing

Explain the structure of a narrative essay (introduction, body, conclusion) and what each part should contain
List ten time connectors used in narrative writing

Why is it important to include descriptive details and emotions in a narrative essay?
What is the difference between narrative and formal letter writing?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a complete narrative essay (250-300 words) titled "The Most Memorable Day of My Life"
Identify the introduction, body paragraphs, and conclusion in your essay by labeling them

List ten descriptive adjectives you could use to make a narrative essay more interesting

Write the opening paragraph (introduction) for a narrative essay titled "The Day I Learned an Important Lesson"
Explain three things you should avoid when writing a narrative essay
WEEK 4 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: DRAMA – FEATURES (THEME, COSTUMES, AUDIENCE, ETC.); IDENTIFYING FEATURES OF DRAMA IN A TEXT
What is Drama? Drama is a type of literature written to be performed by actors on a stage before an audience. It tells a story through dialogue and action rather than narrative description.
Differences Between Drama and Other Literary Forms:

DramaProsePoetryWritten to be performedWritten to be readWritten to be read/recitedUses dialogue and actionUses narration and descriptionUses verse and imageryDivided into acts and scenesDivided into chapters/paragraphsDivided into lines/stanzasNeeds actors and stageNeeds only a readerCan be read or performed

FEATURES OF DRAMA:

THEME

Definition: The central idea, message, or lesson that the play conveys.

Common Themes in Drama:

Love and relationships

Conflict (internal or external)

Good vs. evil

Revenge and justice

Ambition and power

Betrayal and loyalty

Social issues (corruption, poverty, discrimination)
Family relationships

Coming of age/growing up

Death and mortality

Examples:

In Macbeth, the theme is unchecked ambition leads to destruction

In Romeo and Juliet, the theme is the power of love and the tragedy of family feuds

Multiple themes: Most plays have more than one theme.

PLOT

Definition: The sequence of events in the play; the story being told.

Structure of Plot:

Exposition: Introduction of characters, setting, and background

Rising Action: Complications and conflicts develop

Climax: The turning point; most intense moment

Falling Action: Events after the climax

Resolution/Denouement: Conclusion; conflicts resolved

CHARACTERS

Definition: The people (or sometimes animals/objects) who perform the actions in the play.

Types of Characters:

Protagonist: Main character; hero

Antagonist: Character opposing the protagonist; villain

Supporting characters: Help tell the story
Minor characters: Brief appearances

Character Development:

Revealed through dialogue

Revealed through actions

Revealed through stage directions

Revealed through interaction with others

DIALOGUE

Definition: Conversations between characters; the words they speak.

Purpose of Dialogue:

Advances the plot

Reveals character personalities

Creates conflict

Provides information

Shows relationships

Types:

Dialogue: Conversation between two or more characters

Monologue: Long speech by one character to others

Soliloquy: Character speaks thoughts aloud when alone

Aside: Character speaks to audience; other characters can't hear

STAGE DIRECTIONS
Definition: Instructions in the script telling actors how to move, speak, and behave; describing the setting and atmosphere.
Written in: Italics or brackets [like this]

Types of Information:

Character entrances and exits

Movements and gestures

Tone of voice and emotions

Setting descriptions

Props and lighting

Sound effects

Examples:

[Enter Lady Macbeth]

[He speaks angrily]

[Aside]

[Thunder and lightning]

[Exit]

ACT AND SCENE

Act: Major division of a play (like chapters in a book)

Most plays have 1-5 acts

Each act may contain multiple scenes

Scene: Smaller division within an act

Usually represents a change in time or location
Indicated as Scene 1, Scene 2, etc.

Format:

Act I, Scene 1

Act II, Scene 3

SETTING

Definition: Where and when the play takes place.

Includes:

Time period (past, present, future)

Location (country, city, specific place)

Physical environment (palace, village, school)

Atmosphere/mood

Revealed through:

Stage directions

Dialogue

Props and scenery

CONFLICT

Definition: The struggle or problem that drives the plot.

Types of Conflict:

Man vs. Man: Character against another character

Man vs. Self: Internal struggle; character's own doubts/fears

Man vs. Society: Character against social norms/rules
Man vs. Nature: Character against natural forces

Man vs. Fate/Supernatural: Character against destiny or supernatural forces

COSTUMES

Definition: Clothing worn by actors to represent their characters.

Purpose:

Shows character's social status

Indicates time period

Reveals personality

Distinguishes characters

Shows profession or role

Creates visual impact

Examples:

Royal characters wear crowns and robes

Soldiers wear uniforms

Traditional plays use cultural attire

PROPS (Properties)

Definition: Objects used by actors during the performance.

Examples:

Furniture (chairs, tables)

Hand props (swords, books, cups)

Decorative items (paintings, curtains)
Purpose:

Make scenes realistic

Help tell the story

Reveal character information

AUDIENCE

Definition: The people watching the performance.

Importance:

Drama is incomplete without an audience

Actors perform for the audience

Audience reactions affect performance

Drama aims to entertain, educate, or move the audience

Audience Roles:

Watch and listen

Respond with emotions (laugh, cry, applaud)

Interpret the message

Appreciate the performance

LIGHTING AND SOUND Lighting:
Creates mood and atmosphere

Indicates time of day

Focuses attention
Suggests weather

Sound:

Music and songs

Sound effects (thunder, bells, footsteps)

Background noises

Creates atmosphere

IDENTIFYING DRAMA IN A TEXT:

Textual Indicators:

Character Names Before Dialogue:

JOHN: Good morning, Mary!

MARY: Good morning! How are you?

Stage Directions in Brackets or Italics:

JOHN: [Walking toward her] I've been looking for you.

Act and Scene Divisions:

ACT I SCENE 1
[A classroom in Government Secondary School]

No Narrative Description:

Drama shows, doesn't tell

No "he said" or "she thought"
Everything revealed through dialogue and action

List of Characters (Dramatis Personae):

Usually at the beginning

Names and brief descriptions

SAMPLE DRAMA EXTRACT:

THE WISE DECISION

A One-Act Play

CHARACTERS:

CHIEF ADEBAYO - A wealthy village chief (60s) AMINA - His daughter (16)
TAIWO - A poor but honest young man (20) KUNLE - A rich but dishonest merchant (35)

ACT I

SCENE 1

[Chief Adebayo's compound. Morning. The Chief sits on a stool under a tree. Enter KUNLE with gifts]
KUNLE: [Bowing low] Great Chief! I bring gifts to honor you.

CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Looking at the gifts] You are generous, Kunle. What brings you here? KUNLE: I wish to marry your daughter, Amina. I am wealthy and can give her everything. CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Thoughtfully] Amina must choose for herself.
[Enter TAIWO, empty-handed but confident]

TAIWO: Good morning, Chief. I've come to ask for Amina's hand in marriage. KUNLE: [Laughing mockingly] You? You have nothing!
TAIWO: [Calmly] I have honesty, hard work, and true love for Amina. CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Standing] Let Amina decide. [Calling] Amina! [Enter AMINA]
AMINA: [Looking at both men] Father, I choose Taiwo. Wealth can be lost, but character lasts forever.
CHIEF ADEBAYO: [Smiling] You have chosen wisely, my daughter. [KUNLE exits angrily. TAIWO and AMINA embrace.]
[CURTAIN]

Features Identified:

✓ Character names before dialogue (KUNLE:, CHIEF:, etc.)

✓ Stage directions [in brackets]

✓ Act and Scene divisions

✓ Setting description

✓ List of characters

✓ Theme: True love and character over wealth

✓ Conflict: Taiwo vs. Kunle (Man vs. Man)

✓ Props: Gifts, stool

✓ Plot: Beginning, middle, end
✓ Costumes: Mentioned (Chief's traditional attire)

EVALUATION:

Define drama and state three ways it differs from prose and poetry

What are stage directions and why are they important in drama?

Identify the features of drama in this extract: TEACHER: [Entering the classroom] Good morning, class! STUDENTS: [Standing] Good morning, sir!
TEACHER: [Opening a book] Today we'll study verbs. Who can give an example? BOLA: [Raising her hand] "Run" is a verb, sir.
Explain the difference between dialogue, monologue, and soliloquy

WEEK 6 - SPEECH WORK TOPIC: VOWEL SOUND /Ə/
The Schwa Sound /ə/

The schwa /ə/ is the most common vowel sound in English

It is a weak, unstressed, neutral vowel sound

Pronounced with the mouth in a relaxed position

The tongue is in the center of the mouth

It sounds like a very short "uh" sound

It ONLY occurs in unstressed syllables

Characteristics of the Schwa:

Always found in unstressed syllables

Very short and weak

Neutral position of lips and tongue

Many different letter combinations can produce this sound

Native speakers often use it without realizing

Spelling Patterns for /ə/ Sound:

Letter "a" in unstressed syllables:

about, above, again, ago, alone, along, away, awake

China, drama, sofa, pizza, camera, banana (last 'a')

woman, human, postman, policeman

breakfast, elephant, elegant

Letter "e" in unstressed syllables:
the, open, happen, golden, chicken, garden

become, before, believe, beside

moment, different, children, problem

teacher, mother, father, brother, sister

Letter "i" in unstressed syllables:

animal, definite, family, holiday

pencil, fossil, pupil

terrible, possible, horrible

Letter "o" in unstressed syllables:

today, tomorrow, tonight, police, potato

person, melon, lemon, pardon, lesson

bacon, bottom, problem, freedom

doctor, actor, sailor, visitor

Letter "u" in unstressed syllables:

upon, suppose, support, success

minus, circus, focus, campus

difficult, particular, popular

Letter combinations:

"ous" endings: famous, dangerous, nervous, curious

"ion" endings: nation, action, question, position (first syllable)

"er/or/ar" endings: teacher, doctor, sugar, collar

Important Rules:
The schwa appears in:

Unstressed syllables in multi-syllable words:

a-GAIN (first 'a' is schwa)

ba-NA-na (first and last 'a' are schwa)

CAM-er-a (second and third vowels are schwa)

Function words (when unstressed in sentences):

a, an, the, of, to, from, for, and, but

Example: "a cup of tea" - both 'a' and 'o' in 'of' are schwa

Unstressed grammatical endings:

-er: teacher, worker

-or: actor, doctor

-ar: sugar, collar

NOT Schwa When Stressed: When a syllable is stressed, the vowel is NOT pronounced as schwa:
about /ə-BOUT/ (first 'a' is schwa, second is /au/)

banana /bə-NA-nə/ (middle 'a' is NOT schwa because it's stressed)

Practice Words - Identifying Schwa Position: Two-syllable words:
a-BOUT (first vowel = schwa)

A-go (first vowel = schwa)

to-DAY (first vowel = schwa)
teach-ER (second vowel = schwa)

doc-TOR (second vowel = schwa)

Three-syllable words:

ba-NA-na (1st and 3rd = schwa)

CAM-er-a (2nd and 3rd = schwa)

el-e-PHANT (1st and 2nd = schwa)

choc-o-LATE (2nd = schwa)

to-MOR-row (1st and 3rd = schwa)

Function Words with Schwa: In connected speech, these small words often have schwa:

a: "a book" /ə bʊk/

the: "the man" /ðə mæn/ (before consonants)

to: "to school" /tə skuːl/

of: "cup of tea" /kʌp əv tiː/

for: "for you" /fə juː/

from: "from home" /frəm həʊm/

and: "fish and chips" /fɪʃ ən tʃɪps/

Common Mistakes: Students often pronounce unstressed syllables too clearly:

✗ cam-ER-a (pronouncing 'e' as /e/)

✓ CAM-ər-ə (using schwa)

✗ choc-O-late (pronouncing 'o' as /o/)
✓ CHOC-ə-lət (using schwa)

Why Schwa is Important:

Makes English sound natural and fluent

Distinguishes stressed from unstressed syllables

Essential for understanding native speakers

Improves listening comprehension

Makes your English pronunciation more authentic

Practice Sentences: (Schwa sounds underlined)

A cup of tea and a piece of bread

The teacher asked a question about the lesson

My mother is a doctor at the hospital

Children love chocolate and banana

The police arrived in the afternoon

Articulation Tips:

Relax your mouth completely

Don't make any special shape with your lips

Keep the sound very short and weak

Don't stress the syllable

Think of it as a very lazy vowel sound

EVALUATION:

What is the schwa sound /ə/ and where does it occur in words?

Identify the schwa sound(s) in these words: (a) banana (b) teacher (c) about (d) elephant
camera

Why is the schwa sound important in English pronunciation?

Write ten words containing the schwa sound and mark where it appears

Explain why these vowels are NOT schwa sounds: (a) the 'i' in "machine" (b) the 'a' in "father" (c) the middle 'a' in "banana"

ASSIGNMENT:

List twenty words containing the schwa sound and indicate which syllable(s) contain schwa
Practice pronouncing these words with correct schwa placement: about, again, today, police, problem, family, animal, chocolate, different, banana
Identify all the schwa sounds in this sentence: "The teacher gave a lesson about the history of Africa"
Explain the difference between stressed and unstressed syllables using five examples

Write five sentences using at least three words with schwa sounds in each sentence, underlining the words with schwa
WEEK 6 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: TENSES – PAST CONTINUOUS TENSE

What is the Past Continuous Tense? The Past Continuous Tense (also called Past Progressive Tense) describes actions that were in progress at a specific time in the past, or actions that were happening when another action interrupted them.
Formation of Past Continuous Tense:

Structure: Subject + was/were + verb-ing Using was/were:
I/He/She/It + was

You/We/They + were

Examples:

I was reading

She was cooking

They were playing

We were studying

Forming the -ing Verb: (Same rules as Present Continuous)

Most verbs - add -ing: play → playing, read → reading

Verbs ending in -e - drop -e, add -ing: write → writing, make → making

One-syllable CVC verbs - double final consonant: run → running, sit → sitting

Verbs ending in -ie - change to -y: lie → lying, die → dying

Uses of Past Continuous Tense:

Actions in progress at a specific time in the past:
I was sleeping at 10 PM last night

They were studying at 7 AM yesterday

She was cooking at 5 o'clock

What were you doing at noon?

When one action interrupted another (with Simple Past):

Structure: Past Continuous + when + Simple Past

OR: Simple Past + while + Past Continuous

Examples:

I was reading when the phone rang

She was cooking when her friend arrived

They were playing football when it started to rain

While I was sleeping, someone knocked on the door

He was driving when he saw the accident

Pattern:

Longer action (background) = Past Continuous

Shorter action (interruption) = Simple Past

Two or more simultaneous actions in the past:

Structure: Past Continuous + while + Past Continuous

Examples:

I was reading while she was cooking

They were playing while we were studying

He was watching TV while his sister was doing homework

The children were singing while their teacher was playing the piano
Describing the scene/atmosphere in a story:

The sun was shining brightly

Birds were singing in the trees

People were walking in the park

A gentle breeze was blowing

Repeated actions in the past (showing annoyance with 'always'):

He was always complaining about something

She was always arriving late

They were always fighting

Negative Form:

Subject + was/were + not + verb-ing

Examples:

I was not (wasn't) sleeping

She was not (wasn't) cooking

They were not (weren't) playing

We were not (weren't) studying

Question Form:

Was/Were + subject + verb-ing...?

Examples:

Was I disturbing you?

Was she cooking?

Were they playing?
Were you studying?

Wh- Questions:

What/Where/Why/Who/How + was/were + subject + verb-ing?

Examples:

What were you doing?

Where was he going?

Why were they crying?

Who was she calling?

How were you feeling?

Time Expressions Used with Past Continuous:

At that time, at that moment

At 5 o'clock yesterday, at noon

All day yesterday, all morning, all night

When, while, as

This time last week/month/year

Then, at the time

Past Continuous vs Simple Past:

Simple Past:

Completed action: I read a book (finished)

Short action: The phone rang

Sequence: I woke up, brushed my teeth, and left

Past Continuous:
Action in progress: I was reading a book (not finished, was doing)

Longer background action: I was reading when the phone rang

Simultaneous actions: I was reading while she was cooking

Common Combinations:

Past Continuous + when + Simple Past:

I was walking when I met John

They were eating when the power went off

She was studying when her mother called

While + Past Continuous + Simple Past:

While I was watching TV, the phone rang

While they were playing, it started to rain

While + Past Continuous + Past Continuous:

While I was cooking, she was cleaning

While he was reading, they were playing

Verbs NOT Usually Used in Continuous Form: (Same as Present Continuous)

State verbs: know, believe, understand, like, love, hate, want, need

Possession verbs: have (possess), own, belong

Sense verbs: see, hear, smell, taste (ability)
Use Simple Past instead:

✓ I knew the answer (Correct)

✗ I was knowing the answer (Wrong)

✓ She loved him (Correct)

✗ She was loving him (Wrong)

Common Mistakes to Avoid:

✗ I was went home (Correct: I was going home)

✗ She was cook (Correct: She was cooking)

✗ They was playing (Correct: They were playing)

✗ While I studied, he came (Correct: While I was studying, he came)

✗ I was knowing him (Correct: I knew him)

EVALUATION:

Define Past Continuous Tense and state four uses with examples

Write the Past Continuous form of these verbs: (a) I (read) (b) She (cook) (c) They (play)
(d) He (write) (e) We (study)

Combine these sentences using 'when': (a) I was sleeping. The phone rang. (b) She was cooking. Her friend arrived. (c) They were playing. It rained.
Change to negative: (a) I was working (b) She was singing (c) They were dancing (d) He was eating (e) We were watching TV
Change to questions: (a) You were listening (b) She was reading (c) They were playing
(d) He was sleeping (e) It was raining

ASSIGNMENT:

Explain the difference between Simple Past and Past Continuous Tense with five examples each
Write ten sentences using Past Continuous Tense to describe what was happening in your house yesterday evening
Combine these pairs using 'when' or 'while': (a) I (walk) to school. I (see) my teacher. (b) She (read). Her sister (watch) TV. (c) They (play) football. It (start) raining. (d) He (drive). He (hear) a loud noise. (e) We (have) dinner. The lights (go) off.
Fill in the blanks with correct forms: (a) At 8 PM yesterday, I		(watch) TV. (b) While she	(cook), I	(clean) the house. (c) What	you	(do) when I called? (d) They	(not/sleep) at midnight. (e)	it	(rain) when you left?
Correct the errors: (a) I was went to school (b) She was cook dinner (c) They was playing
(d) While I study, he came (e) Was you sleeping?
WEEK 6 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: READING BETWEEN THE LINES TO DETECT AUTHOR'S ATTITUDE;
VOCABULARY – CHILD LABOUR/TRAFFICKING

PART A: READING BETWEEN THE LINES TO DETECT AUTHOR'S ATTITUDE

What is Author's Attitude? Author's attitude is the writer's feelings, opinions, or stance toward the subject matter or audience. It goes beyond mood (emotional tone) to reveal the author's position—whether they support, oppose, question, or remain neutral about a topic.
Why Detect Author's Attitude?

Helps identify bias or objectivity

Reveals the author's purpose

Helps evaluate credibility

Enables critical reading

Shows whether information is factual or opinion-based

Common Author Attitudes:

SUPPORTIVE/FAVORABLE:

Author approves or agrees with the subject

Uses positive language

Highlights benefits and advantages

Minimizes or ignores drawbacks

Word Clues: beneficial, excellent, commendable, admirable, impressive, effective, successful, valuable, worthwhile
Example: "The new education policy is commendable. It addresses key challenges and provides practical solutions that will benefit millions of students. The government deserves praise for this progressive step."
Attitude: Supportive/Positive toward the policy

CRITICAL/OPPOSING:

Author disagrees or disapproves

Points out flaws and problems

Uses negative language

Questions effectiveness

Word Clues: problematic, flawed, inadequate, disappointing, questionable, unfortunate, concerning, alarming, unacceptable
Example: "The new education policy is deeply flawed. It fails to address fundamental issues and creates more problems than it solves. The government's approach is disappointing and shows lack of proper consultation."
Attitude: Critical/Negative toward the policy

NEUTRAL/OBJECTIVE:

Author presents information without taking sides

Balanced presentation of facts

No obvious emotional language

Shows multiple perspectives

Word Clues: however, on the other hand, according to, statistics show, research indicates, reports suggest
Example: "The new education policy includes several provisions. According to officials, it aims to improve quality. Critics, however, question its implementation strategy. Time will reveal its effectiveness."
Attitude: Neutral/Objective

SARCASTIC/MOCKING:

Author ridicules or makes fun of the subject

Says the opposite of what is meant

Uses irony and exaggeration

Dismissive tone

Word Clues: supposedly, allegedly, so-called, claims, apparently, of course (used ironically)

Example: "Of course, our 'efficient' government completed the project on time—only five years late! The 'quality' work speaks for itself: the bridge collapsed within a week. Truly impressive!"
Attitude: Sarcastic/Mocking

CONCERNED/WORRIED:

Author expresses worry about consequences

Highlights potential dangers

Urges caution or action

Shows care about the issue

Word Clues: unfortunately, worrying, concerning, alarming, dangerous, risky, threatening, urgent
Example: "The alarming increase in deforestation is deeply concerning. If urgent action is not taken, the consequences will be devastating for future generations. We must act now before it's too late."
Attitude: Concerned/Worried
SKEPTICAL/DOUBTFUL:

Author questions truth or validity

Expresses uncertainty

Challenges claims

Demands evidence

Word Clues: allegedly, supposedly, claims, questionable, unclear, doubtful, unproven, debatable

Example: "The company claims their product is '100% natural,' but this assertion is questionable. No independent testing has verified these claims. Consumers should be skeptical of such unproven marketing statements."
Attitude: Skeptical/Doubtful

How to Detect Author's Attitude:

Analyze Word Choice (Diction):

Positive vs. negative words

Strong vs. mild words

Objective vs. subjective language

Look for Qualifying Words:

"Supposedly," "allegedly" = skepticism

"Obviously," "clearly" = strong conviction

"Perhaps," "possibly" = uncertainty

Examine What's Emphasized:

What points get most attention?

What is minimized or ignored?
What examples are chosen?

Note Facts vs. Opinions:

Objective: presents facts without judgment

Subjective: includes personal views

Consider Omissions:

What information is left out?

Which perspectives are ignored?

What questions remain unanswered?

Identify Rhetorical Devices:

Questions (suggest doubt or engagement)

Repetition (emphasizes importance)

Comparisons (show preference)

Practice Passage:

"Child labour continues to rob millions of children of their childhood, education, and future. While some argue it helps poor families survive, this view ignores the severe physical and psychological harm inflicted on innocent children. How can any society justify exploiting its most vulnerable members? The practice must be condemned unequivocally and eliminated immediately."
Analysis:

Attitude: Strongly opposed/Critical

Clues:

"rob," "harm inflicted," "exploiting" (negative words)

"must be condemned," "eliminated immediately" (strong stance)
Rhetorical question shows disapproval

Dismisses opposing view ("this view ignores")

Emotional appeal ("innocent children")

PART B: VOCABULARY – CHILD LABOUR/TRAFFICKING

Definitions:

Child Labour: The exploitation of children through any form of work that deprives them of childhood, education, and dignity, and is harmful to their physical and mental development.
Child Trafficking: The recruitment, transportation, transfer, or harboring of children for exploitation, including forced labor, sexual exploitation, or illegal activities.

KEY VOCABULARY:

Related to Child Labour:

General Terms:

Exploitation: Unfair treatment for someone's benefit

Minor: Person under 18 years old

Hazardous work: Dangerous jobs harmful to children

Forced labour: Work done against one's will

Bonded labour: Work to repay a debt

Domestic servitude: Working as house help in harsh conditions

Street vending: Selling goods on streets

Agriculture work: Farming, fishing, plantation work

Manufacturing: Factory work, production
Child labourer: Child who works inappropriately

Effects on Children:

Deprivation: Being denied basic needs/rights

Abuse: Physical, emotional, or sexual mistreatment

Malnutrition: Poor nutrition, hunger

Illiteracy: Inability to read or write

Trauma: Deep psychological injury

Exploitation: Being used unfairly for others' gain

Vulnerability: Being easily hurt or influenced

Stunted growth: Impaired physical development

Rights Violations:

Right to education: Learning opportunity denied

Right to play: Childhood activities denied

Right to protection: Safety not provided

Right to health: Medical care denied

Child rights: Legal entitlements of children

Violation: Breaking of rules/rights

Related to Child Trafficking:

Process Terms:

Recruitment: Finding and engaging victims

Transportation: Moving victims from one place to another

Harboring: Hiding or sheltering victims
Trafficker: Person who trafficks children

Victim: Person trafficked

Syndicate: Criminal organization

Network: Connected group of traffickers

Types of Exploitation:

Sexual exploitation: Forcing into prostitution/pornography

Forced begging: Making children beg for money

Organ trafficking: Stealing body organs

Drug trafficking: Using children to transport drugs

Armed conflict: Forcing children to fight

Slavery: Ownership and control of a person

Servitude: Being a servant/slave

Prevention & Protection:

Awareness: Knowledge about the problem

Prevention: Stopping before it happens

Rescue: Saving victims

Rehabilitation: Helping victims recover

Prosecution: Legal action against traffickers

Legislation: Laws against trafficking

NGO (Non-Governmental Organization): Groups helping victims

UNICEF: UN Children's Fund
Helpline: Phone number for reporting/getting help

Legal Terms:

Illegal: Against the law

Criminal: Person who breaks the law

Prosecution: Taking legal action

Penalty: Punishment for crime

Imprisonment: Jail sentence

Fine: Money paid as punishment

Conviction: Finding guilty in court

Warning Signs of Child Trafficking:

Child living with non-relatives

Working excessive hours

Signs of abuse or fear

Not attending school

Restricted movement

Malnourished or poorly clothed

No possession of documents

Organizations Fighting Child Labour/Trafficking:

UNICEF

ILO (International Labour Organization)
Save the Children

NAPTIP (National Agency for the Prohibition of Trafficking in Persons) - Nigeria

Anti-Slavery International

Child Rights International Network

Important Statistics/Facts:

Minimum working age in most countries: 14-16 years

Millions of children worldwide affected

More prevalent in developing countries

Girls and boys both affected

Often linked to poverty

EVALUATION:

Explain what "author's attitude" means and why it's important to detect it when reading

Read this passage and identify the author's attitude with evidence: "Technology has revolutionized education in remarkable ways. Students now have access to unlimited information, interactive learning tools, and global collaboration opportunities. These advances deserve celebration."
Define child labour and child trafficking, stating three differences between them

List five harmful effects of child labour on children

Explain five vocabulary terms related to child trafficking

ASSIGNMENT:

Read this passage and answer the questions: "Some business owners claim child labour is necessary for survival. What a convenient excuse for exploitation! These 'employers'
conveniently ignore international laws and children's rights. Meanwhile, innocent children suffer daily, deprived of education and childhood. The situation is unacceptable." Questions: (a) What is the author's attitude? (b) Identify three words/phrases that reveal this attitude (c) What rhetorical device does the author use? (d) Is the author objective or subjective? (e) Does the author support or oppose child labour?
Write a paragraph (100 words) expressing a critical attitude toward child trafficking

Match these terms with their meanings: (a) Exploitation (b) Rehabilitation (c) Prosecution (d) Trafficking (e) Vulnerability

WEEK 6 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY – ROAD TRANSPORTATION IS SAFER THAN AIR
What is an Argumentative Essay? An argumentative essay is a type of writing where you take a position on a topic and support it with reasons, evidence, and examples to convince readers that your viewpoint is correct or valid. You also address opposing views and explain why your position is better.
Characteristics of Argumentative Essay:

Clear Position/Thesis:

States your stand clearly

Usually in the introduction

Tells readers what you believe

Logical Arguments:

Presents reasons supporting your position

Uses facts, statistics, examples

Each paragraph develops one main point

Evidence and Examples:

Concrete proof supporting claims

Real-life examples

Statistics, expert opinions, research

Personal experiences (if relevant)

Counter-Arguments:

Acknowledges opposing views

Shows you've considered other perspectives
Refutes (argues against) them with evidence

Persuasive Language:

Convincing tone

Strong vocabulary

Logical connectors

Confident but respectful

Formal Tone:

Objective language

Third person (mostly)

Professional vocabulary

No slang or contractions Structure of Argumentative Essay: INTRODUCTION (Paragraph 1):
Hook: Attention-grabbing opening

Background: Brief context about the topic

Thesis Statement: Your clear position

Preview: Mention main arguments briefly BODY PARAGRAPHS (3-4 paragraphs): Paragraph 2 - First Argument:
Topic sentence stating your first point

Explanation and elaboration

Evidence/examples supporting it

Concluding sentence linking to thesis
Paragraph 3 - Second Argument:

Topic sentence stating your second point

Explanation and elaboration

Evidence/examples supporting it

Concluding sentence

Paragraph 4 - Third Argument:

Topic sentence stating your third point

Explanation and elaboration

Evidence/examples supporting it

Concluding sentence

Paragraph 5 - Counter-Argument & Refutation:

Acknowledge opposing view

Present it fairly

Refute it with evidence

Show why your position is stronger

CONCLUSION (Final Paragraph):

Restate thesis (in different words)

Summarize main arguments

Final thought or call to action

Strong closing statement

USEFUL LANGUAGE FOR ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAYS:

Stating Your Position:
I strongly believe that...

It is evident that...

There is no doubt that...

This essay argues that...

The evidence clearly shows...

Introducing Arguments:

First/Firstly, To begin with, First of all

Second/Secondly, Furthermore, Moreover, Additionally

Third/Thirdly, Finally, Lastly

Another reason is...

Most importantly...

Providing Evidence:

For example, For instance

According to research/statistics...

Studies show that...

Evidence suggests...

This is illustrated by...

Consider the case of...

Introducing Counter-Arguments:

Some people argue/believe that...

Opponents claim that...

Critics suggest that...

It may be argued that...
On the other hand...

Admittedly...

While it is true that...

Refuting Counter-Arguments:

However, this view fails to consider...

Nevertheless, this argument is flawed because...

Despite this claim, evidence shows...

This overlooks the fact that...

While this may be partially true, ...

On the contrary...

Concluding:

In conclusion, To conclude, To sum up

Therefore, Thus, Hence

It is clear that...

Based on the evidence presented...

For these reasons...

SAMPLE ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY:

ROAD TRANSPORTATION IS SAFER THAN AIR TRANSPORTATION

When planning a journey, the question of safety often arises. While many consider air travel the safest mode of transportation, a closer examination reveals that road transportation offers superior safety advantages. Road travel provides better control, accessibility to emergency services, and survivability in accidents. This essay argues that road transportation is safer than air transportation based on practical, statistical, and logical considerations.
Firstly, road transportation offers greater personal control and immediate response to danger. When traveling by road, drivers can stop instantly if they notice a problem, change routes to avoid hazards, or pull over during emergencies. If a vehicle develops mechanical issues, the driver can safely park and seek help. In contrast, airplane passengers are entirely dependent on pilots and have no control over the aircraft. If problems occur mid-flight, there is limited room for maneuvering or emergency stops. For example, if a car's brakes fail, the driver can use the handbrake and gradually reduce speed. However, if an aircraft experiences engine failure at high altitude, the consequences are far more severe with limited options for immediate intervention.
Secondly, road accidents are more survivable than air crashes. Statistics show that passengers involved in road accidents have a much higher survival rate compared to aviation accidents. Most road accidents occur at relatively low speeds within cities, allowing passengers to survive with injuries that can be treated. Modern cars are equipped with safety features such as airbags, seatbelts, and crumple zones designed to protect occupants during collisions. When road accidents happen, emergency services can quickly reach the scene to provide medical assistance. Conversely, airplane crashes typically occur from great heights at high speeds, leaving little chance of survival. Even with safety protocols, the impact of an aircraft hitting the ground from thousands of feet is usually catastrophic. Furthermore, rescue operations for plane crashes are complicated by remote locations, fire hazards, and the difficulty of accessing survivors quickly.
Moreover, road transportation provides better access to emergency assistance and medical care. If someone falls ill while traveling by road, the vehicle can immediately divert to the nearest hospital. Emergency vehicles can reach road accident victims within minutes in most areas, significantly improving survival chances. Ambulances, fire services, and police can respond quickly to road incidents. On the other hand, if a medical emergency occurs during a flight, passengers must wait until the plane lands, which could take hours depending on the flight duration and availability of nearby airports. The limited medical equipment on board aircraft cannot match the resources available at hospitals. In remote areas where planes may crash, rescue teams may take hours or even days to reach survivors, if any.
Admittedly, some argue that air travel has fewer accidents per mile traveled and that modern aircraft technology makes flying extremely safe. Statistics show that flying has a lower accident rate compared to driving. However, this argument fails to consider the severity and survivability
of accidents. While airplane accidents may be rarer, they are almost always fatal when they occur. A person may be involved in several minor road accidents during their lifetime and survive them all, whereas a single plane crash is likely to be fatal. Furthermore, road safety continues to improve with better vehicle technology, improved road infrastructure, and stricter traffic laws. The mere fact that airplane accidents are less frequent does not make them safer when we consider the devastating outcomes and lack of survival chances.
In conclusion, road transportation is safer than air transportation when considering control, survivability, and access to emergency services. The ability to stop immediately, higher survival rates in accidents, and quick access to medical help make road travel the safer option. While air travel may have statistical advantages in accident frequency, the catastrophic nature of plane crashes and limited survival possibilities cannot be ignored. For these reasons, road transportation remains the safer choice for travelers who prioritize survival and immediate access to assistance in emergencies. When safety is measured not just by accident frequency but by survivability and control, the evidence clearly favors road transportation.

KEY POINTS FOR THIS TOPIC:

Arguments Supporting Road Transportation:

Greater control (can stop, change routes, respond to danger)

Higher survival rates in accidents

Better access to emergency services

Modern safety features (airbags, seatbelts)

Lower-speed collisions (more survivable)

Immediate medical assistance available

Can avoid dangerous situations

Ability to escape quickly from vehicles
Arguments Supporting Air Transportation (Counter-arguments):

Fewer accidents per mile traveled

Highly trained pilots

Strict safety regulations

Advanced technology

Regular maintenance checks

Statistics show it's statistically safer

How to Refute Air Transportation Arguments:

While accidents are rare, they're almost always fatal

Technology failures still occur with devastating effects

No control for passengers in emergencies

Difficult rescue operations

High-altitude crashes are non-survivable

Statistics don't account for severity

WRITING TIPS:

DO:

State your position clearly in the introduction

Use strong, convincing evidence

Address counter-arguments fairly

Use formal language

Provide specific examples
Connect paragraphs logically

Conclude powerfully

Proofread carefully

DON'T:

Use emotional language without evidence

Ignore opposing viewpoints

Make unsupported claims

Use informal language or slang

Write in first person excessively

Make personal attacks

Contradict yourself

Leave arguments unsupported

Other Possible Topics for Practice:

School uniforms should be compulsory

Social media does more harm than good

Homework should be abolished

Sports are more important than academics

Television is harmful to children

Boarding schools are better than day schools

EVALUATION:
Define argumentative essay and state five characteristics

Explain the structure of an argumentative essay (introduction, body, conclusion) and what each part should contain
What is a counter-argument and why is it important in argumentative writing?

List five useful phrases for: (a) stating your position (b) introducing counter-arguments

What is the difference between argumentative and narrative essays?

WEEK 6 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH TOPIC: RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXT
What is a Prose Text? Prose is writing that is structured in sentences and paragraphs, following natural speech patterns. Unlike poetry (which uses verses) or drama (which uses dialogue and stage directions), prose tells stories through continuous narrative.
Types of Prose:

Fiction (Imaginative/Made-up stories):

Novels (long stories)

Novellas (medium-length stories)

Short stories (brief stories)

Non-Fiction (True/Factual):

Biographies

Autobiographies

Essays

Articles

For JSS1, recommended prose texts are usually:

Short novels or novellas

Collection of short stories

Young adult fiction

African literature

Adventure/moral stories
COMMON RECOMMENDED PROSE TEXTS FOR JSS1:

"The Drummer Boy" by Cyprian Ekwensi

"The Passport of Mallam Ilia" by Cyprian Ekwensi

"Tales from the Forest" by Eno Okoko

"Chike and the River" by Chinua Achebe

"The Last Days at Forcados High School" by A.H. Mohammed

"Mr. Johnson" by Joyce Cary

African Folktales Collection

"Beautiful Feathers" by Cyprian Ekwensi

(Note: The specific text varies by school and state. Students should use the text assigned by their school.)

HOW TO STUDY A PROSE TEXT:

UNDERSTAND THE STORY: Plot:
What happens in the story?

Beginning (exposition)

Middle (rising action and climax)

End (resolution)

Main events in chronological order

Setting:

Where does the story take place? (location)

When does it happen? (time period)
What is the physical and social environment?

KNOW THE CHARACTERS:

Main Characters:

Who is the protagonist (hero)?

Who is the antagonist (villain/opponent)?

What are their personalities?

What motivates them?

How do they change?

Character Types:

Round characters: Complex, realistic, develop

Flat characters: Simple, one-dimensional, don't change

Dynamic characters: Change throughout the story

Static characters: Remain the same

Character Analysis Questions:

Physical appearance?

Personality traits?

Relationships with others?

Role in the story?

What they want/desire?

Strengths and weaknesses?

IDENTIFY THEMES:
Theme: Central message or lesson

Common Themes in Prose:

Good vs. evil

Growing up/coming of age

Courage and bravery

Honesty and integrity

Friendship and loyalty

Family relationships

Overcoming challenges

Justice and injustice

Love and sacrifice

Tradition vs. modernity

Corruption

Ambition

Questions to Find Theme:

What lesson does the story teach?

What is the author trying to say about life?

What universal truths are explored?

What message is conveyed?

UNDERSTAND THE CONFLICT:

Types of Conflict:

Man vs. Man: Character against another character
Man vs. Self: Internal struggle within character

Man vs. Society: Character against social norms/rules

Man vs. Nature: Character against natural forces

Man vs. Fate/Supernatural: Character against destiny or supernatural forces

ANALYZE STYLE:

Point of View:

First Person: "I" narrator (character tells story)

Third Person Limited: Narrator knows one character's thoughts

Third Person Omniscient: Narrator knows everything

Second Person: "You" (rare)

Language:

Simple or complex?

Descriptive or straightforward?

Use of dialogue?

Figurative language?

Tone:

Serious, humorous, sad, hopeful?

Author's attitude toward subject?

EXAMINE LITERARY DEVICES:

Common devices in prose:

Foreshadowing: Hints about future events
Flashback: Scene from the past

Symbolism: Objects representing ideas

Irony: Opposite of what's expected

Imagery: Descriptive language appealing to senses

Metaphor/Simile: Comparisons

HOW TO WRITE ABOUT YOUR PROSE TEXT:

Character Question Example: "Describe the character of [name] in the novel."

Answer Structure:

Introduction: Who is the character?

Physical description

Personality traits with examples

Role in the story

Relationships with others

Character development/change

Conclusion: Significance

Theme Question Example: "What is the main theme of the novel?"

Answer Structure:

State the theme clearly

Explain what it means

Give examples from the story

Show how different events relate to theme

Explain the lesson/message
Conclusion: Why it's important

Plot Question Example: "Narrate the story/Give the plot summary."

Answer Structure:

Introduction: Title, author, brief overview

Beginning: Introduce characters, setting, initial situation

Middle: Main events, complications, conflicts

Climax: Turning point, most exciting moment

End: Resolution, how conflicts are resolved

Conclusion: Final situation

Relationship Question Example: "Discuss the relationship between [Character A] and [Character B]."
Answer Structure:

Introduction: Who are they?

How they meet/know each other

Nature of relationship (friends, enemies, family)

How they interact

Conflicts or harmony between them

How relationship develops/changes

Conclusion: Significance of relationship

STUDY TIPS:

Reading the Text:

Read the entire text at least twice
Take notes while reading

Mark important passages

Note unfamiliar words and find meanings

Pay attention to chapter titles

Identify turning points

Character Study:

Create character charts

Note physical descriptions

Track character actions

Quote significant dialogues

Trace character development

Plot Study:

Create plot timeline

Identify cause-and-effect relationships

Note chapter summaries

Identify key events

Understand story structure

Theme Study:

Identify recurring ideas

Note moral lessons

Connect events to themes

Understand author's message

Relate themes to real life
Exam Preparation:

Read sample questions

Practice essay answers

Memorize key quotes

Understand major events

Know character names and spellings

Review chapter by chapter

Discuss with classmates

Create study guides

COMMON EXAM QUESTIONS ON PROSE:

Plot/Summary Questions:

Give an account of...

Narrate the story of...

What happened when...?

Describe the events leading to...

Character Questions:

Describe the character of...

Compare and contrast [two characters]

Discuss the role of...

How does [character] change?

Theme Questions:

What is the main theme?
Discuss the theme of...

What lessons are taught?

What message does the author convey?

Relationship Questions:

Discuss the relationship between...

How do [characters] interact?

Describe the friendship/conflict between...

Literary Device Questions:

Identify examples of...

Discuss the author's use of...

How does the author create suspense?

Personal Response Questions:

What did you learn from...?

Which character do you admire? Why?

What is your favorite part?

How would you have handled...?

EVALUATION:

Define prose and state three differences between prose, poetry, and drama

Explain what theme means and give three examples of common themes in literature

What are the five types of conflict? Give an example of each

ASSIGNMENT:
