WEEK 8 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: VOWEL CONTRASTS: /ɒ/ VS /Ɔ:/ (HOT/PORT); /ɑ:/ VS /æ/ (BAG/PART)

CONTRAST 1: /ɒ/ vs /ɔ:/ Short Vowel /ɒ/ (as in "hot"):
Short, quick sound

Lips slightly rounded

Jaw moderately open

Examples: hot, pot, got, box, top, dog, shop, clock, doctor, coffee

Long Vowel /ɔ:/ (as in "port"):

Long, prolonged sound

Lips more rounded and protruded

Jaw less open

Examples: port, ought, thought, bought, caught, ball, call, tall, warn, morning

Practice Pairs:

cot /ɒ/ - caught /ɔ:/

not /ɒ/ - nought /ɔ:/

spot /ɒ/ - sport /ɔ:/

pot /ɒ/ - port /ɔ:/

shot /ɒ/ - short /ɔ:/

Key Difference: /ɒ/ is SHORT with relaxed lips; /ɔ:/ is LONG with rounded, protruded lips.

CONTRAST 2: /ɑ:/ vs /æ/ Long Vowel /ɑ:/ (as in "part"):
Long, open sound

Mouth wide open

Tongue low and flat

Examples: part, car, far, hard, start, father, class, grass, past, heart

Short Vowel /æ/ (as in "bag"):

Short sound

Mouth moderately open

Tongue higher than /ɑ:/

Examples: bag, cat, hat, man, hand, bad, sad, apple, happy, family

Practice Pairs:

cap /æ/ - carp /ɑ:/

hat /æ/ - heart /ɑ:/

bad /æ/ - bard /ɑ:/

pat /æ/ - part /ɑ:/

mad /æ/ - marred /ɑ:/

Key Difference: /ɑ:/ is LONG with wide-open mouth; /æ/ is SHORT with less mouth opening.

Common Mistakes:

Confusing "hot" /ɒ/ with "heart" /ɑ:/

Pronouncing "bad" /æ/ like "bard" /ɑ:/

Using /ɒ/ when /ɔ:/ is needed: "caught" not "cot"

EVALUATION:

Explain the difference between /ɒ/ and /ɔ:/ sounds with three examples each
Pronounce and identify the correct vowel sound: (a) hot (b) port (c) shot (d) caught (e) spot
Explain the difference between /ɑ:/ and /æ/ sounds with three examples each

Write five word pairs showing /ɑ:/ vs /æ/ contrast

Correct the pronunciation: A student says "I bought a hot dog" but pronounces both words with /ɒ/. Which word should use /ɔ:/?

ASSIGNMENT:

List ten words each for: (a) /ɒ/ sound (b) /ɔ:/ sound (c) /ɑ:/ sound (d) /æ/ sound

Write five sentences using word pairs that contrast /ɒ/ and /ɔ:/

Write five sentences using word pairs that contrast /ɑ:/ and /æ/

Practice reading aloud: "The hot pot was caught in the short spot. My father had a bad heart, so he parked his car far from the grass."
Create a chart showing the four vowel sounds with their characteristics (mouth position, length, lip shape)
WEEK 8 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: MAKING SENTENCES WITH SIMPLE PRESENT & SIMPLE PAST TENSES REVIEW: SIMPLE PRESENT TENSE
Form: Subject + base verb (+ s/es for he/she/it)

Uses:

Habits/Routines: I wake up at 6 AM

General truths: The sun rises in the east

Permanent situations: She lives in Lagos

Time Markers: always, usually, often, sometimes, every day/week

REVIEW: SIMPLE PAST TENSE

Form: Subject + past verb (regular: -ed; irregular: special forms)

Uses:

Completed past actions: I visited Abuja yesterday

Past habits: He played football every Saturday (when young)

Series of past actions: She woke up, ate, and left

Time Markers: yesterday, last week/month/year, ago, in 2020

MAKING CORRECT SENTENCES:

Simple Present Examples:

My sister teaches Mathematics

We go to church every Sunday

The train leaves at 8 PM
He doesn't eat meat

Do you speak French?

Simple Past Examples:

My sister taught Mathematics last year

We went to church last Sunday

The train left at 8 PM yesterday

He didn't eat meat

Did you speak to him?

COMMON ERRORS TO AVOID:

Error 1 - Mixing Tenses:

✗ Yesterday, I go to school (mixing time marker with wrong tense)

✓ Yesterday, I went to school

Error 2 - Wrong Verb Forms:

✗ She don't go (wrong auxiliary with third person)

✓ She doesn't go

✗ He goed (wrong past form)

✓ He went

Error 3 - Forgetting -s/-es in Present:

✗ He go to school

✓ He goes to school

Error 4 - Using Past Form in Negatives/Questions:
✗ Did you went? / He didn't came

✓ Did you go? / He didn't come

SENTENCE PATTERNS:

Affirmative:

Present: Subject + verb(s/es)

Past: Subject + past verb

Negative:

Present: Subject + don't/doesn't + base verb

Past: Subject + didn't + base verb

Interrogative:

Present: Do/Does + subject + base verb?

Past: Did + subject + base verb?

PRACTICE TRANSFORMATIONS:

Present to Past:

I eat rice → I ate rice yesterday

She plays tennis → She played tennis last week

They are happy → They were happy

Past to Present:

I went to school → I go to school every day

She wrote a letter → She writes letters often
They were students → They are students

TIME SIGNAL MATCHING:

Present Signals: now, every day, always, usually, on Mondays Past Signals: yesterday, last night, ago, in 1999, this morning (if past)
Correct Usage:

I always wake up early (present - habit)

I woke up early yesterday (past - completed)

EVALUATION:

Write five sentences in Simple Present Tense about your daily routine

Convert these to Simple Past Tense: (a) I play football (b) She eats rice (c) They go to school (d) He is happy (e) We have a car
Correct the errors: (a) Yesterday I go home (b) She don't like it (c) Did he went there? (d) He teach every day (e) They was happy
Fill in with correct tense: (a) Every Sunday, we	(go) to church. (b) Last Sunday, we	(go) to church. (c) She	(not/eat) meat now. (d) She	(not/eat) meat yesterday.
Write sentences matching these time signals: (a) always (b) yesterday (c) every morning
(d) last week (e) usually

ASSIGNMENT:

Write ten sentences in Simple Present Tense using different subjects and verbs

Write ten sentences in Simple Past Tense about what you did last weekend
Transform: Change these present sentences to past (add appropriate time signals): (a) I study English (b) She cooks dinner (c) They play games (d) We watch TV (e) He goes to work
Create sentences using: (a) always + present (b) yesterday + past (c) every day + present
(d) last year + past (e) usually + present

Write a short paragraph (8 sentences) mixing Simple Present (your habits) and Simple Past (what you did yesterday)
WEEK 8 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: READING TO IDENTIFY OVERALL IMPRESSION FROM AUTHOR'S STYLE
What is Author's Style? Author's style is the unique way a writer uses language, structure, and literary techniques to express ideas. It creates an overall impression or feeling in the reader.
ELEMENTS OF AUTHOR'S STYLE:

WORD CHOICE (Diction):

Simple/Plain: Common, everyday words - easy to understand

Example: "The man was sad"

Complex/Elevated: Difficult, sophisticated words - formal

Example: "The gentleman was melancholic"

Informal/Colloquial: Casual, conversational language

Example: "The guy was really down"

SENTENCE STRUCTURE:

Short sentences: Create tension, urgency, simplicity

Example: "He ran. The door slammed. Silence."

Long sentences: Create flow, detail, complexity

Example: "He ran swiftly through the winding streets, his heart pounding loudly as the door slammed behind him, leaving only eerie silence."
TONE:

Formal vs. Informal

Serious vs. Humorous

Objective vs. Subjective
Optimistic vs. Pessimistic

FIGURATIVE LANGUAGE:

Heavy use: Poetic, imaginative style

Minimal use: Straightforward, direct style

Types: Metaphors, similes, personification, imagery

DESCRIPTIVE DETAIL:

Highly descriptive: Rich imagery, sensory details

Minimally descriptive: Focuses on action, dialogue

NARRATIVE TECHNIQUE:

First person (I): Personal, intimate

Third person (he/she): Distant, objective

Present tense: Immediate, urgent

Past tense: Reflective, storytelling

OVERALL IMPRESSIONS FROM STYLE:

Example 1 - Simple, Direct Style: "The boy walked to school. He was late. His teacher was angry. He sat down quietly."
Impression: Straightforward, childlike, factual, no-nonsense

Example 2 - Descriptive, Flowery Style: "The young lad strolled leisurely along the winding path to the magnificent educational institution, his heart heavy with the knowledge of his tardiness. The stern educator, her countenance displaying unmistakable displeasure, cast a disapproving glance as he settled into his seat with cautious movements."
Impression: Formal, elaborate, sophisticated, slow-paced

Example 3 - Emotional, Dramatic Style: "The boy's heart pounded as he raced toward school. Late again! What would the teacher say? Her face, twisted with anger, made him freeze. He crept to his seat, trembling."
Impression: Tense, emotional, suspenseful, engaging

Example 4 - Humorous, Casual Style: "So there I was, sprinting to school like my life depended on it—spoiler alert: it kinda did. The teacher's face? Picture a volcano about to erupt. I slithered into my seat, trying to become invisible."
Impression: Funny, relatable, conversational, lighthearted

HOW TO IDENTIFY OVERALL IMPRESSION:

Step 1: Read the entire passage Step 2: Notice word choices (simple/complex?) Step 3: Observe sentence length (short/long?) Step 4: Identify tone (serious/funny/sad?) Step 5: Check for figurative language Step 6: Consider the feeling it creates Step 7: Summarize the overall impression

PRACTICE PASSAGE:

"The forest whispered ancient secrets. Shadows danced between towering trees as moonlight filtered through emerald leaves. A gentle breeze carried the sweet scent of jasmine, while distant owls sang their haunting melodies. In this magical realm, time seemed to stand still."
Style Analysis:

Diction: Poetic, descriptive words (whispered, ancient, towering, haunting)

Sentences: Flowing, moderate length

Figurative Language: Personification (forest whispered, shadows danced)
Imagery: Strong sensory details (sight, smell, sound)

Overall Impression: Magical, peaceful, mysterious, dreamlike, enchanting

DIFFERENT STYLES FOR DIFFERENT PURPOSES:

Scientific/Technical Writing:

Clear, precise, objective

Formal vocabulary

Factual presentation

Example: "Water boils at 100 degrees Celsius at sea level."

Literary/Creative Writing:

Imaginative, expressive

Figurative language

Emotional appeal

Example: "The water danced and bubbled joyously over the flames."

Journalistic Writing:

Informative, concise

Active voice

Factual with some opinion

Example: "The water reached boiling point during the experiment."

EVALUATION:

Define author's style and list five elements that contribute to it
Read this passage and identify the overall impression: "Thunder roared. Lightning struck. The child screamed. Rain poured. Darkness everywhere."
What impression does this style create? "The magnificent sunset painted the sky in brilliant hues of orange, pink, and purple, while gentle waves whispered softly against the golden shore."
Explain how sentence structure affects the overall impression of a text

Compare these two sentences and explain which creates a more formal impression: (a) "He went home" (b) "The gentleman proceeded to his residence"

ASSIGNMENT:

Rewrite this sentence in three different styles (simple, formal, dramatic): "The girl cried when she lost her bag"
Read any short story or article and identify: (a) Type of diction used (b) Sentence structure pattern (c) Tone (d) Use of figurative language (e) Overall impression created
Write two paragraphs (5 sentences each) about a rainy day: First paragraph - simple, direct style; Second paragraph - descriptive, emotional style
Analyze this passage for style: "Bang! The door flew open. A stranger stood there. Nobody moved. Everyone stared." What impression does it create?
Explain how an author's word choice affects the reader's overall impression. Give three examples.
WEEK 8 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: EXPOSITORY ESSAY – HIV/AIDS

What is an Expository Essay? An expository essay explains, informs, or describes a topic clearly and factually. It presents information objectively without trying to persuade or tell a story.
Purpose: To educate the reader about a subject

Characteristics:

Factual and informative

Clear and logical organization

Objective tone (no personal opinions)

Uses facts, statistics, examples

Third-person point of view

Formal language

STRUCTURE: INTRODUCTION:
Hook (interesting opening)

Background information

Thesis statement (main idea)

BODY (3-4 paragraphs):

Each paragraph covers one main point

Topic sentence

Supporting details, facts, examples

Smooth transitions
CONCLUSION:

Restate main points

Summary

Final thought

TOPIC: HIV/AIDS

Key Information to Include:

Definition:

HIV: Human Immunodeficiency Virus (virus that attacks immune system)

AIDS: Acquired Immune Deficiency Syndrome (advanced stage of HIV)

How HIV is Transmitted:

Unprotected sexual contact

Sharing needles/syringes

Mother to child (pregnancy, childbirth, breastfeeding)

Blood transfusion (with infected blood)

Contact with infected blood/body fluids

How HIV is NOT Transmitted:

Casual contact (hugging, shaking hands)

Sharing food, utensils

Mosquito bites

Toilet seats

Swimming pools

Airborne transmission
Symptoms:

Early: Flu-like illness, fever, fatigue, swollen glands

Later: Weight loss, persistent cough, night sweats

AIDS stage: Severe infections, opportunistic diseases

Prevention:

Abstinence

Safe sex (condom use)

Avoid sharing needles

Screen blood before transfusion

Antiretroviral drugs for pregnant women

Avoid contact with infected blood

Treatment:

Antiretroviral Therapy (ART)

No cure, but treatment controls virus

Regular medical monitoring

Healthy lifestyle

Effects:

Weakened immune system

Susceptibility to infections

Social stigma

Economic burden

Psychological impact

Important Facts:
HIV can remain undetected for years

Anyone can be infected

People with HIV can live long, healthy lives with treatment

Stigma and discrimination are wrong

Testing is important

USEFUL VOCABULARY:

Immune system, virus, infection, transmission

Antiretroviral therapy (ART), treatment, prevention

Symptoms, diagnosis, testing, screening

Stigma, discrimination, awareness

Counseling, support, care

TRANSITIONS FOR EXPOSITORY WRITING:

First, Second, Third, Finally

Additionally, Furthermore, Moreover

For example, For instance, Such as

In addition, Also, As well as

Therefore, Consequently, As a result

In conclusion, To summarize, In summary

SAMPLE EXPOSITORY ESSAY:

HIV/AIDS: UNDERSTANDING THE DISEASE
HIV/AIDS is one of the most serious health challenges facing the world today. Understanding this disease is crucial for prevention and reducing stigma. HIV (Human Immunodeficiency Virus) is a virus that attacks the body's immune system, while AIDS (Acquired Immune Deficiency Syndrome) is the advanced stage of HIV infection. This essay examines the transmission, prevention, and treatment of HIV/AIDS.
HIV is transmitted through specific body fluids including blood, semen, vaginal fluids, and breast milk. The primary modes of transmission are unprotected sexual contact with an infected person, sharing needles or syringes, mother-to-child transmission during pregnancy or breastfeeding, and transfusion of infected blood. However, it is important to understand that HIV cannot be transmitted through casual contact such as hugging, shaking hands, sharing food, or using the same toilet. Mosquitoes also cannot transmit the virus. This knowledge helps reduce unnecessary fear and discrimination.
Prevention of HIV/AIDS involves several strategies. The most effective method is abstinence from sexual activity. For those who are sexually active, consistent and correct use of condoms significantly reduces transmission risk. Avoiding the sharing of needles and ensuring blood is screened before transfusion are also critical preventive measures. Pregnant women living with HIV can take antiretroviral drugs to prevent transmission to their babies. Additionally, regular testing allows for early detection and treatment, preventing further spread.
Although there is no cure for HIV/AIDS, treatment has advanced significantly. Antiretroviral Therapy (ART) helps people living with HIV control the virus, maintain their immune system, and live long, healthy lives. With proper treatment and medical care, HIV-positive individuals can prevent progression to AIDS. Treatment also reduces the risk of transmitting the virus to others. Regular medical monitoring, adherence to medication, and a healthy lifestyle are essential components of HIV management.
In conclusion, HIV/AIDS is a serious but manageable disease. Understanding how it is transmitted helps in prevention efforts, while knowing about available treatment offers hope to those affected. Society must combat stigma and discrimination through education and awareness. With continued research, prevention efforts, and compassionate support, we can work toward reducing the impact of HIV/AIDS on individuals and communities worldwide.

WRITING TIPS:

DO:

Use factual, accurate information

Organize ideas logically

Use formal, objective language

Include specific details and examples

Use transitions between paragraphs

Cite sources if using statistics

Proofread carefully

DON'T:

Include personal opinions or arguments

Use emotional or biased language

Write in first person (I, we)

Include unverified information

Make judgmental statements

Use informal language

Deviate from the main topic

EVALUATION:

Define expository essay and state four characteristics that distinguish it from other essay types
What is the difference between HIV and AIDS?
List five ways HIV can be transmitted and five ways it CANNOT be transmitted

Explain the structure of an expository essay (introduction, body, conclusion)

Why is objective tone important in expository writing?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a complete expository essay (300-350 words) on the topic: "HIV/AIDS: Understanding the Disease"
Create an outline for an expository essay about HIV/AIDS with: (a) Thesis statement (b) Three main body points (c) Conclusion summary
List ten facts about HIV/AIDS that should be included in an expository essay

Write the introduction paragraph for an expository essay titled "Preventing HIV/AIDS"

Explain three differences between expository essay and argumentative essay
WEEK 8 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: MYTHS AND LEGENDS – COMPARING MYTHS AND LEGENDS; RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT

PART A: MYTHS AND LEGENDS DEFINITIONS:
Myth: A traditional story explaining natural phenomena, religious beliefs, or the origins of a people/culture. Features gods, supernatural beings, and creation stories. Often considered sacred.
Legend: A traditional story about real or believed-to-be-real people and historical events, passed down through generations. May have some basis in fact but includes exaggerated or fictional elements.

KEY DIFFERENCES:

AspectMythLegendMain CharactersGods, deities, supernatural beingsHeroes, historical figures, humansSettingDistant past, before time beganSpecific historical period/placePurposeExplain origins, natural phenomenaCelebrate heroes, teach lessonsTruth StatusSacred, symbolic truthMay have historical basisTimeTimeless, ancient pastIdentifiable time periodFocusCreation, cosmic eventsHeroic deeds, adventures

CHARACTERISTICS OF MYTHS:

Features:

Gods and goddesses as main characters
Explains creation of world/humans

Explains natural phenomena (thunder, rain, seasons)

Religious/spiritual significance

Set in primordial time

Supernatural powers and magic

Common Themes:

Creation of the universe

Origin of death

Why things are the way they are

Relationship between gods and humans

Good vs. evil

Examples:

Greek: Zeus, Prometheus, Pandora's Box

African: How Death Came to the World, Olodumare and creation

Egyptian: Ra (sun god), Osiris and Isis

Norse: Odin, Thor, creation of Yggdrasil

Purpose:

Explain mysteries of life

Teach religious beliefs

Provide moral guidance

Establish cultural identity

CHARACTERISTICS OF LEGENDS:
Features:

Heroes and warriors as main characters

Based on historical events (exaggerated)

Set in identifiable times/places

Contains some factual elements

Extraordinary human achievements

May include some supernatural elements

Common Themes:

Heroic deeds and bravery

Good triumphing over evil

Love and sacrifice

Founding of nations/cities

Great battles and conquests

Examples:

African: Sundiata (Mali), Queen Amina of Zaria, Shaka Zulu, Moremi of Ile-Ife

European: King Arthur, Robin Hood, El Cid

Asian: Mulan, Rama and Sita

American: Johnny Appleseed, Paul Bunyan

Purpose:

Preserve history (embellished)

Inspire heroism

Build national/cultural pride

Teach values and courage

SIMILARITIES BETWEEN MYTHS AND LEGENDS:

Both are traditional stories passed orally

Both teach moral lessons

Both may include supernatural elements

Both preserve culture and heritage

Both feature extraordinary events

Both reflect values of their society

Both have been retold over generations

Both can be found in all cultures

AFRICAN EXAMPLES:

Myths:

How the first man and woman were created

Why the sky is far from earth

Origin of death (Mawu-Lisa story)

Why animals don't speak anymore

The creation story of Olodumare (Yoruba)

Legends:

Sundiata Keita (founder of Mali Empire)

Queen Moremi of Ile-Ife (saved her people)

Queen Amina of Zaria (warrior queen)

Shaka Zulu (great warrior king)
Bayajida and the founding of Hausa states

SAMPLE COMPARISON:

Myth Example: "The Creation of Humans"

Gods molded humans from clay

Breathed life into them

Gave them intelligence

Set rules for them to follow

Analysis: Explains origin of humanity, features gods, no historical basis

Legend Example: "Queen Moremi"

Real ruler of Ile-Ife

Sacrificed herself to save her people

Discovered enemy secrets

Became a hero

Analysis: Historical figure, identifiable place/time, heroic deeds

PART B: RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT

Common Recommended Drama Texts for JSS1:

"The Gods Are Not to Blame" by Ola Rotimi

"Our Husband Has Gone Mad Again" by Ola Rotimi

"The Lion and the Jewel" by Wole Soyinka

"Wedlock of the Gods" by Zulu Sofola

"The Trials of Brother Jero" by Wole Soyinka
African drama anthologies

(Note: Use the specific text assigned by your school)

STUDYING YOUR DRAMA TEXT:

Plot:

What happens in each act/scene?

Conflict and resolution

Climax and turning points

Characters:

Protagonist and antagonist

Character traits and development

Relationships and motivations

Themes:

Main messages

Moral lessons

Social issues addressed

Setting:

Time and place

Social/cultural context

Atmosphere

Dramatic Elements:

Dialogue and language

Stage directions
Symbols and motifs

Conflict types

Performance Aspects:

Costumes, props, lighting

Audience impact

Acting techniques

KEY QUESTIONS FOR DRAMA STUDY:

Plot Questions:

Summarize Act I/II/III

What is the main conflict?

How is the conflict resolved?

Character Questions:

Describe the protagonist

Compare two characters

How does [character] change?

Theme Questions:

What is the main theme?

What lessons are taught?

How is [theme] developed?

Technical Questions:

Identify stage directions in Scene X

What props are important?
Describe the setting of Act II

EVALUATION:

Give three examples each of African myths and African legends

What are the main purposes of: (a) myths (b) legends?

Compare myths and legends in terms of: (a) characters (b) setting (c) purpose

State the title and author of your recommended drama text. Name three main characters.

Explain why both myths and legends are important for preserving culture (5 sentences)
