WEEK 9 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: VOWEL CONTRASTS: /e/ VS /ɜ:/ (EGG/BURN, PET/SHIRT)

Short Vowel /e/ (as in "egg"):

Short, quick sound

Tongue in mid position

Lips spread slightly

Jaw moderately open

Examples: egg, pen, bed, red, pet, set, get, end, men, best, desk, help, tell, fetch

Long Vowel /ɜ:/ (as in "burn"):

Long, prolonged sound

Tongue in central position

Lips slightly spread

Tense sound

Examples: burn, turn, bird, word, hurt, first, learn, earth, girl, work, heard, church, serve, term
KEY DIFFERENCE:

/e/ is SHORT with mid-tongue position

/ɜ:/ is LONG with central tongue position, more tense

PRACTICE PAIRS:

bed /e/ - bird /ɜ:/

pen /e/ - burn /ɜ:/

pet /e/ - pert /ɜ:/
get /e/ - girt /ɜ:/

end /e/ - earned /ɜ:/

debt /e/ - dirt /ɜ:/

dead /e/ - dread /e/ - third /ɜ:/ Practice Sentences:
The pet bird hurt its head

Get the first pen from the desk

The girl in the red shirt learned well

Ten men worked on the earth

The nurse kept the medicine on the bed

Articulation Tips:

For /e/:

Keep sound short

Relax jaw moderately

Don't prolong the sound

For /ɜ:/:

Prolong the sound

Keep tongue in center

Make it tense and steady

Common Mistakes:

Pronouncing "bird" as "bed"

Using /e/ when /ɜ:/ is needed in "work" → sounds like "weck"

Saying "girl" with /e/ instead of /ɜ:/

EVALUATION:

Explain the difference between /e/ and /ɜ:/ sounds with their characteristics

Identify the vowel sounds: (a) pen (b) burn (c) pet (d) shirt (e) get (f) first

Write five word pairs contrasting /e/ and /ɜ:/ sounds

Which sound is used in these words: (a) help (b) turn (c) desk (d) church (e) end?

Pronounce and differentiate: bed/bird, pen/burn, get/girl, end/earned

ASSIGNMENT:

List fifteen words each containing: (a) /e/ sound (b) /ɜ:/ sound

Write ten sentences using word pairs that contrast /e/ and /ɜ:/

Practice reading aloud: "The nurse in the red dress served ten men. She kept the first pen on the desk and learned her lesson well"
Circle words with /ɜ:/ sound: bed, word, pet, girl, help, turn, desk, heard, fetch, church

Explain why correct pronunciation of these contrasts is important in communication
WEEK 9 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: FUTURE TENSE – SIMPLE FUTURE (WILL, SHALL) WHAT IS SIMPLE FUTURE TENSE?
Simple Future Tense expresses actions that will happen in the future.

FORMATION:

Structure: Subject + will/shall + base verb Using Will and Shall:
Modern Usage:

Will: Used for all persons (I, you, he, she, it, we, they)

Shall: Rarely used in modern English, mainly with I/we in formal contexts

Examples:

I will go / I shall go

You will come

He/She/It will arrive

We will study / We shall study

They will play

USES OF SIMPLE FUTURE:

Predictions about the future:

It will rain tomorrow

She will become a doctor

They will win the match

Nigeria will develop
Spontaneous decisions (made at the moment of speaking):

I'm thirsty. I'll get some water

The phone is ringing. I'll answer it

I'll help you with that

Promises:

I will help you tomorrow

We will come to your party

He will pay you back

Offers:

I'll carry your bag

Shall I open the window?

Will you have some tea?

Threats/Warnings:

You will regret this

I'll report you to the principal

They will punish you

Facts about the future:

The sun will rise tomorrow

She will be sixteen next year

School will resume in September

NEGATIVE FORM:

Structure: Subject + will not (won't) / shall not (shan't) + base verb
Examples:

I will not (won't) go

She will not eat meat

They won't come tomorrow

We shall not (shan't) forget (formal)

QUESTION FORM:

Structure: Will/Shall + subject + base verb...?

Examples:

Will you come tomorrow?

Will she pass the exam?

Shall we go now? (suggestion)

Shall I help you? (offer)

Will they arrive on time?

Wh- Questions:

What will you do tomorrow?

Where will she go?

When will they arrive?

Who will help us?

How will you travel?

TIME EXPRESSIONS USED WITH FUTURE:

Tomorrow, tonight
Next (week/month/year/Monday)

In the future, someday, soon

Later, eventually

In + time period (in two days, in a month)

This evening/weekend

CONTRACTIONS:

I will = I'll

You will = You'll

He will = He'll

She will = She'll

We will = We'll

They will = They'll

Will not = Won't

Shall not = Shan't (rare)

DIFFERENCE: WILL vs SHALL

Traditional Rules (less common now):

Shall with I/we for simple future: I shall go

Will with you/he/she/it/they: They will come

Will with I/we for determination: I will succeed (promise)

Shall with you/he/she/it/they for commands: You shall obey

Modern Usage:
Will is used for all persons in most contexts

Shall is mainly used in:

Formal British English

Questions for suggestions: Shall we dance?

Legal/official documents

Offers: Shall I help you?

COMMON MISTAKES:

Error 1 - Using base verb alone:

✗ I go tomorrow

✓ I will go tomorrow

Error 2 - Adding -s/-es to verb:

✗ She will goes

✓ She will go

Error 3 - Using past form:

✗ He will went

✓ He will go

Error 4 - Double future markers:

✗ I will going to come

✓ I will come / I'm going to come

Error 5 - Wrong negative form:

✗ He will not goes
✓ He will not go

BE GOING TO vs WILL:

Be going to:

Planned intentions: I'm going to study medicine

Evidence-based predictions: Look at those clouds! It's going to rain

Will:

Spontaneous decisions: I'll have tea (deciding now)

Uncertain predictions: It will probably rain

Promises: I'll call you tomorrow

PRACTICE EXAMPLES:

Affirmative:

I will visit my grandmother next week

She will write the exam tomorrow

They will travel to Abuja soon

Negative:

I won't forget your birthday

He won't come to the party

We won't be late

Interrogative:

Will you help me?
When will they arrive?

Shall we start the meeting?

EVALUATION:

Define Simple Future Tense and explain how it is formed

Write five sentences in Simple Future Tense about your plans for next week

Change to negative: (a) I will go (b) She will help (c) They will come (d) He will study (e) We will travel
Change to questions: (a) You will attend (b) She will cook (c) They will play (d) It will rain (e) He will arrive
Correct the errors: (a) I will goes (b) She will went (c) Will he comes? (d) They will not comes (e) I go tomorrow

ASSIGNMENT:

Write ten sentences using Simple Future Tense with different subjects and verbs

Write five predictions about the future using "will"

Write five promises or offers using "will" or "shall"

Fill in with correct future forms: (a) Tomorrow, I	(visit) my friend. (b) She  		 (not/come) to school. (c)	you	(help) me? (d) What	they	(do) next? (e) We	(travel) next month.
Change these Present Tense sentences to Future Tense: (a) I study English (b) She cooks dinner (c) They play football (d) He goes to school (e) We watch TV
WEEK 9 - READING AND COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: READING TO INTERPRET DIAGRAMS, MAPS, SKETCHES; VOCABULARY – WORD FORMATION (PREFIXES)
PART A: INTERPRETING DIAGRAMS, MAPS, AND SKETCHES WHY INTERPRET VISUAL INFORMATION?
Not all information is in text form

Visual aids summarize complex data

Maps show locations and directions

Diagrams explain processes/relationships

Sketches illustrate concepts

READING DIAGRAMS

What is a Diagram? A visual representation showing how something works, relationships between parts, or a process.
Types:

Flow charts (show processes/steps)

Venn diagrams (show relationships/comparisons)

Labeled diagrams (show parts of objects)

Pie charts (show percentages/proportions)

Bar graphs (compare quantities)

Tree diagrams (show hierarchies/classifications)

How to Read Diagrams:

Read the title/heading
Identify what is being shown

Look at labels and keys

Follow arrows or connecting lines

Understand relationships between parts

Check for legends or explanations

Draw conclusions from the information

Example - Simple Flow Chart:

Start → Wake up → Brush teeth → Bath → Breakfast → Go to school → End

Questions from this diagram:

What is the first activity? (Wake up)

What comes after brushing teeth? (Bath)

What is the final activity? (Go to school)

READING MAPS

What is a Map? A visual representation of an area showing locations, directions, distances, and features.
Map Components:

Title: What area is shown

Key/Legend: Symbols and their meanings

Scale: Distance representation

Compass/Direction: N, S, E, W

Labels: Names of places

Symbols: Icons representing features
Common Map Symbols:

Roads (lines)

Rivers (wavy blue lines)

Buildings (rectangles/squares)

Trees/Forests (green areas)

Mountains (triangles/brown areas)

Hospitals (H or +)

Schools (flag/building symbol)

How to Read Maps:

Check the title

Find the key/legend

Identify cardinal directions (N, S, E, W)

Locate specific places

Determine relationships (distance, direction)

Use scale to estimate distances

Sample Questions from Maps:

What is located north of the school?

How many roads lead to the market?

What is the symbol for hospital?

Which direction is the river from the church?

What is between the market and school?

READING SKETCHES
What is a Sketch? A simple, quick drawing showing basic features of an object, place, or person.
Types:

Object sketches (show items)

Scene sketches (show places/events)

Layout sketches (show arrangements)

Anatomical sketches (show body parts)

How to Read Sketches:

Identify what is drawn

Look for labels

Note important features

Understand proportions/sizes

Identify relationships between parts

TIPS FOR INTERPRETING VISUALS:

Before Reading:

Read the title first

Check for instructions

Look at the whole visual

During Reading:

Pay attention to labels

Follow arrows and lines

Note colors and symbols
Read all text provided

After Reading:

Summarize what you learned

Answer questions carefully

Check your understanding

PART B: WORD FORMATION - PREFIXES

What is a Prefix? A group of letters added to the BEGINNING of a word to change its meaning or create a new word.
Structure: Prefix + Root Word = New Word

COMMON PREFIXES AND MEANINGS:

UN- (not, opposite of):

happy → unhappy (not happy)

lucky → unlucky (not lucky)

able → unable (not able)

fair → unfair (not fair)

Other examples: untrue, unkind, uncertain, unfold, unlock, unwrap

RE- (again, back):

write → rewrite (write again)

read → reread (read again)

build → rebuild (build again)

turn → return (turn back)
Other examples: replay, retell, review, redo, refill, reconsider

DIS- (not, opposite of, reverse):

agree → disagree (not agree)

like → dislike (not like)

appear → disappear (not appear)

obey → disobey (not obey)

Other examples: dishonest, discomfort, disconnect, discover, disqualify

MIS- (wrongly, badly):

use → misuse (use wrongly)

spell → misspell (spell wrongly)

understand → misunderstand (understand wrongly)

lead → mislead (lead wrongly)

Other examples: misplace, misbehave, miscount, misjudge, misread

PRE- (before):

view → preview (view before)

pay → prepay (pay before)

school → preschool (before school)

fix → prefix (fix before)

Other examples: prehistoric, preview, precaution, predict, prepare

POST- (after):

war → postwar (after war)

pone → postpone (put after)

date → postdate (date after)
Other examples: postgraduate, postscript, postpone

OVER- (too much, excessive):

eat → overeat (eat too much)

work → overwork (work too much)

sleep → oversleep (sleep too much)

charge → overcharge (charge too much)

Other examples: overflow, overload, overcome, overlook, overseas

UNDER- (too little, below):

pay → underpay (pay too little)

estimate → underestimate (estimate too low)

stand → understand (stand under → comprehend)

Other examples: underground, underwater, underline, undergraduate

SUB- (under, below):

marine → submarine (under sea)

way → subway (underground way)

title → subtitle (title below)

Other examples: subway, subheading, subdivide, substandard

INTER- (between, among):

national → international (between nations)

act → interact (act between)

change → interchange (change between)

Other examples: internet, interview, interrupt, intermediate

TRANS- (across, beyond):
port → transport (carry across)

late → translate (carry meaning across languages)

Other examples: transfer, transform, transmit, transparent

BI- (two):

cycle → bicycle (two wheels)

lingual → bilingual (two languages)

weekly → bi-weekly (every two weeks)

Other examples: bilateral, biannual

TRI- (three):

angle → triangle (three angles)

cycle → tricycle (three wheels)

Other examples: tripod, trilogy, triple

MULTI- (many):

colored → multicolored (many colors)

purpose → multipurpose (many purposes)

Other examples: multiply, multimedia, multinational

ANTI- (against):

social → antisocial (against social)

clockwise → anticlockwise (against clockwise)

Other examples: antibiotic, antiseptic, anti-war

FORMING NEW WORDS:

Example Process:
Base word: happy

Add prefix un-: unhappy (not happy)

Can add suffix too: unhappiness (state of not being happy)

Practice:

appear → disappear → disappearance

use → misuse → misused

do → redo → redone

PREFIX RULES:

Prefix changes meaning, not word class

happy (adjective) → unhappy (adjective)

Some exceptions exist

No spelling changes usually

un + necessary = unnecessary (double n)

dis + satisfied = dissatisfied (double s)

Some prefixes have hyphens

ex-president, self-made, co-operate (sometimes)

EVALUATION:

Explain what diagrams, maps, and sketches are and why we need to interpret them

List five components of a map and explain their purposes

Define prefix and give five examples with their meanings
Form new words by adding appropriate prefixes to: (a) happy (b) write (c) agree (d) understand (e) national
Using the prefix "un-", "re-", or "dis-", create three words each and use them in sentences

ASSIGNMENT:

Draw a simple map of your school compound showing at least five locations. Include a title, key, and direction indicator
Look at any diagram in your textbook and write five sentences explaining what it shows

Complete this table:

PrefixMeaningExampleNew Word Meaningun-nothappynot happyre-dis-mis-pre-
Add prefixes to these words and use the new words in sentences: (a) view (b) pay (c) place (d) behave (e) historic
Create a simple flow chart showing the steps you take to prepare for school in the morning (at least 6 steps)
WEEK 9 - COMPOSITION

ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY: ARRANGING IDEAS IN LOGICAL SEQUENCE WHAT IS AN ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY?
An argumentative essay presents a position on a topic with reasons and evidence to convince readers.
Basic Structure:

Introduction (thesis statement)

Body paragraphs (arguments with evidence)

Counter-argument and refutation

Conclusion

WHY LOGICAL SEQUENCE MATTERS

Makes essay easy to follow

Strengthens arguments

Convinces readers effectively

Prevents confusion

Remember: Poor Sequence = Weak Essay | Good Sequence = Strong Essay

METHODS OF ARRANGING IDEAS

ORDER OF IMPORTANCE

Climactic (Weakest to Strongest) - Most common

Build up to strongest argument

Save best for last

Creates memorable ending

Example: "School Uniforms Should Be Compulsory"

Saves time in morning (weakest)

Reduces peer pressure (medium)

Promotes equality (strongest)

CHRONOLOGICAL ORDER
Arrange by time sequence for historical topics or processes.

PROBLEM-SOLUTION ORDER

Present problem

Explain seriousness

Propose solution

Explain benefits

CAUSE-AND-EFFECT ORDER

Show what leads to what, or start with result and trace back to causes.

COMPARE-AND-CONTRAST ORDER

Block Method: Discuss all of A, then all of B

Point-by-Point: Compare A and B for each point

BODY PARAGRAPH STRUCTURE

Each paragraph should:

Have ONE main idea

Start with topic sentence

Provide evidence

Explain how evidence supports argument

Link to next paragraph

IMPORTANT TRANSITION WORDS

Adding: Additionally, Furthermore, Moreover, Also

Order: First, Second, Third, Finally, Next
Importance: Most importantly, Significantly, Above all Contrast: However, Nevertheless, On the other hand, Although Cause/Effect: Therefore, Thus, Consequently, As a result Examples: For example, For instance, Such as
Concluding: In conclusion, To summarize, Finally, Overall

STEPS TO ARRANGE IDEAS

BRAINSTORM: List all arguments and evidence

EVALUATE: Identify strongest arguments, remove weak ones

ORDER: Arrange (usually climactic order is best)

OUTLINE: Create detailed outline

CONNECT: Add transitions for smooth flow

SAMPLE OUTLINE

Topic: "Students Should Wear School Uniforms"

Introduction: Thesis statement

Argument 1 (Weakest): Save time

Argument 2 (Medium): Reduce peer pressure

Argument 3 (Strongest): Promote equality

Counter-argument: Address opposing view

Conclusion: Restate thesis and summarize

COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID
❌ Jumping between topics randomly

❌ Placing strongest argument first (usually)

❌ No transitions between ideas

❌ Multiple ideas in one paragraph

❌ Misplaced counter-argument

Follow logical progression

Build to strongest point

Use smooth transitions

One paragraph = one point

Counter-argument before conclusion

EVALUATION

Why is logical sequence important in argumentative essays?

Describe climactic and problem-solution arrangements

When do you use climactic vs anticlimactic order?

List five transition words and their functions

Create an outline for "Mobile Phones Should Be Banned in Schools"

ASSIGNMENT

Arrange in climactic order: (a) Improves vocabulary (b) Develops imagination (c) Increases knowledge (d) Enhances critical thinking. Explain your choice.
Write an outline for "Sports Are More Important Than Academics" with thesis, three arguments, counter-argument, and conclusion.
Add transitions: "School uniforms are beneficial. They save money. Parents don't buy expensive clothes. Uniforms promote equality. Rich and poor students look the same."
Identify the arrangement method: Problem introduction → effects → solutions → benefits.
Create a 5-point checklist for reviewing logical sequence in your essay.
WEEK 9 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

MYTHS AND LEGENDS: FEATURES (MAGIC, SUPERSTITION, ETC.); DRAMA TEXT

PART A: FEATURES OF MYTHS AND LEGENDS FEATURES OF MYTHS
SUPERNATURAL BEINGS

Gods and goddesses with special powers

Immortal characters controlling natural forces

Examples: Zeus (Greek), Olodumare (Yoruba), Ra (Egyptian)

MAGIC AND SUPERNATURAL POWERS

Transformation/shape-shifting

Flying, invisibility, creating from nothing

Controlling weather, healing, immortality

CREATION AND ORIGINS

Explains how world/humans were created

Origin of natural phenomena (thunder, rain, day/night)

How death came to the world

TIMELESSNESS

Set in ancient/primordial times

"In the beginning," "Long before time"

No specific dates

SACRED/RELIGIOUS SIGNIFICANCE
Explains religious beliefs

Moral and spiritual lessons

Basis for rituals and ceremonies

UNIVERSAL THEMES

Good vs. evil

Creation and destruction

Life, death, and rebirth

Punishment and reward

SYMBOLIC ELEMENTS

Objects representing ideas (tree of life)

Animals symbolizing qualities

Significant numbers and colors

EXPLANATORY PURPOSE

Answers "why" questions

Explains mysteries of existence

Gives meaning to natural phenomena

FEATURES OF LEGENDS

HEROIC CHARACTERS

Brave warriors and leaders

Exceptional human abilities

Historical or believed-to-be-real people

Examples: Sundiata, Queen Amina, Shaka Zulu
HISTORICAL BASIS

Connected to real events

Identifiable time periods and places

Exaggerated but rooted in fact

EXTRAORDINARY DEEDS

Impossible achievements

Superhuman strength or intelligence

Founding cities/nations, defeating enemies

EXAGGERATION

Feats enlarged beyond reality

Numbers inflated (killed 1000 enemies)

Makes heroes larger than life

MORAL LESSONS

Teaches courage, loyalty, honesty

Shows consequences of actions

Warns against vices

CULTURAL PRIDE

Celebrates national heroes

Preserves historical memory

Builds identity and unity

SOME SUPERNATURAL ELEMENTS

Magic weapons or objects

Prophetic dreams
Divine assistance (less than myths)

ORAL TRANSMISSION

Passed through storytelling

Multiple versions exist

Performed by griots/storytellers

SHARED FEATURES (Myths & Legends)

Oral tradition passed down through generations

Teach moral, social, cultural values

Cultural preservation

Archetypal characters (heroes, villains, helpers)

Symbolic meaning

Dramatic structure

Entertainment value

SUPERSTITION IN MYTHS AND LEGENDS

Definition: Beliefs in supernatural causation, magic, and forces beyond scientific explanation.

Common Elements:

Omens and Signs: Animal behavior, dreams, natural signs predicting events

Taboos: Forbidden actions with supernatural consequences

Charms and Amulets: Magical objects for protection

Curses and Blessings: Words with magical power

Magical Rituals: Ceremonies, sacrifices, incantations
Supernatural Creatures: Spirits, ghosts, demons, shape-shifters

MAGIC IN MYTHS AND LEGENDS

Types of Magic:

Transformation Magic: Changing form, size, nature of objects

Elemental Magic: Controlling fire, water, earth, air

Healing/Harmful Magic: Curing diseases, causing illness, love potions

Divination: Seeing the future, reading omens, prophetic visions

Protective Magic: Shields, invisibility, invulnerability, charms

African Examples:

Myths: Anansi stories, Tortoise tales, Mami Wata

Legends: Moremi of Ife, Sundiata, Shango

PART B: DRAMA TEXT ANALYSIS KEY ELEMENTS TO ANALYZE
PLOT ANALYSIS

Exposition, rising action, climax, falling action, resolution

CHARACTER ANALYSIS

Protagonist's goals and obstacles

Character development and relationships

Static vs. dynamic characters

THEME IDENTIFICATION

Central message revealed through dialogue, actions, conflicts
DRAMATIC TECHNIQUES

Dialogue, stage directions, soliloquies, asides

Dramatic irony, symbolism

SETTING AND ATMOSPHERE

Time period and location significance

Mood created

CONFLICT TYPES

Internal (within character)

External (with others, society, nature)

STUDY QUESTIONS FOR DRAMA

Plot: Summarize acts/scenes, identify main conflict, climax, resolution Character: Describe main characters, their changes, motivations Theme: Identify central theme, lessons taught, author's development Technical: Stage directions, props, dialogue significance, title meaning

EVALUATION

List and explain five features of myths

List and explain five features of legends

What role does magic play in myths and legends? Give three examples

Define superstition and give five examples in African myths/legends

State your drama text title/author and describe one conflict
ASSIGNMENT

Create a comparison table: Myths vs. Legends (characters, magic, purpose, time setting, historical basis)
Write a short myth (150 words) explaining a natural phenomenon. Include magical elements.
List ten superstitious beliefs from your/African culture with explanations

Read Act II of your drama text and write:

Summary

Main conflict

Character development

One important quote and its significance

Explain how magic and superstition in myths/legends reflect cultural beliefs and values (10 sentences)
WEEK 10 - SPEECH WORK: INTRODUCTION TO DIPHTHONGS WHAT IS A DIPHTHONG?
A vowel sound that glides from one vowel to another within the same syllable.

Characteristics:

Two vowel sounds in one syllable

Tongue/lips move during pronunciation

First sound is longer and stronger

Creates a gliding effect

THE EIGHT ENGLISH DIPHTHONGS

/eɪ/ (day): make, rain, they, eight, play

/aɪ/ (my): fly, time, like, high, night

/ɔɪ/ (boy): toy, oil, coin, noise, choice

/aʊ/ (now): how, house, town, cloud, sound

/əʊ/ (go): no, boat, show, hope, home

/ɪə/ (here): near, ear, fear, clear, year

/eə/ (hair): care, chair, fair, bear, wear

/ʊə/ (pure): sure, tour, poor, cure (disappearing in modern English)

GROUPING DIPHTHONGS

Closing Diphthongs (glide to /ɪ/ or /ʊ/):

/eɪ/, /aɪ/, /ɔɪ/ → glide to /ɪ/

/aʊ/, /əʊ/ → glide to /ʊ/
Centering Diphthongs (glide to /ə/):

/ɪə/, /eə/, /ʊə/ → glide to schwa

DIPHTHONGS VS PURE VOWELS

Pure Vowel: Single sound, no movement (e.g., /iː/ in "see") Diphthong: Two sounds blended, tongue/lips move (e.g., /eɪ/ in "say")

COMMON SPELLING PATTERNS

/eɪ/: a_e (make), ai (rain), ay (day), ey (they)

/aɪ/: i_e (like), igh (high), y (my), ie (tie)

/ɔɪ/: oy (boy), oi (coin)

/aʊ/: ou (house), ow (now)

/əʊ/: o_e (home), oa (boat), ow (show)

ARTICULATION TIPS

Start clearly with first vowel ✓ Glide smoothly to second sound ✓ Don't pause between sounds ✓ First sound stronger/longer ✓ Keep within one syllable
Common Mistakes:

Pronouncing as two separate sounds

Making both sounds equally strong

Confusing similar diphthongs

EVALUATION
Define diphthong and explain difference from pure vowel

List eight English diphthongs with examples

Identify diphthongs in: time, boat, boy, house, hair

Write five sentences using at least two different diphthongs

ASSIGNMENT

Create table: phonetic symbol, example word, spelling patterns for all eight diphthongs

List ten words each for /eɪ/, /aɪ/, /ɔɪ/

Identify diphthongs in: "My friend and I like to play near the house."

Explain difference between monophthongs and diphthongs with examples
WEEK 10 - GRAMMAR: SIMPLE PAST TENSE SENTENCES AFFIRMATIVE SENTENCES
Structure: Subject + Past Verb + Object

Regular Verbs:

I walked to school yesterday

She cooked rice last night

They played football last Saturday

Irregular Verbs:

I went to Lagos last month

She ate breakfast early

We had a party on Friday

NEGATIVE SENTENCES

Structure: Subject + did not (didn't) + base verb

Examples:

I didn't go to school yesterday

She didn't cook dinner

They didn't play football

Common Errors:

✗ I didn't went → ✓ I didn't go

✗ She didn't cooked → ✓ She didn't cook
INTERROGATIVE SENTENCES

Structure: Did + subject + base verb...?

Examples:

Did you go to school yesterday?

Did she cook rice?

Did they play football?

Short Answers: Yes, I did. / No, I didn't.

WH- QUESTIONS

Structure: Wh-word + did + subject + base verb...?

Examples:

What did you do yesterday?

Where did she go last week?

When did they arrive?

Why did he leave early?

Special Case: Who came? (NOT: Who did come?)

COMBINING IDEAS

Using Conjunctions:

I went to school and played football

She studied hard but failed the exam

They didn't come because they were sick

Time Clauses:
When I arrived, they had left

After she ate, she slept

While I studied, he played

COMMON IRREGULAR VERBS

go → went, come → came, see → saw

eat → ate, drink → drank, write → wrote

take → took, make → made, give → gave

buy → bought, say → said, think → thought

EVALUATION

Write five affirmative sentences about last weekend

Change to negative: (a) I went to school (b) She ate breakfast (c) They played well

Change to questions: (a) You saw the film (b) She cooked dinner (c) They came early

Make Wh-questions: He went to Lagos (Where...?)

Correct errors: (a) I didn't went (b) Did she came? (c) He didn't ate

ASSIGNMENT

Write paragraph (10 sentences) about your last birthday in past tense

Write five compound sentences using: and, but, or, so, because

Transform to past, negative, then question: (i) I play football (ii) She reads books

Complete with past tense: Yesterday, I	(wake) up early. I	(brush) my teeth...

Write five sentences using: saw, bought, went, ate, wrote

WEEK 10 - READING: HOW DIAGRAMS/MAPS CLARIFY TEXT WHY USE DIAGRAMS AND MAPS?
Make complex information easier to understand

Show relationships and connections

Help locate information quickly

Support written explanations

Appeal to visual learners

HOW DIAGRAMS CLARIFY TEXT

SHOW PROCESSES

Arrows connecting steps

Visual cycle easier to remember

Example: Water cycle diagram

ILLUSTRATE STRUCTURES

Show location of parts

Display relative sizes

Example: Parts of a flower

COMPARE AND CONTRAST

Venn diagrams show similarities/differences

Clear visual comparison

SHOW RELATIONSHIPS

Organizational charts show hierarchy

Chain of command visible
DISPLAY DATA

Bar graphs/pie charts

Easy comparison of numbers

HOW MAPS CLARIFY TEXT

SHOW LOCATIONS

Exact positions

Spatial relationships

Distances between places

ILLUSTRATE ROUTES

Visual path to follow

Landmarks along the way

SHOW GEOGRAPHICAL FEATURES

Topographical features

Size and extent

Use of symbols and colors

STEPS TO READ TEXT WITH DIAGRAMS/MAPS

Preview Both: Read titles, scan content

Read Text First: Understand main ideas

Study Diagram/Map: Examine all parts, labels, key

Cross-Reference: Match text to visual

Integrate Understanding: Combine both sources

WHAT TO LOOK FOR

In Diagrams:

Labels and arrows

Scale and key

Relationships between parts

Direction of flow

In Maps:

Compass/direction

Scale for distance

Symbols and legend

Starting/ending points

EVALUATION

Explain three ways diagrams clarify text

What five elements to look for in diagrams/maps?

Describe steps for reading text with diagrams

How does a map clarify written directions?

How would a labeled diagram clarify plant parts?

ASSIGNMENT

Draw map from house to school. Write directions (5 sentences). Explain how map clarifies.
Create flow chart for morning routine. Explain how it clarifies (3 sentences).

Find diagram in textbook. Explain what text and diagram show, how they work together.

Explain why these need diagrams: (a) solar system (b) food chain (c) math problem

Create Venn diagram comparing two animals. Explain what it shows.
WEEK 10 - COMPOSITION: GUIDED COMPOSITION - A FESTIVAL WHAT IS GUIDED COMPOSITION?
Writing based on given points/guidelines. Organize provided ideas into complete, structured paragraphs.

STRUCTURE FOR "A FESTIVAL IN MY VILLAGE"

Paragraph 1 - Introduction:

Name and type of festival

When celebrated

Brief background/origin

Importance

Paragraph 2 - Preparations:

Cleaning homes

Cooking special foods

Buying new clothes

Organizing committees

Paragraph 3 - Main Events:

Traditional dances/music

Processions/parades

Masquerade displays

Cultural performances

Paragraph 4 - Food and People:

Special dishes prepared
Who participates

Community involvement

Paragraph 5 - Conclusion:

Significance of festival

Personal reflection

Benefits to community

USEFUL VOCABULARY

Festival Words: celebration, ceremony, ritual, procession, masquerade, festivities

Action Verbs: celebrate, honor, perform, display, participate, feast

Descriptive: colorful, vibrant, joyous, traditional, cultural, abundant

TRANSITIONAL PHRASES

Between Paragraphs: On the festival day..., During celebrations..., More importantly...

Within Paragraphs: First, Additionally, Meanwhile, For example, Therefore

WRITING TIPS DO:
Follow guided points

Expand points into full sentences

Use descriptive language

Connect with transitions

Include specific details
DON'T:

Deviate from given points

Write single-sentence paragraphs

Use informal language

Make paragraphs too long/short

EVALUATION

What is guided composition?

List five types of festivals in Nigeria

Outline structure for "A Festival in My Village"

List ten festival-related vocabulary words

Why are festivals important? (three reasons)

ASSIGNMENT

Write complete composition (250-300 words) on "A Festival in My Village"

Create outline: Introduction, three body paragraphs, conclusion

List ten special foods during festivals

Describe three traditional festival activities (5 sentences each)

Write introduction paragraph for any festival
WEEK 10 - LITERATURE: DRAMATIZATION & TEXT REVIEW PART A: DRAMATIZATION
WHAT IS DRAMATIZATION? Acting out scenes from a play through dialogue, movement, gestures, and emotions.

STEPS TO PREPARE

SELECT SCENE

Manageable length (5-10 minutes)

Important dialogue

Contains conflict

Suitable number of characters

ANALYZE SCENE

What happens?

Who are the characters?

What is the conflict?

What is the mood?

ASSIGN ROLES

Match actors to characters

Assign director, props coordinator

Assign prompter

MEMORIZE LINES

Read multiple times

Understand meaning
Practice with emotion

Rehearse with partners

DEVELOP CHARACTERIZATION Voice: Tone, volume, pace, pitch
Body Language: Facial expressions, gestures, posture, movement

Emotions: Show feelings believably

BLOCKING (Stage Movement)

Where actors stand/sit

When/where they move

Entrances and exits

Face audience when speaking

PROPS AND COSTUMES

Props: Objects actors use (book, letter, crown)

Costumes: Simple but representative of character

REHEARSE

First reading with scripts

Blocking rehearsal (add movements)

Line rehearsal (memorization)

Character rehearsal (emotions)

Technical rehearsal (props/costumes)

Dress rehearsal (full performance)

PERFORM During Performance:
Project your voice

Face audience

Stay in character

Listen to other actors

Show energy and enthusiasm

COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID

Speaking too softly

Rushing lines

Turning back to audience

Breaking character

Standing still

Over-acting

Not listening to cues

PART B: REVIEW OF TEXTS PROSE TEXT REVIEW
PLOT: Beginning, rising action, climax, resolution

CHARACTERS: Protagonist, antagonist, supporting characters; their traits and development

SETTING: Time, place, social environment

THEMES: Central messages, moral lessons (honesty, courage, family)

LITERARY DEVICES: Foreshadowing, flashback, symbolism, irony
DRAMA TEXT REVIEW

STRUCTURE: Acts and scenes, plot development

DIALOGUE: Character revelation through speech

STAGE DIRECTIONS: Instructions for actors, setting descriptions

DRAMATIC TECHNIQUES: Aside, soliloquy, dramatic irony

THEMES: Social issues, moral lessons

MYTHS AND LEGENDS REVIEW

Myths: Gods, supernatural beings, explain creation, timeless Legends: Heroes, historical basis, exaggerated deeds, cultural pride Common Features: Magic, moral lessons, oral tradition

STUDY TIPS

Create summary notes (plot timeline, character charts)

Practice questions

Reread key sections

Make connections to real life

Test yourself without notes

EVALUATION

List five steps in preparing scene for dramatization

Difference between props and costumes? Give examples

State title/author of your drama text. Choose scene to dramatize and explain why
Review prose text: summarize plot, describe main character, state theme

Three common dramatization mistakes to avoid

ASSIGNMENT

Choose short scene from drama text. Prepare by: analyzing, listing characters, describing emotions, planning movements, identifying props
Write character profiles for three main characters: physical description, personality, role, quote
Create plot timeline: exposition, three main events, climax, resolution

List themes in texts. Explain one from each in detail

Design costume and list props for one character. Explain choices
JSS1 ENGLISH STUDIES LESSON NOTES THIRD TERM

WEEK 1 - SPEECH WORK: DIPHTHONGS

A diphthong is a complex vowel sound that begins with one vowel sound and glides into another within the same syllable. Unlike pure vowels (monophthongs), diphthongs involve continuous tongue movement during pronunciation, creating a smooth glide between two vowel qualities.
The eight English diphthongs are:

/eɪ/ as in place, make, day, eight, gate, remain

/əʊ/ as in home, go, boat, phone, stone, although

/aʊ/ as in mouse, house, now, found, cloud, coward

/ɪə/ as in clear, hear, beer, fear, near, sincere

/eə/ as in care, hair, bear, wear, chair, staircase

/ɔɪ/ as in boy, coin, noise, join, voice, exploit

/aɪ/ as in find, time, like, fight, sky, describe

/ʊə/ as in tour, poor, sure, pure, mature, allure

Key Points:

Diphthongs differ from two separate vowels because they form a single syllable

The first vowel sound is typically longer and receives more stress

The glide to the second sound is smooth and continuous

Incorrect pronunciation of diphthongs can lead to communication errors

Regional accents may influence diphthong pronunciation
EVALUATION:

Define a diphthong and explain how it differs from a pure vowel sound.

Pronounce five words containing the diphthong /eɪ/ and five words containing /aɪ/.

Identify the diphthongs in these words: "noise," "tour," "home," "fear," "mouth."

Why is the first vowel sound in a diphthong usually stronger than the second?

Write two sentences using at least three different diphthongs, then circle the words containing diphthongs.

ASSIGNMENT:

Create a list of ten words for each of these diphthongs: /əʊ/, /aʊ/, and /ɔɪ/.

Record yourself (or practice with a partner) pronouncing all eight diphthongs with example words.
Find five words in your recommended prose text that contain diphthongs and identify which diphthong each contains.
Write a short paragraph (5-7 sentences) about your school, deliberately using words with at least five different diphthongs. Underline these words.
Explain in your own words why learning correct diphthong pronunciation is important for effective communication.
WEEK 1 - GRAMMAR: INTRODUCTION TO ACTIVE AND PASSIVE VOICE

Voice in grammar refers to the relationship between the subject of a sentence and the action expressed by the verb. There are two voices in English: active voice and passive voice.
Active Voice: In active voice, the subject performs the action expressed by the verb. The focus is on who or what is doing the action.
Structure: Subject + Verb + Object Examples:
The teacher teaches English grammar. (Subject: teacher performs the action)

Students write essays every week. (Students perform the action)

The chef prepared a delicious meal. (Chef performs the action)

My brother painted the fence yesterday. (Brother performs the action)

The committee will announce the results tomorrow. (Committee performs the action)

Passive Voice: In passive voice, the subject receives the action expressed by the verb. The focus shifts from the doer to the receiver of the action.
Structure: Subject + Form of "be" + Past Participle (+ by + Agent) Examples:
English grammar is taught by the teacher. (English grammar receives the action)

Essays are written by students every week. (Essays receive the action)

A delicious meal was prepared by the chef. (Meal receives the action)

The fence was painted by my brother yesterday. (Fence receives the action)

The results will be announced by the committee tomorrow. (Results receive the action)

Formation of Passive Voice:
Identify the object of the active sentence

Make the object the subject of the passive sentence

Use the appropriate tense of the verb "to be"

Add the past participle of the main verb

Optionally, add "by + agent" (the original subject)

When to Use Passive Voice:

When the doer is unknown: "My bicycle was stolen last night."

When the action is more important than the doer: "The bridge was completed in 2020."

When the doer is obvious: "The suspect has been arrested."

In formal or scientific writing: "The experiment was conducted under controlled conditions."
To maintain focus on a particular subject throughout a text

When to Use Active Voice:

For clear, direct, and concise writing

When the doer of the action is important

In most everyday communication

To make writing more engaging and dynamic

Key Points:

Active voice is generally preferred for clarity and directness

Passive voice can make sentences wordier

The agent (by + doer) can be omitted in passive voice

Not all verbs can be made passive (intransitive verbs cannot)

EVALUATION:

Explain the difference between active and passive voice with one example of each.

Convert these active sentences to passive: (a) "The dog bit the man." (b) "She will complete the project tomorrow."
Identify whether these sentences are in active or passive voice: (a) "The letter was

delivered by the postman." (b) "The children are playing football."

Why might a writer choose to use passive voice instead of active voice?

Rewrite this passive sentence in active voice: "The school was built by the government in 1995."
ASSIGNMENT:

Write five sentences in active voice about activities in your school.

Convert all five sentences from question 1 to passive voice.

Find three examples of passive voice sentences in your recommended prose text and rewrite them in active voice.
Write a short paragraph (6-8 sentences) describing what happens in your school cafeteria during lunchtime. Use both active and passive voice sentences, then label each sentence as A (active) or P (passive).
Explain in your own words when it is appropriate to use passive voice in writing.
WEEK 1 - READING & COMPREHENSION: READING TO MAKE MEANING FROM SPATIAL DESCRIPTION
Spatial description refers to the method of describing the physical location, arrangement, position, and relationship of objects, people, or places in space. Understanding spatial descriptions is crucial for comprehending settings in texts, following directions, and visualizing scenes accurately.
ELEMENTS OF SPATIAL DESCRIPTION:

Position and Location Words:

Above, below, beneath, under, over, on top of

Beside, next to, adjacent to, alongside

Between, among, amid, amidst

In front of, behind, in back of

Inside, outside, within, without

Near, close to, far from, distant from

Opposite, across from, facing

Directional Words:

Toward, away from, to, from

Into, out of, onto, off of

Through, across, along, past

Up, down, upward, downward

North, south, east, west (cardinal directions)

Left, right, straight ahead

Forward, backward, sideways
Relative Position: Writers often describe locations relative to known landmarks or reference points. Example: "The library is located between the science lab and the cafeteria, directly opposite the main administrative block."
Layered Description: Effective spatial descriptions often work from general to specific (or vice versa), helping readers build a complete mental picture. Example: "The airport is a massive structure. In the center stands the main terminal building. To its left, three hangars house aircraft under maintenance. The control tower rises prominently on the right side, overseeing the runways that stretch out before it."
Vocabulary - Air Transport:

Aircraft/Airplane: A powered vehicle designed for air travel

Runway: A long strip of ground where aircraft take off and land

Terminal: The building where passengers check in, board, and collect luggage

Hangar: A large building where aircraft are stored and maintained

Control Tower: The tall building from which air traffic is monitored and directed

Cockpit: The area where the pilot and co-pilot operate the aircraft

Fuselage: The main body of an aircraft

Wings: The flat surfaces that provide lift for the aircraft

Tarmac/Apron: The paved area where aircraft park, load, and unload

Boarding Pass: A document that allows a passenger to board an aircraft

Check-in Counter: Where passengers register and receive boarding passes

Baggage Claim: The area where passengers collect their luggage after a flight

Immigration: The process of entering or leaving a country

Customs: Where officials check goods being brought into a country

Flight Attendant: Crew members who ensure passenger safety and comfort
Pilot/Captain: The person who flies the aircraft

Passenger: A person traveling in an aircraft

Cargo: Goods transported by aircraft

Altitude: The height of an aircraft above sea level

Turbulence: Irregular air movements that cause bumpy flights

Reading Strategy: When reading spatial descriptions:

Identify the main reference point or central location

Note directional and positional words

Visualize the scene mentally or sketch it

Pay attention to distances and relationships between objects

Consider the perspective (bird's eye view, ground level, etc.)

EVALUATION:

What is spatial description and why is it important in reading comprehension?

List five position words and five directional words used in spatial descriptions.

Read this passage and draw a simple diagram of the airport layout: "Murtala Muhammed International Airport in Lagos has two terminals. The international terminal is on the western side, while the domestic terminal sits on the eastern side. Between them lies a large parking area. The control tower stands prominently behind the international terminal, overlooking the three parallel runways that stretch northward."
Define the following air transport terms: runway, hangar, terminal, cockpit.

Using spatial description words, describe the location of your classroom within your school building in four sentences.
ASSIGNMENT:
Write a detailed paragraph (8-10 sentences) describing the layout of your home using at least ten different spatial description words. Underline the spatial words.
Create a vocabulary list of fifteen additional words related to air transport beyond those provided in the lesson.
Read the first chapter of your prose text and identify at least three examples of spatial descriptions used by the author. Write them out and underline the spatial words.
Draw and label a diagram of an airport, including at least eight different components (terminal, runway, control tower, etc.).
Write five sentences describing the route from your classroom to the school library using directional and positional words.
WEEK 1 - COMPOSITION: INTRODUCTION TO EXPOSITORY ESSAY

An expository essay is a type of writing that explains, informs, describes, or clarifies a topic to the reader using facts, statistics, examples, and logical explanations. The word "expository" comes from "expose," meaning to reveal or explain. Unlike narrative essays (which tell stories) or persuasive essays (which argue a point), expository essays focus on providing clear, objective information.
Purpose of Expository Essays:

To explain a process or concept

To inform readers about a topic

To describe something in detail

To analyze a situation or idea

To present facts without personal opinions

Characteristics of Expository Essays:

Objective Tone: Written in third person, avoiding personal opinions

Clear Thesis Statement: States the main topic that will be explained

Factual Information: Uses facts, examples, statistics, and evidence

Logical Organization: Information presented in a clear, systematic manner

Formal Language: Uses standard English without slang or colloquialisms

Transitions: Uses connecting words to link ideas smoothly

STRUCTURE OF AN EXPOSITORY ESSAY:

Introduction (Opening Paragraph):

Hook: An interesting opening sentence to grab attention (question, fact, or quote)

Background Information: Brief context about the topic
Thesis Statement: A clear statement explaining what the essay will explain

Example: "Have you ever wondered how airplanes stay in the sky? Air travel has revolutionized global transportation since the Wright brothers' first flight in 1903. This essay will explain the basic principles that allow aircraft to fly safely through the atmosphere."
Body Paragraphs (Usually 3-5 paragraphs): Each body paragraph should:

Begin with a topic sentence introducing the main point

Provide supporting details, facts, examples, or explanations

Use transition words to connect ideas

End with a concluding sentence that relates back to the thesis Example Topic Sentences:
"The first principle of flight is lift, created by air flowing over the aircraft's wings."

"In addition to lift, thrust from the engines propels the aircraft forward."

"Understanding drag and gravity helps explain how pilots control aircraft movement."

Conclusion (Closing Paragraph):

Restate the thesis in different words

Summarize main points briefly

Leave the reader with a final thought or implication

Do NOT introduce new information

Example: "Understanding these four forces—lift, thrust, drag, and gravity—reveals how aircraft achieve and maintain flight. Modern aviation depends on balancing these forces effectively. As technology advances, engineers continue to refine these principles to make air travel safer and more efficient."

TYPES OF EXPOSITORY ESSAYS:
Process Essay: Explains how to do something or how something works

Descriptive Essay: Describes characteristics of a person, place, or thing

Compare and Contrast Essay: Shows similarities and differences

Cause and Effect Essay: Explains reasons and results

Problem and Solution Essay: Identifies a problem and proposes solutions

Classification Essay: Divides a topic into categories

Language Features:

Present tense (mostly)

Third person (he, she, it, they)

Technical or specific vocabulary related to the topic

Transition words: firstly, secondly, furthermore, moreover, however, therefore, in conclusion
Definition of key terms

Examples and illustrations

Writing Tips:

Research your topic thoroughly before writing

Organize information logically

Use specific, concrete examples

Define technical terms for readers

Avoid personal pronouns (I, we, you) unless appropriate

Write in clear, straightforward sentences
Proofread for clarity and accuracy

EVALUATION:

Define an expository essay and explain its main purpose.

What are three key characteristics that distinguish expository essays from other essay types?
Outline the three main parts of an expository essay and briefly explain what each part should contain.
Why is it important to use facts and examples rather than personal opinions in expository writing?
Write a thesis statement for an expository essay on "How Students Can Improve Their Reading Skills."
ASSIGNMENT:

Choose one of these topics and write a complete introduction paragraph (4-6 sentences) with a clear thesis statement: (a) How to Prepare for Examinations (b) The Importance of Clean Water (c) How Mobile Phones Work.
Write three topic sentences for body paragraphs that could follow your introduction in question 1.
List ten transition words commonly used in expository writing and use five of them in complete sentences.
Read an article from your textbook or a newspaper and identify whether it is expository writing. Explain your answer by identifying three features of expository writing in the article.
Write a conclusion paragraph (4-5 sentences) for an expository essay on "The Process of Photosynthesis in Plants."
WEEK 1 - LITERATURE: THEMES AND CHARACTERIZATION IN PROSE

THEMES: A theme is the central idea, message, or underlying meaning that runs throughout a literary work. It is the author's insight about life, human nature, or society that the reader can extract from the story. Themes are universal truths or observations that transcend the specific story being told.
DIFFERENCE BETWEEN TOPIC AND THEME:

Topic: What the story is about (love, war, friendship)

Theme: What the story says about that topic (love requires sacrifice; war destroys innocence; true friendship overcomes obstacles)
Types of Themes:

Major/Central Theme: The most important message running throughout the work

Minor Theme: Secondary ideas that support or complement the major theme

Common Universal Themes in Literature:

Good versus evil

Coming of age/loss of innocence

The power of love

Individual versus society

Courage and heroism

The corruption of power

Survival and resilience

Betrayal and loyalty

Appearance versus reality

The search for identity

Justice and injustice
The effects of war

Family relationships

Death and mortality

Tradition versus change

How to Identify Themes:

Consider the title—it often hints at the theme

Examine what characters learn or how they change

Look for repeated ideas, symbols, or motifs

Analyze conflicts and how they are resolved

Consider the author's message or purpose

Note important statements made by characters

Think about the story's outcome and its implications

Expressing Themes: Themes should be stated as complete sentences expressing an idea, not just as single words.
Weak: "Friendship" (This is just a topic) Strong: "True friendship requires honesty and sacrifice." (This is a theme)
Weak: "War" Strong: "War destroys the innocence of those who fight in it."

CHARACTERIZATION: Characterization is the process by which an author creates, develops, and reveals the personalities, traits, and qualities of characters in a story. It helps readers understand why characters behave the way they do and makes them believable.
Types of Characters:

By Role:

Protagonist: The main character around whom the story revolves
Antagonist: The character or force opposing the protagonist

Supporting Characters: Minor characters who help develop the plot

Foil: A character who contrasts with another to highlight particular qualities

By Development:

Round Characters: Complex, multi-dimensional characters with many traits

Flat Characters: Simple, one-dimensional characters with few traits

Dynamic Characters: Characters who change significantly throughout the story

Static Characters: Characters who remain essentially the same throughout

Methods of Characterization:

Direct Characterization: The author explicitly tells readers about a character's traits. Example: "John was a brave and honest young man who never told lies."
Indirect Characterization (STEAL Method): The author shows character traits through:

S - Speech: What the character says and how they say it

Dialogue reveals personality, education, emotions, and attitudes

Example: A character who speaks rudely shows disrespect

T - Thoughts: What the character thinks, feels, and believes

Internal monologue reveals motivations and values

Example: A character constantly worrying about others shows compassion

E - Effect on Others: How other characters react to and speak about the character

Other characters' opinions provide insight

Example: If everyone respects a character, they likely possess admirable qualities

A - Actions: What the character does; their behavior and choices

Actions often speak louder than words
Example: A character who shares food with the poor demonstrates generosity

L - Looks: The character's physical appearance and how they present themselves

Physical descriptions can reflect personality or social status

Example: A character always dressed neatly suggests discipline and self-respect

Character Traits: These are the distinctive qualities and characteristics that define a character's personality:
Physical traits: tall, short, strong, weak, beautiful, scarred

Personality traits: brave, cowardly, kind, cruel, honest, deceitful

Emotional traits: calm, anxious, cheerful, melancholy

Intellectual traits: intelligent, foolish, wise, naive

Moral traits: good, evil, virtuous, corrupt

Analyzing Characters: When analyzing characters, consider:

Their role in the story (protagonist, antagonist, etc.)

Their physical description and significance

Their personality traits and how they're revealed

Their relationships with other characters

Their motivations and goals

How they change or develop (or don't)

What they represent symbolically

Their contribution to the theme

Relationship Between Theme and Characterization: Characters often embody or learn the themes of a story. Their development and the choices they make frequently illustrate the author's message. For example, a character who learns to value honesty despite consequences might illustrate the theme "Integrity is more important than personal gain."

EVALUATION:

Define theme and explain the difference between a topic and a theme. Give one example.

What is characterization? Describe the two main types of characterization with examples.

Explain the STEAL method of indirect characterization and give one example for each letter.
Identify one major theme in your recommended prose text and explain how it is developed through the story.
Choose one character from your prose text. Is this character round or flat? Dynamic or static? Explain your answer with evidence from the text.

ASSIGNMENT:

Identify three themes in your recommended prose text. Write each theme as a complete sentence and provide evidence from the story that supports each theme.
Select the protagonist from your prose text and write a detailed character analysis (one paragraph of 8-10 sentences) describing their personality, motivations, and role in the story.
Find three examples of indirect characterization in your prose text. For each example, identify which STEAL method is being used and explain what it reveals about the character.
Create a character profile for one character in your prose text including: name, physical description, personality traits, role in the story, relationships with other characters, and one important action they perform.
Write a paragraph (6-8 sentences) explaining how one character in your prose text helps to develop or illustrate one of the story's major themes.
WEEK 2 - SPEECH WORK: IDENTIFICATION OF DIPHTHONGS IN WORDS

This lesson focuses on recognizing and pronouncing diphthongs /eə/ and /ɔɪ/ in words.

Diphthong /eə/ (as in "air"): Glides from /e/ to /ə/. Common spellings: "air," "are," "ear," "ere," "eir"

Examples:

Mary, care, fair, hair, pair, stair, chair, dare, rare, spare
Bear, pear, wear, swear, tear (rip)
There, where, their, prayer, layer, mayor
Square, aware, declare, parent, apparent

Diphthong /ɔɪ/ (as in "boy"): Glides from /ɔ/ to /ɪ/. Common spellings: "oi," "oy" Examples:
Boy, toy, joy, employ, destroy, enjoy, annoy
Boil, coil, foil, oil, soil, spoil, toil
Coin, join, point, joint, appoint
Noise, voice, choice, rejoice
Avoid, loyal, royal, voyage

Identification Strategies:

Learn spelling patterns (air, oi, oy)
Listen for the gliding quality between two vowel sounds
Feel tongue movement during pronunciation
Practice minimal pairs: bear/beer, boil/bile, care/car

Common Errors:

Pronouncing /eə/ as pure /e/
Confusing /eə/ with /eɪ/ (Mary vs. merry)
Pronouncing /ɔɪ/ as two syllables
Confusing /ɔɪ/ with /aɪ/ (boil vs. bile)

EVALUATION:

Identify the diphthong in these words: chair, loyal, repair, destroy, aware.
Provide five words containing /eə/ and five containing /ɔɪ/.
Explain the pronunciation difference between: bear/beer, boil/bile.
Underline words with diphthongs in this sentence: "The boy's hair was fair, and he enjoyed playing near the stairs."
Why is correct diphthong pronunciation important? Give two examples.

ASSIGNMENT:

List ten words with /eə/ and ten with /ɔɪ/, organized by spelling patterns.
Write five sentences using words with /eə/ or /ɔɪ/. Underline the diphthong words.
Find eight words containing /eə/ or /ɔɪ/ in your prose text. Note the page numbers.
Practice these minimal pairs: bear/beer, care/car, fair/fear, boil/bile, boy/buy.
Write a paragraph (6 sentences) using at least six different words with /eə/ or /ɔɪ/.
WEEK 2 - GRAMMAR: HIGHLIGHTING ACTIVE VOICE

Active voice occurs when the subject performs the action. It creates clear, direct, and effective sentences.

Structure: Subject + Verb + Object

Why Use Active Voice:

Clarity: Makes it clear who does what
Active: "The teacher explained the lesson."
Passive: "The lesson was explained."
Directness: Straightforward and easy to understand
Active: "The committee made a decision."
Passive: "A decision was made by the committee."
Brevity: Uses fewer words
Active: "Lightning struck the tree." (4 words)
Passive: "The tree was struck by lightning." (6 words)
Engagement: Creates dynamic writing
Active: "The detective solved the mystery."
Accountability: Shows who is responsible
Active: "The company increased prices."

Identifying Active Voice:

Subject comes before verb
Subject performs the action
Answers "Who did what?"
No "be" + past participle Examples Across Tenses: Simple Present:
Students write essays.
The sun rises in the east.
Dogs bark at strangers.

Simple Past:

The chef cooked a meal.
She finished her homework.
They built a new school.

Present Continuous:

The children are playing football.
I am reading a novel.

Present Perfect:

She has completed the project.
We have visited the museum.

Future Tense:

The school will announce results tomorrow.
I will finish the assignment tonight.

When Active Voice is Most Effective:

Everyday communication and conversation
News reports and journalism
Academic writing (in many cases)
Business correspondence
Instructions and directions
Narrative and creative writing
When the doer is important
Practice Identifying Active Voice: Identify which sentences are active:

"The farmer planted corn." (Active)
"The cake was baked by Mary." (Passive)
"John rides his bicycle daily." (Active)
"The letter was delivered yesterday." (Passive)

EVALUATION:

Define active voice and explain why it is preferred in most writing.
Write five sentences in active voice using these verbs: teach, play, build, write, cook.
Identify whether these sentences are active or passive: (a) "The boy kicked the ball." (b) "The ball was kicked by the boy." (c) "Mother prepared breakfast."
Rewrite these passive sentences in active voice: (a) "The book was read by Sarah." (b) "The house was painted by workers."
Explain three benefits of using active voice in writing.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write ten sentences in active voice about daily activities at home and school.
Identify five active voice sentences in your prose text. Write them out with page numbers.
Create five sentences in each of these tenses using active voice: simple present, simple past, present continuous.
Find three passive voice sentences in a newspaper or textbook and rewrite them in active voice.
Write a short paragraph (7-8 sentences) describing your journey to school using only active voice.
WEEK 2 - READING & COMPREHENSION: APPROPRIATE INTRODUCTIONS AND CONCLUSIONS; FISHING VOCABULARY

INTRODUCTIONS

An introduction is the opening paragraph that presents the topic and captures the reader's attention.

Functions:

Grab reader's attention
Introduce the topic
Provide background information
Present the thesis/main idea
Set the tone

Types of Introductions:

Question: "Have you ever wondered why fish can breathe underwater?"
Fact/Statistic: "Over 70% of the Earth's surface is covered by water, making fishing one of humanity's oldest professions."
Quotation: "Benjamin Franklin once said, 'Give a man a fish and you feed him for a day; teach a man to fish and you feed him for a lifetime.'"
Anecdote: "Last summer, I watched a fisherman patiently cast his net into the river at dawn."
Definition: "Fishing is the activity of catching fish from seas, rivers, or lakes for food or sport."
Bold Statement: "Fishing has sustained human civilization for thousands of years."

Structure: Hook (1 sentence) → Context (2-3 sentences) → Thesis Statement (1 sentence)

Example: "Have you ever wondered how fish end up on your dinner plate? Fishing is one of the world's oldest industries, providing food and employment for millions. From small river
fishermen to large ocean trawlers, fishing methods have evolved over centuries. This essay explores the various methods of fishing and their importance to human society."

Characteristics of Good Introductions:

Brief and focused (4-6 sentences)
Relevant and engaging
Clear thesis statement
Appropriate tone
No unnecessary details

CONCLUSIONS

A conclusion is the closing paragraph that summarizes main points and leaves a final impression.

Functions:

Signal the end
Restate the main idea
Summarize key points
Provide final thoughts
Leave lasting impression

Types of Conclusions:

Summary: "In conclusion, fishing provides food, employment, and economic benefits to communities worldwide."
Call to Action: "We must all work together to prevent overfishing and protect marine life for future generations."
Prediction: "As technology advances, fishing methods will become more efficient and sustainable."
Question: "Will we manage our fish resources wisely, or will future generations face empty oceans?"
Recommendation: "Governments should enforce fishing regulations to ensure sustainable practices."

Structure: Transition word → Thesis restatement → Summary (2-3 sentences) → Final thought

Example: "In conclusion, fishing remains vital to human survival and economic development. Traditional and modern methods each have advantages, from simple nets to advanced sonar technology. Sustainable practices ensure fish populations for future generations. By balancing human needs with environmental protection, fishing can continue to nourish humanity for centuries to come."

Characteristics of Good Conclusions:

Brief (4-6 sentences)
No new information
Clear ending
Connects to introduction
Appropriate tone

Common Mistakes to Avoid:

Introductions: Starting too broadly, lacking clear thesis, using clichés, being too long Conclusions: Introducing new ideas, repeating introduction word-for-word, apologizing, being too abrupt

VOCABULARY - FISHING WORDS General Terms:
Fishing: Catching fish
Fisherman/Fisher: Person who catches fish
Angler: Fishes with rod and line
Catch: Fish caught
Bait: Food to attract fish
Hook: Sharp curved device for catching fish
Line: Cord attached to rod
Rod: Long flexible stick for fishing
Reel: Device for winding line
Net: Mesh for catching fish

Types of Fishing:

Trawling: Dragging large nets through water
Angling: Fishing with rod, line, and hook
Spear fishing: Using sharp pointed weapon
Netting: Using nets
Ice fishing: Fishing through holes in frozen water

Equipment:

Trawler: Large fishing boat
Canoe: Small narrow boat
Tackle: Fishing equipment
Lure: Artificial bait
Sinker: Weight to sink line
Float/Bobber: Shows when fish bites
Creel: Basket for caught fish
Trap: Device for catching fish

Locations:

River: Flowing watercourse
Stream: Small river
Lake: Large still water body
Pond: Small still water body
Ocean/Sea: Large salt water body
Shore: Land along water's edge
Bank: Sloping land beside river

Fish and Marine Life:

School: Group of fish swimming together
Spawn: Lay eggs (reproduction)
Fry: Young fish
Scale: Protective plates on fish
Gill: Breathing organ
Fin: Extension for swimming

Actions:

Cast: Throw line into water
Reel in: Pull line back
Bite: When fish takes bait
Land: Bring fish to shore
Release: Return fish to water

EVALUATION:

What are three functions of an introduction?
Identify this introduction type: "Over 3 billion people depend on fish as their primary source of protein."

List three elements a good conclusion should include.

ASSIGNMENT:

(c) anecdote.

Read an article in your textbook. Analyze its introduction and conclusion, identifying strengths.

WEEK 2 - COMPOSITION: ELEMENTS OF WRITING (CONTINUED) THE FIVE ELEMENTS:
PURPOSE: Why you're writing

To inform (expository)

To persuade (argumentative)

To entertain (creative)

To describe (descriptive)

To narrate (narrative)

AUDIENCE: Who will read it

Consider: age, knowledge level, interests, culture

Adjust language and complexity accordingly

TONE: Attitude conveyed

Formal: Professional, serious

Informal: Casual, friendly

Others: optimistic, serious, humorous, respectful

ORGANIZATION: Logical arrangement

Chronological: Time sequence

Spatial: By location

Order of importance: Most to least
Comparison/contrast: Similarities/differences

Problem-solution

Cause-effect

CLARITY: Easy to understand

Use simple language

Write complete sentences

Be specific

Avoid ambiguity

Use transitions

Stay focused

Define technical terms

Additional Elements:

Unity: All sentences support one main idea

Coherence: Ideas flow smoothly

EVALUATION:

List and explain the five main elements of writing.

How does knowing your audience affect writing? Give two examples.

Differentiate formal and informal tone with examples.

Explain three ways to achieve clarity.

Which pattern for: (a) "How to Prepare Jollof Rice" (b) "My Bedroom"?

ASSIGNMENT:
Write two paragraphs on "Benefits of Reading" for: (a) classmates (b) parents.

Improve clarity: (a) "The thing was very nice." (b) "He went to the place."

Write a paragraph on "My School" using one organizational pattern. Label it.

Identify purpose, audience, and tone in a passage from your prose text.

WEEK 2 - LITERATURE: SETTING AND PLOT IN PROSE

SETTING

Setting refers to the time and place where a story occurs.

Components of Setting:

Place/Location:

Geographical: Country, city, village, building

Physical Environment: Indoors/outdoors, natural/urban

Atmosphere: Weather, lighting, sounds, smells

Examples: "A small fishing village on the coast of Lagos" / "The dark, dusty attic filled with old trunks"
Time:

Historical Period: Past, present, future, specific era

Season: Summer, rainy season, harmattan

Time of Day: Morning, noon, night, dawn, dusk

Duration: One day, several years

Examples: "During the Nigerian Civil War in the late 1960s" / "One hot afternoon in July"

Social/Cultural Environment:

Customs and traditions

Social class structure

Political climate

Religious beliefs

Economic conditions
Examples: "A traditional Igbo community where elders hold authority" / "A wealthy urban neighborhood in modern Lagos"
Importance of Setting:

Creates Mood: Dark night = suspense; sunny day = happiness

Influences Characters: Village character may be more traditional than city character

Advances Plot: Storm prevents characters from reaching destination

Reveals Theme: Decaying mansion symbolizes moral corruption

Provides Authenticity: Makes story believable

Types of Setting:

Integral Setting: Essential; story couldn't happen elsewhere (pearl divers need ocean)

Backdrop Setting: Less important; could occur anywhere (friendship story in any school)

PLOT

Plot is the sequence of events that make up a story.

Five Elements of Plot Structure:

EXPOSITION (Introduction):

Introduces characters

Establishes setting

Provides background

Presents initial situation

Example: "Okonkwo was a wealthy farmer in Umuofia village, known for his strength."

RISING ACTION (Complication):

Conflict develops
Tension builds

Characters face challenges

Suspense increases

Example: "When Okonkwo accidentally kills a clansman, he must flee for seven years."

CLIMAX (Turning Point):

Highest point of tension

Most intense moment

Main conflict peaks

Point of no return

Example: "Okonkwo kills the messenger, realizing his people will not fight the colonizers."

FALLING ACTION:

Events after climax

Tension decreases

Consequences unfold

Loose ends tie up

Example: "Okonkwo realizes he stands alone. Colonial officials come looking for him."

RESOLUTION (Denouement):

Conclusion

Conflicts resolved

Final outcome revealed

Story ends

Example: "The District Commissioner finds Okonkwo has taken his own life."

Types of Conflict:
Man vs. Man: Character against another character

Man vs. Self: Internal struggle

Man vs. Society: Against social norms/rules

Man vs. Nature: Against natural forces

Man vs. Fate/Supernatural: Against destiny or supernatural

Plot Devices:

Foreshadowing: Hints about future ("Dark clouds suggested trouble ahead")

Flashback: Return to past events

Suspense: Creates uncertainty

Surprise/Twist: Unexpected turn

Characteristics of Good Plot:

Causality (cause and effect)

Unity (all events contribute)

Plausibility (believable)

Suspense (keeps interest)

Conflict (provides tension)

Relationship between Setting and Plot:

Setting creates conflict (harsh environment challenges characters)

Plot events change setting (character moves)

Time setting affects possibilities (technology, social norms)

Place setting limits or enables actions

EVALUATION:
Define setting and list its three main components.

Explain how setting can influence plot with one example.

List and briefly describe the five plot elements.

Identify conflict type: (a) Character surviving a hurricane (b) Student fighting unfair school rule.
Describe the setting (time and place) of your prose text.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a detailed description of your prose text's setting covering place, time, and social/cultural environment (8-10 sentences).
Identify and describe the five plot elements in your prose text with specific examples.

Draw a plot diagram for your prose text, labeling all five elements and key events.

Explain how setting influences plot in your prose text (three specific examples).

Choose one chapter: identify (a) setting details (b) main conflict (c) how events contribute to overall plot.
WEEK 3 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: INTRODUCTION TO CONSONANT SOUNDS

Meaning of Consonant Sounds:
Consonant sounds are speech sounds produced when the flow of air from the lungs is partly or completely blocked by the tongue, teeth, or lips. In English, there are 24 consonant sounds represented by different letters or combinations of letters.
Consonant sounds are categorized according to place of articulation (where the sound is made in the mouth) and manner of articulation (how the sound is made).
Classification of English Consonant Sounds:

Plosives (Stops): Air is completely blocked and suddenly released.
Examples: /p/, /b/, /t/, /d/, /k/, /g/

Examples in words: pen, ball, table, dog, cat, goat.

Fricatives: Air passes through a narrow opening causing friction.
Examples: /f/, /v/, /θ/, /ð/, /s/, /z/, /ʃ/, /ʒ/, /h/

Examples in words: fish, van, thin, this, sun, zoo, shoe, vision, hat.

Affricates: A combination of a plosive and a fricative sound.
Examples: /tʃ/, /dʒ/

Examples in words: church, judge.

Nasals: Air passes through the nose while the mouth is closed.
Examples: /m/, /n/, /ŋ/

Examples in words: man, nine, song.

Lateral: The air passes along the sides of the tongue.
Example: /l/

Examples in words: love, bell.
Approximants (Semi-vowels): Produced with little obstruction of air.
Examples: /r/, /w/, /j/

Examples in words: red, window, yes.

Importance of Consonant Sounds:

They help in correct pronunciation.

They differentiate meaning between words (e.g., fan vs van).

They contribute to rhythm and clarity in speech.

EVALUATION:

Define consonant sounds.

How many consonant sounds are there in English?

Mention three types of consonant sounds and give one example each.

Differentiate between /p/ and /b/ sounds with examples.

Explain why consonant sounds are important in speech.

ASSIGNMENT:

List all 24 English consonant sounds and write two example words for each.

Identify the consonant sounds in the following words: goat, fish, man, shoe, judge.

Write five pairs of words that differ by one consonant sound (e.g., fan–van, pat–bat).
WEEK 3 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: USE OF PASSIVE VOICE WITH EXAMPLE
The passive voice is used when the subject of the sentence receives the action instead of performing it. The focus is on the action or the object rather than the doer.
Structure:
Object + Verb (be + past participle) + by + Subject Example:
Active: The boy kicked the ball.

Passive: The ball was kicked by the boy.

Formation of Passive Voice:
To form the passive, the verb “to be” is combined with the past participle of the main verb.

is/are + past participle (present tense)

Active: They clean the room.

Passive: The room is cleaned.

was/were + past participle (past tense)

Active: They wrote the letter.

Passive: The letter was written.

Uses of the Passive Voice:

When the doer of the action is unknown:

The window was broken last night.

When the action is more important than the doer:

The new bridge was opened yesterday.

In formal or scientific writing:

The experiment was conducted in a laboratory.
More Examples:

Active: The teacher marked the scripts.
Passive: The scripts were marked by the teacher.

Active: A mechanic repaired the car.
Passive: The car was repaired by a mechanic.

EVALUATION:

What is the passive voice?

Write the structure of a sentence in the passive voice.

Change these sentences to passive voice:
The boy ate the mango.
The nurse treated the patient.

State two reasons for using passive voice.

Convert: They will build a new school. to passive.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five sentences in the active voice and change each to the passive voice.

Identify the tense used in each sentence after conversion.

Explain the difference in meaning or focus between the active and passive forms.
WEEK 3 – READING & COMPREHENSION

TOPIC: IDENTIFYING KEYWORDS IN PASSAGES THAT SIGNAL DIRECTION; VOCABULARY – KITCHEN WORDS

Understanding Keywords in Directional Passages:
When reading a passage that gives directions or describes movement, certain keywords help the reader to follow the movement or arrangement of things. These are known as directional words or prepositions of movement and place.
Examples of Keywords That Signal Direction:

Prepositions: in, on, under, between, behind, beside, near, through, across, along, around, opposite, behind.
Directional Words: left, right, north, south, forward, backward, inside, outside, top, bottom.
Example Passage:
From the main gate, walk straight ahead until you reach the roundabout. Turn right and continue until you see a tall building with a green roof. The kitchen is behind that building, next to the dining hall.
Explanation:
The words straight ahead, right, behind, next to are keywords that show direction and help the reader visualize the path described.

VOCABULARY – KITCHEN WORDS:
Kitchen utensils and tools are used in food preparation and cooking. Examples include:

Spoon, fork, knife, plate, pot, frying pan, gas cooker, stove, cup, bowl, kettle, tray, blender, chopping board, grater.

EVALUATION:
What are directional keywords?

Mention five examples of words that signal direction.

In the passage, where is the kitchen located?

Explain the importance of identifying directional words in a passage.

Write five vocabulary items found in the kitchen.

ASSIGNMENT:
