WEEK 3 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: EXPOSITORY ESSAY – THE EBOLA VIRUS

Meaning of Expository Essay:
An expository essay explains, informs, or describes a topic in a clear and logical manner. The writer gives facts, examples, and explanations without expressing personal opinion or argument.
Features of an Expository Essay:

Introduction that states the topic clearly.

Logical presentation of facts.

Use of examples and illustrations.

Conclusion that summarizes the main points.

Topic: The Ebola Virus
The Ebola Virus Disease (EVD) is a severe and contagious illness that affects humans and some animals. It was first discovered in the Democratic Republic of Congo near the Ebola River in 1976.
Causes:

The disease is caused by the Ebola virus, which is transmitted from infected animals like fruit bats, monkeys, and chimpanzees to humans.
It also spreads through direct contact with blood, body fluids, or contaminated materials of an infected person.
Symptoms:

Fever, headache, muscle pain, sore throat, vomiting, diarrhea, internal bleeding, and rash.

Prevention:

Avoid contact with infected people or animals.

Wash hands regularly with soap and water.
Use gloves and protective clothing when caring for the sick.

Avoid eating bush meat.

Report any suspected case to health authorities immediately.

EVALUATION:

What is an expository essay?

What causes the Ebola Virus Disease?

Mention four symptoms of Ebola.

List three ways of preventing Ebola.

Why is the Ebola Virus dangerous?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write an expository essay of about 200 words on “The Ebola Virus.”

Include introduction, body, and conclusion in your essay.

Suggest two ways the government can help prevent the spread of Ebola.
WEEK 3 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: POETRY – IDENTIFYING LANGUAGE IN A SELECTED POEM

Meaning of Poetry:
Poetry is a type of literary work written in lines and stanzas to express feelings, ideas, or experiences through rhythm, imagery, and sound. Poets use special language called poetic language to make their poems more expressive and beautiful.
Language in Poetry:
Poetic language includes figures of speech, sound devices, and imagery that create musicality and emotion.
Features of Poetic Language:

Figures of Speech:

Simile: comparison using “like” or “as.”
Example: Her smile is as bright as the morning sun.

Metaphor: comparison without using “like” or “as.” Example: Time is a thief.
Personification: giving human qualities to non-human things. Example: The wind whispered through the trees.
Sound Devices:

Rhyme: repetition of similar sounds at the end of lines. Example: The sun is high / up in the sky.
Alliteration: repetition of initial consonant sounds. Example: Peter Piper picked a peck of pickled peppers.
Imagery:

Words that appeal to the senses (sight, sound, smell, touch, taste). Example: The fragrant roses danced under the soft moonlight.
Example Poem:
“The Sun”

The golden sun climbs up the hill, Warming the earth so calm and still. Birds sing sweet songs to greet the day, As children laugh and run to play.
Language Analysis:

Rhyme: hill / still and day / play

Personification: The sun climbs (as if it were human).

Imagery: golden sun, warm earth, birds sing — appeals to sight and sound.

EVALUATION:

What is poetry?

Mention three features of poetic language.

Identify two figures of speech used in the poem above.

Explain one example of imagery from the poem.

Why do poets use poetic language?

ASSIGNMENT:

Select any short poem and identify three figures of speech used in it.

Explain how the poet uses sound devices to create beauty in the poem.

Write your own four-line poem using rhyme and imagery.
WEEK 4 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: SYLLABLES – WORDS OF TWO, THREE, AND FOUR SYLLABLES

A syllable is a single, uninterrupted sound of a spoken word. It is the smallest unit of pronunciation containing one vowel sound, with or without surrounding consonants. Every English word is made up of one or more syllables.
How to Identify Syllables:

Clapping or tapping method: Each time you clap when pronouncing a word, you hear one syllable.
Example: teacher (tea/cher) – two claps → two syllables.

Listening for vowel sounds: Count the number of vowel sounds (not letters) in the word. Each vowel sound represents a syllable.
Example: important (im/por/tant) – three vowel sounds → three syllables.

Using pronunciation stress patterns: Long words can be divided into syllables based on where natural pauses or stress occur.
Examples of Words According to Syllable Count:

Two-syllable words: mother, father, table, garden, pencil.

Three-syllable words: beautiful, remember, hospital, tomorrow, exciting.

Four-syllable words: electricity, calculator, opportunity, motivation, celebration.

Importance of Syllable Division:

It helps in accurate pronunciation and spelling.

It aids in word stress and rhythm in speech.

It assists in poetic meter and musical rhythm in language.
EVALUATION:

Define a syllable.

State two methods of identifying syllables in words.

Classify these words by the number of syllables: computer, doctor, opportunity, banana, education.
Explain two reasons why syllable identification is important.

Write down three words each for two-, three-, and four-syllable categories.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five four-syllable words and use each in a sentence to show their meaning.
WEEK 4 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: EXERCISES ON ACTIVE VOICE

The active voice is used when the subject of a sentence performs the action expressed by the verb. The focus is on the doer of the action rather than the receiver.
Structure:
Subject + Verb + Object

Example: The boy (subject) kicked (verb) the ball (object).

In contrast, the passive voice focuses on the receiver of the action:

The ball was kicked by the boy.

Features of the Active Voice:

The subject performs the action.

The verb form is usually simple and direct.

The sentence sounds stronger and clearer.

It makes writing more engaging.

Examples:

Ngozi baked a cake. (Active)

A cake was baked by Ngozi. (Passive)

The driver repaired the car. (Active)

The car was repaired by the driver. (Passive)

Importance of Using Active Voice:

It creates clearer and more concise sentences.

It improves communication by showing responsibility for actions.
It enhances reader engagement.

EVALUATION:

What is the active voice?

Mention two features of the active voice.

Change these sentences to active voice:
The prize was won by the pupil.
The song was sung by the choir.

Construct two original sentences in active voice.

State one reason why the active voice is preferred in speech and writing.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five sentences in the active voice.

Convert each into the passive voice.

Identify the subject, verb, and object in each sentence.
WEEK 4 – READING

TOPIC: MAKING MEANING FROM SPATIAL DESCRIPTION; VOCABULARY – WORDS FROM THE SICK BAY

Spatial Description means describing where things, people, or objects are located in a particular space. It helps the reader to visualize or picture a place in their mind.
Key Words Used in Spatial Description:

Prepositions of place: in, on, under, beside, between, behind, near, above, below, around.
Directional words: north, south, east, west, left, right, front, back, opposite.

Example Passage:
When I entered the sick bay, I noticed two white beds placed on the left side of the room. Between the beds stood a small table with a water jug and a glass on it. On the right side, there was a large cupboard where medicine bottles were neatly arranged. The nurse sat behind her desk near the window, and a patient lay quietly on one of the beds.
Making Meaning:
From the passage, we can picture how the room looks based on the arrangement of objects and their positions. The use of words like on the left, between, behind, near, and on the right helps us create a mental image.
Vocabulary – Words from the Sick Bay:
Doctor, nurse, thermometer, injection, medicine, patient, stretcher, syringe, plaster, bandage, prescription, tablet.

EVALUATION:

What is spatial description?

Mention four prepositions used in describing position.
From the passage, where was the nurse sitting?

Explain how spatial description helps a reader understand a passage.

Give the meanings of these words: thermometer, stretcher, plaster.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a short paragraph describing your classroom using spatial description.

Underline all the prepositions of place used in your paragraph.

List ten vocabulary words related to health or the sick bay.
WEEK 4 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY – TRIBALISM IS A LESSER EVIL THAN CULTISM

Meaning of Argumentative Essay:
An argumentative essay is a form of writing in which the writer expresses opinions for or against a given topic. The aim is to convince the reader to agree with your point of view using logical reasoning and evidence.
Features of an Argumentative Essay:

Introduction: States the topic and the side you are supporting.

Body Paragraphs: Present your arguments with examples and evidence.

Opposing Viewpoint: Acknowledge the opposite side and refute it.

Conclusion: Summarize your arguments and restate your stand.

Topic Discussion: Tribalism is a Lesser Evil than Cultism

For the Motion (Agreeing):

Tribalism, though wrong, mainly shows preference for one’s tribe; it rarely leads to crime or violence.
Cultism involves killings, drug abuse, and destruction in schools and society.

Tribalism can be corrected through education and unity campaigns.

Against the Motion (Disagreeing):

Tribalism destroys national unity and peace.

It promotes hatred and discrimination in employment and leadership.

Cultism affects mostly individuals, but tribalism divides a nation.
EVALUATION:

What is an argumentative essay?

List three features of an argumentative essay.

Mention two arguments for the motion.

Mention two arguments against the motion.

What should be included in the conclusion of an argumentative essay?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write an argumentative essay of about 250 words either for or against the motion “Tribalism is a lesser evil than cultism.”
Highlight the topic sentence of each paragraph.

Identify and label the introduction and conclusion of your essay.
WEEK 4 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: MYTHS/LEGENDS – IDENTIFYING MORAL LESSONS; RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT

Meaning of Myths:
Myths are ancient traditional stories that explain natural or supernatural events. They often involve gods, spirits, or ancestors and are used to explain the origin of life, customs, or phenomena.
Meaning of Legends:
Legends are old stories about real people who performed extraordinary deeds. They are partly historical and partly imaginative.

FEATURES OF MYTHS AND LEGENDS:

They are passed down orally from generation to generation.

They involve supernatural beings or heroes with special powers.

They explain natural events or historical happenings.

They usually teach moral lessons or reflect the values of a community.

Examples:

Myth: The story of Sango, the Yoruba god of thunder, teaches that pride and anger can lead to destruction.
Legend: The story of Queen Amina of Zazzau teaches courage, leadership, and patriotism.

Myth: The Igbo story of The Tortoise and the Birds teaches against greed and deceit.

IDENTIFYING MORAL LESSONS IN MYTHS AND LEGENDS:

Good triumphs over evil.
Greed and pride lead to downfall.

Honesty and hard work bring success.

Disobedience leads to punishment.

Recommended Drama Text (Example):
In a play such as The Gods Are Not to Blame by Ola Rotimi, moral lessons are conveyed through the fate of characters who disobey divine instruction, showing that destiny and pride can bring tragedy.

EVALUATION:

Define myth and legend.

Mention three features of myths or legends.

State three moral lessons common to myths.

Mention one myth or legend from your culture and its moral lesson.

Explain one moral lesson from your recommended drama text.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a short myth or legend from your culture and state two moral lessons it teaches.

Identify any character from your recommended drama text who learned a moral lesson and explain it.
List three ways myths and legends are useful to society today.
WEEK 6 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: SYLLABLES (CONTINUED)

A syllable is the smallest unit of pronunciation in a word. It usually contains a single vowel sound, with or without surrounding consonants. Every word in English has at least one syllable.
In this lesson, we continue our study of syllables with a focus on word stress and syllable division in longer words.
Word Stress:
In English, not all syllables are pronounced with equal strength. One syllable in a word is said more strongly or loudly than the others — this is the stressed syllable. The others are unstressed.
Examples:

TAble – the first syllable is stressed.

beGIN – the second syllable is stressed.

comPUter – the second syllable is stressed.

eLECtricity – the third syllable is stressed.

Rules for Stress Placement (General Guide):

Two-syllable nouns – stress the first syllable.

TAble, WINdow, DOCtor

Two-syllable verbs – stress the second syllable.

beGIN, reLAX, aRRIVE

Longer words (3+ syllables) – stress often falls on the third syllable from the end.

eDUcation, opPORtunity, comPUter.

Dividing Words into Syllables:
Divide long words based on vowel sounds and pronunciation patterns.
Information → in/for/ma/tion (4 syllables)

Hospital → hos/pi/tal (3 syllables)

Electricity → e/lec/tri/ci/ty (5 syllables)

Importance of Syllable Awareness:

It improves pronunciation and spelling.

It helps in correct stress placement.

It aids rhythm and fluency in speech.

EVALUATION:

What is a syllable?

Define word stress.

Identify the stressed syllable in these words: banana, begin, teacher, important, opportunity.
Mention two rules for placing stress in words.

Divide these words into syllables: hospital, education, wonderful, electricity, table.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write ten English words of three or more syllables and mark the stressed syllable in each.

Explain how stress affects the meaning of words (e.g., record as a noun vs record as a verb).
Write five two-syllable nouns and five two-syllable verbs.
WEEK 6 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: EXERCISES ON PASSIVE VOICE

The passive voice is used when the focus is on the receiver of the action, not the doer. It is formed by combining the verb “to be” with the past participle of the main verb.
Structure:
Object + be (is/are/was/were) + Past Participle + by + Subject (optional) Example:
Active: The teacher praised the student.

Passive: The student was praised (by the teacher).

Step-by-Step Conversion:

Identify the subject, verb, and object.

The boy (subject) kicked (verb) the ball (object).

Move the object to the front to make it the new subject.

Add the correct form of be (is, are, was, were, etc.).

Use the past participle of the main verb.

Add the original subject (if needed) after by.

The ball was kicked by the boy.

Examples in Different Tenses:

Present tense: They clean the room → The room is cleaned.

Past tense: They built the house → The house was built.

Future tense: They will open the shop → The shop will be opened.

Present perfect: They have written the test → The test has been written.
Reasons for Using Passive Voice:

When the doer of the action is unknown or unimportant.

The letter was posted yesterday.

To emphasize the action or result rather than the doer.

The bridge was completed last year.

In formal or scientific writing.

The data were collected and analyzed.

EVALUATION:

What is the passive voice?

Write the structure of the passive voice.

Convert the following to passive voice:
The tailor sewed the dress.
The students answered the questions.

State two uses of the passive voice.

Change this sentence to active: The car was repaired by the mechanic.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five sentences in the active voice and change them to the passive voice.

Identify the tense in each passive sentence.

Explain one situation where the passive voice is preferred in writing.
WEEK 6 - READING

TOPIC: READING COMPREHENSION PRACTICE; VOCABULARY – COOKING WORDS

Reading Comprehension Practice involves understanding, interpreting, and answering questions based on a given passage. It requires identifying main ideas, supporting details, cause and effect, and vocabulary meanings from context.
Example Passage (Excerpt):
Cooking is an important daily activity in every home. It involves preparing food to make it safe and enjoyable to eat. In the kitchen, different tools such as pots, frying pans, spoons, and knives are used. Good cooking requires patience, cleanliness, and knowledge of ingredients. A person who cooks well is respected in the family because food brings people together.
Comprehension Focus:

Main Idea: The importance and process of cooking in homes.

Supporting Details: Cooking involves patience, cleanliness, and use of utensils.

Moral Lesson: Cooking is both a skill and a responsibility.

Vocabulary – Cooking Words:

Utensils: tools used in cooking, e.g., spoon, pot, knife.

Ingredients: materials used to prepare a meal, e.g., salt, pepper, oil, tomatoes.

Boil: to heat a liquid until it bubbles.

Fry: to cook in hot oil.

Stew: a dish made by cooking meat or vegetables slowly in liquid.

Simmer: to cook just below boiling point.

Recipe: a set of instructions for preparing a dish.
EVALUATION:

State the main idea of the passage.

Mention three qualities of a good cook.

Define the word “ingredient.”

Explain the difference between “boil” and “fry.”

Write two moral lessons you learned from the passage.

ASSIGNMENT:

Read any short comprehension passage about “Food and Health” and answer five questions from it.
Write ten cooking-related words and use each in a sentence.

Write a paragraph describing how to cook your favorite meal.
WEEK 6 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: ARGUMENTATIVE ESSAY – CULTISM ERODES TRADITIONAL VALUES

Meaning of Argumentative Essay:
An argumentative essay presents two opposing opinions about a topic. The writer must choose one side and support it with reasons, examples, and evidence.
Topic Discussion: Cultism Erodes Traditional Values

FOR THE MOTION (Agreeing):

Cultism destroys respect for elders and authority. In many schools, cult members disobey teachers and parents.
It promotes violence and hatred. Traditional values such as peace, unity, and love are replaced by rivalry and fear.
Cultism encourages immorality. Members engage in evil acts like fighting, stealing, and drug use, contrary to traditional discipline.
It breaks community harmony. Traditional societies valued togetherness, but cultism divides students and groups.
AGAINST THE MOTION (Disagreeing):

Traditional values are already declining due to modernization, not cultism alone.

Some people join cults to seek protection or belonging, not to destroy values.

The real problem lies in poor moral upbringing and lack of parental guidance.

Features of an Argumentative Essay:

Introduction stating your stand.

Logical arguments supported by examples.

Counter-arguments and rebuttals.

Conclusion restating your position.

EVALUATION:

Define an argumentative essay.

Mention three features of an argumentative essay.

Give two points for the motion.

Give two points against the motion.

What should be included in the conclusion?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write an essay of about 250 words arguing for or against the motion “Cultism erodes traditional values.”
Underline your topic sentence in each paragraph.

Highlight one argument you consider your strongest and explain why.
WEEK 6 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: POETRY – MEANING OF SELECTED POEM; IDENTIFICATION OF FIGURES OF SPEECH

Meaning of Poetry:
Poetry is a form of literary expression that uses carefully chosen words arranged in lines and stanzas to convey emotions, ideas, or experiences. Poets often use figures of speech, imagery, and sound devices to make their poems more expressive and musical.
Meaning of a Poem:
To find the meaning of a poem, readers must understand:

The subject matter (what the poem is about).

The theme (the central idea or message).

The tone (the poet’s feeling or attitude).

Selected Poem (Example):
“Peace”

Peace is like a gentle dove, Flying softly high above.
It calms the hearts of those who fight, And fills the world with shining light.
Meaning of the Poem:
The poem describes peace as a gentle and precious quality that brings calmness and unity to the world. The poet uses the image of a dove, which symbolizes love, purity, and harmony.
Identification of Figures of Speech:

Simile: Peace is like a gentle dove – compares peace to a dove using “like.”

Personification: It calms the hearts of those who fight – peace is given human qualities.

Imagery: shining light – appeals to the sense of sight.
Rhyme: dove/above and fight/light create musicality.

Themes Found in the Poem:

The value of peace in society.

The power of love and unity.

The hope for a better world.

EVALUATION:

What is poetry?

State the theme of the poem “Peace.”

Identify two figures of speech used in the poem.

Explain the imagery used in the poem.

Why is rhyme important in poetry?

ASSIGNMENT:

Select any short poem and write out its central theme.

Identify three figures of speech used in your selected poem.

Write a short four-line poem about “Peace” or “Unity” using at least one figure of speech.
WEEK 8 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: CONSONANT CLUSTERS (ASKS, SCHOOL, THRIVE, PLEASE, RESPECT)

A consonant cluster is a group of two or more consonant sounds that appear together in a word without any vowel sound between them. These clusters can occur at the beginning, middle, or end of words.
Definition:
A consonant cluster is a combination of consonant sounds pronounced closely together, such that each sound is heard distinctly.
Types of Consonant Clusters:

Initial clusters: occur at the beginning of a word.

Examples: school (sk), thrive (thr), please (pl), respect (sp), blue (bl), drive (dr).

Medial clusters: occur in the middle of a word.

Examples: respect (sp), transport (ns), complain (mpl).

Final clusters: occur at the end of a word.

Examples: asks (sks), helps (lps), camps (mps), months (nθs).

Pronunciation Practice:
Students should pronounce each consonant distinctly but smoothly without inserting extra vowels.
Asks → /æsks/ (not /askiz/)

School → /skuːl/

Thrive → /θraɪv/

Please → /pliːz/

Respect → /rɪˈspekt/
Common Difficulties:
Learners often insert vowels between consonants, especially in clusters like str-, spl-, thr-, skr-, etc.
For instance:

School (not “iskool”)

Thrive (not “tirive”)

Respect (not “irespect”).

Importance of Learning Clusters:

It improves pronunciation and fluency.

It enhances spelling accuracy.

It helps in correct reading of English words.

EVALUATION:

What is a consonant cluster?

Identify the cluster in each of these words: school, thrive, respect, blue, asks.

Give two examples of initial and final consonant clusters.

Explain one pronunciation challenge of clusters.

Pronounce these words correctly: please, thrive, scream, camps, months.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write ten English words containing consonant clusters and underline the clusters.

Record yourself pronouncing the words school, respect, thrive, helps, and asks for self- assessment.
State three reasons why learning consonant clusters is important in speech.

WEEK 8 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: STATEMENTS AND TAG QUESTIONS

A statement is a sentence that expresses a fact, opinion, or idea. It usually ends with a full stop. A tag question is a short question added to the end of a statement, often to seek agreement or confirmation.
Examples of Statements:

She is a teacher.

They play football every evening.

The weather is hot today.

Structure of Tag Questions:
A tag question consists of an auxiliary verb and a pronoun that refers to the subject of the main sentence.
Basic Rules:

If the statement is positive, the tag is negative.

She is a teacher, isn’t she?

They play football, don’t they?

If the statement is negative, the tag is positive.

She isn’t here, is she?

They don’t like rice, do they?

The auxiliary verb in the tag must agree with the tense of the main verb.

He was at school, wasn’t he?

They will come, won’t they?

Uses of Tag Questions:
To confirm information: You’re coming, aren’t you?

To show politeness: It’s a lovely day, isn’t it?

To express doubt or surprise: You didn’t see him, did you?

Common Errors:

Using the wrong auxiliary verb: He can swim, isn’t he? ❌ → He can swim, can’t he? ✅

Using the wrong pronoun: Mary is kind, isn’t it? ❌ → Mary is kind, isn’t she? ✅

EVALUATION:

What is a statement?

Define a tag question.

Change these into tag questions:
She is very tall.
They have gone home.
He cannot drive.

Mention two uses of tag questions.

Correct this error: They will come, isn’t it?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five statements and attach correct tag questions.

Explain the rule for forming negative tags.

Write two reasons why people use tag questions in speech.
WEEK 8 - READING

TOPIC: IDENTIFYING TOPIC SENTENCES; VOCABULARY – TEACHING WORDS

A topic sentence expresses the main idea of a paragraph. It tells what the paragraph is about. It can appear at the beginning, middle, or end of a paragraph.
Characteristics of a Good Topic Sentence:

It states one main idea clearly.

It is general enough to allow for supporting details.

It gives direction to the paragraph.

Example:

Teaching is one of the most important professions in the world.
Supporting details may then explain why teaching is important.

Identifying Topic Sentences:
To identify a topic sentence, look for:

The sentence that captures the main idea.

Sentences that other sentences explain or support.

Example Paragraph:
Teachers play an important role in the development of every nation. They prepare students for future leadership by teaching knowledge and values. Without teachers, society cannot progress.
Topic Sentence: “Teachers play an important role in the development of every nation.”

Vocabulary – Teaching Words:

WordMeaningExampleTeacherA person who educates studentsThe teacher explained the lesson clearly.LessonA period of instructionThe first lesson is on Mathematics.ClassroomA room where students learnOur classroom is clean and bright.SubjectA branch of learningEnglish is my favorite subject.HomeworkWork assigned to be done at homeWe submitted our homework this morning.Pupil/StudentA learner in a schoolThe pupils listened attentively.

EVALUATION:

What is a topic sentence?

Identify the topic sentence in this paragraph: Education shapes the future of every child. It teaches discipline and knowledge. It helps individuals become useful citizens.
Write three features of a good topic sentence.

Mention three teaching-related words and their meanings.

Use “teacher” and “lesson” in meaningful sentences.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a short paragraph on “My Best Teacher” and underline the topic sentence.

List five teaching-related words and use each in a sentence.

Explain why identifying topic sentences is important in reading comprehension.
WEEK 8 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: DIFFERENCES BETWEEN ARGUMENTATIVE AND EXPOSITORY ESSAYS

Both argumentative and expository essays aim to inform, but they differ in purpose, tone, and method.
Argumentative Essay:

The writer takes a stand on an issue (for or against).

It involves persuasion using reasons, facts, and examples.

The writer presents both sides but supports one.

Example Topic: School uniforms should be abolished.

For: Uniforms limit freedom.

Against: Uniforms promote equality.

Expository Essay:

The writer explains or describes something.

It gives information without taking sides.

It uses facts, definitions, and examples.

Example Topic: The Importance of Good Eating Habits.

Explains what good eating habits are and their benefits.

Key Differences Between Argumentative and Expository Essays:

FeatureArgumentative EssayExpository EssayPurposeTo argue or persuadeTo explain or describeToneSubjective and persuasiveObjective and informativeStructurePresents two sides of an issueExplains one topic logicallyLanguageUses emotional and logical appealUses clear, factual languageExamplesFor/Against Cultism, Capital
PunishmentHow Rain Is Formed, The Effects of
Pollution

EVALUATION:

Define an argumentative essay.

Define an expository essay.

Mention two differences between both types of essays.

Write one topic suitable for each essay type.

Why is an argumentative essay persuasive in nature?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write an expository essay on “How to Prevent Malaria.”

Write an argumentative essay on “Television does more harm than good.”

List four major differences between argumentative and expository essays.
WEEK 8 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT – WRITE A SHORT PLAY OR STORY

Meaning of Drama:
Drama is a story written to be performed by actors on stage. It involves characters, dialogue, actions, and conflict.
Elements of Drama:

Characters: The people in the play.

Setting: Where and when the story happens.

Dialogue: The words spoken by the characters.

Plot: The sequence of events.

Theme: The main idea or message of the play.

Writing a Short Play:
When writing a short play, include the following:

Title: e.g., Respect for Elders

Characters: e.g., Father, Mother, Bayo, Teacher.

Setting: In a school compound.
Dialogue Example: Sample Scene:
Title: Respect for Elders
Characters: Teacher, Bayo, Mother

Teacher: Bayo, why did you walk away when your mother called you?
Bayo: I was in a hurry, sir.
Mother: You must learn to respect elders, my son.
Bayo: I’m sorry, mother. I won’t do that again.
Teacher: Good. Respect brings peace to the home and society.

Theme: The importance of respect and good manners.

Features of a Good Play:

Short and clear dialogue.

Teaches moral lessons.

Realistic actions and characters.

EVALUATION:

Define drama.

Mention four elements of a play.

State the theme of the sample play above.

Write two lines of dialogue between a teacher and a student.

Why is dialogue important in a play?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write a short three-character play titled “Honesty Pays.”

Identify the theme of your play.

Mention two lessons that readers can learn from it.
WEEK 9 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: WORD BOUNDARIES – LINKING SOUNDS (MATTER OF FACT, BREAD AND BUTTER, POOR AND BLIND)

In connected speech, English speakers often link words together to make speech flow smoothly. This is known as linking sounds or connected speech. The boundaries between words are not always clearly heard; instead, one word runs into the next.
Meaning of Word Boundaries:
Word boundaries are the places where one word ends and another begins in speech. When we speak naturally, we do not pause between every word; instead, we link the final sound of one word with the beginning sound of the next.
Linking Sounds:
Linking happens when the final consonant of one word is joined with the initial vowel sound of the next word.
Examples:

Matter of fact → /ˈmætər əv fækt/ (sounds like matər əv fækt)

Bread and butter → /bred ən bʌtə/ (sounds like bredan bʌtə)

Poor and blind → /pɔːr ən blaɪnd/ (sounds like pooran blaind)

Types of Linking:

Consonant to Vowel Linking:

Look at it → /luk æt ɪt/

Go on → /gəʊ wɒn/

Vowel to Vowel Linking (with /j/ or /w/ sound):

He is → /hiː jɪz/

Go out → /gəʊ waʊt/
Why Linking Is Important:

It makes speech sound natural and fluent.

It improves listening comprehension.

It reduces unnatural pauses between words.

EVALUATION:

What are word boundaries?

Define linking sounds.

Identify the linking sounds in: bread and butter, matter of fact, poor and blind.

Mention two benefits of linking sounds.

Give two examples of consonant-to-vowel linking.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five examples of linked expressions in English.

Record and listen to yourself pronouncing bread and butter, matter of fact, poor and blind.
Explain why word boundaries are important in fluent speech.
WEEK 9 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: DIFFERENTIATING BETWEEN POLAR AND TAG QUESTIONS

Meaning of Questions:
Questions are sentences used to ask for information. English questions can be grouped into polar questions and tag questions.
Polar Questions:
These are questions that require only two possible answers — Yes or No.

Examples:

Are you coming?

Did she pass the test?

Will it rain today?

Has he eaten?

Features of Polar Questions:

Begin with auxiliary verbs (is, are, do, did, will, have, etc.).

Expect short answers like Yes, I am or No, I’m not.

Used for confirmation, inquiry, or curiosity.

Tag Questions:
A tag question is a short question added at the end of a statement to seek agreement or confirmation.
She is a nurse, isn’t she?

They didn’t come, did they?

You can drive, can’t you?

Differences Between Polar and Tag Questions:
FeaturePolar QuestionTag QuestionFormBegins a sentenceComes at the end of a statementAnswer TypeYes/NoAgreement/ConfirmationExampleIs he ready?He is ready, isn’t he?UseTo ask for informationTo confirm or agree

EVALUATION:

Define a polar question.

Define a tag question.

Give two examples of each.

State two differences between polar and tag questions.

Complete this: You were there yesterday,	?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write three polar questions and their expected answers.

Convert these to tag questions:
You are happy.
She can sing.
They didn’t come.

Explain why tag questions are used in conversation.
WEEK 9 - READING

TOPIC: READING COMPREHENSION PRACTICE; VOCABULARY – SPELLING DRILLS

Reading Comprehension Practice:
Comprehension passages test understanding of ideas, vocabulary, tone, and inferences. Students should read carefully to identify:
Main idea

Supporting details

Meaning of new words

Sample Passage (Excerpt):
Tree planting is one of the best ways to protect the environment. Trees provide oxygen, shade, and prevent erosion. They also make the air fresh and reduce heat. Without trees, the earth would become too hot for living things.
Questions:

What is the main idea of the passage?

Mention two benefits of trees.

What would happen without trees?

Vocabulary – Spelling Drills:
Correct spelling helps improve writing and pronunciation.

Commonly Misspelled Words:

receive, separate, believe, environment, beginning, tomorrow, occurred, necessary, government, beautiful.
Tips for Correct Spelling:

Use syllable division: en-vi-ron-ment.
Remember spelling rules: “i before e except after c.” (believe, receive)

Practice regularly.

EVALUATION:

What is the main idea of the passage on tree planting?

Mention two benefits of trees.

Spell correctly: recieve, enviroment, tommorow, goverment.

State two reasons why correct spelling is important.

What does the rule “i before e except after c” mean?

ASSIGNMENT:

Copy and correctly spell ten common English words often misspelled.

Write a short passage about “Why We Should Plant Trees.”

Read a comprehension passage in your textbook and answer five questions from it.
WEEK 9 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: EXPOSITORY ESSAY – HOW TO PLANT TREES

Meaning of Expository Essay:
An expository essay explains how something works or how something is done. It gives clear information without trying to argue.
Example Topic: How to Plant Trees

Outline:

Introduction – Importance of tree planting.

Steps in planting trees.

Benefits of tree planting.

Conclusion – Encourage everyone to plant trees.

Sample Essay:
Tree planting helps protect the environment and beautify the surroundings. To plant a tree, first, choose good soil and dig a hole about one foot deep. Next, place the young plant in the hole and cover it with soil. Water the plant regularly and protect it from animals. Trees provide shade, prevent erosion, and produce oxygen. Everyone should plant at least one tree to make the world a better place.

EVALUATION:

Define an expository essay.

State three steps involved in planting trees.

Mention two benefits of tree planting.

What is the purpose of an expository essay?

Write one sentence from the essay that shows conclusion.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write an essay titled How to Keep the Environment Clean.

List four steps in tree planting.

Write two sentences explaining why trees are important.
WEEK 9 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: RECOMMENDED DRAMA TEXT – USING SIMILES AND METAPHORS IN SENTENCES

Revision of Figures of Speech:

Simile: A direct comparison using like or as.

He is as brave as a lion.

Metaphor: An indirect comparison without like or as.

He is a lion in battle.

Application in Drama:
Writers use similes and metaphors to make dialogue more vivid.

Examples in Dialogue:

Mother: “My son works like a machine!” (Simile)

Teacher: “Knowledge is light.” (Metaphor)

Exercises:
Convert similes to metaphors and vice versa:

As white as snow → Snow-white (metaphor).

He is a rock → He is as strong as a rock.

EVALUATION:

Define simile.

Define metaphor.

Give two examples of each.
Convert these similes to metaphors:
As wise as an owl.
As busy as a bee.

Why do writers use these figures of speech?

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five similes and five metaphors.

Create a short dialogue using at least two similes.

Convert three of your similes to metaphors.
WEEK 10 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: CONTRASTING DIPHTHONGS /Eɪ/ VS /ɪƏ/ (DATE/HERE), (LATE/CHEER)

A diphthong is a vowel sound formed by the combination of two different vowel sounds within one syllable. The tongue glides from one position to another while pronouncing it.
/eɪ/ Sound:

Glide from /e/ to /ɪ/

Found in words like: date, late, make, name, face, rain, play, day.
Examples in Sentences:

I will play with my mate.

The rain came late.

/ɪə/ Sound:

Glide from /ɪ/ to /ə/

Found in words like: here, cheer, fear, near, appear, career, idea.
Examples in Sentences:

We sat near the window.

She felt fear in the dark.

Contrasting the Two:

/eɪ//ɪə/datedearlateleergategearmalemerefadefear

EVALUATION:

What is a diphthong?

Give three examples of words with /eɪ/.

Give three examples of words with /ɪə/.

Differentiate between /eɪ/ and /ɪə/.

Pronounce these pairs: date/dear, late/leer, play/peer.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five word pairs contrasting /eɪ/ and /ɪə/.

Use three of them in sentences.

Record and listen to yourself pronouncing both sounds.
WEEK 10 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: EXERCISE ON ADVERBIALS

Meaning of Adverbials
An adverbial is a word, phrase, or clause that gives more information about a verb, an adjective, or another adverb. It tells us how, when, where, or why something happens.

Adverbials perform the function of adverbs in sentences, but unlike single-word adverbs, adverbials can be phrases or clauses.

Examples:

She sings beautifully. (adverb)
She sings with great passion. (adverbial phrase)
She sings because she is happy. (adverbial clause)

Types of Adverbials

There are four main types of adverbials according to the kind of information they give:

Adverbials of Time – tell when something happens.
Examples: yesterday, last week, soon, in the morning, after dinner.
Sentence examples:
We shall travel tomorrow.
He arrived in the evening.
Adverbials of Place – tell where something happens.
Examples: here, there, everywhere, at home, in the market, to school.
Sentence examples:
The children are playing outside.
She lives in Lagos.
Adverbials of Manner – tell how something happens.
Examples: quickly, slowly, carefully, with joy, in anger.
Sentence examples:
The boy ran quickly.
She spoke with confidence.
Adverbials of Reason or Purpose – tell why something happens.
Examples: because of hunger, to get food, in order to learn.
Sentence examples:
He left early to catch the bus.
She is crying because she lost her book.

Other subtypes include:

Adverbials of Frequency: always, often, seldom, rarely, sometimes.
Adverbials of Degree: very, quite, almost, too, rather.

Forms of Adverbials

FormExampleSentenceSingle-word adverbquickly, slowlyHe ran quickly.Adverbial phrasein the morning, with careShe reads in the morning.Adverbial clausebecause he was lateHe ran because he was late.

Position of Adverbials in Sentences
Adverbials can appear:

At the beginning: Yesterday, we went to the zoo.
In the middle: We usually eat rice on Sundays.
At the end: She danced gracefully.

Notes:
Adverbials of manner, place, and time often come at the end of sentences.
Example: She sang sweetly at the concert yesterday.
When there is more than one adverbial, the usual order is:
Manner → Place → Time
Example: She danced gracefully (manner) in the hall (place) yesterday (time).

Common Errors with Adverbials

Misplacing the adverbial in a sentence.
❌ He quickly speaks English.
✅ He speaks English quickly.
Using double negatives with adverbials of frequency.
❌ He doesn’t go nowhere.
✅ He doesn’t go anywhere.
Confusing manner and reason adverbials.
❌ She left carefully she was tired.
✅ She left because she was tired.

Exercises on Adverbials

Identify the adverbials and state their types:

She will return next week.
The dog slept under the table.
He smiled with joy.
They waited because the teacher was coming.
We go to church every Sunday.

Fill in the blanks with suitable adverbials:
He reads his Bible	.
They are working	the classroom.
The meeting started	.
The girl danced	the music stopped.
The children shouted	the teacher entered.

Combine these sentences using adverbials:

She cried. She lost her book.
→ She cried because she lost her book.
He walked. He was in a hurry.
→ He walked quickly.
The students are learning. They are in the library.
→ The students are learning in the library.

EVALUATION:

Define an adverbial.
State four main types of adverbials and give two examples of each.
Differentiate between an adverb and an adverbial.
Identify the adverbials in this sentence: The boy ran quickly to school yesterday.
Arrange the adverbials in correct order of manner, place, and time.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write ten sentences using at least one adverbial in each. Underline the adverbials.
Write three sentences using adverbial phrases and three using adverbial clauses.
Explain why correct placement of adverbials is important in English sentences.
WEEK 10 - READING

TOPIC: READING COMPREHENSION PRACTICE; VOCABULARY – SPELLING DRILLS (CONTINUED)

Reading Comprehension Practice Meaning of Comprehension:
Comprehension means understanding what is read. It is the ability to make meaning from written text by identifying the main ideas, supporting details, and the writer’s intention. Reading comprehension develops vocabulary, thinking ability, and general knowledge.
Purpose of Reading Comprehension:

To understand the meaning of a text.

To answer questions based on what is read.

To develop the ability to infer or draw conclusions.

To improve spelling, grammar, and general language use.

Skills Involved in Reading Comprehension:

Skimming: Reading quickly to get the general idea of a passage.

Scanning: Reading quickly to locate specific information.

Detailed Reading: Reading carefully to understand all ideas and information.

Inferring: Drawing conclusions or hidden meanings from what is read.

Summarizing: Giving a brief statement of the main points in a passage.

Steps in Answering Comprehension Questions:

Read the passage carefully at least twice.
Identify the main idea and key details.

Note unfamiliar words and their meanings from context.

Read the questions thoroughly and understand what is asked.

Answer in complete sentences, using your own words where possible.

Check for correct spelling, grammar, and punctuation.

Example Practice Passage (Excerpt):

The government of Nigeria has continued to encourage tree planting across the nation. Trees help to prevent erosion, provide shade, and improve the quality of air. In schools and communities, pupils are encouraged to plant and nurture trees as part of environmental awareness campaigns.

Sample Questions:

What does the passage say about tree planting?

Mention two benefits of trees according to the passage.

Why does the government encourage pupils to plant trees?

Find the meaning of “nurture” as used in the passage.

Suggest a suitable title for this passage.

Tips for Effective Comprehension Reading:

Read in a quiet environment.

Avoid reading word by word—read in sense groups.

Keep a dictionary nearby to learn new words.
Read different types of texts: stories, articles, letters, and reports.

Vocabulary – Spelling Drills (Continued) Meaning of Vocabulary:
Vocabulary refers to the total number of words a person knows and uses correctly in speech and writing. A rich vocabulary helps students express themselves more clearly and understand texts better.
Meaning of Spelling:
Spelling is the correct arrangement of letters in a word. Spelling drills are exercises that help learners master the accurate spelling of words.
Importance of Good Spelling:

It improves written communication.

It prevents misunderstanding in writing.

It increases confidence in reading and writing.

It helps in word pronunciation and vocabulary building.

Commonly Misspelled Words (Revision and Continuation):

Wrong SpellingCorrect SpellingMeaningrecievereceiveto get somethingoccuredoccurredhappenedenviromentenvironmentsurroundingsbeleivebelieveto accept as truegovermentgovernmentsystem of rulingdefinatedefinitecertain, clearseperateseparatedivide or set apartaccomodateaccommodateto provide lodgingneccessarynecessaryrequired, neededtommorowtomorrowthe next day

Techniques for Improving Spelling:

Look, Say, Cover, Write, and Check Method:

Look at the word carefully.

Say it aloud.

Cover it.

Write it from memory.

Check your spelling.

Use the word in a sentence to help remember its meaning.

Break words into syllables for easier memorization.

Example: ac-com-mo-date, sep-a-rate.

Write difficult words repeatedly until correct spelling is mastered.

Keep a personal spelling book of words you find challenging.

Spelling Drill Practice:

Write each of the following words correctly three times: environment, occurred, government, necessary, believe.
Use each of these words in a sentence of your own.

Find two new words you often misspell and learn their correct spellings.

EVALUATION
Define comprehension and explain its purpose.

List and explain three skills involved in reading comprehension.

Write down three steps to follow when answering comprehension questions.

Explain what spelling means and why it is important.

Correct the following misspellings: recieve, goverment, seperate, definate, tommorow.

ASSIGNMENT

Read any short passage from your English textbook and summarize it in five sentences.

Write ten words you find difficult to spell correctly, and write their correct spellings.

Use five of the corrected words in meaningful sentences of your own.
WEEK 10 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: ORAL COMPOSITION – SCHOOL RULES

Oral composition involves speaking clearly, fluently, and confidently on a given topic. It helps to develop public speaking and communication skills.
Topic Discussion – School Rules
Every school has rules to guide students’ behavior. These rules promote order, discipline, and safety. Examples of common school rules include:
Students must be punctual.

Uniforms must be neat.

No fighting or bullying.

Respect teachers and prefects.

Keep the school clean.

Speaking Tips:

Speak loudly and clearly.

Use correct grammar and simple words.

Maintain eye contact with your audience.

Organize your ideas in order.

EVALUATION:

Define oral composition.

Mention three common school rules.

Why are school rules important?

State two qualities of a good speaker.
Mention one way oral composition improves communication.

ASSIGNMENT:

Prepare a 1-minute oral presentation on “Why I Obey School Rules.”

Write five rules you would make if you were a prefect.

Record and listen to your presentation for clarity and fluency.
WEEK 10 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: DRAMATIZATION OF SCENES FROM DRAMA TEXT; TURNING SIMILES TO METAPHORS AND VICE VERSA

Dramatization:
Dramatization means acting out parts of a story or play. It helps students understand character roles, tone, and expression.
Example Scene:
From a play about honesty:

Teacher: Who took the missing money?
Student: It was me, sir. I’m sorry.
Teacher: I forgive you. Always tell the truth.

Theme: Honesty and forgiveness.

Turning Similes to Metaphors and Vice Versa:

As brave as a lion → He is a lion in battle.

As gentle as a dove → She is a dove.

He is a rock → He is as strong as a rock.

The world is a stage → Life is like a play.

EVALUATION:

What is dramatization?

Mention one benefit of dramatization.

Convert these:
As fast as lightning →
He is a tiger →
What lesson is learned from dramatization?

State one difference between a simile and a metaphor.

ASSIGNMENT:

Act out a short play about “Truthfulness.”

Write five pairs of similes and metaphors.

Identify the theme in your play.
WEEK 11 - SPEECH WORK

TOPIC: CONTRASTING /ƱƏ/ VS /OƱ/ (TOUR/POLE)

A diphthong is a combination of two vowel sounds pronounced together in one syllable, where the tongue glides from one position to another. In this lesson, we focus on the contrast between
/ʊə/ and /oʊ/.

The /ʊə/ Sound:

This sound begins with /ʊ/ and glides to /ə/.

It is commonly found in words spelt with “our”, “oor”, “ure”, or “ure.”

It occurs in British English, but in American English, /ʊə/ is often replaced with a pure vowel /ɔː/ or /ʊr/.
Examples:

tour /tʊə/

sure /ʃʊə/

poor /pʊə/

cure /kjʊə/

endure /ɪnˈdjʊə/

The /oʊ/ Sound:

This sound begins with /o/ and glides to /ʊ/.

It is heard in words with spellings like “o”, “oa”, “ow”, “oe”, “old.”

It is common in both British and American English.

Examples:

pole /poʊl/

go /goʊ/
home /hoʊm/

boat /boʊt/

window /ˈwɪndoʊ/

Contrast Between /ʊə/ and /oʊ/:

/ʊə//oʊ/tourtoepoorpolesureshowcurecoatpurepole

Pronunciation Tips:

/ʊə/: The lips are rounded, then relaxed.

/oʊ/: The lips start mid-rounded and become more rounded.

Practice slowly, then increase fluency.

EVALUATION:

Define a diphthong.

Differentiate between /ʊə/ and /oʊ/ in pronunciation.

Give three examples of words with /ʊə/.

Give three examples of words with /oʊ/.

Pronounce these pairs: tour/toe, sure/show, cure/coat.
ASSIGNMENT:

Write five pairs of words contrasting /ʊə/ and /oʊ/.

Use three pairs in short sentences.

Record yourself pronouncing all examples correctly.
WEEK 11 - GRAMMAR

TOPIC: REVISION – NOUNS, PRONOUNS, VERBS, ADJECTIVES

This revision covers four key parts of speech previously taught.

Nouns:
A noun is a name of a person, place, animal, thing, or idea.

Types of Nouns:

Proper Nouns: Names of specific people or places (Nigeria, Musa, Abuja).

Common Nouns: General names (boy, city, car).

Abstract Nouns: Names of ideas (love, truth, beauty).

Collective Nouns: Names of groups (flock, team, class).

Examples in Sentences:

Musa lives in Abuja. (Proper Noun)

The team won the match. (Collective Noun)

Pronouns:
Pronouns are words used instead of nouns to avoid repetition.

Types:

Personal Pronouns: I, you, he, she, it, we, they

Possessive Pronouns: mine, yours, hers, ours, theirs

Reflexive Pronouns: myself, himself, herself

Relative Pronouns: who, which, that

Example:

John said he was tired. (“he” replaces “John”)
Verbs:
A verb shows action or state of being.

Action Verbs: run, jump, write

Linking Verbs: is, seem, appear

Auxiliary Verbs: have, do, will, can

Examples:

She runs every morning.

He is a teacher.

Adjectives:
Adjectives describe nouns or pronouns.
They tell quality, quantity, number, size, or color. Examples:
A tall man.

Three girls came late.

The red car is fast.

EVALUATION:

What is a noun? Give two examples.

Mention three types of pronouns.

State two kinds of verbs.

What is an adjective? Give two examples.

Identify the adjectives in this sentence: The old man bought three large bags.
ASSIGNMENT:

Write five sentences showing the use of nouns, pronouns, verbs, and adjectives.

Underline all verbs in your sentences.

Classify each noun in your sentences as common, proper, or abstract.
WEEK 11 - READING

TOPIC: READING FOR EVALUATION

Meaning of Reading for Evaluation:
Reading for evaluation means reading a passage critically to judge the ideas, arguments, and opinions expressed. The reader must identify what is good, bad, or questionable in the text.
Purpose of Reading for Evaluation:

To form opinions based on facts.

To identify bias or exaggeration.

To judge the logic or reasonableness of ideas.

Steps to Reading for Evaluation:

Read carefully and understand the author’s viewpoint.

Identify evidence or examples used.

Decide whether the argument is balanced or one-sided.

Form your own opinion and support it with reasons.

Example Passage (Excerpt):
"Television has both advantages and disadvantages. It educates, informs, and entertains. However, too much television can lead to laziness and poor academic performance."
Sample Evaluation:
The writer presents both good and bad sides, showing a balanced argument. The passage is reasonable because it warns against excessive watching while recognizing its benefits.

EVALUATION:

What is reading for evaluation?

Mention two purposes of reading for evaluation.
List three steps in evaluating a passage.

What is the author’s opinion in the passage about television?

Do you agree or disagree with the writer’s point? Why?

ASSIGNMENT:

Read a passage on “The Use of Mobile Phones by Students” and write whether you agree or disagree with the writer.
State two benefits and two disadvantages of mobile phones.

Identify one example of bias in the passage, if any.
WEEK 11 - COMPOSITION

TOPIC: REVISION OF ALL ESSAY TYPES

Major Types of Essays:

Narrative Essay: Tells a story.
Example: The Day I Got Lost.

Descriptive Essay: Describes a person, place, or thing.
Example: My Best Friend.

Expository Essay: Explains or gives information.
Example: How to Prepare for an Examination.

Argumentative Essay: Argues for or against a point of view.
Example: Boarding Schools Are Better Than Day Schools.

Differences Between Essay Types:

TypePurposeExampleNarrativeTo tell a storyMy First Day in SchoolDescriptiveTo describe vividlyMy VillageExpositoryTo explainHow to Stay HealthyArgumentativeTo convincePhones Should Be Banned in Class

Essay Writing Tips:

Begin with a clear introduction.

Use paragraphs for organization.

Include examples or evidence.

Conclude neatly.

Check grammar and spelling.

EVALUATION:

Mention four main types of essays.

What is the main purpose of an argumentative essay?

State two features of an expository essay.

Give one example each of narrative and descriptive essay topics.

List three tips for good essay writing.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write one paragraph each on the four types of essays.

Write an expository essay titled How to Manage Time.

Identify the essay type used in your English textbook passage.
WEEK 11 - LITERATURE IN ENGLISH

TOPIC: FIGURES OF SPEECH – EUPHEMISM AND PUN

Euphemism:
Euphemism is a mild or polite expression used in place of a harsh or unpleasant one. It helps to soften sensitive messages.
Examples:

He passed away (instead of He died).

She is expecting a baby (instead of She is pregnant).

They are economically disadvantaged (instead of They are poor).

Functions of Euphemism:

To show respect and politeness.

To reduce the emotional effect of unpleasant information.

To maintain social harmony.

Pun:
A pun is a play on words that have similar sounds but different meanings, often used to create humor or wit.
Examples:

I used to be a banker but I lost interest.

Time flies like an arrow; fruit flies like a banana.

The tallest building in town is the library — it has the most stories.

Differences Between Euphemism and Pun:

FeatureEuphemismPunPurposeTo be polite or gentleTo be humorous or wittyExample“He passed on.”“I lost interest (in banking).”

EVALUATION:

What is a euphemism?

Give three examples of euphemism.

What is a pun?

Give two examples of puns.

State one difference between euphemism and pun.

ASSIGNMENT:

Write five sentences using euphemisms.

Create three short jokes or puns.

Explain why writers use euphemisms and puns in literature.
